The Unsigned Book, Part One

No One in Particular

Chapter 1. The Vacancy

There was no reason at all for how I came to sit in that seat.

That's the first thing anyone reading this record should know, so I'm putting it in the first line. I wasn't chosen. I wasn't recommended. I never sat an exam. The person who'd been sitting there quit, and the next number on the roster was mine. That's all there is to it.

I tried, later, to rewrite that sentence any number of times. Meaning I tried to find a slightly more plausible reason. I couldn't. So I decided to leave it as it was. There are a few things in this book I left uncorrected, and this is the first one.

I should talk about the Equity first.

This is a city where nobody gets wronged. That isn't something the city says about itself — it's something anyone who's lived here says. I say it too. I still say it.

Nobody stands in line. There's no one you need to know. The loud never win and the quiet never get pushed aside. In sixteen years of living here I've never once seen a person ask another person for a favor. There's no one to ask.

It's easier to understand if I explain how the word "favor" is used in this city. We only use "please" when handing something over. Please pass the salt. Please hold the door. It doesn't go beyond that. There's no occasion for it to. There's no way here of talking to a person to make something happen, or not happen.

I grew up thinking this was normal. When I was fourteen, I remember a kid who'd come from outside saying, "I'll talk to my uncle about it," and not understanding what she meant. What happens if you talk to your uncle? She heard that question and looked at me for a long time.

People from outside can't stand this at first. They say people get angry because they have nowhere to direct their anger. Then about half a year in, they go quiet. Not because they've realized there's nowhere to direct it, but because there really is less to be angry about.

This isn't said with irony. I never once came to hate this city. If you read this record to the end, you'll see — I never managed to hate it to the end. It would have been much easier if I could have.

What runs this city is the Consensus.

To explain what the Consensus is, I'd have to talk about other cities, and I've never been to another city, so I'll only write what I've heard. In some cities, they say, there's something that measures people. Weighs them, or ranks their standing, or puts a price on them. So in that city, they said, people split into those who measure and those who are measured.

The Consensus doesn't measure. What the Consensus does is gather.

When a case comes up, the Consensus gathers everything connected to it. Records, precedent, opinion, objection, statistics. It gathers it all and produces the will of the whole. That becomes the decision.

Which means no one hands down the decision. It's the will of the whole. And the whole includes me, and it includes my next-door neighbor, and it includes people I've never once seen.

I first heard this explained at school when I was ten. At the time I thought it was an extremely humble system. Because it doesn't claim to be right. Because it doesn't call itself a judgment.

I still don't think that thought was entirely wrong.

I have to talk about my mother, but I'll keep it short.

My mother stopped when I was eleven. She was sick a long time. While she was sick, she kept writing something. I thought it was something like a will, and I was too frightened to look at it.

I was eleven then, and I don't remember where I'd picked up the word "will." But I remember that the word frightened me. It meant the last thing a person says. I thought that once I heard the last thing, there would truly be nothing more, ever, after.

So I sat beside my mother while she wrote and looked somewhere else. My mother knew that and said nothing about it.

After my mother stopped, I was given those papers. Twelve pages. I read them one page at a time and gave up on the third page. There wasn't much to them. Where things in the house were kept, when to bring out the winter clothes, which house to buy rice from. Things like that.

At the end of the third page it said this: "That should be enough to get through winter." I read that sentence and closed the pages. I thought, so this is a paper about getting through winter. I thought that was all there was.

I still think, even now, about why my mother wrote in that order. Was she someone who put the important things last? Or did it just end up that way as she wrote? Not being able to ask my mother is the thing I resent most about this part.

I put it away in a drawer.

Two years later, looking for something else, I took it out again. And this time I read all twelve pages.

On the last line of the last page it said this:

"Hora, it doesn't matter to me what work you do. I only hope you'll be someone who reads to the end. I missed a great deal because I wasn't."

I had left that line unread for two years.

I didn't know, at thirteen, how long two years is. I know now. My mother must have written that line thinking of me, and for two years I hadn't received that thought.

After that, I read everything to the end. It wasn't some great resolution. I simply never wanted to do that again.

I can't say this is a good habit. I'm slow at everything. It takes me twice as long as anyone else to read a ration notice. People standing in line with me often walked off before I finished. There were times at school when I ran out of exam time — because I was still reading the instructions printed at the end.

My aunt told me many times, "Hora, the important part is at the front." She was right. Usually the important part is at the front.

That usually and always aren't the same thing — I learned that two years too late.

When my number was called from the roster, I didn't even know what it meant.

The notice read: Owing to a vacancy among the assistant members of the Fourth Deliberation District, you have been assigned by roster number.

I read that sentence three times. Even after three readings, it never said who had assigned me.

Only that I had been assigned. Not who had done the assigning — only that I had been assigned.

That day I went to the front desk and asked whether I could learn why the person before me had quit.

The person at the desk looked at the screen for a while and said,

"…The reason field is empty."

"…They didn't write anything?"

"…It's a box you don't have to write in."

I thanked them and left. That person hadn't hidden anything from me. It really had been empty.

In this city, there are only ever two reasons you can't find something out. It wasn't written down, or you didn't ask. There is no third reason. It took me two years to work that out, and after I worked it out I had to spend a long time deciding whether that was reassuring or not.

This is something I only noticed later. At the time, I just assumed documents were written that way. I assumed documents simply didn't put people's names down. I assumed that was politeness.

The notice was two pages. The first page held the assignment. The second held the guidelines for the post. I read the second page to the end too.

At the very bottom of the second page there was a box. A long, narrow horizontal box, with small letters above it reading the signature box.

It was empty.

I looked at that box for a long while. I had half-raised my pen, wondering whether it was mine to fill in, when I saw the line beneath it — (This notice does not require a signature) — and set the pen back down.

Does not require. That isn't the same as forbids. I couldn't read that difference at the time. I sometimes wonder what would have changed if I had, but probably nothing would have. A sixteen-year-old can't work out the structure of a city from a single sheet of notice paper.

Still, I looked at that box that day. That I looked is a fact. That's where this book begins.

I folded the notice and put it in the drawer. The same drawer that held my mother's pages. There wasn't any particular reason I put it there. I only had the one drawer.

The night before my first day, I couldn't sleep.

It wasn't fear. Starting a new job in this city isn't a frightening thing. No one was going to evaluate me, no one would scold me if I did badly, and no one would praise me if I did well. I already knew that much.

What kept me awake was something else.

I wanted to know why I had been chosen.

I knew it was because of the number. But I couldn't help wondering whether a number was really that simple a thing. Why had the person before me quit. If they hadn't, what would I be doing right now. Who had held the number before mine on the roster. Why hadn't it worked out for that person.

That was the night I asked that question for the first time, and I don't let go of it until this record ends. This book was, in effect, written out of that one question.

And I still don't know the answer. This isn't a preview. It's a warning. There is no answer in this record.

My aunt came into the room that night and told me to turn off the light. I turned it off and lay down, and then, in the dark, I asked her,

"…Auntie, why do you think it was me?"

"…It was you because your number came up."

"…I know that part."

My aunt was quiet for a moment, then said,

"…Hora, if you start thinking about that, there's no end to it."

She was right. There was no end to it.

On my way to work, I saw the water channel.

At the western edge of the city, I don't know since when, a place had appeared where water ran. Something like a narrow ditch, roughly set with stones. It wasn't deep, and it wasn't wide. Water was simply passing through it.

People walked past it without a glance. No one stopped, no one looked in.

I stopped, for a moment. Not because it was strange, but because of the sound.

The water wasn't clear. It was the color of soil, and I could see small stones laid along the bottom. I wanted to touch it, and didn't. I didn't know whether it was all right to, and in this city, not doing what you don't know is all right to do had become a habit.

Someone passed by beside me. I asked,

"…How long has this been here?"

The person glanced once at the water channel and once at me. It wasn't an irritated look. It was a genuinely thoughtful one.

"…Hm. Was it always here?"

And they went on.

That person hadn't lied. They truly didn't know. The people of this city truly don't know anything about what has never come up as an agenda item. Not because they've been made not to know, but because that's the only channel there is for knowing anything at all.

This city is quiet. The Equity is a city with no occasion for noise. But that water made a sound. A small, continuous sound.

Later I would learn where that water came from. And I would learn that it appears nowhere in any document of this city. It hadn't been hidden. No one had simply brought it up.

I didn't know yet, in this city, that those two things mean the same thing.

I walked on. I couldn't be late.

As I walked, I looked back once. The water was still making its sound. Whether or not anyone was listening.

The Fourth Deliberation District's meeting room was smaller than I'd expected.

There was one long table, and twenty-two chairs. Nothing hung on the walls. There was a window, but it was a window you couldn't see outside through.

A stack of papers sat at my seat. They said it was today's deliberation material.

Nineteen of the twenty-two seats were filled. Everyone was older than me, and everyone was quiet. Some greeted me and some didn't, and not greeting me didn't look rude. Relationships that require a greeting don't tend to form here.

No one asked me who I was. Whoever I was would already be written in the material. Not asking again about what's already written down is the courtesy here.

I picked the stack up and checked its thickness. About forty pages.

The person in the next seat looked at me and said, smiling,

"…The summary's up front. All you have to look at is that."

"…Yes."

"…Really. The summary is accurate."

"…Yes. Thank you."

"…First time?"

"…Yes."

"…You'll get used to it fast. Everyone does."

That person was genuinely kind. I learned their name later, but I won't write it here. There are several people in this record whose names I don't write, and I'll get to the reason later on.

That person didn't deceive me. The summary was accurate. Not one single person in this city ever told me a lie. Not one.

I end up writing that sentence again, several times, in this record. Each time I write it, it grows a little heavier.

I thanked them and read the summary.

The summary was a page and a half. It took less than five minutes to read. It set out, in order, what the case was, why it had come up, and how it was expected to be handled. There wasn't a wasted word in it.

The session began. People spoke one at a time. What they said was mostly the same. It was put in order for you, it was passed on and received, it was confirmed. No one said what they thought, and that didn't sound strange to me. It wasn't a seat for saying what you thought.

It was over in twenty minutes. The decision came out.

That day I went home and my aunt asked how my first day had gone, and I answered, "…It finished faster than I expected." My aunt laughed. That was all.

And then, after I'd finished the summary, I read all forty pages to the end.

After the meeting ended and everyone else had left, I stayed behind and read alone. It took two hours. Partway through, the caretaker came to turn off the lights, saw me, and went back out.

Reading it through, the summary was correct. Not a single character was off. There were things in the forty pages that weren't in the summary, but none of it mattered. None of it truly mattered.

I want to set this part down precisely. There was nothing I discovered on that first day. This book isn't a story about me discovering something. I simply read, and that day, nothing happened from the reading.

Days like that go on for about two hundred days. Nothing happened for two hundred days. I won't write out all two hundred, but I'm noting here that they happened. So that whoever reads this record doesn't think I was carrying some kind of premonition around with me the whole time.

I had no premonition at all. I was just a girl who read to the end.

There was a box at the bottom of the last of the forty pages too.

A long, narrow horizontal box, with small letters above it reading the signature box.

It was empty.

That day, I didn't think anything of it.

(Continued in Chapter 2.)

Chapter 2. The Equity

_Previously_ — Hora, seated at the Fourth Deliberation District's assistant
member desk by a vacancy and a number. Her first session ended in twenty
minutes, and she stayed behind alone to read all forty pages to the end.
The summary was accurate, and the signature box on the last page was
empty. That day, she thought nothing of it.

People hearing this city's name for the first time usually laugh. The Equity. I think it's because the name is so bald about itself. Haven lets you rest, the Reckoning squares the accounts, the Harvest gathers you in, the Preserve keeps you safe — so the Equity, people assume, must mean it treats you fairly.

I've watched that reaction many times. And I've heard, many times, what those same people say about half a year later. After about half a year, everyone says the same thing.

"…It really does live up to the name."

I'm not writing that sentence to be ironic. It's simply true. This city lives up to its name. I'm going to write a great many hard things about this city later in this book, and before I write them, I wanted this chapter written first. I didn't want the things I say later to turn this chapter into a lie.

I'll start with the ration office.

Every winter, heating fuel runs short. What's short has to be divided, and of course someone ends up with less. In other cities, I've heard, this is when people line up. First come, first served, and whoever comes late gets nothing.

Here, no one lines up. You only apply. It doesn't matter when. You can apply in the morning or in the evening. The Consensus gathers the year's need, the year's supply, and the number of households, calculates the allotment, and posts the result on the door. Who applied at what hour never enters the calculation.

When I was twelve, I once thought this was strange. I asked my aunt, "…Shouldn't the person who applies first get more?"

My aunt looked at me for a while and said,

"…Applying first doesn't make you any less cold."

She was right. I still can't explain, even now, why that sentence felt so clean.

Someone who's never stood in a line doesn't know how much a line can divide people. I first learned it listening to kids who'd come from outside talk about lines — that a whole day gets decided by who stands in front and who stands behind. Here, there is no such day.

The word for pulling strings is almost never used here either.

As far as I know, no one in this city goes to find someone and ask, separately, to be looked after well. There's no one to go find. Every counter runs the same procedure, and the procedure runs the same way no matter who's staffing it. There's no part left to a clerk's discretion.

This isn't because the officials are unbending. I've watched a good many people working the counters, and they were all kind. But kindness never changed the outcome. What kindness couldn't get done stayed undone, and what a curt manner got done, got done.

I only learned later how rare this is. In other cities, I've heard, the outcome changes depending on who you smile at. Here, smiling changes nothing. I've never once thought this made the place bleak. If anything it was the opposite.

My aunt once said she'd worked briefly in another city. There, she said, remembering a superior's birthday was part of the job. Whether you remembered or not is what split people apart at promotion time. She laughed telling me that, but there was a slight bitterness at the end of the laugh. "…Coming here, I found out you didn't have to remember things like that. At first, not having to felt strange. Like I was forgetting something." Hearing that, I wanted to tell her she wasn't forgetting anything — there had never been anything there to remember — but I could tell she already knew that, so I said nothing.

I've never seen the loud win here either. In meeting rooms, at counters, at school, I've never once watched anyone raise their voice and get something out of it. Raising your voice just makes you a somewhat strange person. That's the whole of it.

There's something I saw when I was thirteen. Two houses on our lane got into a dispute over a fence line. One side was loud and one side was quiet. In another city, the loud side would have won — a neighbor who'd come from outside told me that afterward. Here, the surveying office came out, checked the old land register against the current fence, and a number gave the answer. The loud side's adult fumed for a good while after getting the result, but fuming didn't move the fence. Three days later that house had gone quiet too. It had no choice but to go quiet. There was no longer anyone left to fight — you can't fight a number the way you can fight the surveying office.

I still remember those three days. Watching that fuming adult go quiet after three days, I thought this wasn't losing — it was simply ending. Losing means losing to someone, and there had been no someone there.

I crossed paths with that adult a few more times afterward, and they never seemed especially uncomfortable seeing me. There was no trace of someone who'd lost a fence dispute. I found that strange. In another city, I imagine, seeing that fence every time would have stung. Here, looking at the fence, there was no one left to be stung by. You couldn't hate the surveying office. The surveying office had only measured.

Housing reassignment worked the same way. When I was fifteen, the place my aunt and I lived became too small, and I applied for reassignment. The application only asked for household size and current square footage. It didn't ask what my aunt used to do for work, or how long we'd lived in this city. Two months later a new place was assigned. It wasn't bigger, but it had one more room. Why that particular place wasn't written down. The Consensus must have matched that year's list of empty homes against the list of applicants, the counter clerk explained. That explanation was enough for me. I had no reason to press further.

I should say something about the old man next door. He was among the longest-lived people in this city. His legs were bad, and every year he applied for care assignment, and the application asked nothing but that his legs were bad. How many children he had, what work he used to do — none of that was asked.

I thought that was strange. It seemed to me that knowing such things would let people understand his particular hardship better. I asked the old man about it, and he answered,

"…Where's the sense in one hardship being harder than another? Bad legs are bad legs, same as anyone's."

That might sound curt, but I thought there was relief inside it. The old man didn't have to sell his story. He didn't have to plead to anyone. The single fact that his legs were bad was enough.

When I heard that in some places you have to sell your story to get help, I couldn't believe it at first. What happens to someone with no story? Someone who isn't good at telling one? Here, the question itself was never needed.

That old man received his care assignment that winter, and when spring came, he filed the same application again. The same form, every time. I once asked whether he didn't find it tedious. He answered, "…Tedious is better than having to explain yourself fresh every time." Hearing that, I thought for the first time about how fortunate it actually was, for a form to be tedious. Explaining yourself fresh every time meant having to persuade someone, every time.

Work assignments were the same. There's no such thing as an interview when this city assigns you a job. You submit an aptitude test and a preference, and the Consensus places you. At first I found this disappointing too — it meant there was no chance to show how good I was at something. But looked at the other way, it also meant no one was rejected for a first impression.

I filed my first preference form at fifteen. I wrote that I wanted to work in the records room. I didn't have to give a reason, but I gave one anyway. "I'm good at reading things all the way to the end." Looking at it now it's a slightly embarrassing sentence, but at the time it was everything I had. When the result came, I was told I'd been placed on the preliminary list for the deliberation district instead of the records room. Why it had changed wasn't written down. The Consensus must have matched the aptitude results against that year's projected vacancies, the officer explained. I was disappointed, but not wronged. There's a real difference between disappointed and wronged, and that was the first time I learned it.

People hear talk like this and always ask, "So nobody has any complaints?"

There are complaints. I have them too. There was a winter I felt the allotment was too small, and a time my aunt's application was processed late. But the problem was always who those complaints were supposed to be aimed at. There was no one to aim them at.

The allotment had been small because that year there truly hadn't been enough. My aunt's application had been late because that year applications had piled up. I was angry, but I couldn't find anyone worth being angry at, so in the end I just let it go.

Reading this sentence back now, I'm still not sure myself whether this is a good thing or a bad thing to say. Whether not being able to get angry is the same as not needing to — I still don't know. But at the time, it truly was a relief. It wasn't that I'd been wronged. It was just that the year had been the way it was.

There's something I learned at school that I still remember.

The teacher wrote this on the board: "The Equity takes no one's side." And underneath, one more line: "And it sees no one as a person."

At the time I thought that second sentence was frightening. The teacher noticed and explained.

"…Not seeing you as a person doesn't mean treating you carelessly. The Consensus doesn't see you as Hora. It sees an application number. But that isn't a bad thing. If it saw you as Hora, then someone who liked you could look after you more, and someone who didn't could look after you less. Seen as a number, that can't happen."

At the time I thought I understood what she meant. Thinking about it now, I only half understood.

At that same moment, the kid in the next seat raised a hand and asked, "Then what happens if the number is wrong?" The teacher paused a moment, then answered, "…The number has never once been wrong." I didn't realize, at the time, that this wasn't an answer to the question. Whether the number has never been wrong, and what happens when it is, are two different questions.

Whether seeing a number and not seeing a person are truly the same thing is a question this book comes back to later. That day in the classroom, I didn't even have the question yet.

I should write down the clinic too. This city has no such thing as an appointment. When you go in sick, it's not the order you arrived in that decides when you're seen — the Consensus judges how severe your symptoms are and sets the order from that. I went through this myself when my aunt was badly hurt. There were six people in the waiting room, and my aunt, who'd arrived after us, was called in third. Not one of the people ahead of her complained. I asked afterward, and I was told the screen had shown, along with the queue order, the reason it had been set that way. My aunt's bleeding needed to be stopped urgently; the people ahead of her weren't urgent. Seeing the reason, the anger didn't come, a man in the waiting room told me afterward.

"…When they show you the reason, what's left to argue?"

I remembered those words for a long time. That showing the reason settles the anger. This is always how this city calms people down. Not persuasion — inspection. Instead of someone stepping forward to say, "I'm sorry, it came out that way because of my judgment," the grounds appeared on the screen. I've never once felt that was cold. There are times when grounds comfort a person more than an apology does — I learned that for the first time in that waiting room.

There's one reason I'm writing this chapter.

A day is coming when I write about this city while I'm angry at it. I wanted to set this down so that day's version of me won't betray the me writing now. I like this city right now. I genuinely like it.

No one envies me here, and I envy no one. No one lines up here, no one asks for a favor, no one raises a voice. I think this is a good way to live. I still think so.

Writing this sentence, I don't yet know how hard it will come to make things for me later. It would have been so much easier if I could have said this wasn't a good place. It was a good place. That was the problem.

I've tried, a few times, to explain to people from outside why this city is good. It never went well. If I said, "No one here gets wronged," they'd always ask back, "So no one here does especially well, either?" That was true. No one did especially well. I never once thought of that as a loss — but every time I got that question, I did doubt, briefly, whether I might be missing something. Looking back now, I think that doubt was the tiniest seed of this book.

On my second day of work, on my way to the meeting room, I passed the ration office. A notice was posted. This winter's heating fuel allotment results. No names, only numbers. I found my number and checked the amount. It was a little higher than last year's.

Why it had gone up wasn't written down. I wasn't curious about it at the time. There was that same box at the very bottom of the notice too. A box marked "approval." It was empty. I didn't think anything of it that day either. It was only natural for a notice to have an approval box, and only natural for it to be empty. I didn't yet know there was a gap between those two kinds of natural.

I arrived at the meeting room and took my seat. The same seat as yesterday. The person next to me only nodded instead of greeting me. That was fine too. I was already growing a little more comfortable with the fact that not greeting someone here wasn't rude. Today's stack of material was thinner than yesterday's. I started with the summary, and I meant to read the full text behind it to the end as well. For now that was simply a habit of mine. It meant nothing more.

(Continued in Chapter 3.)

Chapter 3. The First Deliberation

_Previously_ — Hora going back over, one by one, just how fair a city the
Equity is. No lines, no favors, the loud never win. She likes this city.
She genuinely likes it. On the morning of her second day, she sits down
again with a thin stack of material.

I should describe the Fourth Deliberation District's meeting room in more detail, since the time I spent in that room is going to make up most of this record.

One long table, twenty-two chairs. At first I wondered why twenty-two, but no one knew. They only said it had always been that way. I checked whatever regulations I could find, and none of them said why the seat count was twenty-two. It was simply twenty-two.

Nineteen of those seats were filled. The other three, I heard, had stood empty for a long time. I learned only later that I'd nearly become one of those three — that the seat I sat in had, in fact, originally been one of them.

Nothing hung on the walls. There was one window, of frosted glass. Light came through, but you couldn't see outside. On the first day I thought that was strange. But after a few days, not being able to see out was actually a relief. There was nothing to let your mind wander off toward.

Of the nineteen people, I knew almost none of the faces. Their ages were all different. The oldest was older than my aunt, and the youngest only a couple of years older than me. I wondered what everyone had been doing before they came here, but no one introduced themselves. There was simply no occasion for it. I thought that was strange, but after a few days I saw it wasn't a bad thing either. If you didn't know what a person did, you could hear their words as just words, not as that person's words.

In front of each seat sat a small nameplate, but it held a number, not a name. My seat was number seventeen. I got confused for a while over whether that number was mine or the seat's, but I found out later it belonged to the seat. Meaning I was the person sitting in seat seventeen, not that I was seventeen myself. I didn't understand, then, why that distinction mattered.

There was no trace on the nameplate of who had sat in seat seventeen before me. The nameplate was as spotless as if it had been replaced with a fresh one the very day that person quit. Sitting down in that seat, for the briefest moment, I wondered whether some warmth from the person before me might still be left in it. There wasn't. The chair was just a chair. I still don't know, even now, whether that left me disappointed or relieved.

The first case up for deliberation was a materials reassignment.

The proposal was to divert part of a building-materials request from the eastern district out of the western district's supply. The west had a surplus, the east had a shortfall, and the Consensus had calculated the difference and sent it up to this room.

There was no chair's seat, as such. One of the people seated ran that day's session, but that person wasn't called a "chairperson." They were only called whoever was handling the session that day. A different person might handle the next one, I was told.

I asked the person next to me, that day, who decided who ran it. "…We take turns. There's an order to it." "…Who sets the order?" "…Seat number order. Today happened to be seat five's turn." I liked that answer. Even running the session wasn't anyone's choice. It was simply the order. Not because the person in seat five was especially capable, and not because the others trusted them. It was only that the order had come around to them.

Running the session didn't make that person's voice carry any more weight, either. The one running it still had to raise a hand and file an objection like anyone else, and never got to put a case in order first just because it was their turn to run it. I liked that this was the rule I found most agreeable in that room. A word from whoever held the microphone weighed exactly the same as a word from whoever didn't.

I looked up that rotation schedule later. The twenty-two seats turned in a fixed cycle. Which meant that seventeen, my seat, would someday come up for its turn running the session. I tried imagining how that day might feel. I couldn't quite picture it.

The rotation schedule still carried the three empty seats in it, unchanged. Even a seat no one sat in came up in its turn to run the session. When that happened, I was told, it was simply skipped. Something about that skipping stayed with me, somehow. The schedule remembered who was missing, but nowhere did it say why they were missing. The schedule knew who was absent but not why.

The person running that first session skimmed the material once and said,

"…The eastern request and the western surplus match what the Consensus calculated. Any objections?"

No one raised a hand.

"…If there are none, it's put in order."

And that was the end of it. I sat waiting for what came next, but the session had already moved on to the next case. I thought, at the time, that it had gone by so fast I must have missed something.

Later I opened the material again and read this first case from beginning to end. The request, the calculation's basis, a comparison table of holdings by district, three past cases of the same kind. It was all there. I didn't know, at the time, whether anyone besides me had read it to the end. The summary alone was already enough. I only read it to the end because I read slowly, not because I suspected anything. This distinction is one I end up making again, many times, later in this book.

The second case took a little longer.

This time there was an objection. Someone described as the western district's representative raised a hand and said,

"…The west also has construction planned for next spring, so if it's diverted now, there could be a shortfall come spring."

The person running the session wrote that down. Then said,

"…We'll check."

And they truly did check. An aide beside them tapped at a terminal and brought up next spring's planned construction on the screen. There really was planned construction in the western district. The Consensus recalculated it. It took a few minutes. A new result came back. This proposal would reduce the reassigned amount slightly, and draw a little more surplus from another district instead.

"…Will this do?"

The western representative looked at the new figures and nodded.

"…That's fine."

I still call this scene to mind often, even now. No one got angry. There was no sense of anyone having beaten anyone. The western representative hadn't gotten everything they'd asked for, and their face was truly satisfied all the same. That satisfaction struck me as strange. In another city, I've since heard many times from people who came from outside, this moment would have had raised voices in it. Here, only numbers passed back and forth twice, in place of voices.

I asked that western representative separately, later, why they'd been satisfied when they'd received less than they'd first asked for. They smiled briefly and said,

"…It's not that I got less. I got as much as I needed. What I first asked for, I'd padded generously."

"…Why pad it?"

"…In case it fell short. But once the Consensus recalculated, it turned out I didn't need to pad it after all."

Hearing that answer, I confirmed again that this person had neither lost nor won. The numbers had simply been squared. Squaring the numbers needed neither a winner nor a loser.

That person then went on gathering their materials and added one more thing. "…When I first started here, I couldn't break the habit of padding generously either. After a few years, I just started asking for what I actually needed. Because I knew the Consensus would square it either way." I wondered whether that could be explained with the word trust. It looked, to me, less like trust than like a state where being on guard had simply stopped being necessary. Whether not needing to be on guard is the same thing as believing — I didn't know then, and I still don't.

The way people spoke was strange.

No one said "In my opinion." No one said "I object." Everyone said, "This needs to be checked," "It will be put in order for you," "If that's what was passed on."

I couldn't tell whether this was modesty, or simply how these people talked. I asked the person next to me, quietly,

"…Does no one say what they actually think?"

"…This is what they think. Saying it needs checking is an opinion too. Saying it's fine is an opinion too."

"…But why not say it that way? …Just, like, I'm worried about this."

The person beside me thought for a moment, then said,

"…Say it that way, and it becomes my worry. Say it needs checking, and it becomes something everyone has to check."

I thought about that late into that night. That there was a way of speaking that kept a worry from becoming anyone's own — that was the first time I noticed such a thing existed.

For a while after that I practiced that way of speaking. At home, instead of saying "In my opinion" to my aunt, I tried saying "This seems like it needs checking." My aunt laughed. "…You've been going to that meeting room and now you talk like a government office." I was embarrassed and stopped, but the moment I stepped back into that room, the speech came out of me on its own. As natural as changing clothes.

Looking back now, I think this was the beginning. Change how you speak, and how you think changes a little too, along with it. I may have been losing, little by little, the way of saying "what I think," even then. I didn't notice the losing, at the time. That becoming comfortable and losing something can arrive together — I only learned that much, much later.

By the third case on, I'd already grown a little used to it.

A number was called, the summary was read aloud, objections were asked for, and if there were none it moved on, and if there were, it was checked and recalculated. Not one of the twenty-two seats ever said they had decided this. Everyone said it "was put in order."

The session ended after the sixth case. I checked the clock — it had only been a little over twenty minutes. I couldn't believe six matters had been handled in that short a time.

Growing used to something is a frightening thing. On the first day, this pace felt so foreign that I kept checking the clock. But after sitting in that room more than two hundred times since, I stopped checking it. I've never measured exactly how long it takes for the foreign to stop feeling foreign. I think it would have frightened me, if I had.

The fourth case was an adjustment to school assignments. The fifth was the order of road repairs. The sixth was a proposal to move one of the ration counters, because several complaints had come in that its current location was hard to reach, and the Consensus had calculated a new site. All six cases followed the exact same pattern. Summary, objection, check, put in order. Some finished in three minutes, some took nearly ten. The difference in time came down to whether there had been an objection or not — it didn't seem to have anything to do with how big the matter was. Something that touched children's lives directly, like a school assignment, and something as small as a counter's location, were handled at the same speed.

I thought that was strange, and then, thinking it over again, saw there was no reason it should be. To the Consensus, it was all the same calculation. The Consensus made no distinction between what people considered important and what they considered unimportant. Whether not making that distinction meant fairness, or meant something was being missed — I couldn't answer that, that day.

After the session ended, people filed out one by one. As I gathered my material, I asked the person who'd run the session,

"…What's decided today — who finally confirms it?"

That person looked at me and said, smiling,

"…It's already confirmed. Just now."

"…But the, um, signature, or something like that—"

"…Ah, the signature box. You don't have to fill that in. No one's ever said to fill it in."

I nodded at that answer. That day, I really did believe it was that simple. No one had said to fill it in, so it wasn't filled in. There was nothing more to it.

Reading this exchange back now, I know that person wasn't deceiving me. They genuinely believed it that way too. That it wasn't filled in because no one had said to fill it in. They had probably never once wondered why no one had said so.

"…Then why does that box even exist?" I asked again. The person thought for a moment and answered, "…Hm. It's probably just been there from the start. Maybe it just never got removed when the form was updated." I thought that answer sounded plausible too. Old forms tend to keep boxes no one needs anymore, after all. That was truly all I thought, at the time. That the box might still be there out of laziness, or because remaking the form was too much trouble. The thought that it might still be there because there was no one to fill it in — that never once crossed my mind, that day.

On the way home I passed the water channel. The water was still making its sound. This time I didn't stop. My head was full of the meeting room's twenty-two seats, and the frosted window, and six decisions no one had decided. The water channel was pushed far down my list of thoughts that day.

Leaving the meeting room, I looked once more at the frosted window. Outside was still invisible. That day, for the first time, I wondered whether that window had been frosted from the start, or had become that way later. There was no one suitable to ask, so I let it go.

My aunt had dinner ready and waiting when I got home. She asked how it had gone. I started to tell her what had happened that day, in order, and realized, once I actually tried to put it into words, that there wasn't much to say. "…We handled six cases. They all went fine." My aunt nodded and said, "…That's how it always is there. It goes fine."

I thought she was right. It really had gone fine. Not one thing about it had been bad. But that night, before I fell asleep, for no reason, I called back each of those six "it will be put in order" moments, one by one. I never did manage to remember who, exactly, had put any of them in order. The verb "put in order" had no subject. At the time, I only thought of that as a habit of speech.

(Continued in Chapter 4.)

Chapter 4. The Consensus

_Previously_ — Hora, through her first deliberation. Nineteen of the
twenty-two seats handled six cases. No one called anything their own
opinion, and even running the session only came around in turn. That
night she caught a flicker of a thought — that "it will be put in order"
had no subject — but took it for nothing more than a manner of speech.

Ask me to explain exactly what the Consensus is, and I still can't do it well. Most people born and raised in this city probably can't either. It's a bit like being asked what air is. It's always there, so no one ever bothers building the words to explain it.

I'll try anyway. Only as much as I know.

The Consensus doesn't hand down decisions — it gathers them.

I first learned that difference at school. The teacher explained it by comparing the Consensus to a scale. Put weight on one side of a scale, and the other side tips. The heavier side wins. The Consensus, she said, is not a scale.

"…The Consensus doesn't load anything on. It only gathers. Every record of everyone living in this city, every precedent, every statistic, every objection. It gathers it all and finds the center of that weight. No one decides where the center is. It simply appears there on its own, because that's how the things are arranged."

I liked that comparison at the time. I liked the phrase "center of weight." A point that comes into being on its own, without anyone pushing. A scale shows you who won. A center of weight doesn't show you who won. It's simply there. I believed, then — truly believed — that this difference explained everything about this city.

I still call that comparison to mind sometimes, even now. And every time, I run into the same question. For it to be true that the center of weight appears on its own, it matters, from the start, how the things were arranged. Who put those things there. I couldn't ask that question that day. I was ten, and the comparison was pretty. That's all it was.

The teacher drew that comparison on the board too — a picture of a scale, and beside it a scatter of dots with one small dot marked at the center. "This dot in the middle is the Consensus." I copied that drawing into my notebook. That notebook must be somewhere still; I've never gone looking for it. I'm a little afraid that if I did, I'd only confirm all over again how little I doubted, back then, taking it all down.

At that same moment, some kid raised a hand and asked, "Who put those dots there?" The teacher answered, smiling, "All of us did. Every record we leave behind, living our lives — that's the dots." I thought, at the time, that answer was enough. All of us. I didn't know then how wide that phrase was, and at the same time, how empty.

At break I talked to that kid again. They still looked unsettled about something. "…But if you say all of us, doesn't that end up meaning no one in particular?" I wanted to argue with that, but I didn't really have anything to argue back with. "…What's the difference between no one and all of us?" I asked back. They couldn't answer either. We left that question sitting there that day and moved on to the next class. I never once pulled that question back out again for years afterward. A question left sitting doesn't vanish — it only gets put off.

The first time I heard the sheer volume of material the Consensus handles, I was startled.

Every application in this city, every meeting record, every complaint, every statistic goes into the Consensus. Material from ten years ago, I was told, and from a hundred years ago too. Nothing, they said, had ever been erased.

I liked that phrase, "nothing erased." I'd heard that elsewhere, old records tend to disappear, or get quietly removed whenever it's convenient for someone. Here there was none of that. Look up an old record, and it really was all there.

Once, for a school assignment, I looked up a ration record from a hundred years back. It really existed. What had been short that winter, who received how many units, even how that decision had come out — all of it remained. That was when I truly felt, for the first time, that this city hides not one thing of its past.

Hiding nothing, and knowing nothing — those are different sentences. I used those two sentences as if they meant the same thing, for a long time. A great deal of this book is the process of learning that they're actually different.

Working on that assignment, I also tried to look up the name of the person in charge of rations a hundred years ago. There wasn't one. Only the department's title. I didn't think that was strange, at the time. A hundred years had passed, I figured, of course the name wouldn't survive. But when I asked the teacher, she said names had never been kept, not even a hundred years ago. "…It's been that way from the start. Leave an individual official's name on record, and eventually someone ends up resenting that person, or praising them." I nodded at that explanation. Records with neither resentment nor praise in them. That was how this city had kept itself for a hundred years.

I got a good mark on that assignment. I'd copied the department title and the procedure exactly. Other kids got similar marks. No one pointed out that there was no name for the person in charge. There was no reason to point it out. Writing down that something wasn't there, when it truly wasn't there, was never grounds for losing marks. Walking home that day, I felt, all over again, how entirely natural it was that no one wondered about what wasn't there.

There's a separate reason I came to respect the Consensus.

The Consensus has never once claimed to be right. Whenever a deliberation's result came out, the grounds for it were always attached beside it. Not "This decision is correct," but "Gathering these materials produces this result."

And when an objection came in, the Consensus never defended itself. It recalculated. From the start it assumed that a result could change if new material came in. I thought this was an extremely humble attitude. That the possibility of being wrong was something the system itself acknowledged.

It might sound strange to attach the word humility to a system. But I still can't take that word away from it, even now. The Consensus has never once said, "I decided this." That it never says that struck me, at the time, as a virtue. That not saying it might actually mean it couldn't say it — that never once crossed my mind, at the time.

I used to compare this humility to stories of other cities. Whenever kids who'd come from outside talked about where they used to live, a name always came up. That person decided it, it turned out this way because of that person, if not for that person it would have gone differently. Every time I heard stories like that, I felt tired. I kept wondering why someone had to be dragged into it every single time. Here, there was no need for that. Even when a result didn't sit right, there was no face to resent. Having no face to resent felt hollow at first, but once I got used to it, it felt freeing instead. I enjoyed that freedom for a long time. I haven't entirely given it up, even now. But that this freedom carried a price — I only learn that later in this book. Having no face to resent meant, also, having no face to apologize. At the time, I had no need to look at the reverse side of that sentence.

I once asked the person next to me how the Consensus actually calculates. They said they didn't know the exact method either.

"…I don't know the calculation method itself. I can see all the underlying material, but how it's all combined to produce a result…"

"…No one knows that?"

"…It's less that no one knows, more that there's no need to know. If there's an objection to a result, you just look at the material again. What matters is being able to verify the result without knowing the process."

I nodded at that answer, and then thought about it again on the way home. Whether being able to verify a result without knowing the process, and no one knowing the process, are really the same thing. I reached no conclusion that day.

For a few days after, I went looking for material that explained how the Consensus calculates. The library had a few introductory volumes. I read them all, and every one of them, however detailed about how a result was verified, blurred over how the calculation happened before that verification with a single phrase — "through a complex process." I didn't think that came from carelessness — I thought the people who'd written it simply didn't know any more than that either. No one had hidden anything. Everyone had written exactly as much as they knew. The problem seemed to be that no one knew — but at the time, I didn't even think to call that a problem.

I also wondered whether there was anything the Consensus couldn't handle.

One day I asked the person running the session, "…Is there anything the Consensus can't decide?" They thought for a moment and answered,

"…It can't decide something that's never been brought up as a case."

"…Why wouldn't something get brought up?"

"…Because it only gets brought up if someone brings it up."

I let that answer pass without much thought, at the time. It sounded like the obvious thing to say. You need a case before you can handle it — how would you handle something that doesn't exist. I didn't think of the water channel, in that moment. I truly didn't think of it, that day.

Writing this down again now, I know that exchange was one of the most important in this book. I didn't know it then. That I didn't know is the truly frightening part of this book. It isn't that I missed something important — I had no material to even judge whether it was important or not.

I asked the person running that session one more thing. "…Then who can bring something up? Can anyone?" They answered. "…Yes. Anyone. Write your name and your reason, and it's accepted." "…Why does a name have to be written?" "…So you know whose case it is. Without a name, it isn't accepted." I thought, at the time, that this rule was fairly fair. That anyone could bring up a case at all. Why the condition of writing a name existed, I let go as nothing more than administrative procedure. I still didn't know how rare a thing it was, in this city, to write down a name.

Walking back to my seat that day, I suddenly wondered. Of the nineteen people sitting in this room, had any of them ever brought up a case under their own name. I didn't ask. Even if I had, I wouldn't have caught the weight of the answer, not then.

A few days later, I looked up one of the Consensus's calculated results — not the summary this time, but the full text of the grounds behind it. There was no particular reason. It was just my habit of reading.

The underlying material was vast. More than twenty related cases, an opinion paper from each district, five past decisions of a similar kind. It took half a day to read it all. And on the last page was a list of everyone who had handled this deliberation. About four hundred people. I paused for a moment, looking at that number.

Four hundred people had been involved in this one matter, and there was no one who had decided it. Each of the four hundred had done something, but adding up all of it, there wasn't a single person who said, "I decided this."

This is the first time I set this number down here. Four hundred. This number comes up again later. When it does, it comes back far heavier. Right now it was only a curious number. That so many people could be tied up in one case and still no one carried the responsibility — that didn't feel bad to me. It felt safe, if anything.

I looked more closely at that list. The titles were all different. Intake, cross-checking, confirmation, passing on, putting in order, convening. About six titles, divided across four hundred people. Each person's job was exactly that one title, nothing more. Whoever handled intake did only intake; whoever cross-checked did only cross-checking. Passing through all six stages was what produced a decision — and yet, among all six stages, not one of them carried the title "decision." I only found that curious, at the time. If you asked who did the job of deciding, this list couldn't answer precisely. And yet the decision was plainly there, right at the end.

That night, I told my aunt about the Consensus.

"…Auntie, I get why the Consensus is good. Nobody claims to be right."

My aunt looked up from her meal.

"…Why's that good?"

"…Then no one can be cornered for being wrong either. Everyone just gathered it, after all."

My aunt thought for a moment and said,

"…No one being wrong, and no one being right — isn't that a hair's difference apart?"

I let that pass, that day, without giving it much thought. My aunt sometimes said cryptic things like that. I finished my meal, and that sentence sat quietly inside me for a while.

Sitting quietly doesn't mean it disappeared. I end up pulling that sentence back out, months later, in a place entirely different from this one. My aunt, eating her meal that day, probably didn't know how precise a thing she'd said. Neither did I.

Finishing her meal and clearing the bowls, my aunt added one more thing. "…But that's not to say it's a bad thing. I've lived here over forty years myself, and I've never once been badly wronged." That remark stayed with me more than the last one had. No one being right wasn't a frightening thing to my aunt. The fact of never having been wronged outweighed that fear, and then some. I thought, that day, that her arithmetic was sound. Forty years without being wronged. I thought that might be enough, all on its own.

I lay down and went back over, in order, what I'd learned that day. The Consensus gathers. It doesn't measure. It's humble. It hides nothing. Four hundred people can be involved and there's still no one who decided. Laid side by side, these sentences were strangely harmonious. Consistent, as if written by the same hand. I liked that consistency. Every explanation this city gave never contradicted another. I believed, at the time, that consistency and truth were the same thing.

The days that followed were no different from before. I sat in the meeting room and read the summaries, and once everyone else had left, I read the full text to the end. The result always matched the grounds. That reassured me. The Consensus had never once told a lie.

(Continued in Chapter 5.)

Chapter 5. Tyche

_Previously_ — Hora learning that the Consensus gathers rather than
measures. A system that looks humble, a structure where four hundred
people can be involved and still no one decides. In passing, her aunt
said, "No one being wrong, and no one being right — isn't that a hair's
difference apart?" and the sentence settled quietly inside her.

I met Tyche for the first time on the third day.

I was still, that day, holding onto the material and struggling to understand something. There was an unfamiliar term in the third case's underlying material, and I couldn't pin down its meaning from the surrounding context either way. I thought about asking the person beside me, but everyone looked busy before the session started, so I gave up on it.

That was when someone came and sat down next to me. Someone who looked about a year older than me. They had a bundle of material tucked under one arm, and their expression was at ease.

"…Something you're stuck on?"

I looked at them, startled. I started to ask how they knew, then realized I'd been sitting there for a while with my finger on that unfamiliar word. It must have shown.

"…Ah, I don't know what this word means."

They looked at the screen and answered right away.

"…That's the calculation-basis adjustment value. It takes the original baseline figure and multiplies it by that year's variable to correct it — in this case, rainfall was the variable used."

They were so fast and so exact that I sat there stunned for a moment.

"…How do you know that so instantly?"

"…I see this material all the time. I've pretty much memorized the material this meeting room uses."

I liked that it didn't sound like boasting. It had the tone of someone simply stating a fact. I looked at their face again. There was ease in the expression. Not rushed, but not slow either. I'd never seen a person like that before. I was always one or the other — rushed, or slow. That day was the first time I'd actually seen the middle ground exist in a person.

Their hair was short, and their clothes were similar to the other assistants'. There was nothing especially eye-catching about them. Except that their gaze never wavered when they spoke. I thought that, if anything, was what stood out. It was rare, in this room, for someone speaking not to look away. Everyone else looked at their material, or their hands, or somewhere else. Tyche looked me in the eye while they spoke.

That person's name was Tyche.

A deliberation assistant, they said. A few were posted to each meeting room, to help members understand the material, they said. I hadn't even known such a role existed.

"…It's always been here. It's just that everyone only looks at the summary, so there isn't much occasion to call one of us."

"…Then what about me?"

"…You read the full text, Hora. So of course you get stuck somewhere. It's only natural."

That surprised me a little. That they already knew I read the full text. I'd never told anyone that.

"…How did you know?"

"…It's in the material checkout records. People who only look at the summary never even open the full-text file. You've opened it every time."

I thought, for a moment, that this was slightly frightening — and then let the thought go. It wasn't surveillance. It was just a record, and someone with the standing to view that record had looked. Nothing had been hidden. I could have looked at anyone else's checkout record too, if I'd wanted to.

Tyche's own introduction was brief too. Born and raised in the Equity, and doing this deliberation-assistant work for about two years now, they said. "…I haven't really been anywhere else. This is all there is for me." It was much the same for me. I was glad of that. Being with someone who didn't need to talk about the outside. With kids who talked about the outside, I always felt like I was talking to them across one remove. With Tyche, none of that was necessary.

Whatever I asked Tyche, the answer came fast.

"…When did this provision come into being?"
"…It was revised eleven years ago. Before that it used a different standard."

"…Why does this district get more materials?"
"…Higher population density. More people live in the same area, so the demand for materials is higher too."

"…Where does this number come from?"
"…This table, third row. It's the average over the last five years."

"…Why are this provision and that one filed in this order?"
"…That's the numbering order. That's just how the provision numbers were originally assigned. Not by content order."

I found it striking that Tyche never got stuck no matter what I asked. I never once heard them say they didn't know.

"…Is there nothing you don't know, Tyche?"

Tyche laughed.

"…There is. But if I don't know something, I can just look it up. I'll find it and come back with the answer."

And that's exactly what happened. There was a question that afternoon they hadn't been able to answer, and the next morning Tyche came in ahead of me with the answer already in hand.

That question was why some district's old boundary line had been drawn the way it currently was. Tyche hadn't had an answer that day and had only said, "…I'll check on it." The next morning, before I'd even sat down, Tyche came over holding a single document. "…Found it. The boundary was redrawn twenty-three years ago during a river maintenance project. Here's the map from that time." I took that document from them, amazed that Tyche had truly found a twenty-three-year-old map in a single day. "…How did you find it?" "…It's all in the records room. It's just a matter of knowing how to look." I thought that was modesty, but I found out later it was simply the truth. Everything really was in the records room.

That was the day I saw the records room for the first time too — the underground stacks I followed Tyche into. Shelves stretched on endlessly, each one labeled with a year and a classification number. There was hardly anyone there. Tyche walked with a familiar stride, turned a few corners down the aisles, and pulled a folder from exactly the right slot. "…Do you come here often?" "…Yes. A couple times a week." I liked that space. It was quiet, and whatever you were looking for was, more often than not, there.

Walking further between the shelves, I asked Tyche,

"…Can you find anything in here, Tyche?"

"…Most of it. There are a few sections I've gotten lost in, but I do end up finding it eventually, even then."

"…You've gotten lost before?"

"…Of course. There was one classification once where the numbering system changed partway through — took me half a day to find what I needed because of that."

I was glad to hear that. That Tyche had moments of being lost too — somehow it made them feel a little closer to me.

A few days later, Tyche once took me to their own desk. It was a small room the deliberation assistants used, one whole wall covered in index cards. It looked hand-organized.

"…What's all this?"

"…An index of provisions I look up often. You can find them electronically too, but keeping them organized by hand is faster."

I looked over the cards one by one. Dates, provision numbers, one-line summaries, all in neat handwriting. There must have been several hundred.

"…When did you make all of these?"

"…A little at a time, over two years. Whenever I had a spare moment."

Looking at that bundle of cards, I could tell Tyche truly loved this work. These weren't the cards of someone doing a job because they'd been told to.

I picked up one of the cards. It read "Roster Assignment Regulation upon Vacancy." Seeing it, I thought of my own situation and asked,

"…Isn't this the regulation that got me sitting here?"

Tyche took the card back and looked at it, then nodded.

"…That's right. Assignment by roster number when a vacancy comes up."

"…Do you know why it was decided this way?"

"…Hm, apparently there used to be a recommendation system. But since the outcome ended up split depending on the recommender's own feelings, it was changed to a roster system. Something like twenty-some years ago."

I nodded at that explanation. Which meant that my sitting here was the result of someone, twenty-some years ago, working hard to get rid of the problems with the recommendation system. I thought that wasn't a bad lineage to come from. That I sat here because of a number, not because of someone's feelings.

"…So I'm just here because of a number, then."

"…Right. But why is that a problem? Is being here because of a number a bad thing?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. I'd never thought it was a bad thing. It was just that, for the first time, hearing the phrase "because of a number" applied to myself made it sound a little different.

I liked Tyche. I still like Tyche.

They were kind. There wasn't a cynical bone in them. They never seemed impatient with how slowly I read. If anything, there were times they found things and brought them to me before I even asked.

"…This is the background material for this case. It's a bit thick, but you like this kind of thing, Hora."

"…I don't like it, exactly. I just read it."

"…Isn't that the same as liking it?"

I couldn't answer that. It did seem like it might be true.

Tyche was the first person who never looked at me strangely for it. Back in school, everyone found my slowness frustrating. Here, no one did that either, but Tyche went one step further and simply took it as a given that I read to the end.

I went home and told my aunt about Tyche. "…Auntie, I met a deliberation assistant today, and they know the answer to everything I ask." My aunt smiled and asked, "…Are they nice?" "…Yeah. Really kind. But it's kind of amazing — they find everything faster than I do." My aunt paused, mid-ladle over the soup, and looked at me. "…Being quick and being kind are two different things, but it sounds like you've seen both in one person." I started to say something to that, then thought, it really is true, and just laughed.

The next day I passed that exact exchange on to Tyche. They laughed, amused. "…Your aunt's right. I could easily not be kind at all — maybe I just look kind because I love this work." I thought Tyche knew themselves quite precisely, hearing that. The distinction between not being kind and only looking kind because they loved the work — I'd never made a distinction like that before.

"…Then why do you love this work, Tyche?"

"…Because there's an answer. You ask something, and an answer comes out. I like that. I'm not really a fan of holding onto something that doesn't have an answer."

Hearing that, I looked back at myself. I was the type to hold onto something even without an answer. The way I'd left my mother's last line unread for two years. Tyche and I were different on that point. At the time, I only thought of it as a simple difference in personality.

Once, a deliberation result came out different from what I'd expected, and I asked Tyche about it.

"…I thought this district would get more — why did it come out this way?"

Tyche flipped through the screen a few times and answered.

"…What you saw was the request amount. The actual assignment also factors in past usage rates. This district didn't use up everything it was allotted last year. So this year it was reduced."

"…How do you check what wasn't used?"

"…Here — the usage performance report. Want to see it?"

Tyche opened the document right away and handed it over. I took it and read it. It really did say exactly that. Tyche had never once deceived me.

Writing this chapter, I keep turning over that precision of Tyche's. Tyche always said what was true. But that being true isn't everything — I only learn that a very long time later. That wasn't Tyche's fault. I want to set that down clearly.

That day I finished reading the usage performance report beside Tyche. Tyche did other work next to me until I finished. Not someone waiting for me, but someone simply staying beside me while getting their own work done. I liked that distinction. Being waited for can feel like a burden sometimes; just having someone beside you doesn't.

"…Finished reading?"

"…Yes. It really is exactly what you said."

"…Why would I lie? Anyone can look at it."

I agreed with that. It really was true. In this city, hiding something looked like it would actually be more trouble. To hide something you have to conceal it, and to conceal it you have to manage it, and managing it creates yet another record all its own. Not hiding anything was simply easier.

Once, walking down the hallway together after a session, we talked. I asked, that day, why this city was so exact about everything.

"…Do you like this city, Tyche?"

"…I do. It's exact."

"…Is being exact a good thing?"

"…Yes. When things are exact, no one gets wronged. Getting wronged is the worst thing there is."

I nodded at that. It was true.

"…But do you ever think, sometimes, if you suppose all of this is just chance…"

Tyche looked at me and smiled.

"…So what if it's chance? Even if it's chance, isn't it fine as long as it's exact?"

Something snagged in me at that answer, but I couldn't put into words, at the time, what it was. I just laughed it off. Tyche's words were always like that. Nothing to argue against, and yet never quite fully settling into comfort either.

We stood for a moment that day in front of the meeting room's window. Only a dim light came through the frosted glass.

"…Do you know what's outside that window, Tyche?"

"…The street. Nothing much."

"…Have you seen it?"

"…I've seen it from a window on another floor. It really is just a street."

That answer made me laugh a little. I must have been secretly hoping there was something special behind that window. Tyche seemed like they'd never once held a hope like that. If you were curious, you found out; once you found out, you had your answer; and that answer was, more often than not, nothing much. That was simply how the world was arranged, for Tyche.

"…Don't you get disappointed? When it's nothing much?"

"…What's there to be disappointed about. Better to know something's nothing much than to keep wondering without knowing."

I thought about that for a long time. That knowing something's nothing much is better than wondering without knowing. That was, without question, true. And yet even hearing it, some part of me never stopped wanting to look through that window myself.

Walking home, I went back over, in order, everything Tyche and I had talked about that day. Exactness. Answers. Numbers. Chance. Inside Tyche, these words sat side by side without ever colliding. I wondered how that was possible. For me, those words kept colliding somewhere. For Tyche, they didn't seem to.

(Continued in Chapter 6.)

Chapter 6. The Secretary

_Previously_ — Hora meeting Tyche, the deliberation assistant. Whatever
she asked, Tyche answered exactly, kindly, without a trace of cynicism.
"So what if it's chance? Isn't it fine as long as it's exact?" left
something hanging inside Hora, though she still couldn't put into words
what it was.

I met the Secretary for the first time around the end of my first week.

Before that, I'd only seen the title "the Secretary" on paper. In the sender field of notices, in the approval history of meeting material, it always simply read "the Secretary." No name. I assumed that was the formal way the title was written. I didn't know yet that this wasn't a person named the Secretary, but a person seated in the post called the Secretary.

That day I'd filed a material request wrong. I'd meant to request more of the underlying material from a past deliberation, but I got the form confused and wrote it in the wrong box. The counter told me to fill it out again, and then someone from inside that counter came out and offered to look at it themselves.

That person was the Secretary.

Their age was hard to guess. They looked to be around forty, but their manner of speaking was calmer than that. Their clothes were neat, nothing about them dressed up. A low voice sat in their throat, and it only struck me later how that tone never once shifted, all day. They took the paperwork, looked it over, and pointed at a couple of spots.

"…Here, this box needs the previous session's number. Right now it has this session's number in it."

"…Ah, I'm sorry."

"…No, everyone gets confused by this at first. I'll fix it for you."

And they really did fix it, right there, on the spot. I didn't even know whether that was the Secretary's job to do or not, but they did it without a second thought.

After looking the corrected paperwork over once more, they stamped it. An intake-confirmation stamp. Watching that stamp come down, I suddenly thought of the signature box. Wondering whether this stamp stood in for a name, I asked,

"…Is that your own stamp?"

"…No, it's the counter's stamp. Whoever's working, the same stamp gets used."

"…You don't have your own stamp?"

The Secretary looked thoughtful for a moment, then answered.

"…No. There's never been a need for one."

I nodded at that answer without much thought. "There's never been a need for one" sounded, at the time, like nothing more than a practical explanation.

"…Then how would you go about getting a new one made?"

"…It's applied for. But no one's applied for one yet, as far as anyone's seen. Everyone seems to think the counter stamp is enough."

I took that answer without much thought too. If it's enough, of course a new one wouldn't get made.

As the Secretary put the paperwork into the intake tray, they added one more thing. "…You can receive the material with this now. It'll take two or three days." "…Thank you." "…No need. That's simply how the procedure runs." I heard "that's simply how the procedure runs" many times that day. Every time I thanked them, the Secretary deflected it with that phrase. As if it wasn't something they'd done for me, but something that had been done because the procedure ran that way. At first I took it for politeness. But after watching a few more days, I came to see it wasn't politeness at all. They meant it.

"…May I ask your name?"

That was the first time I'd asked. They smiled slightly and answered.

"…You can call me the Secretary."

"…No, I mean your name."

"…Just the Secretary. That's what everyone calls me here."

I thought, at first, they were avoiding the question. I started to ask again, then stopped, because there wasn't a trace of concealment in their expression. It was the face of someone genuinely accustomed to being addressed that way.

"…But on things like application forms, there's a box for a name, isn't there? What do you write there?"

"…The title. 'The Secretary,' like that."

"…Is not writing your name a rule?"

"…It isn't a rule. But that's how everyone writes it. When I first started here, I used to write my name too, but at some point I just started writing the title instead."

I was curious about that "at some point," but I didn't press further. That day I just let it be. People's habits change over time, after all.

From that point on, I never once called this person by a name. Not in this record either. I learn the reason later, and it isn't that this person was hiding anything. This person themselves simply took it as a given that their own name would never be called.

I told my aunt about it that evening. "…Auntie, the Secretary won't tell me their name. Just says to call them 'the Secretary.'" My aunt nodded. "…That's how it always is. I don't know our district's Secretary's name either, even after years." "…Doesn't that seem strange?" My aunt thought for a moment and said, "…Not so much strange as — I suppose that's just what the post is. It's not like knowing the name would change anything." I had nothing to argue back with. Nothing really would have changed.

"…But Auntie, how many years has your district's Secretary been in the post?"

My aunt counted for a moment, then answered. "…Five years or so, I think. I don't really remember the one before them." "…You didn't even notice they'd changed?" "…No, I just realized it one day when the voice was different. I never remembered the face well to begin with." That answer struck me as strangely sad, but my aunt set the soup bowl down without a trace of concern.

The Secretary handled convening the committee and its records. Setting meeting dates, distributing material, writing up the minutes, passing confirmed results on to each department. Everything I ever asked them about got explained back to me as one of these four things.

"…Don't you ever give your own opinion on what's being deliberated?"

"…Only convening happens here."

"…But you write the minutes yourself, don't you? Doesn't that mean you're deciding what goes in and what stays out?"

"…That's written to a fixed format. It isn't chosen."

I sensed that this answer didn't come from modesty so much as from genuinely believing it. A modest person sometimes laughs and says, "Oh, it's nothing I did." This person didn't laugh. It was truly the face of someone who believed nothing had been done by them at all.

I got curious about where that stamp was kept too, and asked someone at the counter. There was one at every counter, they said, and even when the person staffing it changed, the same stamp stayed in use. The stamp had a counter number engraved on it, not a person's name. I felt, all over again, that this was the same pattern as everything else in this city. Marks that attach to a seat, not to a person.

I looked up a few of the meeting minutes and read them too. The minutes the Secretary wrote really were free of any wasted word. Who had said what, what material had been submitted, what the result was — all set down in order, nothing more. Not a single line of their own thought or judgment went into it. I thought this made for good records. Since none of the writer's own color bled into it, anyone reading it would come away with the same content.

"…How do you decide what goes into the minutes?"

"…Everything goes in. What was said, material submitted, the result. Nothing left out."

"…So there's nothing you write as more important?"
