"…No. It's all written the same."

I was impressed by that answer. I thought it made for a genuinely fair record. Later I'd come to think about what price that fairness was paid at, but that day I was simply impressed.

What stayed with me most from that first meeting was the way they spoke.

Not "This is how I'll handle it," but "This is how it gets handled." Not "I confirmed it," but "It was confirmed." At the time, I took this for nothing more than a bureaucrat's manner of speech. Since that's how everyone talked, I figured, this person talked that way too.

"…When does this case get handled?"

"…It gets handled next session."

"…Who handles it?"

The Secretary looked at me for a moment. Not because they couldn't answer — it looked more like the question had struck them as slightly unfamiliar.

"…It gets handled by the deliberation district."

In that moment I felt like I should ask something more, but I didn't know exactly what to ask, so I just moved on to the next question. I sensed, dimly, that this answer wasn't an answer to my question. I already knew the district handled it. What I'd asked was who. But that day I let the mismatch go. It hadn't seemed like a question important enough to press for a real answer to.

For the next few days I listened back carefully, in my head, to the conversations I'd had with the Secretary. There truly was no subject. Instead of "I put it in order," it was "It was put in order." Instead of "I got in touch with you," it was "Word went out." Instead of "I confirmed it," it was "It was confirmed." In sentence after sentence, nothing stood where the subject should be. I thought this was too consistent to be one person's mere manner of speech. But I didn't hold onto that thought for long. A consistent manner of speech can exist too, after all.

Once, the Secretary slipped and put in a subject by accident. "…I got in touch with you at the time, so—" they started, then stopped, and corrected themselves. "…Word went out, but it seems it didn't reach you." I didn't let that correction slip past me. I asked, before I even meant to.

"…You just said 'I' and then corrected yourself."

The Secretary looked at me for a moment, then laughed.

"…Ah, did I? Must have been a slip of habit."

That laugh wasn't awkward, and that was exactly why it stayed with me all the more. This person truly regarded that correction as a mistake. That putting in a subject was the mistake, and leaving it out was the normal state — that day was the first time I saw it clearly, laid bare.

The Secretary was neither especially kind to me nor especially curt. Whatever I asked, they answered. Even when I filed paperwork wrong, they never showed irritation. I liked that manner.

Once I was late because I'd missed a change in meeting time. I fully expected to be scolded for it. But the Secretary only said this.

"…Word must have gone out late. From now on, it'll be checked ahead of time for you."

I was bewildered by that. It was my mistake — why blame the notice for it, I wondered. But thinking it over again, it didn't seem like they were covering for me — it seemed like they genuinely thought that way. That being late, too, was a result of the procedure, not something to be pinned down as my own fault.

At the time I thought this was consideration. Thinking about it again now, consideration and avoidance may not be things this person's mind actually distinguishes between. But I know clearly that this isn't because this person is a bad person.

Feeling sorry about being late that day stayed with me for a while, so the next day I went back to the Secretary and apologized. "…I'm sorry about being late yesterday." The Secretary looked at me for a moment and said,

"…What's there to be sorry about. The notice went out late, that's all."

"…Still, I should have checked ahead of time—"

"…It isn't that you didn't check, Hora. The very notice there was to check simply went out late. How do you check something that isn't there?"

Hearing that, I sat stunned for a moment. How do you check something that isn't there. I didn't know, at the time, that those words would come back before me later, in an entirely different weight. That day, I just thanked them and let it go.

As I turned to leave, the Secretary added one more thing behind me. "…From now on, I'll check ahead of time myself and let you know." I stopped and turned back at that. "I'll check ahead of time" had "I" attached to it again. This time it wasn't corrected. I quietly filed that moment away. This person, too, sometimes — very rarely — put themselves into a sentence.

A few days later, I asked Tyche about the Secretary.

"…Is the Secretary always like that?"

"…Like what?"

"…Whatever you ask them, they never say they did it themselves."

Tyche thought for a moment and answered.

"…Huh. I'd never thought about it, but you're right, they really are like that."

"…Doesn't that seem strange to you?"

"…Hmm, I don't think it's strange, exactly. That's just what the Secretary's job is, isn't it. Convening, passing things on. It's not a post where you make decisions yourself, so maybe that's why they talk that way."

That explanation seemed right to me. That day I really did just accept it and move on. The title is what it is, so of course the manner of speech would be too. I hadn't yet seen the other possibility hiding behind that sentence.

Tyche added, as if something had just occurred to them.

"…Actually, come to think of it, I heard my parents did Secretary work too. My grandmother as well. The Secretary's post has been around a pretty long time in this city."

"…So is it passed down through families or something?"

"…No, it's just a post you can apply for. But strangely enough, a lot of similar-natured people seem to end up going into it."

"…What kind of nature?"

Tyche thought for a moment and answered.

"…Hmm, people who are fine not stepping forward themselves? Apparently people who want to be the one out front don't last long in this job. It frustrates them too much."

Hearing that, I felt like I understood, a little, why the Secretary had come to speak the way they did. If someone who doesn't want to step forward takes on a job where they don't have to, maybe the manner of speech that fits that job just settles into the body on its own.

That evening I turned that conversation back over in my mind, and it kept nagging at me that there had once been a time, apparently, when the Secretary used to write their own name. This person hadn't gone without writing their name from the very start — at some point, they'd simply stopped. What had happened at that "some point," I didn't even think to ask that day. I only pull that question back out much later, and by then it's sitting in a far heavier place.

Walking home that day, I took out the paperwork the Secretary had smiled and fixed for me again. The paper where the previous session's number was correctly filled in now. There was an intake-confirmation box at the very bottom of that paper too. The stamp was there, but the officer's signature box beside it was still empty. Looking at it, nothing occurred to me. The stamp seemed like it should be enough. Why a signature would be needed separately from that — I hadn't thought about it, not then.

Putting that paperwork away in the drawer, I turned back over, one more time, the several faces the Secretary had shown me that day. Kind, exact, almost never using a subject, and once — just once — using one and correcting it themselves. I couldn't find a single reason to think ill of this person. I still can't.

(Continued in Chapter 7.)

Chapter 7. Open to Inspection

_Previously_ — Hora meeting the Secretary. Kind and exact, but always
speaking without a subject. "It was put in order." "It was confirmed."
Once, just once, an "I" slipped out, and was corrected on the spot. She
took the manner of speech for nothing more than a habit of the post and
let it go.

One day I got curious. Could you really look at anything, all of it.

People in this city often said "the Consensus hides nothing." I'd learned it at school, heard it in the meeting room, heard it from Tyche too. I believed it, but believing it and checking it were two different things. I was someone who checked.

So that afternoon, I decided to pick a deliberation I had no part in and go read the full text. Any case would do. I just went looking for a district entirely apart from the Fourth, an issue entirely unfamiliar to me.

I'd had the thought for a few days before that, but that day was the first time I actually acted on it. What I picked was a case out of the Second Deliberation District. It concerned a road-widening project, something I had no connection to whatsoever. There was a reason field on the application, and I wrote "practice at inspection." There was no box beneath it asking for further purpose.

The counter handed the material over with no particular verification procedure.

"…Can anyone look at this?"

"…Yes. You just fill out the application. We only check your identity."

"…You don't ask why?"

"…Why would we need to. It's public material."

That person finished the identity check and handed the paperwork straight over. The procedure was so simple that I wondered if something had been left out, and asked again.

"…Is that all? There's nothing else needed?"

"…No. Just an identity check. Anyone who lives here can see all of it."

"…People from outside too?"

"…Yes. Though they need a temporary registration card. That doesn't take long to issue either."

"…What about someone without the card, just looking briefly?"

"…That's not allowed. The identity check is required. But once you have the card, you're shown everything, exactly the same as a citizen. There's no difference in treatment."

I nodded at that once more. That there was a procedure, but no discrimination. I liked that distinction.

Hearing that, I felt, all over again, just how wide open this city keeps its own records. Even someone who wasn't a citizen could see everything, so long as they went through the proper procedure.

I liked what they'd said. Why would we need a reason, they'd asked back. If there's nothing to hide, a reason isn't needed either.

It occurred to me, suddenly, that I'd heard other cities pry into your reason for wanting to see a record. They said whoever wanted to see it was questioned first about why, and if the reason wasn't acceptable, they weren't shown it. I couldn't make sense of that approach. Who decided whether a reason was acceptable or not, I wondered. Here, that question didn't even exist.

The counter clerk added one more thing as they handed the paperwork over.

"…Come back if you need anything more. I can pull the related material for you too."

"…Related material?"

"…Yes, past cases connected to this one. I can print you out a list, if you'd like."

I found that offer striking. Kindness that told me something I hadn't even asked for. I requested the list right there, and that afternoon I walked out with far more material than I'd originally planned on.

The list had twelve past cases on it. I'd meant to read only three of them, but reading one led to a mention of another case, and chasing that down led me to another, until I'd ended up reading all twelve. I lost track of how much time had passed. Only when the window outside went dark did I hear the announcement that the reading room would soon close.

The librarian looked at me and said, smiling,

"…That's quite a long session for practice at inspection."

"…Yes, it was interesting."

"…It's been a while since I've seen someone like you. Most people find exactly what they need and go."

Hearing that, I felt, once again, that I was a somewhat unusual person in this room. It wasn't an unpleasant feeling.

The material was vast. The original request, opinion papers from the districts involved, the calculation's grounds, records of objections filed, all the way through to the final confirmation — it was all there. I turned through it page by page.

The objection records were especially striking. Who had filed the objection, what the objection was, and how the Consensus had recalculated in response were all set down. Some objections had been accepted, some had been rejected, and even the rejected ones had grounds attached explaining why.

I was struck, reading through these grounds for rejection. Rejection hadn't been handled carelessly. Instead of closing the matter with one line — "insufficient grounds" — it explained, with numbers and cases, exactly why the grounds were insufficient. I thought, at the time, that this city was truly conscientious.

The names of the people who'd filed objections were written as numbers too, and I noticed the same number recurring across several cases. I realized this was the trace of one person filing an objection more than once. The first and second had been rejected, and on the third, partly accepted. Reading through those three attempts in order, I tried to picture the face of someone who hadn't given up. There was no face. Only a number. And yet I could still sense that this person had been tenacious. That a record can show you an attitude even when it won't show you a face — that was the first time I understood that.

I read back over the passage where the third objection was partly accepted. It set down, too, exactly what had changed from the first two times. One newly submitted piece of material had been decisive — material that hadn't existed for the first two objections. Seeing that, I confirmed once more that this city truly did change its conclusion when the material changed. This wasn't a stubborn system. It was flexible in the face of new grounds.

That day I gathered the material and settled at an empty desk near the meeting room to read. No one asked me why I was sitting there. That evening I told Tyche about the experience.

"…I looked into a case that was completely unfamiliar to me today. It was really all there."

"…Of course. It's all there."

"…But the strange thing is, nobody says anything to me about it. About why I'm looking at it."

Tyche laughed.

"…Why would they ask? It's there to be looked at."

"…But other cities don't show any of this, apparently."

"…So I've heard. I've heard that too. But I don't really understand why they wouldn't show it. Isn't it because there's something to hide?"

I nodded at that. It sounded right. If there's nothing to hide, of course it would be shown.

I came to enjoy this habit of inspection often, after that. Not for any particular purpose — it was simply interesting. Picking an entirely unfamiliar case from an entirely unfamiliar district and reading it start to finish. I wondered why other people didn't do this.

I once asked the reading room librarian about it.

"…Does anyone besides me come look at unfamiliar cases like this?"

The librarian searched their memory for a moment and said,

"…Sometimes. But most people come looking only for what's connected to themselves. Someone who just reads something unrelated for its own sake — well, they're rare."

"…Has there been anyone else like me?"

"…There has. People like that were usually researchers. History, or statistics."

Hearing that, it struck me as a little odd that I was doing this without being a researcher at all. But being odd wasn't an unpleasant feeling.

The librarian gave me a further look around the reading room. The stacks weren't large, and a few electronic terminals sat around. A usage notice was posted on one wall, and it read, among other things: "All material in this reading room may be inspected by any citizen of the Equity. No separate approval procedure is required." I read that sentence twice. That no approval was required seemed to sum up this city better than any explanation could.

"…When was this reading room established?"

"…I don't know exactly, myself. It's old. I've heard it's been here since the city was founded."

I was satisfied with that answer. That it had been there from the beginning. It sounded to me like this hadn't been thrown together hastily later on, but that this city had decided, from the moment it was founded, never to hide anything.

Feeling good that day, before I left the reading room, I asked the librarian one more thing.

"…Is there anything you truly can't see here? Really?"

The librarian thought for a moment and answered.

"…Hmm, well, obviously nothing that hasn't been filed yet. Once it's filed, you can see all of it."

"…What about what hasn't been filed?"

"…That doesn't exist yet, so. How would you show something that isn't there."

I laughed at that answer. It was the obvious thing to say. Asking to be shown something that doesn't exist was a request that made no sense to begin with. I didn't find that answer strange in the slightest.

The librarian added, "…But once it's filed, you really can see everything. If there's ever something we can't show you, it's genuinely because it hasn't been filed yet. There's no other reason." I believed that. I had every reason to. Up to that day, I had never once had the experience of this city hiding something from me. So I had no reason not to believe it.

Leaving the reading room, I confirmed one thing to myself all over again. Every document I'd seen that day, anyone else could see exactly the same way. I hadn't been given some special privilege. I liked that equality.

I looked up the inspection regulations too. Surprisingly simple. Identity check, application, inspection. The only restrictions were on a small portion of personal information, and even that wasn't fully concealed — it was replaced with a number.

I liked that restriction. A method that protected a person while never hiding the record itself. A name got swapped for a number, but what had happened to that person stayed on the record, unchanged.

Looking back now, this part strangely locks together with something else. Being turned into a number meant becoming anonymous — but it also meant that person could never again find their own name in this record. At the time I read this only as protection. I didn't know yet how to read it any other way.

Since I'd gone looking through the regulation document, I looked over the old history of revisions to the inspection rules too. There had been several revisions, and most of them moved in the direction of making the procedure simpler. At first you'd had to write three pages of application; now it was one. At first approval had taken days; now it was instant. I liked that every one of these revisions moved in the direction of making inspection easier. This city was moving, over time, in the direction of ever more transparency.

I read that regulation document from beginning to end. The front held the inspection procedure, the back the restrictions. The restrictions were genuinely short. Exactly three lines. Personal information replaced with a number; certain items concerning minors carry a notice when inspected; and the last line read this: "Anything not specified in this regulation is not restricted from inspection."

I liked that last line best of all. That not restricting was the default. I guessed that in other cities it was probably the opposite — that only what was specified could be inspected, and everything else was forbidden. Here it was the reverse. Prohibition was the exception.

In the margin of that revision-history document, I found a note someone had left by hand, long ago. The ink had faded badly. It read: "Inspection is not a right — it is the baseline." Who had written it, and when, wasn't recorded. I liked that sentence enough to copy it into my notebook.

A few days later, I looked up a very old case. Something from about twenty years back. It was all there. Only the form from that era differed slightly from now — the content was every bit as thorough. Grounds, objections, confirmation, and at the very bottom, the signature box.

It was empty.

I didn't think that was strange then, either. That a twenty-year-old form had a signature box too, and that it was empty too, looked to me like nothing more than this city's consistency. If anything, it was reassuring. It meant this wasn't only true of recent documents — it had been this way, straight through, from long ago. Nothing had suddenly changed. This had simply always been what kind of city it was.

Returning that material as I left, I thought this had been a pretty good day. That in a city that hides nothing, you could open up anything at all. I thought that was freedom.

I told my aunt about it when I got home too. "…Auntie, I read a case today that had absolutely nothing to do with me. I even looked at one from twenty years ago." My aunt asked, looking curious, "…Why would you look at something like that?" "…No reason. It was interesting." My aunt laughed. "…That's such a you answer." I couldn't tell whether that was a compliment, but looking at her face, it seemed closer to one.

"…But Auntie, it really was all there. Nothing was hidden at all."

My aunt paused over the dishes and looked at me for a moment.

"…That's a good thing. But everything being there doesn't necessarily mean you can know everything."

"…What do you mean by that?"

"…No, never mind. I don't really know myself. Anyway, it's good that it's all there."

I didn't pay much attention to my aunt trailing off like that, at the time. She sometimes said things and then pulled them back in mid-sentence. I only thought of it as a habit of hers.

That night, before I fell asleep, I went back over, once more, the twelve cases I'd read that day, in order. None of them had felt forced. None of them had felt manufactured. The grounds had piled up the way grounds should, and the conclusion had followed from the grounds. I thought that was proof of a truly good city. A bad city, I thought, wouldn't have gone to the trouble of piling up its grounds this conscientiously. I fell asleep easily that night, thinking that thought.

The next morning, on my way to the meeting room, I passed the reading room again. The door wasn't open yet, but I slowed my steps a little and looked at it anyway. The fact that everything about this city was held inside, in that moment, felt like nothing more than reassuring.

(Continued in Chapter 8.)

Chapter 8. The One Who Reads to the End

_Previously_ — Hora confirming with her own eyes that the Consensus
truly hides nothing. An unrelated case, material from twenty years back —
a single application form was enough to see it all. Her aunt let slip,
almost in passing, "Everything being there doesn't necessarily mean you
can know everything," but Hora didn't hold onto it.

I still haven't seen anyone in that meeting room, besides me, read the full text to the end.

I don't want to write this as if it were something to boast about. I've never once thought of it that way. Reading it all didn't make me any smarter, and it didn't lead me to any more correct a judgment. I simply couldn't sit comfortably if I hadn't read it all. That's all there was to it.

To be precise, it's less that I haven't seen anyone and more that I'm certain I never have. Every time a session ended, I stayed behind alone and read. Everyone else looked only at the summary and judged whether to object from that. That was enough. The summary was accurate.

I once asked the person next to me about it.

"…You don't read the full text?"

"…Why bother? It's all in the summary."

"…In case something's missing from the summary, maybe."

They looked at me like I was strange.

"…The summary is made by the Consensus too. If the Consensus could leave something out, it wouldn't be the summary you couldn't trust — it'd be the full text itself."

I couldn't argue with that. It was true. Making the summary was, in the end, the same system. If you trusted the system, you had to trust the summary too.

That person told me about when they'd first come to this room too. "…I used to read it all too, at first. About six months, I think. But it matched the summary every single time, so at some point I figured looking at just the summary was enough." I found that six months striking. I was already nearing two hundred days of reading, and this person had stopped after six months. I found myself wondering, in that moment, why I couldn't stop. No answer came.

And yet I kept reading. Believing and habit lived on different levels. I believed in the Consensus, and at the same time, I couldn't stop reading to the end. At the time, I didn't think of these two things as a contradiction.

That person went on to say a bit more about themselves. "…I read it too, at first. But after a few months it all started to look alike. The summary was never wrong, not once, so at some point you just stop reading." I understood what they meant. I checked too, every time I read. The summary really was never wrong, not once. But that checking itself couldn't stop in me. It wasn't that checking reassured me — checking was simply what I did.

"…How long do you think you'll keep reading everything like that, Hora?"

"…I don't know. I think I'll keep doing it."

"…Doesn't it wear you out?"

I answered that question honestly.

"…It does. But not reading makes me more anxious."

"…Anxious? About what?"

I couldn't answer that one right away either. I didn't know exactly what I was anxious about myself. It was just that if there was a document I'd skipped over without reading, thoughts of it kept coming back to me that night before I fell asleep. Only checking what I'd missed let me sleep easy.

"…I don't know. It's just how it is."

They nodded at that without any particular reply. I found that silence comfortable. It wasn't a silence that tried to talk me out of it.

A few days later, I happened to overhear that person talking about me to someone else. Not to badmouth me — just explaining me to another member. "…The new one's a little unusual, but not unusual in a bad way. The questions she comes up with after reading everything turn out to be useful, more often than not." Hearing that made my face heat up a little. At the same time it wasn't an unpleasant feeling. That was the first time I'd heard "unusual" and "useful" sitting in the same sentence.

Once, they asked me in passing, "…Doesn't it get boring, staying behind alone reading for two hours?" I thought about it and answered, "…Sometimes it does. But once I've read it all, that boredom fades fast." They laughed and shook their head. "…I can't stand that boredom long enough to read it all. You're really something." I still couldn't tell if that was a compliment, but it didn't sound bad.

After that, a few members started asking me things directly. "…Was there anything unusual in the full text of this provision?" "…What did the full text look like for that last case?" I answered conscientiously every time. Mostly I said there was nothing much. There really wasn't, most of the time. Sometimes I'd point out one small detail, though it never once changed a decision. But the fact that people had started asking me at all — that struck me as a little remarkable, in itself.

Tyche asked a similar question once too.

"…Why do you always read all the way through the full text, Hora?"

I couldn't answer right away. After a moment I said,

"…Just, it's a habit."

"…Why did the habit start?"

I hesitated, wondering whether to bring up my mother. In the end I didn't. It felt too personal.

"…I don't know. It just turned out that way."

Tyche nodded. They didn't press further. I was grateful for that consideration, but at the same time I felt my own answer had fallen short. It wasn't that I really didn't know. I just hadn't wanted to say it.

Tyche asked something else instead.

"…So what changes, once you've read it all?"

"…Hmm, nothing really changes. It's just that I end up in a state of knowing."

"…A state of knowing?"

"…Yes. A state where I've confirmed everything there is myself. Passing over something without confirming it makes me more uncomfortable."

Tyche looked thoughtful, hearing that.

"…I'm the opposite. It's a relief knowing the summary's right even without checking. But don't you not trust that knowing itself, Hora?"

That question caught me off guard. It wasn't that I didn't trust it. I did trust the Consensus. And yet I couldn't bear not checking. I couldn't find an answer, that day, for how to explain both of these things together.

Tyche looked a little sorry after saying that. "…Ah, that might have come across kind of harsh. I didn't mean it that way." "…No, it's fine. It's a question I'd never thought about myself." I really was fine. Tyche's question had been sharp, but not in a tone that pushed me into a corner. It had simply been curious. I liked that about them.

Once I'd read the full text in the meeting room, I'd also gotten into the habit of laying the summary and the full text side by side to compare them. The summary really was accurate. Never once wrong. But there were things in the full text that weren't in the summary. Mostly minor details. A past case similar to one in a given district, say, or the origin of a particular provision.

Every time I found details like this, I wrote them in my notebook. Not for any particular use. Writing them down was simply the habit.

I once showed that notebook to Tyche. They looked through it, one entry at a time, and said,

"…This is all correct. But why does it matter?"

"…I don't know if it matters. It was just there."

"…Being there doesn't mean it all matters."

I couldn't answer that either. It was true. Being there didn't mean it all mattered. But that day, too, I didn't throw the notebook away.

I'd actually started keeping notebooks before the meeting room, back when I was still in school. I had a habit of writing things in the margins of my exam answer sheets too. Teachers sometimes frowned on it, since it made the sheets messy. I always started to erase it, and ended up handing it in as it was, unable to. Erasing it felt stranger than leaving it.

The notebook had grown fairly thick over the course of a few months, that way. Sometimes, when I was bored, I'd flip back through it from the start. Most of it really was trivial. The origin of some district's name, why some provision's numbers had come out the way they had, which year had had an unusually high number of objections. None of it seemed connected to any of the rest. I thought that was only natural. This notebook hadn't been kept to dig up some suspicion — it was just things that had caught my eye while reading, copied down.

There was no particular order or classification anywhere in the notebook. It was just written in whatever order things caught my eye. Sometimes I actually liked that disorder. It wasn't an organized record — it was just the trace of the spots where I'd paused while reading.

I once showed the notebook to my aunt too. She flipped through a few pages and said, "…This is just like something your mother used to do." Surprised, I asked, "…Mom did this too?" "…Yes, she used to write things in the margins of her ledgers. Didn't seem to serve any particular purpose, but she liked it." Hearing that gave me a strange feeling. I couldn't tell whether this was a habit I'd inherited, or just a coincidental resemblance.

I mentioned something similar to the Secretary once too. I'd gone to return material at the counter, and since the Secretary happened to be there, I took out the notebook and showed them. They looked it over and their face turned briefly interested.

"…You record all of this?"

"…Yes, just a habit."

"…That's a good habit. I used to do something similar, myself."

"…Not anymore?"

The Secretary thought for a moment before answering.

"…At some point it just stopped. Once the work piled up."

I took that answer without much thought. It seemed plausible enough that a heavier workload could do that. I didn't notice, at the time, that "at some point" had come up again.

"…Did you used to carry a notebook around too?"

"…Something like that. A small notepad. I don't even know where it is anymore, now."

"…Wouldn't you want to look for it?"

The Secretary smiled briefly. "…Hmm. I wonder what the point would be, even if I found it." I sensed something like regret in that answer, but it never showed on their face at all, and I wondered if I'd only imagined it.

The Secretary looked back over my notebook and added a few more words.

"…Seeing this, you must like observing things, Hora."

"…Not so much observing — it's just that things catch my eye while I read."

"…That's what observing is. I used to be the same. I'd write down whatever caught my eye."

"…Why don't you anymore, then?"

The Secretary smiled briefly.

"…Convening and passing things on takes all day, so there's no room left for anything to catch my eye. And besides, whatever I might write down would become my own opinion, wouldn't it. My own opinion isn't allowed to go into the minutes."

I nodded at that explanation. This person had given up the habit of observing on their own. So that their own opinions wouldn't get mixed into the record. I thought that was a kind of professional restraint. How long, and how deeply, that restraint had changed this person — I couldn't gauge it, not then.

"…So what opinions are left in you now?"

I asked, on impulse. The Secretary looked at me for a moment, then laughed.

"…Hmm. That's a question I've never thought about."

That answer was light, like a joke, but I felt there was something behind that lightness. I didn't know exactly what. All that stayed with me was that the laugh had ended a little too quickly.

There was a time I wondered myself why I was so especially slow. Ever since school, everything took me longer than it took other people. Exams, reading, even eating.

I once asked my aunt about it.

"…Auntie, why am I so slow?"

My aunt thought for a moment and answered.

"…You're not slow. You just hate missing anything."

"…That's the same thing as being slow."

"…Slow and thorough are different things. It's just that the world usually only notices the slow part and doesn't give the thorough part its due."

That felt like comfort, hearing it, but it didn't entirely settle me either. The fact of being slow didn't change.

Once the meal was ready, my aunt told me an old story. When I was little, reading picture books, the other kids would skim just the pictures and move on, but I traced every single letter, she said. "…Your teacher worried you were slow. But I never worried." "…Why not?" "…The questions you asked after finishing were always different from everyone else's. They were questions that came out of being slow. They weren't bad."

Hearing that, I felt, all over again, grateful that there had been at least one person at home who never worried about my slowness. At school it had always been something to worry about; at home, it wasn't. I think that difference is part of what carried me this far.

"…But Auntie, in the meeting room, I'm the only one who's like this. Doesn't that look strange?"

My aunt laughed.

"…So what if it does. You're just doing it your way."

That comforted me a little. Your way. My aunt used that phrase so easily, but it always sounded difficult to me.

That evening, I stayed in the meeting room and read a new case from beginning to end. I read the summary first, then the full text, then the underlying material all the way through. It took three hours. The caretaker came and went twice.

Once I'd finished, I looked back over the last page one more time. The list of grounds, the progress of the handling, and down to the blank box at the very bottom. I wrote the gist of the day's case in my notebook in two lines and cleared my seat. That was all there was to that day. Nothing in particular weighed on me.

On the way home I passed by the water channel. The sound of the water was the same as ever. I didn't stop that day either. My legs ached from three hours of reading, and all I could think of was getting home quickly to lie down.

My aunt was waiting by the door. "…Late again today." "…Yeah, there was a thick one." My aunt served dinner without asking me anything. I was grateful for that. If I'd had to explain what I'd read every single day, I think I would have given up this habit much sooner than I did.

(Continued in Chapter 9.)

Chapter 9. Four Hundred People

_Previously_ — Hora is the only person in the meeting room who reads the
full text to the end. Asked by Tyche why, she only said, "Just a habit."
Asked what changes once she's read it all, she said, "Nothing changes.
I just end up in a state of knowing." Tyche wasn't entirely convinced by
that answer, but didn't press further either.

I started paying close attention to the handling-progress page for a very small reason.

That day, as usual, I'd finished reading the full text and was skimming the handling progress attached at the very back. A table listing, in chronological order, which department had done what and when. I always flipped through this table fast. It was nothing but numbers and department names lined up, no name in sight, so there wasn't much pleasure in reading it.

That day the table was unusually long. Over two pages. Bored, I counted the lines.

Four hundred and twelve lines.

I thought at first I must have miscounted, and counted again. Same result. Four hundred and twelve lines. I pulled out the handling-progress tables from cases I'd read before and looked them over again. None of them fell below a hundred lines. Even a short case ran to about a hundred and thirty; a long one ran past five hundred. That was the first day I truly felt, in my bones, just how many hands had touched the forty pages of material I'd been reading, session after session. Until then I'd taken that part as nothing more than a table and passed over it.

I asked Tyche about that number the next day.

"…Is it right that four hundred and twelve people were involved in this case?"

Tyche flipped through the screen and nodded.

"…That's right. The average is around four hundred. Bigger cases run higher."

"…Four hundred people? Really?"

"…Yes. Why, does that surprise you?"

I was surprised. Having watched the sessions end in that twenty-two-seat meeting room, I'd been vaguely assuming those twenty-two people were the ones deciding. Four hundred didn't fit that picture at all.

"…But there are only twenty-two people in the meeting room."

"…The decision doesn't happen in the meeting room. The meeting room is only the last step. Before that there are people who compile the material, people who review it, people who take in objections, people who pass it on, people who confirm it. All separate people."

Tyche pulled out one more document to show me. The numbers were broken down by department. Sixty people for the material-gathering team, eighty for the grounds-review team, a hundred for opinion collection by district, and then everyone else who handled passing it on, recording, and confirming.

"…All of this is for one case?"

"…Yes. Bigger cases get split into even more."

I told my aunt about it that evening.

"…Auntie, apparently four hundred people are involved in a single case."

My aunt stopped what she was doing and looked at me.

"…Four hundred? What do you mean?"

"…There's a separate person who gathers material, a separate person who reviews it, a separate person who passes it on. All separate. So added up, it's over four hundred."

My aunt thought for a moment and said,

"…Then if something goes wrong, it's not one person's fault."

Hearing that, I realized it was exactly what I'd wanted to say. I took it as a good thing, that day. My aunt seemed to mean it as a good thing too.

"…Right. Since it's not one person deciding the whole thing, even if that person had bad intentions, the other four hundred would just check it again anyway."

My aunt nodded, ladling out the soup.

"…Hearing it like that, it's not a bad system. If anything's off anywhere, it'd get filtered out somewhere else."

I remember this evening precisely. That day, the number four hundred put me at ease. I still don't think that ease was entirely wrong, even now. But at the time, I didn't think, even for a moment, to look at the other side of this number. I don't see that side until years later. It doesn't appear in this chapter. I've decided to write this record honestly, so I won't write what I didn't see then as if I saw it now.

A few days later, curious about why this number had come to be, I went looking for the regulation behind it. The reading-room librarian found me an old piece of material — discussion records from forty-some years back, from the early days when the Consensus was first introduced.

Back then, far fewer people had been involved, it said. Around ten to a case. But there had been a problem. A few times, when someone acquainted with one of those ten filed an objection, the speed of handling or the outcome shifted, subtly.

Checking into it, it wasn't anything you'd call manipulation. No bribes, no threats. Just listening a little longer to someone you knew, and passing over someone you didn't a little faster. A very small difference. But when that small difference piled up, it split the outcome.

So the city chose to increase the number of people involved. It broke up what any one person could see into small pieces, so that no one could hold an entire case in their own hands. Whoever gathered the material didn't review it; whoever reviewed it didn't pass it on; whoever passed it on didn't confirm it. Each person touched exactly one small fragment.

The last line of the document read: "The expansion of the number of participants is a measure to structurally dilute the influence of any given individual."

I read that sentence twice. Dilute. I liked that word. Even if someone wanted to put a hand on something, the portion they could reach was so small that reaching it left them with no power at all. I thought that was a truly clever design.

The librarian found me one more piece from that old period. A deliberation over one district's ration amount, preserved as the last case on record from the ten-member-committee era. It later came out that one of the ten members lived on the same lane as the person who'd filed the objection. There hadn't been any improper favor. The investigation record said so plainly. But when that member had reviewed the objection, they'd said, in passing, "Ah, I know that household's situation" — and it had stayed on the record in the minutes exactly as said. The real problem was that this one remark hadn't tipped the outcome to either side. Because no one could prove it hadn't.

Reading this record, I didn't feel, even for a moment, that this member had been a bad person. They'd simply recognized a neighbor. And yet this city judged that even that much recognizing was dangerous. I didn't think that judgment was excessively strict. If anything, I thought it had to be exactly that strict to live up to the name the Equity.

A few days later, I told the Secretary this story too, when I stopped by the counter.

"…I read material from forty-some years ago today. Back then there were only ten members, apparently."

The Secretary paused their paperwork and looked at me.

"…That's right. Things were very different then."

"…Why did it grow all the way to four hundred?"

"…Because knowing someone keeps creeping in. When you know someone, you can't help but favor them a little. Even trying hard not to favor them, it still tips a little, in proportion to what you know. That happens even without any bad intent."

The Secretary glanced toward the window while saying that. The window you couldn't see through, and yet there were people who looked that way out of habit anyway.

"…So now no one knows each other, at all?"

"…No one does. Whoever gathers material doesn't know who reviews it. Whoever reviews it doesn't know who passes it on. Not knowing each other is what keeps things from tipping."

I found that a little sad to hear, and at the same time reassuring. That sadness and reassurance could sit together in one sentence didn't strike me as strange, at the time.

"…Do you know either, who these four hundred are?"

"…I don't. I only convene. The convening list is pulled by the Consensus and handed over. It isn't chosen by me."

I nodded, hearing that answer. Here too, once again, the Secretary said that nothing was chosen by them. That day I let the sentence pass. My mind had been taken up by the number four hundred; there was no room left to weigh that sentence.

"…Don't you think four hundred is a lot, though?"

The Secretary answered, starting to sort the paperwork again.

"…It is a lot. But knowing what happened when it was fewer — I think more is better."

I agreed with that. That day, I truly thought so.

I showed that document to Tyche the next day. Tyche already knew about it.

"…I learned about this too. At school."

"…This really seems like a great method."

"…It is. And that's why I've never once heard, in this city, of anyone being asked for a favor. Asking for one wouldn't do any good."

"…No good at all?"

"…None. Nothing is held in any one person's hands — so who would you even ask? There's no one to ask, from the start."

Hearing that, I thought of the "I'll talk to my uncle about it" I'd heard when I was fourteen. Not understanding it, back then, had been because in this city, there truly was nothing that could be settled by talking to an uncle. This number, four hundred, was exactly why. I found that connection striking.

"…But with as many as four hundred, isn't it actually harder to manage?"

Tyche shrugged.

"…It is harder. But that's exactly what the Consensus does. Gathering everything four hundred people did into one. A person couldn't do it. Because it's the Consensus, it can."

I nodded at that answer. If a person had to do it, this number would have been impossible from the start. To weave together the work of four hundred people, someone would have to know all four hundred, and no such person could exist. So this was, necessarily, work only a system could do, not a person. That day, for the first time, I clearly understood this was one of the reasons the Consensus was needed.

Out of curiosity that day, I picked one line at random from the handling-progress table. "Passed on 217, 2:20 p.m." I wanted to try finding out who this 217 was, and asked Tyche.

"…This 217 — can you find out who this person is?"

Tyche tapped at the screen a few times and shook their head.

"…That number is the counter that handled it. Not a person's number. Whoever was sitting at that counter that day becomes 217."

"…So yesterday's 217 and today's 217 could be different people?"

"…Yes, absolutely. Counters rotate in shifts."

That let a little of the air out of me. I'd been secretly hoping I might be able to follow one person all the way through this table. But there was no "one person" in the table to follow in the first place. The number was attached to a seat, not a person.

"…So you can't find anyone at all from this table?"

"…There's no need to find anyone, that's why. What matters isn't who did this work — it's that the work got done."

Hearing that answer, I felt hollow for a moment, and then found myself agreeing again. Thinking it through, Tyche was right. There wasn't really any reason I needed to know who 217 was. What I'd wanted to confirm was whether this had been done properly, and that was already fully set down in the table. Finding a person and confirming that work had been done were different matters. That day was the first time I drew that distinction clearly.

Leaving the meeting room that evening, I opened the handling-progress table once more. Four hundred and twelve lines. Each line held a number, a time, and one short word — "passed on," "reviewed," "confirmed," "convened."

I traced those lines with a finger and counted them all the way through. It took a while to count them all. While I counted, I felt strangely at ease. That four hundred and twelve people had each added one very small portion to make this single sheet of paper. It seemed like the higher that number climbed, the smaller the room got for any one person to harbor a bad intention.

On the way home I passed by the water channel. The sound of the water was still there. That day, for once, I stopped and stood watching it for a moment. The water, too, must have come flowing from somewhere, through many branches, to reach this point — and each of those branches felt, somehow, like one person's share out of the four hundred, and I laughed a little at that. I knew it was a comparison that made no real sense, but that's just what occurred to me, that day.

My aunt was waiting at the door.

"…What did you count this time?"

"…People. Four hundred and twelve."

My aunt laughed. "…Such a you kind of day." I only laughed back, without a reply.

Over dinner, I explained to my aunt everything I'd learned that day, from the start. That there used to be only ten people; that one acquaintance could tip the outcome; that this was why they'd grown it to four hundred. My aunt listened all the way through, spoon still in hand.

"…So, when there are fewer people, feeling gets mixed in, and when there are more, there's no room left for feeling to get in."

"…Yeah, exactly that."

"…But then, if there's someone really good among those four hundred, doesn't their share get diluted just the same?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. I'd never thought about it that way.

"…That's true, but isn't that unavoidable? To cut down a bad person's share, a good person's share has to shrink along with it."

My aunt looked at me for a moment and nodded.

"…I suppose so. I don't know which way is better, myself. But if it means fewer people get wronged, that's enough, I'd say."

I nodded at that answer. That day, I thought so too — that was enough.

I slept very soundly that night. I slept trusting the number four hundred as a safeguard. When that trust begins to shake, reading on in this record will tell you. But in this chapter, I still doubted nothing. That my aunt's one remark — that a good person's share gets diluted too — was, in truth, the most precise thing said about this city that whole evening, I only come to understand much, much later.

(Continued in Chapter 10.)

Chapter 10. Numbers and Categories

_Previously_ — Hora learning that, on average, four hundred people are
involved in a single case. Long ago, she was told, about ten people
decided things, until one acquaintance tipped an outcome, and the number
grew to what it is now. Her aunt asked whether a good person's share
gets diluted along with it too, but that day Hora didn't hold onto the
question for long.

I first found the way deliberation subjects were referred to strange not because it was strange, but because it wasn't strange at all.

That day I was reading a ration-adjustment case. A matter of deciding who got a little more and who got a little less. Nowhere in the full text was there a person's name. Only notations like "Third Classification, 217" or "Fifth Classification, 40." I assumed that was simply how it had always been and read on without much thought. About five pages in, it suddenly occurred to me to wonder why it was set up this way.

I asked the person next to me.

"…Why don't these people have names?"

"…They never do. It's only issued by classification and number."

"…Why?"

They answered more seriously than I'd expected.

"…If there's a name, other things keep creeping in. Oh, that's that family's kid, oh, that person had that thing happen before, that kind of thing. With only a number, none of that creeps in at all."

I nodded, hearing that. It sounded right. I kept turning through the document for a while even after that answer. Table after table of nothing but numbers and classifications went by, and strangely, my reading speed never slowed. Having no person's name made it possible, if anything, to read more calmly.

I asked Tyche again that afternoon.

"…Why do deliberation subjects only ever come out as numbers and classifications?"

Tyche paused mid-sort of some material.

"…Do you know the history behind this, Hora?"

"…No."

"…Up until about thirty years ago, apparently, names went out plainly. But looking at the records from back then, there were quite a few cases where two people with the exact same conditions but different names got different results."

"…Different how?"

"…For instance, people whose names carried a certain district weren't likely to get their objections accepted. That district had had some trouble in the past, apparently, and people quietly looked down on it just from seeing the name. Even when the person had nothing to do with it at all."

Hearing that, my heart grew heavy.

"…How many classifications are there?"

"…Nine now. There used to be twelve, but it dropped after some got merged."

"…What's the basis for dividing them?"

"…The reason for the application and the conditions. Ration-related goes into the ration classification, housing-related into the housing classification. Within that, it splits further by number."

I found that system striking for being simpler than I'd expected. That there were only nine boxes to sort people into was, if anything, a relief. I'd have thought more boxes would mean more room for people to be seen differently — but nine seemed to leave little room for that.

"…Is that why names were taken out?"

"…Yes. Once they started using only numbers and classifications instead of names, that kind of gap nearly vanished. From the statistics I learned, the district-by-district variance in how often objections were accepted was cut in half."

I told my aunt about this that evening.

"…Auntie, apparently names used to go out plainly, back when deliberations happened. But people from some district ended up worse off because of it."

My aunt stopped what she was doing and looked at me. Her face seemed more serious than usual.

"…I remember that. That's from when I was young."

"…You went through it, Auntie?"

"…Not went through it, exactly, but that's the kind of talk that went around back then. If a name that lived in a certain place got printed plain on a document, everyone somehow looked at it a bit more harshly. Thinking about it now, I can't say for sure whether that was really true, but it's true that people believed it."

"…So that's why it changed to numbers?"

"…Yes. After it changed, that kind of talk went completely quiet. I think that was a genuinely good change."

Listening to that, I felt what I'd learned overlap with what my aunt had actually lived through. Something I'd only seen as statistics was filled in by my aunt's memory.

A few days later I looked up the records from before and after that revision myself, in the reading room. I laid a few pre-revision deliberation documents side by side with a few post-revision documents of the same type and read them.

The pre-revision documents had the applicant's name and home district written from the very first line. Reading through one of them, I saw the phrase "given the nature of that district" slipped, almost casually, into the objection review comments. After that, the objection had been rejected with no further grounds given.

I stopped for a long while, looking at that sentence. "Given the nature of that district" wasn't grounds. And yet at the time, it had passed for grounds. I thought this was frightening. It hadn't been written by a bad person. It had simply been written by someone who thought the way people thought, back in an era when everyone thought that way.

I found a few more documents from applicants out of that same district and laid them side by side to read. Their conditions were all different, but strangely, their grounds for rejection were similar. "Difficult to accept given the circumstances." "Rejected upon comprehensive judgment." There were almost no numbers. The calculation-grounds table that never failed to appear in documents from other districts was missing from about half of this district's documents. I wanted to believe that was chance, but past five cases, it was too uniform to call chance.

I copied those five cases into my notebook. Not to reach any particular conclusion. It just caught my eye, so I wrote it down. I didn't think, at the time, that this notebook would ever be used in any way later on.

The post-revision documents didn't have a single line like that. Every rejection and acceptance came with nothing but numbers and grounds attached. Things like "This application's standard-fulfillment rate is 72 percent, falling short of the threshold for acceptance." No district, no name, no hint of what kind of person this was. I found that dryness, if anything, a relief.

I also looked separately at records from the first few years right after the revision. There had been pushback in the early days, it noted. Several objections had come in arguing that being called only by number felt like being treated as an object. The Consensus's responses to those objections were on record too, and they all carried the same substance: "This measure is not intended to harm the applicant's dignity, and is instead meant to prevent disadvantage arising from factors outside the stated conditions." Reading that response, I could understand the hearts of the people who'd pushed back, and I could understand the intent behind the Consensus's answer too. Neither side had been wrong.

The objections from a few more years on had an entirely different character. Objections to being called by number at all had nearly disappeared, and what remained were only objections that a number had been assigned incorrectly. I found that shift striking. People seemed to have stopped resenting the number itself, and started caring only about the number being used correctly.

Within that same week, I asked the Secretary the same thing too. I'd gone to the counter to hand in paperwork, and there happened to be a quiet moment when I could ask.

"…I hear names used to go out plainly on deliberations."

The Secretary paused mid-sort of a folder and looked at me.

"…That's right. When I first started this work, I saw paperwork with names on it too."

"…Is it very different, from then to now?"

"…Very different. Back then, if I was flipping through paperwork and an unfamiliar name — a name I knew — came up, my hand would stop without my meaning it to. That doesn't happen now. A number gives no reason to stop."

I was a little surprised by that. That the Secretary, too, had had moments like that felt striking, all over again.

"…What changes, when your hand stops?"

"…It changes. The longer you stop, the longer you end up looking at that paperwork, and the longer you look, the more you want to add something to it. Even without bad intent. That's giving this one piece of paperwork something the others don't get. I think that's the most dangerous thing there is."

I nodded at that explanation. Only then did I feel, a little more in my bones, why this city had gone to such lengths to erase names.

"…So is it easier for you now?"

The Secretary thought for a moment before answering.

"…It's easier. There's nothing to stop for."

Hearing that answer, I sensed that the word "easier," coming out of this person's mouth, carried some other weight. I didn't know, at the time, exactly what it was.

"…Do you remember that paperwork from back then too? From the days when names were still on it?"

"…A few of them, I remember. Not all of them. There was less paperwork back then. Probably only possible because there were fewer people."

"…Was it better then, or is it better now?"

This time the Secretary answered without hesitating.

"…Now is better. That much I'm sure of."

I was glad of that certainty. I'd seen this person speak this plainly, about anything, almost never before that point.

I confirmed this again with Tyche the next day.

"…So is there really no gap like that anymore?"

"…Almost none. I can't say completely none, but compared to before, it's not even close. It's been checked by district, by gender, by age group — all of it comes out nearly even."

"…Is all of that made public?"

"…Yes. It goes out as statistics every year. I can show you all five of the last five years, if you'd like."

Tyche really did bring me those statistical tables the next day. I flipped through them one page at a time. It really was true. The acceptance rate by classification and by district all fell within a similar range. There was no section unusually low or unusually high.

"…Seeing this really makes me feel, all over again, what a genuinely good city this is."

Tyche smiled.

"…I like this table too. It reassures me every time I look at it."

Of course, I also thought, that day, about what had been lost behind that ease. Being left as only a number also meant no one knew what kind of person that was. I said as much to Tyche.

"…But if there's only a number, doesn't that mean no one knows what kind of person that is?"

"…That's exactly what makes it fair. Not knowing what kind of person it is means judging only by the conditions."

"…But isn't it a little sad? That person must have had a name."

Tyche looked thoughtful at that.

"…If I had to choose between sadness and fairness, I'd choose fairness. If having a name is such a good thing, we could go back to a world with names like before — but then the gap comes back with it."

I couldn't argue with that. It was true. In this city, names and the gap had disappeared together.

That day, leaving the meeting room, I ran into someone in front of the counter. Someone who'd come to have their classification confirmed, requesting a correction because their number had been written wrong on the paperwork. I waited beside them while the counter clerk fixed it, and once the correction was done, I spoke to them.

"…Is it a big hassle, when a number gets wrong?"

They looked at me for a moment and answered.

"…It's a hassle. But hearing about how it used to be, this is nothing at all. In my mother's time, apparently, it wasn't classification — people were just split by what neighborhood they lived in. Someone from one neighborhood, whatever they applied for came out late; someone from another, it came out fast. There's none of that now, is there. As long as the number's right, everyone's handled exactly the same."

I nodded, hearing that. They said it without any particular resentment. If anything, their face looked satisfied with how things worked now.

"…So this way is better?"

"…Much better. I don't care if no one knows who I am, at all. As long as my conditions get looked at properly."

I turned that answer over for a long time. For this person, losing a name wasn't a loss — it was a gain. Hearing that, I found myself weighing, all over again that day, just how much of a relief the system of numbers and classifications must genuinely have been, for so many people.

After that person left, I read, once more, the notice posted on the counter's bulletin board. "This system protects every applicant from factors outside their stated conditions." That day was the first time I truly read that one line. I must have passed it dozens of times before, but that day the sentence reached me differently.

On the way home, I called back to mind the statistical table I'd seen that day. A bar graph stretching evenly across every district. I liked it. No district stuck out unusually far, and no district was unusually pressed down.

Passing by the water channel, it occurred to me, suddenly, that this water had no name either, no matter where it had come from. It was only ever called the water channel. People, too, were each called by number and classification, but that water didn't even have that much — it was simply "the water channel." I found the thought amusing and laughed a little to myself. I couldn't decide whether to call that treatment worse than a person's, or freer than a person's. I let the thought go without dwelling on it long, that day.

My aunt asked, as she was setting dinner out, "…What did you learn today?" "…About numbers and classifications. That taking out names made things more fair." My aunt nodded. "…That's right. That one's a genuine improvement. That's one I can confidently say got better."

I held onto that for a long while. It was rare for my aunt to speak with this much certainty. Leaning on that certainty, I closed out the day as a very good one.

After we'd finished eating, my aunt suddenly asked, "…But Hora, do you know your own number?" I was caught off guard for a moment. I knew my committee assignment number, but I'd never once looked up my classification number as a citizen. "…I don't know. I should look it up." My aunt laughed. "…Knowing it won't really do much for you. But it's fun to know. It's seeing how you're written down as a figure, inside this city." Hearing that, I thought, for the first time, that I too might already exist, somewhere in some document, with no name at all, only a number. That thought wasn't frightening to me, that day. If anything, it sounded fair.

(Continued in Chapter 11.)

Chapter 11. The Water Channel

_Previously_ — Hora learning that deliberation subjects are called only
by number and classification, never by name. There was once a time when
outcomes split on the basis of a name, and now that kind of gap has
nearly vanished. Her aunt said, "That one, I can confidently say got
better." Leaning on that certainty, Hora closed the day out as a good
one.

The first time I went right up close to the water channel was purely because I had time to spare.

The session ended early that day. There were about two hours free before the next one. Ordinarily I would have spent that time reading more material, but that day, as it happened, there was no new case in hand yet. I left the meeting room and, walking, turned toward the western water channel I'd only ever passed by before.

Up close, the channel was wider than I'd expected. I'd always taken it for nothing more than a spot roughly set with stones, but in places it was actually wide enough for two people to lie down side by side. The current wasn't strong. Soil-colored water moved between small stones, making its sound the whole way.

I crouched at the water's edge. I thought about touching it, hesitated again, and didn't. Instead I walked along the channel. The closer I got to the city's edge, the more natural the channel's shape became. It didn't look like a place anyone had shaped — it looked as though the water had carved its own way.

I didn't think, that day, to try to find out where this water came from. I was only curious. I didn't know, then, where that curiosity would lead.

There was a small signpost standing beside the channel. Its color had faded. It read only "Western Boundary of the Equity." Not one line about the water.

I studied that signpost for a long while. That a signpost marking the city's boundary said nothing at all about the water flowing right beside it seemed, at the time, like nothing more than the sign having gone up long ago, with the water appearing later. I walked around behind it too. There was nothing there.

Walking a little further along the channel, I saw an old man sitting by the water. I went over and spoke to him.

"…Do you come here often?"

"…Sometimes. I've been coming since I was young."

"…Do you know how long this water's been here?"

The old man looked at the water for a long while before answering.

"…It wasn't here when I was young. It can't have been here long. But I don't know exactly when it appeared. One day I noticed it there."

"…Doesn't that strike you as strange?"

The old man laughed.

"…It does. But strange and worrying are different things. The water's not doing anyone any harm — it's just flowing, is all."

I had nothing in particular to argue against that. It was true. The water was just flowing.

The old man sat a while longer, then got up to leave. As he went, he left one thing behind him. "…If you're curious, you can file an application. This city's good at that sort of thing." I laughed, hearing that. I thought it was such a fitting piece of advice for this city. Whatever you're curious about, an application. That day, that advice sounded entirely natural to me.

I brought this up with Tyche the next day.

"…I went up close to the water channel yesterday. Seeing it up close, it's bigger than I thought."

"…Ah, that water channel. I walk that way sometimes too."

"…Has it ever been taken up as a case? It's come right into the city."

Tyche looked thoughtful for a moment.

"…Hmm. As far as I know, no. Should I check?"

Tyche searched right there. After flipping through the screen a few times, they shook their head.

"…Nothing. There's not a single case related to the water channel."

"…Doesn't that seem strange? Water's come all the way into the city's boundary."

"…Not so much strange as — no one's brought it up yet, that's all."

I didn't quite understand that, that day.

"…No one's brought it up? Then who's supposed to?"

"…Anyone. Anyone in this city can bring up a case. But there has to be someone bringing it up before the Consensus can handle it."

"…But everyone walks right past that water. Why hasn't anyone brought it up?"

Tyche shrugged.

"…That, I couldn't say. Maybe everyone thought it wasn't their jurisdiction. Maybe they thought it wasn't urgent."

That answer felt lacking to me, somehow.

"…So administratively, this water doesn't exist right now?"

Tyche looked at me straight on and answered.

"…That's right. If it's never been submitted, it doesn't exist. Not because it's hidden."

I repeated that sentence to myself again, right there. If it's never been submitted, it doesn't exist. I thought that sentence was strangely clean. Far less frightening than the word "hidden."

"…But it's strange. Water this big is flowing, and there's no record of it at all."

"…It isn't that there's no record. It's that there's no case. A record gets created when someone observes something. But someone has to bring what they observed up as a case for the Consensus to move on it. Just looking isn't a case."

I turned that distinction over. There was a very clear threshold between seeing something and bringing it up. Up to now I'd thought this city knew everything, about anything. But hearing Tyche, what this city knows and what this city handles were two different matters.

"…So could I bring this up as a case?"

Tyche looked at me, surprised.

"…You could. But why? It's not dangerous or anything."

"…Just curious. It struck me as strange that no one's brought it up."

"…Hmm, that's so you, Hora. But the form for bringing up a case is a little demanding. You need an observation record, and the applicant's name has to go on it too."

"…A name?"

"…Yes. The case-application form has a box for the applicant's name. It's different from the signature box. An applicant has to write their own name for it to be accepted."

Hearing that, I felt a little puzzled. Why the signature box was always empty while the application still needed a name — I was curious about that difference. But I didn't push that curiosity further, that day. I figured it was probably just a different kind of form.

I didn't bring up the case that day. I was only curious.

I stopped by the reading room that afternoon too. I asked the librarian for every piece of material related to the western boundary. They pulled a list for me — only a handful of documents about roads, boundary markers, and district divisions. Not one mention of the water.

"…Is there anything related to the water near there, by any chance?"

The librarian tried a few more searches, changing the search terms each time.

"…Nothing. Nothing comes up for 'water channel,' nothing for 'waterway,' nothing for 'stream.' It doesn't seem to have ever been applied for."

"…Doesn't that seem strange? It really exists."

The librarian shrugged.

"…Existing in reality and existing on paper are different things. We can only find what exists on paper for you. If there's no document, there's nothing I can do either."

Hearing that answer, that day was the first time I saw clearly exactly where this city's full disclosure ended. Disclosure was complete. But there was no way to disclose something for which there was nothing to disclose in the first place. Asking to inspect something that didn't exist was a request that made no sense. I'd already learned that once before. Something never filed doesn't exist, the librarian had said then, smiling.

I called that exchange back to mind again that day. How do you show something that isn't there, and if it's never been submitted, it doesn't exist — only then did I properly connect the two as the same thing. In this city, the word "doesn't exist" always meant this. Not that it had been concealed — only that it had never been brought up.

A few days later I told the Secretary about this too.

"…The western water channel — I hear it's never been taken up as a case."

The Secretary paused mid-sort of some paperwork.

"…Yes, not as far as I know."

"…Why do you think no one's brought it up?"

The Secretary thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. But in this city, when something never gets brought up as a case, there are usually one of two reasons. It isn't urgent, or the jurisdiction isn't clear."

"…What does unclear jurisdiction mean?"

"…A case where it's ambiguous which district should handle it. The western boundary isn't fully inside the city, and it isn't fully outside it either. With a case like that, it's common for no one to step forward first. When you can't be sure it's your jurisdiction, everyone tends to just wait and see."

I nodded, listening to that explanation.

"…Then couldn't you bring it up, yourself?"

The Secretary looked at me for a moment.

"…Only convening happens here. A case can only be accepted once there's an applicant. I could become the applicant, but doing that would mean writing my own name down."

Hearing that, I sensed, dimly, that writing a name down wasn't a simple thing in this city. Exactly why, I didn't know, then.

"…And you don't?"

The Secretary was silent for a moment before answering.

"…Only convening happens here."

The same words, repeated twice. I didn't think this was avoiding the question. It simply looked, to me, like that was genuinely all there was for this person.

"…So will this water channel just keep on being like this, forever? If no one brings it up?"

"…Probably. Until someone does."

"…Who's supposed to bring it up? Is there someone assigned?"

"…No one. Anyone can bring it up, and it's also possible that no one does. There are almost no cases in this city with someone specifically assigned who has to bring them up. Only the urgent ones have anyone assigned to them."

Hearing that answer, I began to grasp, for the first time, just how much this city simply leaves sitting between "anyone could do it" and "no one does." Not out of any bad intent. It was just that, somewhere in that gap, there really was nothing at all.

"…But doesn't that worry you? If anyone could do it and no one does, doesn't that mean it never gets done, forever?"

The Secretary looked at me for a moment and answered.

"…Not so much worrying as — that's simply how this city works. Whatever's urgent, someone ends up stepping forward for it. Whatever isn't, stays as it is, for a long time. I don't think that's a bad way to work."

I nodded at that. That day, I truly thought so.

That evening I went back to the water channel. The sun was setting. The sound of the water carried louder than it had by day. People still walked past it. No one stopped.

I asked a few passersby again.

"…This water channel — isn't the city managing it?"

One person answered, "…Hmm, wasn't it always there?" Another answered, "…I don't know either. I just walk by." No one was irritated. No one thought it strange.

I didn't resent any of these people, that day. I still don't. They truly didn't know. The only way to know was through a case, and there was no case.

I sat by the water for a long while. It kept flowing. Where it came from, and where it went, unwritten in any document of this city.

I walked a little further along the channel, out past the city's boundary. Crossing the boundary marker, the road disappeared and there was only open field. The channel kept going, even there. It curved and vanished among distant trees. I was curious where that water flowed from, but the sun had already fallen far, and I wasn't prepared to go further in that direction. I turned back.

I had no idea, that day, how far off that water had come from. I only learn that much later, and it isn't this record's part to tell. But what I saw that day is plain. The water had come into the city, and the city didn't know it.

That was the first time I had a thought like this. If this city hides anything, it's probably like this. Not concealing it — just no one ever picking it up.

I didn't push that thought any further at the time. The water channel was only the water channel, and I still couldn't imagine, even then, that it would come to connect to anything else.

On my way home, I called Tyche's words back to mind. If it's never been submitted, it doesn't exist. Not hidden. I turned that over again and again. Strangely, there was nothing in it to argue against. And that there was nothing to argue against was, that day, the first thing that made me a little uneasy.

I didn't bring up the water channel to my aunt right away that day. Not that I was hiding it, exactly. I just didn't know how to explain it.

But partway through dinner, my aunt asked first. "…You're quiet today. Something happen?" I hesitated for a moment, then told her about the water channel. That water had come into the city's very boundary, and had never once been brought up as a case, and so administratively it was as good as not existing.

My aunt set down her spoon and looked at me.

"…What do you mean, as good as not existing. The water's really flowing."

"…No, I mean, on paper it doesn't exist. Because no one's brought it up."

My aunt was quiet for a while before answering.

"…That sounds strange to me. Existing, but not existing."

"…But it's not hidden, apparently. Just no one's brought it up."

"…Isn't the result the same, whether it's hidden or no one's brought it up? Being treated as not existing is the same either way."

I couldn't answer that question. That day, for the first time, I felt Tyche's explanation and my aunt's question pointing in different directions. I thought about that difference late into that night, but I fell asleep without reaching an answer.

My aunt added, almost to herself, as she washed the dishes. "…Well, someone will bring it up, when it's time." I couldn't tell whether that was meant to comfort me or was just something to say. Probably both.

(Continued in Chapter 12.)

Chapter 12. The Empty Box

_Previously_ — Hora learning that the water channel, having come right
into the western edge of the city, has never once been brought up as a
case, and so administratively doesn't exist. Tyche answered precisely:
"If it's never been submitted, it doesn't exist. Not hidden." But her
aunt asked back, "Isn't being treated as not existing the same thing
either way?" Hora fell asleep without resolving that difference.

Starting the next day, I began looking at the signature box longer than I usually did.

It wasn't that I was trying to confirm anything in particular. My aunt's question from the night before had simply stayed with me, and I was someone who couldn't settle until I'd checked whatever stayed with me. It was the one habit in this city I'd never managed to change.
