The moment I opened my eyes that morning, I thought again of what my aunt had said the day before. "Isn't being treated as not existing the same thing either way?" I turned it over even while eating breakfast. My aunt was ladling out soup as if it were nothing at all, but that one question from the night before kept circling in my head.

I thought about it walking to the meeting room too. Being treated as if the water channel doesn't exist, and the water channel truly not existing. I knew those were different things. Tyche had told me exactly that. But knowing they were different didn't settle my mind. I wanted to know where that unsettled feeling was coming from. So that day, I decided to look at the signature box again. It had nothing to do with the water channel, and yet somehow, in my mind, the two kept landing in the same place.

That day, once I'd finished reading the case I'd been given in the meeting room, I looked, as always, at the bottom of the last page. The signature box. Empty.

I'd seen that box dozens of times before. Before, I'd always just passed over it. That day, I looked at it longer.

A long, narrow horizontal box. Small letters above it read "the signature box," and beneath that, nothing at all. I read the small print beside it again. "This document does not require a signature." Does not require. Not the same as forbidding. I'd read that sentence once before. I'd let it pass then.

That day, for the first time, I pulled out other cases too and checked only the signature box.

Empty.

I looked again at the intake stamp beside it. The stamp was clear. The date was clear. The handling progress was recorded in close detail. The list of underlying material was attached, nothing missing. The objection field and the response field were both filled in. Nothing was missing from this document. Nothing except that one box.

It occurred to me, suddenly, to compare this box to all the others. The date box had a date in it. The stamp box had a stamp in it. The intake-number box had a number in it. Every box in this city existed to be filled. Every one, except this one.

That day was the first time I had this thought. More precisely, that day was the first time I noticed that this thought had occurred to me at all. It had probably crossed my mind before, a few times, but I think I'd let it slip past without holding onto it, back then.

I pulled out the next case. This was one I had no part in, from the Second Deliberation District. The signature box.

Empty.

I went to the reading room and requested three more completely unfamiliar cases. I checked all three.

Empty.

I pulled out the twenty-year-old material again too. The same material I'd seen once before. The signature box.

Empty.

From that point I decided to go about this a little differently. Instead of just looking at a lot of cases at random, I varied the kind. Ration cases, housing cases, objection-handling cases, old ones, recent ones, evenly across the First through Sixth Deliberation Districts.

I checked them one by one, writing the list down in my notebook.

First District, ration adjustment, three years ago. Empty.
Third District, housing relocation, eight years ago. Empty.
Fifth District, objection re-review, last year. Empty.
Second District, road widening, eleven years ago. Empty.
Sixth District, materials assignment, two years ago. Empty.

I kept going this way. The reading-room librarian noticed me filling out one new application after another and said something.

"…Looking for anything special today?"

"…No, just something I need to check."

The librarian didn't press further and kept handing me material.

At one point, flipping through the twelfth document, I stopped breathing for a moment. Something faint showed at the bottom box. I carried the document to the window and held it up to the light. It was a pressure mark, from age — where the paper had been pressed by something. Not writing. Just a trace left by another document pressed against it. My heart raced for a moment, thinking I'd found something, and then, once I confirmed the mark was nothing, I felt strangely deflated instead. I didn't know, myself, what I'd been hoping for.

I want to write this moment honestly. I was looking for something, at the time. Without even knowing what I was looking for. This wasn't an investigation. An investigation needs a purpose, and I had none. My eyes just kept going to that box.

I looked at a few more cases after that. The form had changed slightly, era to era. The very old ones were handwritten tables; the recent ones were printed. The shape of the box had shifted a little too — some square, some a long horizontal line. But that the box always sat at the very bottom of the last page, and that it was always empty — not one of those two things had ever once changed.

Curious whether this was something the regulations had set down, or just something carried on as custom, I looked up the form's history of changes. The regulation said only, "A confirmation field shall be placed at the end of the document." It didn't say exactly when the term "confirmation field" or the term "signature box" had first come into use. It didn't say who had first created this box. Not even the name of whoever had made this box remained, anywhere, now.

By that afternoon I'd checked twenty cases.

All twenty — the signature box was empty.

I looked back through my notebook. The districts were different. The kinds of cases were different. The years were different. Some were three pages, some over forty. There was exactly one thing in common.

The box at the very bottom was always empty.

I stared at that list for a long while. I thought it was strange. But exactly what was strange about it, I couldn't put into words. I closed the notebook, then opened it again, and read it through from the start, in case I'd missed something. There was nothing. The twenty lines stayed exactly twenty lines.

I went to Tyche and showed them the notebook.

"…Look at this. All twenty — the signature box is empty."

Tyche flipped through the notebook and answered.

"…Yeah, that's how it always is. I know that."

"…Doesn't that seem strange?"

"…Hmm, why would it be strange? It says right there that a signature isn't required."

"…No, but not required and no one writing it are different things. If it isn't forbidden, couldn't at least one person have written something?"

Tyche looked thoughtful at that question.

"…That's true, I guess. But it's empty because no one's written anything. What's strange about that?"

I didn't know what to say back to that. Tyche wasn't wrong. That it was empty because no one had written anything was simply a fact.

"…But is there anything in the regulations that says not to fill it in? Did you check?"

Tyche searched the screen for a moment.

"…No. Nothing comes up, even searching. There's no line saying not to fill it in."

"…Then why does no one fill it in?"

"…Huh. I've never thought about that, myself."

Tyche really did fall into thought for a moment, right there. I would go on to remember that expression for a long time. That was the first time I'd seen Tyche wear the face of not knowing an answer.

"…But Hora, why does this bother you so much? The document's effect is the same whether it's filled in or not."

"…It's not about the effect. It's just that there's a box, and it's never filled, and that's a little…"

I couldn't finish the sentence. Tyche looked at me for a moment and shrugged.

"…I don't really know. But if it bothers you, maybe that's just how it is."

I couldn't tell whether that answer was meant to comfort me or just to let it go. Tyche went back to their own work after that. There wasn't a trace of looking at me strangely. Just a face a little sorry for not having an answer to give. Watching that face, I wondered, briefly, whether Tyche had ever found this box as unfamiliar as I did. I didn't ask.

"…I don't know. It's just that all twenty were the same."

"…You could look at two hundred, two thousand, and they'd all be the same. Because that's just what this box is."

I thought that was true too. It was true, and still something caught.

That evening I showed the Secretary the notebook too, on my way to return material at the counter.

The Secretary looked through the notebook carefully. Page by page, taking their time.

"…You confirmed all of this yourself?"

"…Yes. I was curious."

The Secretary closed the notebook and looked at me. Their expression showed no particular change.

"…That's right. They'll all be empty."

"…Do you know why?"

The Secretary thought for a moment.

"…Because there's no one to fill it in."

"…Not because it's not allowed?"

"…It isn't that it's not allowed. There's no one for it."

Hearing that answer, I thought of what I'd heard at the water channel a few days before. If it's never been submitted, it doesn't exist. If there's no one to fill it in, it doesn't get filled in. I sensed, dimly, that the two statements shared the same shape. Exactly how they were the same, I couldn't put into words.

"…But why is there no one for it?"

This time the Secretary was silent for longer.

"…That much, I don't know myself."

That day was the first time I noticed the words "I don't know" coming out of this person's mouth. Up to then, the Secretary had answered whatever was asked. Either they already knew, or, not knowing, they went and found the answer. This time they only said they didn't know.

I was about to put the notebook back in my bag when I noticed a blank form lying on the counter. The same signature box at the very bottom. I looked at that box for a long while. A pen sat beside it. I reached out and picked up the pen.

The Secretary was watching me. They didn't stop me. They just watched.

I touched the tip of the pen to the box for a moment, and then, in the end, set it down without writing anything.

"…Why not write anything?"

The Secretary asked. Not a scolding tone. Just a curious one.

"…I don't know. It just doesn't feel like a place I'm meant to write."

"…Even though no one's ever decided that?"

"…Yes. Even though no one's ever decided that."

Saying that answer, I thought it strange myself. Knowing it wasn't forbidden, I still couldn't put my hand to that box. Why I couldn't — that, too, I didn't know, that day.

That night I sat for a long while with the notebook open in front of me.

Twenty lines. District, case name, year, and in the last column, the three words I'd written in myself — "empty" — repeated twenty times.

I never did manage, that day, to work out why this unsettled me. It wasn't hidden. Tyche had been right about that. It wasn't forbidden either. The Secretary had been right about that too. I didn't think either of them was wrong.

I remember this night precisely, even now. I reached no conclusion that day. Writing this record, I've felt the temptation, more than once, to attach some plausible sentence to this passage. Something like, "That day I sensed, in my gut, that something was wrong." But that would be a lie. I sensed nothing, that day. I was simply unsettled, and I didn't know why. That was all there was to it.

I put the notebook away in the drawer. The same drawer that held my mother's pages and my first notice. Closing the drawer, it occurred to me, suddenly, to think again of the last line of my mother's twelve pages. It crossed my mind, briefly, whether there might be some connection between having left that line unread for two years, and these twenty empty boxes going unfilled, year after year, by anyone at all. It felt too forced a connection, and I pushed the thought away quickly.

My aunt opened the door a crack and asked, "…Still up?" "…Just about to sleep." "…Something wrong?" I hesitated, then answered. "…No, just — there was something a little strange, and I don't know what's strange about it."

My aunt came in and sat for a moment on the edge of the bed.

"…What did you see?"

"…I looked at twenty documents today, and the signature box was empty in every one. But I don't even know why that bothers me."

My aunt thought for a moment and asked,

"…Is it strange, the thing itself? Or does it just start feeling strange from looking at it too long?"

I couldn't answer that right away. I genuinely didn't know. Whether it was strange, or whether it only felt strange because I'd stared at it too long — I couldn't tell the two apart.

"…I don't know."

My aunt nodded.

"…Then that's not getting answered today. Go to sleep."

"…Okay."

Before leaving the room, my aunt added one more thing. "…But Hora, when something keeps bothering you, there's usually a reason. You just haven't found it yet." I closed my eyes at that and didn't answer. I had nothing to say back.

My aunt looked at me for a moment and said, "…Then look at it again after you've slept. Everything looks strange at night." I laughed at that and turned off the light.

Lying there in the dark, I kept calling those twenty empty boxes back to mind. None of it was wrong, and none of it sat entirely easy either. I fell asleep that night still not knowing why it unsettled me, and in that state, I slept.

(Continued in Part Two.)

The Unsigned Book, Part Two

Nobody Did It

Chapter 13. The Seventh Classification

_Previously_ — Two hundred days into her posting at the Fourth Deliberation District, Hora was still the only person who read deliberation documents to the end. The summary was always accurate, and nothing ever happened. Only the signature box at the bottom of the document — every document she opened, without exception — stayed empty.

The assignment notice came on a single sheet of paper, same as always.

The Seventh Classification residential clearance and relocation matter — assistant member Hora assigned. That was all of it. I read the sentence three times, and it was the same sentence all three times. Reading it a third time did not make the meaning any longer.

I remember exactly which day I received this notice. I remember it exactly not because that day was in itself special, but because later I had to go back to that day again and again, to count something. Not to count what happened that day — to count what didn't.

I went to the reception desk and asked what the Seventh Classification was.

"…It's a classification for residents with incomplete registration."

"…Why is the registration incomplete?"

"…Because their residence is on unregistered land."

"…Is that their fault?"

"…The word 'fault' doesn't appear anywhere in the file. It just says incomplete registration."

The clerk was doing her best to help me. There wasn't the slightest trace of impatience in her voice. I found that strangely steadying. That no one ever treated me as a bother was, in this city, simply how things were — but that day, of all days, it felt unusually kind.

I thought I understood what she meant. It was like the area near the west water channel. Just as the channel administratively did not exist because it had never come up as an agenda item, the land where the people beside it lived also administratively did not exist, for the same reason. People living on land that did not exist had been gathered into a classification that did not exist.

I thought that made logical sense. At the time.

I hardly questioned the word "registration" itself back then. If something wasn't registered, I assumed the unregistered side was the one with the problem. It was much later that I asked why land not being registered should be the fault of the people living on it.

The clerk handed me a thin information sheet along with the file. It was a list of the departments involved. Twelve departments, each with the number of staff assigned written beside it. Added together, it came to more than four hundred people.

"…This many people are involved?"

"…The bigger the matter, the more there are. That way it never comes down to one person's judgment."

"…So all four hundred of them decide?"

"…No. No one decides. Everyone just does their own share. Passing it on, putting it in order, confirming, convening. Gather all of that up, and it becomes a decision."

"…Then who do you ask, if it goes wrong?"

"…If it's wrong, you file an objection. Objections are handled the same way."

"…What does the same way mean?"

"…Another four hundred or so people pass it on, put it in order, confirm it, convene over it."

That didn't sound strange to me. That objections, too, went through a formal procedure was, if anything, reassuring to hear. It meant no one could look the other way on impulse — that even an objection went onto the same scale as everything else.

I heard that and nodded. It sounded like a safeguard. One person turning bad-hearted could never talk four hundred people into agreeing.

I believed, at the time, that these four hundred people were a safeguard. I remember exactly how long that belief lasted. Until I met twelve of them.

I ran into Tyche in the corridor. I asked if she was assigned to this one, and she said she only put together the supporting materials.

"…What exactly is the Seventh Classification?"

"…It's the seventh item on the classification table. Numbered in order — not ranked."

"…Why are these particular people grouped into one classification?"

"…On the one thing they have in common: living on residences with no basis for registration. Nothing else in common. Different ages, different work, different places they lived before."

"…Then why are they all being processed at once?"

Tyche looked at me and smiled. It wasn't an unkind smile.

"…Because processing them all at once is fair. Looked at one by one, the outcome would depend on each person's circumstances. Then some would end up looked after and some wouldn't. Grouped by classification and processed by the same standard, nobody gets special treatment."

I heard that and nodded. At the time it sounded right. Even now I can't say the statement itself was wrong. What I didn't ask, back then, was who that fairness was for.

The clerk handed me a file. This one was thick.

"…You read all of this?"

"…Yes."

"…Everyone else just looks at the summary."

"…I always read the whole thing."

She looked at me for a moment. It wasn't a strange look. Just a mildly curious one.

"…Then this'll take you a while. This one's big."

"…How big?"

"…Eleven hundred and forty people."

I heard that number and felt nothing. I'm ashamed to say so, but that's the truth. The number was just a number. The deliberations I'd seen so far had run, at most, a few dozen people, so eleven hundred and forty didn't feel real to me at all.

I've crossed this paragraph out and rewritten it more times than I can count, writing this now. There's a temptation to write it as though I felt something I should have felt. But that isn't true. I heard the number and worried about the thickness of the file. That was all.

When the matter was first brought before the Fourth Deliberation District's meeting room, the seats were about half full.

The officer in charge stood at the front and explained. Unregistered residences had accumulated over several years, he said, and this time a consolidated clearance agenda had been brought up. Someone asked what "clearance" meant.

"…Recovery of the residences and relocation."

"…Relocation to where?"

"…That's in the detailed proposal."

"…This looks like a big one — why is it only coming up now?"

"…The cumulative count crossed the processing threshold. Below the threshold, cases were handled individually. Once it crossed, it converted to a consolidated agenda item."

I sat through that exchange without much thought. The phrase "detailed proposal" was familiar. This city's documents were always layered that way. The broad shape gets stated at the meeting, the detail lives in the document — that had held true up to now.

The officer handed out the summary. A page and a half. I read it. Clearance of unregistered residences, eleven hundred and forty people affected, a relocation plan to be established, an implementation date specified. Clean.

The person in the next seat said,

"…This one's big, so read carefully, everyone."

"…Right."

"…Not that it matters — it'll all go by the summary anyway. It always does for these."

Near the end of the meeting, someone asked whether children were among the eleven hundred and forty. The officer checked his materials and answered.

"…Two hundred thirty are fourteen or under."

"…And the elderly?"

"…A hundred ninety are sixty or over."

The room went quiet for a moment. But the quiet didn't last. Someone cleared their throat, and someone asked whether we were ready to move to the next item. I didn't read that quiet as meaning anything, at the time. I just thought everyone had gone briefly solemn hearing the numbers.

Looking back, that quiet changed nothing. It went quiet, then it moved on. In this city, quiet was a feeling, not an action.

That man hadn't meant to deceive me. He truly believed what he said. And in a certain sense he was right. This matter, too, ended up going by the summary. Nothing he said was wrong.

The meeting ended and people filed out. I stayed behind holding the thick file.

The custodian came to lock up and saw me still there.

"…Staying late again today?"

"…Yes."

"…This one's a big one."

"…Yes, I saw."

"…I won't turn off the lights."

The custodian said that and left. I stayed alone and turned to the first page of the file.

The first section was background: the history of unregistered residences, how they arose, the relevant regulations. I read it slowly. None of the sentences were difficult. Just long.

According to the background section, unregistered residences generally arose like this. A piece of land had never come up as an agenda item. Never having come up, it went unregistered. Unregistered land got no management budget. With no budget, no one touched it. Since no one touched it, no one stopped people from moving in and living there. A few years, a few decades passed that way, and by then people were living on land that was still, on paper, nowhere at all.

Reading this circular structure, I thought of the water channel. I'd learned that the west channel, too, was treated as not existing because it had never come up as an agenda item. That land, too, could vanish the same way — I learned that for the first time reading this document.

Something should have clicked for me at this point. It didn't. Reading through the circular structure, I just thought this city's administration was remarkably thorough. How neatly it kept records of what wasn't there, I thought.

I gauged the thickness of the file. It looked to be more than three hundred pages. It would take several days to finish.

I looked at the bottom of the first page. There was a signature box. Empty. Of course it was. It always was.

The first fifty pages were mostly definitions and grounds — the legal definition of incomplete registration, the relevant ordinance numbers, a list of similar small-scale clearances over the past ten years. The small cases had run twenty or thirty people at a time, and each had gone through without any particular problem, the file said. This one was unusually large, it explained, only because those small cases had gone unresolved and kept piling up.

I read about fifty pages that night before going home. My aunt asked why I was so late, and I only said this one was pretty big.

"…How big."

"…Eleven hundred forty, they said."

My aunt looked at me a moment.

"…You're going to read all of it?"

"…Yes."

"…Hora, that's exactly why the summary exists — because nobody reads all of it."

"…I know. I'm reading it anyway."

My aunt didn't try to stop me any further. Instead she spooned me another bowl of rice. Thinking back on it now, I think she wanted to stop me, some. Not because reading it all the way through was a bad thing to do, but because she may have had some sense of what I'd end up seeing once I finished. Of course, this is a thought I've attached to it afterward. That evening she just spooned out more rice. That's all she did.

From the next day on, I stayed and read every day.

Nothing struck me as strange as I read. Every sentence was clean, every claim was backed, every procedure was in order. No clause was hidden. No page was blocked from view.

I want to be clear about this part. Writing this record now, I don't want it to look as though I were uncovering something bit by bit. The file was open from the first page to the last. Anyone could have read it. I was simply reading what no one else read.

On the second day I read the roster of those affected. No names — only numbers and classification codes. 7-001 through 7-1140. Beside each number, a brief age range, household size, and length of residence. At first I thought the absence of names was for privacy, and in fact it partly was.

Someone else was in the reading room that day. Someone from a neighboring deliberation district, skimming the same matter. After about ten minutes he pulled out only the summary, took it, and left. He never opened the full text.

I didn't think of that as laziness. Everyone knew the summary was accurate. Reading it a second time, when it was already accurate, was the strange thing to do. At the time I thought I was the strange one. Even now, I don't think that's entirely wrong.

On the third day, around page one hundred fifty, I came to the list of proposed relocation sites. Place names ran down the page. I picked out a few I could try to place, and some I recognized, and some I didn't.

At the end of the list was a footnote in small print.

Of the above list, items 3, 7, and 12 are temporary designations pending detailed confirmation.

I read the footnote and turned the page. It didn't seem all that important at the time. I noticed all three items had a small asterisk beside their place names, and I took that, too, for just a notation convention. "Temporary designation" was a phrase I saw often in this city's documents. I figured it would be finalized eventually.

That's how I let the footnote pass. If someone asked me which is the moment I regret most while writing this record, I'd point to that night. I'm not sure "regret" is the right word. I did, after all, go on to read every page that followed, and I did, in the end, learn everything. Still — in that moment, I could have stopped once more and read that footnote again.

I closed the file and went home. About a hundred pages remained.

I passed the water channel on my way home. The water was still making its sound. I stood beside it for a moment and wondered whether the land this water ran through could one day become an unregistered residence too. No one lived there yet, so it seemed unlikely.

That's what I thought at the time. I can't yet say I was wrong. No one lived there. Still, the thought that there might have been a connection between that channel and this deliberation — one I failed to see — is something I haven't been able to fully shake, even now, writing this record.

My aunt was already asleep, so I went straight to my room without a word. I fell asleep holding two things in my head: that a hundred pages remained, and that three footnotes said "temporary designation."

Tomorrow I would finish the remaining hundred pages. At the time I thought of it as simply one more day's work left. I believed that once the day passed, this matter, too, would end just as the summary described. Exactly when that belief broke is what I set down, unchanged, in the next chapter.

(Continued in Chapter 14.)

Chapter 14. To the End

_Previously_ — Hora is three days into reading the Seventh Classification deliberation. Eleven hundred forty people, two hundred thirty of them children. Three footnotes reading "temporary designation" at the end of the relocation-site list nagged at her, though at the time she didn't know why.

On the fourth morning I got to the meeting room earlier than usual.

The custodian was cleaning and startled to see me.

"…You're early today."

"…Yes, I'm going to finish it today."

"…That thick one? Impressive."

"…It's just a habit."

The custodian laughed and went back to cleaning. I sat down and opened to the page where I'd stopped the day before. A hundred pages remained. I made up my mind to finish all of it that day. There was no other reason. I simply didn't want to split it up any further.

On the fourth day, I read the remaining hundred pages.

I've thought for a long time about how to write this chapter. I don't want to write it dramatically. I didn't suddenly notice something that day. I just turned one page, then another. Even when I reached the last page, I was still just turning pages.

Up to that point I still didn't think I was doing anything remarkable. Reading what others don't read had long been my habit — not some sense of justice. I want this made clear, because I'm worried that what happens after this will read as though I'd meant to dig something up from the start. I was only doing what I always did.

The first sixty of the hundred pages were about execution procedure. Notification method, notification deadline, the channel for filing objections, the formula for relocation support funds. All of it was there. All the numbers were there too. Support funds — so much per household. Moving support — so many days. Where to file the change of residence.

The notification-method clause read: Recipients will be notified in writing thirty days before the implementation date, and the notice will specify the destination site, the relocation deadline, and the support provided. I read this and thought it a considerate clause. Thirty days seemed like no short amount of time.

There was also a clause for filing objections. Anyone with an objection to this decision may submit a written objection within fourteen days of the notification date. I thought this was a good clause too. It meant the path to filing an objection wasn't closed off.

Reading it all, I felt reassured rather than not. Something this carefully woven together seemed unlikely to let anything bad happen. That there were support funds, that there was moving assistance — all of it read as good news.

I read every one of these clauses generously. Even now, looking back, none of these clauses is bad in itself. The problem wasn't the clause. It was what the clause pointed to.

Around page seventy, the detailed relocation-site plan appeared. The same list I'd seen on the third day unfolded again here, this time laid out in a table with each region's capacity, infrastructure status, and expected relocation timing.

I found the three sites the footnote had called temporary — items 3, 7, and 12. I looked at the table. The capacity column held a question mark. The infrastructure status column was blank. Only the expected relocation timing was filled in, the same as everywhere else.

I looked at this table many times over. Seeing the question mark and the blank cell side by side, I felt for the first time that something was strange. I still didn't know what, exactly, that sense of strangeness was pointing to.

Beside the table I noticed a small note in the margin: Final confirmation pending, following inter-departmental consultation, to be separately announced. No date was given for when the consultation would end. I went to the archive and looked into similar past cases. A few small-scale matters had also carried the phrase "separately announced." Half of them had no follow-up announcement that the consultation had ended at all. Whether the absence meant it had been cancelled or was still ongoing, nothing anywhere said.

This was the first time I doubted the phrase "separately announced" in this city's documents. I'm not sure "doubted" is the right word. I only confirmed, with my own eyes, for the first time, that there might be nothing at all behind those words.

I looked closely at the support-fund formula too. The amount changed with household size, and a little more was added for longer residence. The formula itself was written out. I actually applied it to a few households. The math checked out. What wasn't in the clause was whether there was actually a market at the destination site where that money could be used — whether there was somewhere to buy a house with it. That having money and having somewhere to spend it were different problems was something I first thought about right then.

Around page eighty I found the number of people assigned to items 3, 7, and 12.

The three together came to four hundred ten. Four hundred ten out of eleven hundred forty were headed to places whose capacity and infrastructure had not been confirmed.

I flipped back and checked the three place names again. The names were there. I tried to place them on the city map in my head, and I couldn't place them anywhere.

One of the three names was "the West Lowlands." I paused when I saw that name. The word "west" caught at me. It was the direction of the west water channel. I wasn't sure. The west side of the city was wide — it could overlap, or it might not. There was no way for me to check right then, so I just wrote the name down.

Whether that overlap is coincidence or not, I still can't say for certain, writing this record. I didn't compare it against the map until much later, and that result comes later in this book. Here I'll only set down that I stopped in front of that name that night.

This was the first time I thought about going to the archive to check the map against it. I didn't. There was no time. Writing this sentence, I regret not doing it then, though the outcome would probably have been the same either way. That place name had never been on any map to begin with.

Around page ninety, I found out what these three places were. "Found out" overstates it. It was simply written in the next paragraph.

Items 3, 7, and 12 are designated pending sites and, until confirmed, are classified as unregistered regions.

I read that sentence three times. On the fourth reading I read it aloud. There was no one else in the reading room, so no one would hear. Spoken aloud, the meaning was the same.

The three sites were land that did not yet exist. Unregistered regions. Reclassified into exactly the state the Seventh Classification lived in — land that did not exist because it had never come up as an agenda item.

Four hundred ten people, being pushed off land for lack of registration, were headed to other land that was, itself, unregistered.

I stood up for a moment at this point and paced around the reading room. Wondering if I'd misread something, I sat back down and read the paragraph again. Same content. Three readings, all the same.

I also checked what would happen to the remaining seven hundred thirty. That side had its infrastructure status and capacity fully filled in. They were assigned to land that really existed, to plots where houses would really be built. So this matter was not entirely bad. Most of them really were going somewhere. Only four hundred ten were headed to a place that did not yet exist.

I want to set down this distinction precisely. This deliberation did not send all eleven hundred forty people to a place that didn't exist. If it had, it might have been easier to notice. Seven hundred thirty really did have somewhere to go. So when the summary said "relocation plan established," that wasn't a lie. There really was a plan. For most of them.

I catch myself, once again, resisting the temptation to write this sentence so it sounds frightening. Nothing in the document itself read as frightening. It was just one paragraph. The same font size, the same style, as every other paragraph.

The last ten pages were supplementary provisions. I'd heard that supplementary provisions were something nobody ever reads — formal clauses, implementation dates, transitional rules and the like.

I read the supplementary provisions to the end too.

The final clause of the supplementary provisions — the last sentence of the entire document — read:

Upon completion of this procedure, the Seventh Classification will be deleted from the roster.

I read that sentence and sat for a long while.

I checked the clock. It was past ten at night. I was alone in the reading room. The window let in no view of outside, so I couldn't guess the time by that — I only knew from the clock in the corridor.

Being deleted didn't mean a person was deleted. It meant the classification was deleted. I held onto that distinction and thought about it for a long time. When a classification disappears, where do the people who were in it go? Do they get moved to another classification, or do they simply become people belonging to no classification at all? The document didn't say. Not that it left it unsaid — the document simply ended there.

I closed the document and looked at the cover again. There was a viewing-record field — a box where it stamped who had opened it and when.

I opened that field. This, too, had always been public. Not a hidden menu.

There was a long record. The department in charge, related departments, the higher authority. The number of views was high. Over two hundred.

Seeing this record, I knew one thing for certain. No one had stopped me. This document had been opened more than two hundred times. Anyone could have gone all the way to the last page. There was no reason, in this city, to hide the final clause. The Consensus hides nothing. Not hidden — simply that not one of those two hundred had gone all the way to the last page.

This fact made what I had to do next much harder. If someone had hidden it, I could have gone to that person. No one had hidden it. So who was I supposed to go to?

I scrolled down the viewing record line by line. No names — only department and position. Several times from the department in charge, a few times from related departments, once from the higher authority. Each of the twenty-two committee members had a record of having opened it once. I would now be added to that list too.

I wrote the number two hundred in the corner of a piece of paper. I didn't know why I wrote it down then. Later I would find reason to pull that number out again.

Looking at that list, I felt a strange comfort. At least I wasn't the only one who had opened this document. That it had been opened over two hundred times meant that over two hundred times, someone had sat down in front of it. But how many of those two hundred times had eyes actually reached the final clause — the viewing record did not say. It doesn't measure the distance between having opened something and having read it.

Before I left, I checked the signature box once more. It had become a habit by now. There was a signature box on the very last page too, beneath the supplementary provisions. Empty.

Looking at that box, I formed a strange question for the first time. Not that there was no one who had made this document — but that there was no one at all assigned to sign it. The decision sat here, fully complete, and beneath that decision there was a place for a name, and no one was ever meant to fill it. I felt as though the order had been reversed. Normally, someone decides, and then signs. Here, things were decided without a signature, and the signature box simply remained, out of habit.

The custodian was the last one left, and she turned off the light when she saw me.

"…Finished it all?"

"…Yes."

"…How was it?"

I couldn't answer right away. She didn't rush me, just waited.

"…Bigger than I thought."

"…It is. It's a big one."

She said only that and locked the door. I'd expected her to press me further about my answer. She didn't. She hadn't deceived me either. Her job was simply to lock the door.

On the way home, my steps kept slowing. It was late, so the streets were quiet.

Passing the water channel, I felt afraid of its sound for the first time. The water hadn't changed. I had.

I told my aunt nothing that night. I didn't know what to say. If I said "four hundred ten out of eleven hundred forty are headed to a place that doesn't exist," I couldn't guess what she'd say back. I still can't guess, even now.

I went to my room and pulled out the summary again. A page and a half. I read it once more.

There wasn't a lie in the summary. Eleven hundred forty people, a relocation plan, an implementation date. All of it was accurate.

Only the deletion of the roster wasn't anywhere in the summary.

I folded the summary back up and put it in the drawer. The one with my mother's papers. I still had no particular reason. It was still the only drawer I had.

I barely slept that night. I'd like to say it wasn't out of fear, but I think, in truth, I was a little afraid. Only I couldn't tell, at the time, what that fear was pointed at. At the people of the Seventh Classification? At this city? Or simply at not knowing what I was supposed to do next?

Staring at the ceiling, I kept returning to the same sentence. Upon completion of this procedure, the Seventh Classification will be deleted from the roster. Twenty-two characters, in the original. Behind those twenty-two characters stood four hundred ten people.

I remember the first thing I did the next morning. I pulled out the summary again and this time read it word by word, tracing each character with my eye. I wanted to check that the summary really had no falsehood, that I hadn't misread something. To say the result first: the summary really was without error, anywhere. I set down that checking process exactly as it happened, in the next chapter.

(Continued in Chapter 15.)

Chapter 15. The Summary Was Accurate

_Previously_ — Hora read the Seventh Classification deliberation to the end. Four hundred ten of the eleven hundred forty were being relocated to land that did not yet exist, and once the procedure was complete, the classification itself would be deleted from the roster. None of this appeared anywhere in the summary.

The next morning I took out the summary again and laid it side by side with the full text.

I want to set down this act of comparison precisely. This will likely be the most tedious chapter in this book. I know that. But without this tedious comparison, I would have gone on to misunderstand everything that happened next. So I write it out plainly, tedious or not.

I spent the entire day on this comparison. The other members had already moved on to the next matter. I was the only one still sitting on the Seventh Classification case. No one told me that was strange.

The summary's first sentence read:

The Seventh Classification (unregistered residents) residential clearance and relocation matter.

Identical to the document name on the cover of the full text. Not one character different.

The second sentence.

Subject: 1,140 people (7-001 to 7-1140).

I counted the roster. Exactly eleven hundred forty. The numbers matched too.

The third sentence.

Reason: A need for consolidated clearance has arisen from the accumulation of unregistered residences.

I checked it against the background section of the full text. The same phrasing appeared there, unchanged. The circular structure I'd read — not registered because it never came up as an agenda item, not managed because not registered — compressed into this one sentence. It was compression, not distortion.

Up to these three sentences, I already knew everything. I wasn't looking for something wrong here. What I really wanted to confirm was what came after.

I looked at the third sentence once more. The phrase "a need... has arisen" caught at me. Arisen — as though it were weather, something that simply happened rather than something anyone made. I checked whether the full text used the same word. It did. The full text also said "arisen." The summary had simply carried over the full text's own phrasing.

I sat with this one word for a long while. "A need has arisen" and "someone judged this to be necessary" are different in meaning. But this city's documents always used the former. I wondered whether that was a habit of this one document, or a habit of every document in this city. I confirmed later that it was the latter.

The fourth sentence.

Action: Residence recovery and relocation. Relocation support funds and moving assistance to be provided.

This too was accurate. The support-fund clause, the moving-assistance clause, all exactly as they stood in the full text. I checked the support formula again. The summary had no formula, but the word "provided" was true. It hadn't claimed something existed that didn't.

The fifth sentence, the last.

Implementation date: as specified in the timeline.

I paused on this sentence. The full text had a concrete date. The summary only said "as specified in the timeline." I wondered whether the date had been hidden, and checked again — the date was right there in the full text, available to anyone who looked. The summary simply hadn't repeated it, since it was already in the full text.

This was a common enough practice. A summary isn't meant to replace the full text — I'd learned before that it's meant to be a signpost pointing toward it. A signpost has no obligation to show everything at the destination.

The summary was exactly five sentences. It fit within a page and a half.

I read the whole summary through again from the start. Not a single wrong sentence. Not a single exaggerated one. No missing numbers. Nothing like the support-fund matter left out when it should have been there, nothing like the number of people affected differing from the real count. None of that.

I took out a sheet of paper and copied the summary's five sentences on the left and the corresponding sections of the full text on the right. I drew lines connecting them and checked each pair one by one. When I finished, between the five lines on the left and the five paragraphs on the right, there was truly not one place where the meaning had shifted. The right side was simply longer.

I kept this sheet of paper for a long time. I later used it as an attachment to my objection filing. Even now, writing this record, I keep it beside me. This comparison chart is the only thing in this entire book I can call "evidence." And what that evidence proves is only that no one did anything wrong.

I sat for a long while in front of that fact. I think I had wanted, somewhere, to feel deceived. Then I would have had someone to be angry at. But no matter how many times I compared them, there was no deception. The summary was accurate.

I went to find Tyche and asked her.

"…Why is the summary so short?"

"…Summaries are always short. Everyone makes their judgment from it, so."

"…Then why doesn't it have the roster-deletion clause?"

Tyche took the summary and skimmed it.

"…That's a supplementary provision. Supplementary provisions don't usually go into the summary."

"…Why not?"

"…The body's action clauses all go into the summary, and supplementary provisions are about the implementation method for that action. Put every supplementary provision in, and the summary stops being a summary."

I heard that and couldn't say anything back. There was nothing logically wrong with it.

"…But this is an important clause. A whole classification is getting deleted."

Tyche looked at me for a moment.

"…Isn't it up to the reader to decide what's important? I'm not the one who decides what goes into the summary and what stays out."

"…Then who decides?"

"…The regulation decides. Five sentences, it says."

"…Who decided on five sentences?"

Tyche thought for a moment and said,

"…I don't know that either. It was already that way before I ever got here."

Something in that answer caught at me. The person who had written the rule for five sentences seemed to not be there anymore either. Only the rule remained.

"…So the summary being short means people end up judging things carelessly?"

"…Not carelessly — accurately. Because the summary is accurate. You just confirmed that yourself, didn't you? Nothing wrong, right?"

"…Right. Nothing wrong."

"…So what's the problem?"

I couldn't answer that question. I left the room that day without an answer to it. It would take the remaining thirty-some chapters of this book to find my own answer to that question.

Hearing that answer, I knew Tyche hadn't meant it unkindly. She really had only followed the summary's form. Following a form couldn't be a crime.

After leaving Tyche, I went to the archive and looked up a few more summaries from old small-scale clearance cases. All of them were similar. Five sentences — subject, reason, action, implementation timing. One case had involved seventeen people, and reading the summary, all seventeen appeared to have relocated to new residences with no particular trouble. I checked the full text — that really was the case. That case had no temporary designations like this one.

I looked at a few more besides that one. A thirty-two-person case, a nine-person case. All of them matched their summaries. I couldn't find a single case with anything like a temporary designation attached. It might have been because this Seventh Classification matter was especially large. Or it might simply have been bad luck.

Doing these comparisons, I learned one thing. The summary wasn't the problem. The summary's form was always identical. It was just that, this time, the content that went into that form happened to be content in which four hundred ten people's place could not fit. The form was innocent. It was only that the gap between what the form needed to hold and what the form could hold happened, this time, to be unusually wide.

I asked the archive clerk whether the summary being this short had ever caused a problem.

"…I couldn't say. I've never heard of anything like that."

"…Not even once?"

"…I've been here six years, and this is the first objection like this I've seen."

Hearing that, I felt, faintly, that I was doing something new. It wasn't a good feeling. New, in this city, generally meant unfamiliar, and unfamiliar generally meant uncomfortable.

I went to the reception desk and looked up the regulation for drafting summaries. In one corner of the regulation book was the guideline for writing them.

The summary shall include: subject, reason, action, and implementation timing. As a rule, the summary shall not exceed five sentences.

Five sentences. I looked at the number and understood. That this summary had come out to exactly five sentences wasn't a coincidence. The rule had set it that way.

Curious why this rule had been made, I looked up the stated reason attached beside it. "Excessive length increases the review burden on members and causes delay in deliberation," it read. This too wasn't a rule made out of ill will. If anything, it had been made out of consideration. Because everyone was busy, because everyone had to process many matters, it had been made so people could read short and judge.

But between "short" and "less important," whoever wrote this rule had never drawn a line. The shorter something is, the simpler what remains looks. There was no place for four hundred ten people within five sentences. There being no place for them and their having been hidden were different things — and I was, for the first time, sitting with that difference in my hands.

Beside the regulation book was a section on the summary-review procedure. Once a summary was drafted, it said, the department in charge confirmed it, and then it was distributed to the committee. The word "confirm" caught my eye, so I read that procedure closely. Confirmation examines whether the summary is consistent with the full text. The length or emphasis of the summary is not subject to confirmation. So confirmation checked only for accuracy. What was kept and what was cut lay outside the confirmation procedure.

I read that sentence twice. There was confirmation. Confirmation had done exactly what it was meant to do. It was only that what it was meant to do differed from what I had hoped it would do.

I also looked up which department handled the confirmation. It said that department confirmed dozens of summaries this way every week. Each confirmation probably took very little time. It wasn't the fault of the people in that department. They had been taught to look only at accuracy, and they had looked only at accuracy.

Leaving the archive, I thought again about the summary itself. A summary wasn't inherently a bad thing. I'd written many summaries myself — ration application summaries, school assignment summaries. Writing short wasn't laziness — it was a skill. A well-cut summary got more people to read, not fewer.

The problem was that in this city, no one failed to read the summary. Everyone read the summary and nothing else. So what wasn't in the summary became the same as what no one knew. The summary being short wasn't the bad part — the bad part was that everyone reading only the summary meant the summary's gaps became the whole city's gap.

This wasn't the fault of the person who wrote the summary. It wasn't the fault of the people who read only the summary, either. Everyone had simply acted reasonably, each in their own way. And yet when all of those reasonable acts gathered together, a decision came out complete, with four hundred ten people missing from it.

I came home and told my aunt about all this. She listened while getting dinner ready.

"…So no one lied. That's what you're saying."

"…Right. Really — no one."

"…Then why is that a problem?"

I couldn't answer right away. After a long moment I said,

"…I don't know. But something about it feels like a problem."

"…Even without knowing what the problem is?"

"…Right."

My aunt looked at me without laughing.

"…Hora. You're going to keep going like this until you figure it out. Aren't you."

I didn't answer. She knew that was the answer.

She ladled the soup and said,

"…I thought you'd found some huge wrongdoing. But hearing it, it sounds like everyone just did their own job."

"…Yeah. Seems like it."

"…Then isn't that just how it is? If everyone did only their own job and it turned out this way, that's nobody's fault."

I lifted my spoon and stopped.

"…But then who do those four hundred ten people even ask?"

My aunt didn't answer right away this time either. After a long while she said,

"…I don't know that one."

We ate the rest of the meal in silence for a while after that. I still remember that silence. It wasn't that my aunt had gone quiet out of anger. She, too, like me, had gone quiet looking for an answer and not finding one. That I wasn't the only person in this city stuck in front of that question — I learned that for the first time that day.

I slept poorly that night too. Confirming that the summary wasn't a lie had, if anything, made me more frustrated. If someone had lied, I could have gone and confronted them. Since no one had lied, I had no one left to confront. All that remained was the feeling that something was strange.

I tried to give that feeling a name. It wasn't a sense of being wronged — that's a feeling you get when someone has done wrong to you, and no one had done wrong to me. It wasn't anger either — you can't be angry with no one to be angry at. If I had to name it, it was simply a feeling that something was empty. The very same feeling I got looking at the signature box.

We cleared the table and I went to my room, where I pulled out the comparison chart again. Five lines on the left, five paragraphs on the right. However many times I looked, it stayed the same. I put that paper away in the drawer along with the notice. The same drawer that held my mother's papers.

I decided the next day to file an objection. I already knew, from having read it, that there was a channel for filing one. I thought that if there was a procedure, following that procedure would be enough. How naive a thought that was, I would learn over the days that followed.

(Continued in Chapter 16.)

Chapter 16. The Objection

_Previously_ — Hora compared the summary against the full text, line by line. The summary was accurate. There was no lie in it. It was only that within the rule of five sentences, there was no room for four hundred ten people. There was no one to be angry at, no one to confront. So Hora decided to file an objection.

The objection form had three fields.

Deliberation matter under objection, grounds for objection, requested action. I wrote the document name in the first field. My hand stopped at the second.

I didn't know what grounds to write. "The summary is short" didn't seem like it would work as grounds — the summary had followed the rule. "No one decided" didn't seem like it would work either — that was true of every deliberation in this city.

I sat in front of that field for about thirty minutes. Not because I had nothing to write, but because I had too much. If I wrote it all, I thought it would run past three fields.

A sample objection form was posted on the wall at the reception desk. The sample was short and clear. An error exists in the selection criteria for the target site of this deliberation, and a re-review is requested. Reading that sample a few times, I realized what I wanted to say didn't fold up as neatly as this. What I'd felt as wrong wasn't contained in the single word "error."

I redrafted my note three times. The first draft ran too long and spilled past the field. The second draft had too much feeling in it. I wrote the sentence "I think this is wrong" and crossed it out. In this city's documents, the phrase "I think" didn't belong. I already knew, from what I'd learned, that a feeling with no grounds behind it wasn't something a deliberation dealt with.

In the third draft I cut all the feeling and kept only the facts. That turned out to be the shortest version, and also the one with the most force.

In the end, I wrote this:

Among the proposed relocation sites, items 3, 7, and 12 remain in a state pending detailed confirmation and have been redesignated as unregistered regions. This repeats the exact same structure as the original problem. A re-review is requested for the four hundred ten people affected.

I submitted it at the reception desk, and the clerk looked it over and tore off a receipt for me.

"…How does an objection get processed?"

"…It's referred to the deliberation district and reviewed again."

"…Who reviews it?"

"…The whole district. Same method as the original deliberation."

"…The same people?"

"…Yes, usually. Unless the assignment changes."

Hearing that, I found it a little strange. If the same people reviewed it again the same way, why would the result change? But I didn't say that thought out loud. There was a procedure, and I wanted to follow it and see.

Thinking back now, I should have said that thought to the clerk then. Saying it might not have changed anything, but at least I would have known, sooner, what I was expecting.

"…How long does processing take?"

"…This one's a big matter, so plan on it taking a while. About two weeks."

I took the receipt and left. A single sheet of paper. Stamped with a receipt number and the date.

On my way out I looked at the bulletin board. A chart titled "Objection Filing Status" was posted. It listed, side by side, the number of objections filed over the past year and, of those, the number of cases where the original decision had been changed. A hundred twelve filed, three changed.

I stopped for a moment looking at that number. Three. Three out of a hundred twelve. I wondered what those three cases had been about, but I had no time that day and simply moved on. I would look into those three cases later, but that's much further ahead.

Oddly, I wasn't discouraged looking at that chart that day. A hundred twelve to three still meant that three, at least, had been changed. I believed mine could be one of those three. There was no basis for that belief, but at the time I didn't yet know it was a belief without basis.

For those two weeks I handled other deliberations as usual. Other than having been referred, there was no news at all about the Seventh Classification case.

Every day during those two weeks, I passed the reception-desk bulletin board and looked at the objection-status chart again. The numbers stayed the same. A hundred twelve, three. I stopped in front of that chart for a few seconds every time. It was the kind of behavior that would look strange to someone watching, but no one thought it strange of me. No one here thought it strange to stand in front of a chart.

Once, I ran into the person from the next seat in front of that chart. The one who'd told me, on my first day, that just looking at the summary was enough.

"…I heard you filed an objection."

"…I did."

"…It won't be easy. I filed one for a small matter once, myself. Came out the same."

"…Same reason — no procedural defect?"

"…Yes, the exact same phrasing."

Hearing that, I felt puzzled — whether "the exact same phrasing" meant a standardized reply, or whether it really had been that there was no procedural defect every single time. Thinking about it now, both were probably true. The procedure really was followed correctly every time, and that's exactly why the same phrasing kept coming out.

I asked Tyche how it was going.

"…How far has the objection gotten?"

"…It's under review at the district. I don't know much more than that either."

"…Can the person who filed the objection give a statement?"

"…You can. In writing."

"…Not in person?"

"…The original deliberation is mostly written too. Objections are the same."

I wrote up more supporting material and submitted it in writing. I copied the table for items 3, 7, and 12, marked the question mark and the blank cell, and noted that there was no market at the destination site where the support funds could actually be spent. I thought this was fairly concrete grounds for the problem. At the time I believed I'd found something new. I thought I'd seen something no one else had.

I found out later, though. That table had already been reviewed once, during the deliberation itself. The conclusion of "temporary designation" had come out of that very review. I hadn't found anything new — I had simply read, belatedly, something that already existed.

Even after submitting the written statement, I went to the archive every afternoon to check on the objection's progress. The status screen only showed stages. Received → Referred → Under Review → Result Notified. I watched that screen stay stuck at "Under Review" every day. It stayed that way through the tenth day.

On the tenth day, on a hunch, I called the reviewing department. Phones weren't a common way of doing things in this city either, but the receipt had an inquiry number printed on it.

"…Could I ask about the progress on the objection for the Seventh Classification matter."

"…It's under review now."

"…How much longer?"

"…If it stays on schedule, it should come out sometime this week."

"…Could I ask which part is being focused on?"

"…We're looking at exactly what you wrote in your grounds. Items 3, 7, and 12."

That answer wasn't given carelessly. It really meant they were looking at exactly what I had written. Hanging up, I understood that this person hadn't given me a short answer to brush me off. The situation had simply been simple enough to warrant a short answer. There were only the three items I'd written to review.

Two weeks later, the notice of the objection's outcome arrived.

A two-page document. I read this one to the end too.

The first page was a summary of the objection's substance. What I had written had been summarized accurately. The unconfirmed status of items 3, 7, and 12, the request to re-review the four hundred ten people affected. Nothing was missing.

The second page was the review's result.

Upon review, the pending confirmation status of items 3, 7, and 12 is determined not to be a procedural defect, and it is confirmed that inter-departmental consultation is ongoing. The reclassification of the region as unregistered is a temporary measure for administrative convenience prior to implementation and is expected to be finalized by the implementation date. The original decision is therefore maintained.

I read that sentence several times.

It was true that it wasn't a procedural defect. There really was no defect. It probably wasn't a lie that the consultation was ongoing, either. Only when that "ongoing" would end wasn't written here either.

There was a signature box at the bottom of the second page too. I looked at it reflexively. Empty. By now I didn't startle seeing that box empty. If anything, it would have startled me more if it had been filled.

I decided to go find the department in charge directly and ask.

The person who had handled the objection review was a woman who looked to be middle-aged. Documents were stacked on her desk.

"…I received the review result. When does the consultation end?"

"…That's not something I handle. It has to go over to the relevant department to know."

"…Then which department is that?"

"…The department that manages the relocation sites. It should be in the document."

"…Could I ask them and find out?"

"…They'll probably have to pass it further up too. That's usually how it goes."

Hearing this, I had a guess. It wasn't that there was nowhere to ask. There was always somewhere else to ask. Only the end was nowhere in sight.

I remember this moment. I already knew that the structure — passing things up, layer by layer — hadn't been designed badly. If anything, it was a structure built so that no single person could decide unilaterally. But that good structure was making it impossible to find the one single person who knew what I wanted to know.

"…I filed this objection because I thought there was a problem here."

"…Yes, I understand that. But what we confirmed was the procedure. The procedure was all followed."

"…Isn't the procedure being followed and this being okay two different things?"

She looked at me and was silent for a moment. Her face wasn't angry.

"…You're right. They're different things. But I'm the person who looks at the procedure."

Hearing that answer, I knew this person, too, hadn't lied, just as Tyche hadn't. Her job really was to look at the procedure. Whether there was anyone who looked beyond the procedure — I started looking for that from that moment on.

I asked her, on the spot, for the list of everyone involved. It was public material, so it wasn't hard to get.

"…What are you going to do with this?"

"…Go visit them, one at a time."

She looked at me for a moment. Her face wasn't trying to stop me.

"…There are four hundred thirty-two of them."

"…I know."

"…You're going to visit all of them?"

"…Just a few, to start."

She didn't ask any further and printed out the list for me. Several sheets stapled together. No names — only department, position, and stage of involvement. Passing on it, stage 1. Putting it in order, stage 2. Confirming, stage 3. Convening, stage 4. It was laid out that way.

Taking hold of this list, I felt a strange sensation. A list of four hundred thirty-two people with not one name on it. In exactly the same way that the people of the Seventh Classification had no names, the names of the people on this list were also missing. Not the measured and the measured-against — both sides, without names. In this city, that was simply how things were done.

On my way home I took out the receipt and the notice side by side. Receipt number, processing period, review result. All of it connected up properly.

What I want to say in this chapter is one thing. The objection procedure had no flaw. I submitted a written statement, I received a review, I received a result. Every stage was recorded, and every record was open. And yet at the end of that flawless procedure, four hundred ten people's places were still empty.

That night I had a thought for the first time. If the procedure wasn't wrong, was what I was trying to change the wrong direction? I held onto that thought for a long time, but I couldn't reach an answer.

I also thought this. If the objection had been accepted and the original decision reversed, I would have felt satisfaction in this procedure. I would have easily concluded that the procedure had disappointed me. But the procedure hadn't disappointed me. It had simply worked, from beginning to end, honestly — and this was the result of that honest operation. That this was a far more difficult problem to handle was something I realized for the first time that day.

I told my aunt the result, and she was quiet for a moment before asking,

"…So what are you going to do now?"

"…I don't know. But I'm going to meet the people directly."

"…Who?"

"…Everyone involved in this matter. There's a list."

My aunt looked at me. Her face was worried, but she didn't try to stop me.

"…How many is it?"

"…More than four hundred."

My aunt heard that number and said nothing. In front of that number, I wasn't confident myself that I could really meet all four hundred. I just wanted to meet even a few.

My aunt cleared the soup bowls and said,

"…Hora. It might not be those people's fault."

"…I know. I'm going to meet them anyway."

"…What are you going to ask when you meet them?"

I couldn't answer right away. After a long moment I said,

"…I don't know. I just feel like someone must know."

"…Know what?"

"…Why this turned out this way."

My aunt didn't ask any further. That night I spread out the list again and drew a line under the first name — not a name, but the first department and position — and underlined it. Passing it on, stage 1, initial receiving officer.

Drawing that line, I didn't feel like I was starting something great. I had simply decided what to do next. Deciding what to do next was, at the time, the entirety of what I could do.

I fell asleep with the list beside my pillow. The next morning I was going to go find the first person. I had no idea at all, then, what that person would say to me. If I had guessed, I might have been a little less surprised. I had no preparation at all when I went and knocked on that door.

(Continued in Chapter 17.)

Chapter 17. I Only Passed It On

_Previously_ — The objection was formally received, formally reviewed, and dismissed as "no procedural defect." Hora received the list of those involved — four hundred thirty-two people. She decided to go find the first name on it: the initial receiving officer.

At the top of the list it read, "initial receiving officer, Ninth Administrative Office."

I didn't know where that office was, so I asked at an information desk three times. The Ninth Administrative Office was at the far south end of the city, in a district I'd never once been to.

The clerk at the desk marked out the route on a map and asked,

"…What takes you all the way out there? Most people never go that far."

"…I'm going to meet someone."

"…Someone you know?"

"…No. This will be the first time."

The clerk didn't press further and handed me the map. In sixteen years living in this city, I'd received this kind of consideration more times than I could count. This city's people rarely ask why. Not asking is a kindness — that's what I'd learned, and I'd learned it well. But that day, I found the kindness a little disappointing. If anyone had asked me, "Why are you going, is something wrong," I probably would have poured out everything right there.

The walk took more than an hour. As I walked, I rehearsed in my head, several times over, what I would ask. In the end I couldn't use a single word of what I'd practiced.

Writing this chapter, I want to set down precisely how nervous I was that morning. I felt as though I were bracing for a confrontation. I went in ready to fight someone. As it turned out, almost none of that preparation was needed.

After this chapter I go on to meet eleven more people, and I won't write each of those meetings out in this much detail. But I want to set down this first meeting in full. Every subsequent thing that happens has its shape already, entire, inside this one.

The Ninth Administrative Office was a small building. It had three service windows and six waiting chairs. No one was waiting.

A notice board on the wall listed the office's business. Receiving unregistered-residence lists, formal review, forwarding to the higher department. Only those three things. Reading them, I understood that these three things were the whole of what the people here did all day long. Nothing more, nothing less.

I showed the list to the person seated at the reception window and asked,

"…Which of you is the initial receiving officer?"

She looked at the list and pointed at the seat beside her own.

"…That's me. I received it."

I lost my words for a moment. I hadn't expected to find him this easily. Of four hundred thirty-two people, the very first one, sitting in the exact seat the list said — and it really was her. I'd expected finding this person would take some remarkable effort. It hadn't. Even without a name, knowing only the department and the seat, finding a person in this city was not hard.

"…Are you the one who received the Seventh Classification matter?"

"…Yes, that's me. Why?"

There was no wariness in her voice. Only a plainly curious one.

I sat down in a chair and explained the situation — that I'd filed an objection, that it had been dismissed, and that I was going to meet the people involved directly.

She listened to me to the end. She didn't interrupt in the middle. While I spoke, she stopped tidying the papers on her desk and looked at me. Her manner of listening was earnest.

I still think, even now, that her earnestness was striking. She could have treated me as a nuisance visitor. A sixteen-year-old had come to talk about an objection. But there wasn't the slightest trace of that in her. This city's people were mostly like that. Listening to someone to the end wasn't a special thing here.

"…So this is about items 3, 7, and 12?"

"…You know about it?"

"…The summary of your objection came to us too. It gets circulated to every related department."

Hearing that answer, I was surprised for a moment. It meant my objection had reached all four hundred thirty-two people. This, too, hadn't been hidden. I was actually puzzled that she'd agree to see me at all, already knowing about my objection.

"…What do you think about this matter, then?"

She thought for a moment before answering.

"…I can tell you what I processed. But I haven't really thought much about the matter itself."

"…Why not?"

"…What I did was receive it. When the unregistered-residence list comes up, I receive it and forward it to the higher department."

I asked her more specifically what she had done.

"…All I did on this matter was one thing. I took the unregistered-residence list forwarded from the regional management office, checked the format was correct, and sent it up."

"…You didn't make the list?"

"…No, the list comes from the regional management office already made. I just check that it matches the set format."

"…And the relocation sites?"

"…Not my responsibility. Other departments handle that after I forward it."

"…Did you know items 3, 7, and 12 remained temporarily designated?"

"…I saw it in the summary of your objection. I didn't know before that."

"…You didn't know before?"

"…No. What I do stops at receiving. I don't see what happens after that."

Something caught at me in that answer. I say "caught," but I felt no sense at all that she was lying. She really seemed not to have known.

"…Then who makes the list in the first place?"

"…The regional management office. They survey the land, confirm registration status, and make the list."

"…Do they know how this list gets used?"

"…I couldn't say. I don't see that side of it either."

"…So is there actually anyone who knows the whole picture in this matter?"

She thought for a moment and said,

"…The district committee members would, wouldn't they. They're the ones who gather it all and look at it."

I heard that and laughed inwardly. I was that very committee member. And I hadn't known either. Not until I'd read all three hundred pages instead of the forty-page summary.

In this moment I felt something turn over inside me. She believed there was someone out there who knew the whole picture. At first, so had I. But now I know. There's no one appointed to see the whole picture. You only see it if you're the one willing to look.

I asked her, too, where the regional management office was located. I meant to go there next.

"…There's a lot of people there too. I don't know who's in charge, myself."

"…Then how do I find out?"

"…Go and ask, and they'll tell you. Everyone knows exactly what they did."

That turned out to be true. And over the following weeks, I would keep confirming just how accurate that statement was. Everyone knew exactly what they had done. Only no one knew what the whole added up to mean.

"…But with a matter this big, don't you feel like something's off? It's eleven hundred forty people."

She looked at me and thought for a moment.

"…To be honest, I see intakes like this every week. This one was one among them. It was a big one, sure, but I only look at the quantity. If the format's right, I forward it."

"…Doesn't a bigger quantity make you more careful?"

"…It does concern me. But my job isn't to make judgments. I only passed it on."

"…Who makes the judgment, then?"

"…I don't know exactly either. It gets reviewed at the higher department, and that goes up to the district, and the district decides it through the Consensus, doesn't it."

"…Isn't there someone who judges it somewhere in the middle?"

"…It's less a judgment than each person confirming at their own stage. I confirm the format, the next person confirms the quantity, the next confirms the basis. That's how it builds up into a decision. It's not a structure where one person judges."

Listening to that explanation, I realized I was hearing the very same thing this city had taught me from the start. The Consensus doesn't weigh — it gathers. I'd learned to call that a humble system. Hearing her say it, that humility began to show a different face.

I remember this sentence exactly, even now. I only passed it on. She didn't say it like an excuse. It wasn't a defensive tone either. It was simply the tone of someone stating a fact. I couldn't find any shame in that tone anywhere. There was nothing to be ashamed of.

I've used this sentence as the title while writing this book. It's this chapter's title, and it might as well be this Part's title too. Everything about this city is in this one sentence — I've come to think that many times since that day. Saying "I only passed it on" isn't a lie. It really is only passing it on. The problem is that four hundred thirty-two people, each, only passed it on.

There was one more thing I wanted to ask.

"…So what do you do if something like this feels wrong to you?"

"…I point people to the objection window. I've actually guided a few people there myself."

"…Have you yourself ever filed an objection?"

She looked at me a moment and laughed. It wasn't a mocking laugh.

"…Me? What would I know to object over? I'm just someone checking the format."

"…But you must have felt something was strange, at least."

"…I have. But feeling something is strange and being sure it's wrong are different things. I don't know the whole picture of this matter. All I see is the one sheet of list that comes into the intake box."

I thought about this for a long time. She was right. She really did see only one sheet. Almost no one in this city had read all three hundred pages the way I had. Most people saw only the one page in front of them, and if that one page didn't look strange, they moved it along to the next.

Finally I asked,

"…Is there anything you regret, by any chance?"

She thought for a long while before answering.
