"…Regret… I'm not sure. Since I don't know what I did wrong, I don't know what I should regret either. But hearing what you've told me, Hora, I can't say my mind is at ease."

"…What do you mean, not at ease?"

"…That there was a person behind the paper I passed along — it felt more real to me today than it usually does. Usually it was just a number."

"…So what do you want to do now?"

She thought for a moment.

"…I don't think there's much I can do. I still don't know the whole picture. But next time a list like this comes through, I think I'll look at it a little more closely."

"…What difference does that make?"

"…Honestly, I don't know. It might not make any difference at all. But like I said, after today I don't think it'll just look like numbers to me anymore."

I understood this answer wasn't promising any great change. And yet it didn't feel entirely empty either. Writing this chapter, I still don't know how to weigh this answer. Is it more honest to say nothing will change, or to say something will? She said, a little of each, both at once.

I thanked her and left. She stood and saw me out until I was gone. It wasn't a special courtesy. Just this city's ordinary manners.

Leaving the Ninth Administrative Office, I felt something hollow. I'd expected to hear something decisive from her — a hidden fact, someone's wrongdoing. Instead all I'd heard was one person's honest account of her ordinary day's work.

I think I have to set down this hollowness honestly. I was a little disappointed that day. Maybe I'd wanted to find a bad person, someone I could hate. If there'd been someone to hate, this would have all been much simpler. But I couldn't find anyone to hate. Instead, four hundred thirty-one more people I couldn't hate remained.

And I didn't know yet, that day, that this same honest account would repeat itself four hundred thirty-one more times. If I had known, I would have been a little less shocked. Or — maybe I'd have been more shocked. Once can be coincidence. Four hundred times is not.

On my way home I marked the first line on the list. Confirmed. Beside it I wrote in small letters: Not ashamed. No lies.

I told my aunt about the person I'd met today. She stopped in the middle of setting out dinner.

"…What did this person do wrong?"

"…No, she says she didn't do anything wrong. Listening to her, I don't think she did either."

"…Then why did you go see her?"

"…I thought she might know something."

"…Did you find out anything?"

I thought for a moment and answered.

"…I found out this person doesn't know the whole picture."

My aunt looked at me.

"…So will the next one know?"

"…I don't know. But there are four hundred thirty-two on the list, so if I go one by one, maybe someone who knows will turn up."

My aunt didn't answer that and finished laying out dinner. We ate that night mostly in silence.

That night I spread out the list again. I traced the second line, the third line, the fourth line with my finger. Department names and positions ran on. I still believed then that if I met all these people, sooner or later I'd find someone who said, "I decided it."

How long that belief lasted, I write in the next chapter. To say it now, ahead of time: not long.

(Continued in Chapter 18.)

Chapter 18. Twelve More

_Previously_ — Hora met the initial receiving officer. "I only passed it on." An honest statement. No shame in it either. After that, Hora decided to try meeting a few more of the four hundred thirty-two on the list.

Over the two weeks that followed, I met twelve more people. I kept up my other deliberation work too, crossing names off the list whenever I found a spare moment.

I could have written each of these twelve out in detail, one at a time. But then this book would start to read like a roster. Instead I'm gathering here what I heard in common from these twelve. Each was a different person, a different office, a different voice. And yet what they said was strikingly similar.

I went to see one or two a day. Some days I went to the wrong department entirely and wasted half a day. One person happened to be on leave and I had to go back. Finding people in this city was never a hassle, but it certainly took time.

The second person was the surveying officer at the regional management office. Her job was to physically measure unregistered residences. She held a tape measure and a small ledger in her hands.

"…All I do is measure. Whether a place is registered, how wide it is. I measure that and send the list to the listing department."

"…When you're measuring, you must see people living there."

"…I see them. But what I measure is the land, not the people."

"…Isn't it strange to measure only the land when you can see people living there?"

"…At first it felt strange. But that's my job. How many houses, how many people living there — those go in the column beside it, as numbers only. There's no column for faces."

I heard that and glanced at her ledger. There really were only number columns. No name column, no face column. Looking at that ledger, I thought, for a moment, that every piece of paper in this city eventually resembled that ledger.

The third person was a format reviewer.

"…I only check whether the list follows the prescribed form. Whether every field is filled in, whether the numbers run in order."

"…You don't look at the content?"

"…I do look at the content, but not to judge it — to confirm it matches the form."

"…So it passes as long as the form is right?"

"…Yes. Not a single list that's come to my department in the last three years has been rejected for wrong formatting. The regional management office is that meticulous about it."

Hearing that answer, I understood that this review stage was, in effect, purely formal. Formal didn't mean bad. It just meant nothing was actually filtered at this stage. Nothing being filtered didn't mean this person was lazy. There was simply nothing there to filter.

The fourth, fifth, and sixth people were all from higher departments. Their offices were spread across three different floors, and I had to be shown the way freshly each time. All three said much the same thing. "We just consolidate what comes up from below." "We don't create anything new." "We're the seat where the confirmation stamp gets put."

The fourth had been doing consolidation work for over ten years. "…This is my first time seeing a matter this scale. But the way I consolidate doesn't change. Growing in size doesn't change what I do." The fifth was younger, and pointed at the regulation binder tacked up at his seat. "…It's all right here. I don't do anything outside of this."

To the sixth I asked something a little different.

"…Before you put the confirmation stamp, did you ever notice anything that felt strange?"

"…Honestly, yes. The three temporary-designation sites."

I felt a flicker of hope. Something's finally caught, I thought.

"…And?"

"…But the temporary designation itself is a procedure that's in the regulation. It's explicitly stated that something can be temporarily designated before final confirmation. So it felt strange, but it wasn't a violation of the regulation."

"…Couldn't you have stopped it, when it felt strange?"

"…That's outside my authority. I only have the authority to confirm whether something matches the regulation."

"…So when you felt it was strange, who did you say that to?"

"…I didn't tell anyone separately. Strange and a regulation violation are different things. If I raised an objection every time something felt strange to me, our department wouldn't get anything done."

Listening to this answer, I saw clearly, for the first time, the distance between the feeling of "this is strange" and the fact of "I have authority to stop this." There was a person here who had felt it strange. But that feeling wasn't this person's job.

I know this answer might sound cold. But it didn't sound cold to me in that room, hearing it. If anything, it was the voice of someone who knew exactly where they stood. If this person had tried to handle something outside her own authority, in this city that would itself have been an overreach. I nodded along with her words even as some part of me stayed uneasy.

After meeting these six, I sat on a bench for a while to rest. Not because my legs hurt — because my head did. Walking home that evening, for the first time I briefly thought about giving up on all this. It wasn't that it was too hard. It was that I couldn't see an answer anywhere.

I didn't hold onto that thought for long. If I gave up, the four hundred ten would simply go to a place that didn't exist. If I didn't give up, the outcome would probably be the same. But between giving up and not giving up, there seemed to be a difference — at least to me. What that difference was, I couldn't have explained then.

The seventh person was the support-fund calculation officer. Her desk was covered in a calculator and sheets of formulas.

"…The math is accurate. I double-check it myself, too."

"…Is there actually somewhere those support funds can be used?"

"…That's the destination-site management department's responsibility."

"…Even if it's not your responsibility, weren't you curious?"

"…I was curious. But when I asked, I didn't get an answer. They just said it's still not finalized on their end either."

I understood that this person had already asked the exact same question I was asking. She'd simply asked before me, and been stopped before me too.

The eighth person belonged to the destination-site management department, but as it happened wasn't actually assigned to items 3, 7, and 12.

"…Those three aren't my responsibility. I only manage confirmed sites."

"…Then who's responsible for those three?"

"…Probably no one, yet. Since they're not confirmed."

"…So until someone's assigned, those three just stay as they are?"

"…Probably. Once someone's assigned, someone will move on it."

"…Who assigns that?"

"…I don't know that either. Personnel placement's a different department's business."

In this answer I saw a strange hole for the first time. Because it wasn't confirmed, there was a place with no one assigned yet. With no one assigned, no one was looking after it, and with no one looking after it, it stayed unconfirmed. This too was a loop. The same shape as the loop that produced unregistered residences in the first place. Only this time it wasn't land circling inside the loop — it was responsibility.

The ninth and tenth people handled convening for the committee. They set meeting dates and distributed materials.

"…I really only convene. I don't get involved with the substance of the agenda."

"…Do you know when you convene whether an agenda is big or small?"

"…I can see the scale. But it doesn't affect my work. The convening procedure is the same no matter the scale of the matter."

The eleventh person was the record keeper. She wrote the minutes.

"…I write down exactly what's said. I don't add my own opinion."

"…Reading the minutes, no one seems to say their own opinion."

"…Right, everyone just talks about passing it on and putting it in order, so I have no choice but to write exactly that."

"…Has anyone ever raised a different opinion?"

"…Sometimes. But that's written into the minutes exactly as it happened too. 'One member raised the need for a re-review.' And then it moves straight to a vote."

"…What was the vote result?"

"…This matter had a re-review proposal raised, but the original stood after the vote."

I later found and read that minutes record myself. Viewing was never blocked, as always. It really was written just as she'd said. Someone had raised an objection. Who that person was didn't appear in the minutes either. It only said "one member."

I became curious, at this point, for the first time. Who was that "one member"? It meant that someone besides me had felt something was strange. But that person, too, had lost the vote in the end, and hadn't even left a name behind. I wanted to find this person, but I had no way to search for a name that wasn't in the minutes.

The twelfth person was the one who had first brought this entire matter forward. I thought this person, at least, might be different. He was the one who'd raised the agenda in the first place.

"…All I did was confirm that the cumulative count had crossed the threshold, and bring it forward. Once it crosses the threshold, bringing it forward is my job."

"…Who set the threshold?"

"…It was set a long time ago. I only apply it."

"…Have you never thought about whether that threshold is appropriate?"

"…I've thought about it. But that's not something I can change either. Amending the threshold is a different procedure."

I'd hoped this person would be my last chance. He was the one who'd brought it forward, so surely he'd know something. But he too had only confirmed that the threshold had been crossed. Whoever had made the threshold itself was somewhere else again.

I asked him one last thing.

"…So who is the person who actually decided, in this matter, 'let's do it this way'?"

He thought for a long while. It was a genuinely thoughtful expression. He wasn't trying to give a careless answer.

"…I don't know. I brought it forward, the committee deliberated, and the Consensus decided. Inside all of that, the one person who decided 'let's do it this way'... doesn't seem to exist."

"…No one?"

"…No. Not that I know of."

I wrote that answer down on paper exactly as he said it. Doesn't seem to exist. None of the twelve had said it quite this precisely, but every one of them had, in their own way, been saying this same thing. No one decided. And yet a decision exists. I thought I'd already known this sentence from the start — that day was the first time I truly felt its weight.

By the time I'd met all twelve, two weeks had somehow gone by. To all twelve I'd asked the same question. "Do you think this is wrong?"

All twelve answered similarly. "The part I saw wasn't wrong." "I don't know about the whole." "It's outside my authority."

None of them got angry. None of them found me a bother. None of them lied. And none of them felt ashamed. A few of them even offered me coffee or tea.

Not being ashamed might sound strange to hear. At first I thought it was strange too. But after meeting a few of them, not being ashamed came to seem natural. There was no one among them who had done anything to be ashamed of. Each had done their own share, honestly, exactly.

Meeting these twelve, I let go, one by one, of the expectations I'd first carried. At first I went in thinking, "maybe this person is the culprit." By around the third person I'd given up on that hope. From about the sixth on, I started to feel, if anything, sorry for these people. Everyone was diligently doing their own part in their own seat. That this diligence, all gathered together, had become the decision that sent four hundred ten people to a place that didn't exist — that was the part I found hardest to bear.

A few of the twelve, when we parted, asked me back. "Do you think this could change?" Every time, I answered that I didn't know. Because I really didn't.

On the way home I finished marking the list. All twelve lines, confirmed. Beside each I wrote the same words every time. No wrongdoing. Doesn't know the whole.

I asked my aunt.

"…I met all twelve. Not one of them did anything wrong."

"…Isn't that a good thing, then?"

"…But the result stays the same. Four hundred ten people are still going to a place that doesn't exist."

My aunt was quiet for a moment and said,

"…That's the strangest part of all. No one did anything wrong, and the result is still bad."

"…Yeah. That's the strangest part."

That night, for the first time, I wondered whether I was looking for the wrong people. It seemed like meeting all four hundred thirty-two would probably get me the same answer. I decided to go find Tyche the next day. I thought Tyche might be able to explain this structure to me.

My aunt asked one last thing, clearing the rice bowls.

"…Hora. Going around meeting all these people — is it good for them, or bad for them?"

I couldn't answer that question for a long moment.

"…I don't know. A few of them seemed uncomfortable, if anything."

"…So will you stop?"

"…No. I'm going to keep going."

My aunt didn't ask any further. That night I ran through the twelve faces one by one in my mind. The surveying officer, the format reviewer, the person who'd put on the confirmation stamp, the one who calculated the support funds, the convening officer, the record keeper, and the one who'd first brought the matter forward. Not one of them had a bad face. That was what held onto me longest of all.

The next day I told Tyche all of this exactly as it happened. She heard me out to the end and then began explaining, very calmly. That explanation would become the most difficult scene in this whole book to deal with. I still haven't managed, to this day, to fully rebut it.

(Continued in Chapter 19.)

Chapter 19. Tyche Explains

_Previously_ — Hora met twelve people. All twelve were honest, and none of them felt ashamed. Each had done exactly their own share; no one had decided "let's do it this way." Hora went to find Tyche, hoping she could explain this structure.

Tyche was sitting by the window in the deliberation support office. I pulled up the chair beside her.

"…I met twelve people."

"…I know. The departments have been a little stirred up."

"…Stirred up? No one got angry, though."

"…It's not that they're stirred up out of anger. It's that they didn't realize the matter they were each involved in was this big, and they found out through you. That's the stir."

I heard that and stopped for a moment. That my going around asking questions had set something off in these people — I only realized it then, for the first time.

Tyche set aside the document she'd been looking at while I sat down. Her whole manner suggested nothing else was urgent right now.

"…Did I do something wrong?"

"…Not wrong. It's just an uncommon thing to do, in this city. Someone going around and looking for what's behind their own job."

"…Then why has no one ever done this uncommon thing?"

Tyche shrugged lightly.

"…Because everyone's busy. And because the summary is accurate. When there's an accurate summary, there's no reason to read three hundred pages."

Listening to her, I understood this wasn't meant as a criticism. Tyche really believed it. That any reasonable person would trust the summary and move on.

Tyche glanced toward the window for a moment, then looked back at me.

"…I wonder about it myself, sometimes. Why I don't do it."

"…Do what?"

"…Not reading things to the end. I was born and raised in this city, I know the summary is accurate — and even so, I could still read the whole thing sometimes. But I never have. Not once."

"…Why not?"

"…I don't know. I just never felt the need to, I guess. How did you start doing it, Hora?"

I didn't answer that question right away. I didn't want to bring up my mother here. I only said,

"…It's just a habit."

Tyche didn't press further on that answer. Not pressing was this city's manners, and that day I was grateful for those manners too.

"…Tyche, can you explain why this turned out this way?"

Tyche looked at me straight on. Her face wasn't angry, wasn't bothered. If anything, it looked as though she'd been waiting.

"…I can explain it. But it won't be the answer you want."

"…I still want to hear it."

Tyche pulled out a sheet of paper and began to draw. One small circle at the top, several circles below that, more circles below that. At the very bottom, the circles multiplied into dozens. A triangle shape.

"…This triangle."

Tyche tapped the top circle with the tip of her pen.

"…This is the decision. This many people are involved to reach one decision. But the top circle actually isn't a circle. It's an empty seat. No one is sitting there."

Looking at that drawing, I realized this was the same shape I'd been seeing all along in the signature box.

"…This is this city's deliberation structure. There's a decision at the top, and it passes through several stages to get there. Each stage has its person, and each person handles only their own stage."

"…I know that already. I heard it from all twelve."

"…But you don't know why it was designed this way."

I nodded.

"…Where did you learn this triangle?"

"…During training. Everyone who becomes a deliberation assistant learns this. It's the very first thing taught, on the first day — this exact drawing."

"…Doesn't anyone find it strange when they see it? That the top is empty?"

Tyche thought a moment and shook her head.

"…When I first saw it, I actually felt reassured. If someone were sitting at the top, it would all come down to that person's state of mind. If it's empty, it's not resting on anyone at all."

Hearing that, I realized Tyche and I had felt completely different things looking at the exact same drawing. I looked at that empty seat and grew uneasy. Tyche looked at that same empty seat and felt reassured.

"…What would happen if one person decided everything, start to finish?" Tyche asked.

"…It would become that person's responsibility."

"…Right. But what if that person's judgment is wrong?"

"…Then it becomes that person's fault."

"…So that person would feel burdened and try to avoid making judgments. Or, the opposite — they might try to force their own judgment through no matter what. Neither is good."

I couldn't argue with that. It was true. The idea that things go wrong when one person holds everything was common sense I'd grown up hearing in this city since I was small. I'd heard it many times in school too.

"…So this city made it so that no one person decides. The decision is broken into small pieces and spread across many people. Each person only needs to do their own share, exactly. No one has to carry the weight of judgment."

"…But breaking it up that way means no one can see the whole."

"…Right. That's the cost of this method."

"…But that cost is too high. Four hundred ten people are going to a place that doesn't exist — can that be dismissed as a cost?"

Tyche set down the paper and looked at me.

"…Hora, what do you think it would've been like if it hadn't been done this way?"

I couldn't answer.

"…If one person had judged it, that person might have made a mistake, or been biased. It might have split into who gets measured and who does the measuring. Do you know why this city ended up this way in the first place? Because one person's judgment dividing people up turned out wrong far too often."

"…So they made it so no one judges at all?"

"…Yes. And that really did work. Has there ever been a decision in this city made because someone hated someone else, Hora? Have you ever seen that?"

I could only say I hadn't. There really hadn't been.

"…So what about this matter, then? The outcome was bad."

"…A bad outcome and an unfair process are different things. The process was fair. Everyone was reviewed by the same standard, everyone went through the same procedure. The outcome coming out badly isn't the fault of this procedure — the situation itself was simply bad."

"…What do you mean, the situation was bad?"

"…Unregistered residences weren't supposed to exist in the first place. They accumulated, and this time they had to be processed all at once, and among them some of the destination sites still aren't confirmed. This isn't a problem with the procedure — it's a problem with reality."

Listening to this answer, I felt that Tyche really was speaking with complete honesty. She wasn't dressing up her words to persuade me. She was just saying what she saw.

"…Then why did that problem with reality arise? Why did three whole destination sites go unconfirmed?"

"…Because the department in charge hasn't been staffed yet."

"…Why hasn't the department been staffed?"

"…That's a personnel placement issue. As this matter suddenly grew in scale, the manpower needed grew too, and manpower placement is only possible with next quarter's budget."

"…Then couldn't they have just waited for next quarter? Why was the implementation date set before that?"

Tyche paused on this question for a moment.

"…That... I don't know exactly either. Scheduling is set by a different department."

"…Another different department?"

"…Yes."

I listened to that answer and counted silently, in my head. Site confirmation, personnel department's business. Personnel placement, budget department's business. Implementation schedule, yet another department's business. Every time I asked about one problem, a different department came up.

This was the first moment I realized it wasn't that Tyche's explanation was wrong — it was that it was too accurate, and that accuracy was what frustrated me. Tyche could name the exact department for every single question. And yet no matter how far I traced those departments, no one ever appeared at the end who said, "I decided this." The more complete Tyche's explanation became, the more helpless I felt.

"…But Tyche, some of those twelve felt something was strange. The sixth person did. And someone on the committee raised an objection too."

"…Yes, I know that. It's in the minutes."

"…But that strangeness didn't get reflected anywhere."

"…It was reflected. It was raised as an objection, and it was processed through a vote. That's what reflection is."

"…If you lose the vote, that's the same as not being reflected."

"…No. It was reflected. The result simply defeated the strangeness. That, too, is procedure."

I couldn't find words for a long while after that. Tyche's logic had no gaps. Wherever I poked at it, she already had an answer waiting there.

"…So if I file a new objection now, would it come out the same?"

"…Probably. As long as the situation doesn't change. If items 3, 7, and 12 get confirmed, it might turn out different. But since they're not confirmed yet, filing another objection now would probably come out the same."

"…Then what can I do?"

Tyche didn't answer this question right away. She looked at me for a moment and said,

"…I don't really know that either. I can explain this structure, but what can be done outside of it — that's not my territory."

I realized only later that this answer was the first time Tyche had ever said "I don't know." Tyche knew everything there was to know about the structure. It was only outside the structure that she truly didn't know. Or — maybe "outside the structure" was a concept that didn't even exist for Tyche at all.

"…So are you saying this structure is perfect?"

"…I'm not saying it's perfect. No structure is perfect. I'm just saying this structure wrongs people less than other structures would."

"…Wrongs them less? What about those four hundred ten?"

"…Those four hundred ten are having a hard time because the outcome was bad — not because anyone in the process hated them or discriminated against them. It's a fact that they weren't registered, and that fact applied to everyone equally. It was bad luck. Not being wronged."

Something caught at me in that.

"…Why is bad luck different from being wronged?"

Tyche looked at me for a moment, then said, very gently,

"…Being wronged is what you call it when someone does something unjust to you. But this time, no one did anything unjust to those people. They simply happened to be in that position. The way you ended up sitting on this committee, Hora — that was the same thing. A vacancy, a number. That was luck too."

I heard that comparison and couldn't say anything. It was true. I too had ended up in this seat through a vacancy and a number. Those four hundred ten were in their position through registration and a number too. The structure was identical.

"…But I ended up in a good seat, and those people ended up in a bad one. Same luck, different result."

"…Yes, the results are different. But that's simply the nature of luck. Some end up on the good side, some on the bad. It looks unfair, but in the sense that no one intended it, it's fair."

"…Is it fair just because there was no intent?"

"…At least no one did this because they hated someone. I think that matters. If there's no hatred, then at least there's no unfairness that came from hatred."

I heard that and thought for a long while. Tyche wasn't entirely wrong. This city really had no hatred in it. I'd known that fact from the start, and I'd always thought it was one of the good things about it.

"…But even without hatred, bad things can still happen. That's what you're saying."

"…Right. That's exactly what happened here. Bad results can come about even without hatred. I don't deny that."

In this answer, I felt for the first time, with certainty, that Tyche wasn't trying to persuade me. Tyche wasn't defending this structure. She was simply someone who saw this structure accurately. Defending it and describing it accurately were different things, and I had kept confusing the two.

"…So nobody gets wronged."

Tyche said that last.

"…That isn't a bad thing."

I wanted to argue with that. I really did. But that night, and for several days after, I couldn't find the words to argue back. Tyche hadn't said it to win against me. She really believed it, and there weren't many holes in that belief.

Leaving Tyche's room that day, I had a thought for the first time — that maybe I was the one who was wrong. That this structure might really be the best there is. That thought was what troubled me most.

Tyche added one more thing as I was about to get up.

"…Hora, I don't think what you're doing is meaningless. Meeting twelve people was probably a good thing. At the very least, those people are thinking more now about what they did."

"…But the outcome doesn't change."

"…The outcome not changing doesn't mean it's meaningless."

I looked up at that.

"…What do you mean by that?"

"…I don't know. It just feels that way to me."

This last sentence stayed with me oddly, for a long time. Up until then, Tyche had been someone explaining everything through logic. But in this last sentence she said, "I don't know, it just feels that way." That there was a place logic couldn't reach, even for Tyche — I saw that for the first time then.

I left the deliberation support office and walked the corridor. The window let in no view of outside, but that day I found myself unusually curious about what lay beyond it.

That night I told my aunt about Tyche. She listened for a long while and said,

"…It sounds like what she said might be right, or it might not be."

"…Same here."

"…But the fact that you couldn't argue back — that's the stranger part, to me."

I couldn't answer that. That I hadn't been able to argue back was what stayed with me longest that night.

(Continued in Chapter 20.)

Chapter 20. To the Secretary

_Previously_ — Tyche laid out the structure without a single gap. "…So nobody gets wronged. That isn't a bad thing." Hora couldn't argue back. Tyche had also said, "outside the structure isn't my territory." Wondering who stood outside that structure, Hora thought of the Secretary, who handled convening and the record.

That night I opened the list again and closed it again.

I already knew, having met the twelve, that meeting all four hundred thirty-two would only get me the same answer. People who'd only passed it on, people who'd only measured, people who'd only confirmed. All honest, all doing only their own share. And yet no one, from beginning to end, knew the decision that all those shares had added up to.

Except for one person.

The Secretary convened, distributed materials, wrote the minutes, and delivered the confirmed results to each department. If there was anyone in this city who saw a matter from beginning to end in documented form, it wouldn't be someone who judged — it would be someone who carried. And a carrier, at least, would know the route.

The next morning, walking to the reception desk, I passed the water channel. The water was still making its earth-colored sound. Passing it, I briefly wondered whether, if I asked someone about this channel too, it might all get explained someday. I already knew that since it had never come up as an agenda item, there was no one to explain it. But the Seventh Classification matter had come up as an agenda item. So there should be someone who could explain it.

I stopped for a moment outside the reception desk. I wasn't sure going to find the Secretary was the right choice. Meeting the twelve, I'd already heard "that's not my responsibility" more times than I could count. The Secretary would probably say the same. And yet I pushed the door open anyway. I'd been everywhere else — it felt like I ought to try here, one last time.

The Secretary was in a small room off the reception desk. The door was open, and papers sat neatly stacked on his desk. He greeted me first when he saw me.

"…Hora, what brings you here today?"

"…It's about the Seventh Classification matter."

The Secretary pulled out a document folder and offered me a seat.

"…Sit down. What would you like to know?"

I brought out the questions I'd prepared in order, starting with the simplest.

"…Why haven't items 3, 7, and 12 been confirmed yet?"

"…Confirming the relocation sites falls under the destination-site management department, but that department hasn't yet had personnel assigned. Personnel assignment isn't possible until next quarter's budget is approved."

This was already something I knew. I'd heard the same thing from the eighth and ninth of the twelve I'd met. I moved to the next question.

"…Then why wasn't the implementation date set for after next quarter? If there's no manpower, those three sites can't be processed."

For this question the Secretary flipped through the folder to find a page with dates and showed it to me.

"…The implementation date was set based on the processing threshold for the whole Seventh Classification matter. It had already been eight months past the threshold, and there was a review finding that delaying further would only let the unregistered accumulation grow larger. So the overall implementation date was kept as is, and only the three sites were handled through temporary designation."

"…Who reviewed that?"

"…At a higher-department review meeting. There are minutes."

The Secretary really did find and hand me that minutes record. I took it and skimmed through. Detailed dates and grounds that none of the twelve had given me were all right there. I realized this was a document I was seeing for the first time. Viewing had been possible, but I hadn't gotten that far.

"…Is it a problem specific to this matter being unusually big, or would it have turned out this way even if it had been a small matter?"

"…Over the past ten years, small-scale matters ran twenty or thirty people at a time. There were a few temporary designations back then too. But with fewer people, confirmation came quickly. It was a scale that only needed the department to bring on three or four more staff."

"…Why didn't that work this time?"

"…This time, at a scale of eleven hundred forty, the manpower needed for confirmation grew accordingly. Placing that manpower requires budget, and budget only moves on a quarterly basis. Something that never snagged when the scale was small snagged this time."

I heard that and understood this was, in a certain sense, an honest answer. If this had been a small matter, I wouldn't even be sitting in this seat right now. There hadn't been a problem — the problem had simply grown large.

"…So just because of the scale, people end up going to places that aren't even confirmed?"

The Secretary took a moment to choose his words for this question.

"…It's not confirmed that they're going. The temporary designation could still turn into a confirmed one, or get reassigned somewhere else. The problem is only that the implementation date arrives first, in the meantime."

"…Why doesn't the implementation date wait for confirmation?"

"…Because the implementation date for the whole matter was already set based on the other eleven hundred ten sites. If the whole thing were delayed for the sake of three sites, the other eleven hundred ten would be delayed along with it."

"…I don't think I could have found this on my own."

"…If you file a viewing request, it all comes up. It just takes time if you don't know the route to search. I can find it quickly because this is a document I handle every day."

Hearing that, I understood for the first time why the Secretary was the one person who could see the whole structure. Precisely because he doesn't judge, he ends up seeing everything. Someone who judges digs deep into only their own responsibility; someone who convenes passes lightly over every responsibility.

"…So did nothing strike you as odd in this matter, Secretary?"

The Secretary thought for a moment. It wasn't a silence meant to dodge the question. It was a genuinely thoughtful face.

"…The scale being large was visible. Eleven hundred forty is among the largest I've convened for."

"…And?"

"…But scale being large and a defect in the procedure being present are different things. What I look at is whether procedure was followed. Procedure was followed at every stage."

I heard that answer and thought it was similar to what I'd heard from Tyche. But something was different. Tyche had stopped at one point, saying "that's not my territory." The Secretary didn't stop.

"…I've heard the temporary designation is a procedure in the regulation. Who made that regulation?"

"…An amendment seventeen years ago. Back then delays before confirmation kept coming up frequently for small matters, so a method was introduced — designating temporarily and confirming later. Before that, the agenda item itself was held until confirmed, and that caused processing to run far too slow."

"…So thanks to that regulation, the three sites now remain temporarily designated."

"…Correct."

"…What if that regulation didn't exist?"

"…If it didn't exist, this entire matter would have been held until all three sites were confirmed. Then the remaining eleven hundred ten would have been processed that much later too."

I heard that answer and couldn't speak for a moment. What the Secretary had just told me wasn't the answer I wanted. I'd wanted to find some one broken link. Instead, what he showed me was that the regulation had been made, if anything, to process more people, faster.

"…So that regulation isn't wrong either."

"…Not in my view. Though what result this regulation produces when applied to a matter this large — the people who made it probably never fully anticipated that either."

Something caught at me in this last sentence. It was the first time the Secretary had ever said something unconfirmed — "probably never anticipated." Up to that point, every answer he'd given had been settled fact. Dates, minutes, the basis clauses. This sentence alone was pure speculation.

I wanted to press on that, but let it pass that day. There was still a lot left to ask.

"…What about the support funds, then? I heard the calculation was already finished even though the three sites aren't confirmed."

"…Support-fund calculation is done in advance based on the projected number of people. Even if the destination site changes, the calculated amount itself doesn't change. But actual disbursement only happens once the destination site is confirmed."

"…What if it never gets confirmed?"

"…Then disbursement never happens either."

That answer was short and precise. I found that precision, if anything, chilling.

I moved to a different question. That phrase I'd seen in the minutes kept nagging at me.

"…Secretary, in the eighteenth meeting's minutes, there's the line, 'one member raised the need for a re-review.' Who was that?"

The Secretary flipped his folder back to find that minutes record. It took quite a while. He paged through several sheets before finally stopping.

"…The eighteenth meeting, here it is. But the minutes only record the member number. A member from the Fourth Deliberation District."

"…What's the name?"

"…There's no name field in the minutes form. Only a member number is entered."

"…So there's no way to find out who this person was?"

"…Not necessarily impossible. Cross-referencing the member number against that day's attendance sheet might turn it up. But I've never done that cross-reference myself. It's not my job."

I heard that answer and felt a moment's disappointment. I already knew this person was the first one besides me who'd felt something was strange. And yet the Secretary didn't even know that person's name.

"…What happened to that person afterward? They lost the vote."

"…There's no separate procedure for a member whose proposed agenda loses a vote. It just moves on to the next item."

"…Nothing at all happens?"

"…No, nothing at all."

I would come back to this answer many times afterward. That nothing happens to the member who raised the objection. No punishment, no compensation, not even a name left in the record. At the time I thought that was a fair system. I didn't yet know how differently this would come to weigh on me, in another way, later.

"…Secretary, are you the one person who knows this matter from beginning to end?"

"…I don't know all of it. I know the meetings I convened, the materials I distributed, the minutes I wrote. That does overlap with most of the matter. Since the scale is large, a lot of meetings passed through too."

"…Then are you the only one who can explain this decision?"

The Secretary thought a little longer on this question.

"…There are people who can explain it. Everyone who handled each stage, all of them. I'm only someone who can see those stages stitched together. Stitching, too, is my job — but I'm not adding anything of my own into it."

"…Isn't stitching, too, a kind of judgment? Deciding what goes first and what goes after?"

"…The order is set by date. I'm not the one who decides that."

I wanted to argue with that, but couldn't find good grounds to. The dates really were just dates. It wasn't the Secretary who could change their order.

I talked with the Secretary for more than two hours that evening. I asked until I ran out of questions. The delayed site confirmation, the delay in personnel placement, the support-fund calculation basis, even the re-review request and the vote result recorded in the minutes. Not once did the Secretary say, "I can't answer that." What he didn't know, he said he didn't know, and what he knew, he told me all of it. Even when he said he didn't know, he explained why he didn't know.

That day I had, for the first time, the experience of asking something in this city and not hitting a wall. Tyche was honest too, but Tyche stopped at a certain point, saying "that's not my territory." The Secretary didn't stop. Even what fell outside his responsibility, he explained along with the fact that it fell outside his responsibility. I didn't yet fully realize, that day, how rare this was.

Finally I asked one last thing.

"…So, setting everything aside, what did you actually do in this matter, Secretary?"

The Secretary answered while tidying his folder.

"…What I did was convene."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes. Convened, passed things on, kept the record. That's all."

I heard that answer, gathered my papers, and stood. I thanked him. He returned the greeting.

Walking out the door, I retraced, in my head, everything he'd told me that day. The site hasn't been confirmed. Support-fund calculation is finished. The implementation date has been set. The regulation was amended. The meeting was convened.

On the way home I muttered those sentences to myself, one by one. Worried that talking to myself while walking would look strange, I kept my voice low. Not confirmed. Calculation finished. Convening done. As I walked, I vaguely sensed that all these sentences shared a similar shape, but I couldn't quite put my finger on what that shape was, at the time.

I told my aunt about the day's events. Hearing that the Secretary had given me the detailed answers I hadn't gotten from the twelve, my aunt looked a little surprised.

"…The Secretary told you all of that?"

"…Yeah. Nothing blocked me at all. Every question I asked got an answer."

"…So what did you find out?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. After a long while I said,

"…The procedure was all normal. The regulation had its reasons, the delay had its reasons. But somehow I feel like nothing got resolved at all."

My aunt asked, clearing the table.

"…You got every answer, so why doesn't it feel resolved?"

"…I don't know. I got every answer, but every one of those answers was passing off to somewhere else. This one's this department's business, that one's that department's business. But there was nothing of the Secretary's own in any of it."

My aunt heard that and looked at me for a moment. Her face looked like she wanted to ask more, then decided against it.

That night, late, I kept turning those sentences over in my head, until I felt something strange. I couldn't quite put my finger on exactly what was strange, that night. It wasn't until the next morning that I found the name for that strangeness.

(Continued in Chapter 21.)

Chapter 21. There Is No Subject

_Previously_ — The Secretary answered everything Hora asked, without a single stumble. Not confirmed yet, calculation is finished, convening is done. Not one thing snagged, and yet Hora came home feeling, strangely, like nothing had resolved at all. She found the name for that strangeness only the next morning.

The name for the strangeness was simple. There was no subject.

The thought came to me the moment I opened my eyes that morning. Going back over what the Secretary had said the day before, I realized not a single sentence had ever had "I" in it. Not confirmed. Calculation finished. Implementation date set. Regulation amended. Meeting convened. All of it went that way.

I'd already noticed this once before. At the time I'd let it go as simply a manner of speech. Tyche, too, had said, "That's just how the Secretary's job is." I'd accepted that and moved on.

But it wasn't a manner of speech. That morning, I saw for the first time, head-on, that this was too perfect to be called a mere habit. We'd talked for over two hours, and not one sentence began with "I did." If a person keeps a manner of speech without a single slip for two straight hours, that isn't a habit anymore. It's something else.

Eating breakfast that morning, I ran back through the way everyone I'd met had spoken. The initial receiving officer had said, "I only passed it on." The surveying officer had said, "I only measure." All twelve of them had used "I." Even when saying what they'd done was small, that small thing always had an "I" attached in front. Only the Secretary was different. Even though what he'd done was far bigger, he never placed himself in front of it.

I said as much to my aunt too.

"…Aunt, all twelve of them said, 'I did it.' Only the Secretary doesn't."

"…Isn't that because those people think they did something small? They say the important people talk humbly."

"…But it feels different from humility. If you're being humble, you still say 'what, me' but you still put in the 'I.' The Secretary doesn't put anyone there at all."

My aunt tilted her head at that but didn't press any further.

That afternoon I went back to the reception desk. The Secretary was in the same seat as the day before.

"…Hora, back again."

"…Yes, there's something else I wanted to ask, from yesterday."

"…Ask me anything."

I went straight to the point.

"…Secretary, did you know that not once, in everything you told me yesterday, did you say 'I did it'?"

The Secretary looked at me a moment. It wasn't a surprised face. If anything, a thoughtful one.

"…Is that so."

"…Yes. Not confirmed. Calculation finished. Convening done. It was all like that. Not once did you say, I confirmed it, I convened it."

"…I suppose I did."

"…You're doing it again right now. It's not 'I did that,' it's 'I suppose I did.' If it were you saying it normally, I'd think you'd have said something more like, 'I must have said it that way.'"

The Secretary gave a small smile at this.

"…It seems you know my own way of speaking better than I do, Hora."

I didn't feel that smile as unkind. And yet I didn't stop there, and kept asking.

"…Why do you talk that way?"

The Secretary set his papers aside and looked at me straight on.

"…What do you mean by that?"

"…You're missing from everything you've done, Secretary. Convening happened, confirmation didn't happen, results came out. You talk as though it all just happened on its own. But you're the one who actually did it."

"…I did do it, yes."

"…Then why don't you say it that way?"

The Secretary went quiet for a moment at that question. I felt that this silence was different from his usual silences. In front of other questions, he'd pause briefly while looking something up or reaching for a memory. This silence wasn't that. It wasn't a silence of searching for something. It felt more like a silence of looking at something.

I remember those few seconds exactly, even now. The window let in no view of outside, and the room was quiet. During that silence I half-wondered whether I was pushing too hard, and felt a little regret. But before I could put that regret into words, the Secretary spoke first.

"…How do I put in something that isn't there."

I heard that and, for a moment, didn't understand what it meant.

"…Sorry?"

"…The subject. How do I put in something that isn't there."

I stayed still for a moment. This didn't sound like an excuse. It didn't sound like an apology either. His tone was exactly the same as always — the same tone from yesterday explaining the site-confirmation procedure, the same tone from this morning's greeting. No different.

"…What do you mean by that?"

"…If you ask me what I did, I can answer. Convened, passed it on, kept the record. Those are things that exist, so I can answer for them. But putting 'I' at the front of a sentence is a different matter. That's putting in something that isn't there."

"…But you're there. You exist, Secretary."

"…I exist, yes. But the 'I' that would go in that place, in that sentence — that doesn't exist. I'm not the one who made that sentence happen. I'm not the one who made that matter turn out the way it did, either. I only stood on the path the thing traveled along."

Hearing this answer, I felt something strange. This wasn't an excuse. It wasn't an apology. He wasn't trying to dodge his own wrongdoing right now. He really was reporting a fact. That there was no subject to put into his own sentence — he was saying it the way you'd report that an item wasn't in the storeroom.

It was a long while before I spoke again.

"…So do you think you did nothing at all, Secretary?"

"…No. What I did exists. Convening, passing things on, keeping the record. That I did. It's only that I can't say I "made" the result those things add up to."

"…What's the difference?"

"…If I hadn't convened, the meeting wouldn't have opened. That much is true. But that I convened it doesn't mean the decision that came out of that meeting became my decision. I'm the one who opens the door. What gets decided inside the door isn't my share."

"…But you keep opening it. Every single time."

"…Yes. That's my job."

I tried to find something to grab hold of in that answer, and couldn't. His logic was every bit as gapless as Tyche's. Only Tyche's logic hadn't been cold, and neither was the Secretary's. Both were simply exact.

"…Secretary, then what would you do if someone said to you, 'isn't this your responsibility'?"

"…I've rarely heard anything like that. But if I did, I'd tell them the one piece that's mine. What I convened, what I passed on. That's as much as I have to give. I can't give what isn't there."

"…But then no one takes responsibility for anything."

"…I think it would depend on how you use the word responsibility. For the piece I did, I take responsibility. If the convening was wrong, that's my fault. But if you ask who takes responsibility for the whole outcome, I don't know that. I've never seen the person who's supposed to be sitting in that seat."

I copied this down on paper as he spoke, feeling that he'd just said something very important to me, almost in passing. That he'd never seen the person supposed to be sitting in that seat. He hadn't said that seat doesn't exist at all. He'd only said he didn't know whether it existed or not — that he, himself, had simply never seen it.

There was one more thing I wanted to confirm.

"…Did the person who held this position before you speak this same way, too?"

"…The one before me? I never met them. They resigned before I arrived."

"…Do you know why they resigned?"

"…No. It wasn't written in the handover documents."

I paused for a moment, then asked,

"…Was that person's signature box empty too, by any chance?"

The Secretary took an old handover binder from his drawer and leafed through it. It smelled of old paper.

"…Yes. It's empty."

"…So would that person have spoken without a subject too, like you?"

"…That's something I can't know. But this way of speaking doesn't seem to be mine alone. When I was first learning this job, the person who taught me spoke this way too, as I recall."

"…Was that person a Secretary too?"

"…Yes. Secretary of a different deliberation district."

Hearing that answer, I began to think this wasn't one person's personality — it was something attached to the seat itself. I remembered something Tyche had said before: that a person who wants to stand out doesn't last long in this job. If people who don't stand out are the ones who stay in this seat for a long time, and if the speech of those who stay a long time hardens into this shape, then this wasn't any one person's problem.

I wanted to check one more thing right there. I went to the archive and pulled out five years' worth of old minutes. All of them written by the Secretary. I sat at a desk and went through them page by page, counting how many times a sentence began with "I."

I went through nearly a hundred pages. Not one single time. Convened, distributed, confirmed, received. It was all like that. Only in one corner of one very old minutes record was there a trace of a correction by hand. Someone had written "I distributed" and struck a line through it, rewriting it as "was distributed." The ink color was different. It had clearly been corrected later.

I stared at that trace for a long time. There was no way to know who had corrected it, or when. The Secretary himself might have looked back and corrected it later, or someone else might have corrected it while reviewing. Either way, that struck-out "I" felt, to me, like the one remaining trace of something that still lingered somewhere in this city. Long, long ago, in this very seat too, someone had once written "I."

I kept that minutes page in my mind like a photograph and left the archive.

"…Secretary, isn't this hard for you?"

I hadn't known this question was coming, myself. It was only after it left my mouth that I realized it was the question I'd really wanted to ask.

The Secretary thought a little longer for this question.

"…I'm not sure what you mean by hard. I do the work I'm meant to do. I know that work produces something. But to say I made it — I'm only a very small piece of that whole thing."

"…Even a small piece is still a piece."

"…That's true. So I do acknowledge my piece. What I convened, what I passed on, what I recorded. That piece is mine. But attaching my name to the whole picture — that would be manufacturing something that isn't there and pinning it on myself."

I heard that and couldn't press any further. Not because I had nothing left to ask, but because I no longer felt certain he was hiding something from me. He wasn't hiding anything. He really believed, truly, that there was nothing to put in front of his own sentence.

That evening I told my aunt about it. She stopped, mid-spoon, and looked at me for a long while.

"…He said, how do I put in something that isn't there?"

"…Yeah. It wasn't an apology, wasn't an excuse. He just said it, plainly."

"…Why does that stay with you so much?"

I couldn't answer right away. After a long moment I said,

"…It would have been better if he were lying. But he really believed it. That he has no subject."

My aunt didn't say anything more and slid the rice bowl over to me. I turned that sentence over late into the night. How do I put in something that isn't there. I thought again about the moment he'd once slipped and put "I" in by mistake, then corrected himself. At the time I'd thought it was just a habit slipping out. Now I saw it differently. He really was living in a place with no subject, and the occasional "I" that slipped out wasn't a mistake — it was a very small trace still remaining in that place.

The next day I told Tyche this too. She heard me and blinked, wide-eyed, for a moment.

"…He said, how do I put in something that isn't there?"

"…Yes. Those were his exact words."

Tyche went quiet for a moment, then said,

"…That's so like him."

"…What do you mean?"

"…The Secretary really can't lie. I know that. But hearing this, I think maybe not lying and not putting himself into a sentence might be the same thing."

I heard that and thought for a long while. It felt like Tyche had said something important, but exactly what it was, I couldn't quite pin down that day.

That night I copied this sentence onto paper. How do I put in something that isn't there. I decided that night to put this sentence at the center of this record. If this book ever holds anyone guilty of anything, it isn't on account of this sentence. This sentence wasn't a crime. It was simply a fact.

Writing this book, I've rewritten this chapter more times than I can count. Several times I felt the urge to paint the Secretary badly. But every time, I found myself returning to that sentence. How do I put in something that isn't there. There was no falsehood inside that sentence. I couldn't force a crime that wasn't there into him.

I still didn't know, standing before that fact, what I could do. I might have been able to push him and force him to say, "I decided this." But words wrung out that way could never be true. Making him say something that wasn't there was as much a falsehood as making him deny something that was there. That night, for the first time, I accepted that this problem wasn't one I could solve by pushing him.

(Continued in Chapter 22.)

Chapter 22. Confirmed

_Previously_ — Hora pointed out that the Secretary's language had no subject in it. He fell silent a moment, then answered: "…How do I put in something that isn't there." Not an excuse, not an apology — a report of fact. That night, Hora accepted that this wasn't a problem she could solve by pushing him.

About ten days had passed since then.

I'd shown up at the district as usual, and there, on the bulletin board, was a notice. The Seventh Classification residential clearance and relocation matter — Confirmation Notice.

I stood in front of it for a long while.

Over those ten days I'd been busy with other things. I'd talked with the Secretary twice more, dug up more of his old minutes, and asked Tyche about the "one member" left in the minutes. Tyche didn't know who that was either. I'd felt those days, that whole stretch, as time spent drawing closer and closer to the center of this problem. Finding a subject in the Secretary's sentences had happened within those ten days too. I'd thought I had discovered something huge.

But while I was discovering that huge thing, the deliberation had simply been going along at its own pace. I only realized that, standing there in front of the bulletin board.

The notice was short. Deliberation confirmed, implementation date, contact for the department in charge. I read it three times. Three readings and the content stayed the same.

I'm ashamed of the first thought that came to me, standing there. I found myself first recalling what I'd been doing over those ten days. Talking with the Secretary, discovering there was no subject, copying that sentence onto paper. I had been treating those ten days as time spent doing something important. And the whole time, the deliberation had simply been moving, by itself, toward confirmation.

I ran to the reception desk and asked,

"…When did this get confirmed?"

The clerk checked the screen.

"…Four days ago."

"…Four days ago? I was talking with the Secretary then."

"…That's separate from this. The confirmation procedure runs on a set schedule."

"…Wasn't it supposed to be reviewed again before confirmation? For the objection I filed?"

"…The objection was already reviewed and the result came out. Three weeks ago. Since there was no further objection after that, it moved to the confirmation stage normally."

I heard that and had nothing to say back. It was true. The objection had already been reviewed and dismissed, and I hadn't filed a new one. I'd spent those ten days going around meeting people, not submitting documents.

I went to find the Secretary.

"…Why didn't you tell me about this ahead of time?"

The Secretary looked at me with a slightly startled face. That was the first time I'd ever seen him startled.

"…The confirmation schedule goes out by notice. I have no separate procedure for informing people individually."

"…But I'm the member in charge of this matter."

"…You are the member in charge, and the expected confirmation date was already written into the third-session materials. Last page."

I heard that and searched my memory. The third-session materials. I'd read that all the way through too. But I couldn't remember whether an item for the expected confirmation date had been there at all.

"…Let me check."

That evening I pulled out that material again and read it. On the last page, in small print at the bottom of the table, it read: Expected confirmation date: 3 weeks hence. I read that sentence and had no choice but to admit I'd read it and let it pass right by.

I feel an urge, writing this passage, to make excuses. That table really was very small. It was crowded in among a lot of other numbers. But I also know that can't be an excuse. I was someone who read to the end. That table, too, was among the things I read to the end. It's only that I failed, that time, to read the weight of that one sentence.

That evening I went back to find the first person I'd met at the Ninth Administrative Office. I wanted to know whether she'd known about the confirmation.

"…I heard it's confirmed."

"…Yes, I saw the notice too."

"…Did you expect it?"

"…Yes, it was always on that same schedule. I wasn't especially surprised."

"…I didn't know."

She looked at me for a moment and said, carefully,

"…I think you, Hora, were looking into this whole matter so closely that you ended up missing the schedule instead. I only look at my own share, so I always keep track of the schedule. That's the whole of my job, after all."

This felt, oddly, painful to hear. I'd tried to look broadly and missed what was close at hand. This person had looked narrowly and, because of that, missed nothing. Looking broadly and not missing anything didn't seem to go together, in this city.

"…So if, in those ten days, I hadn't gone to see the Secretary and had instead kept meeting people, or filed a new objection — could confirmation have been delayed?"

The Secretary thought for a moment on this question.

"…If you'd filed a new objection, the re-review procedure would have started again, and confirmation would have been delayed by that much."

"…So I was late."

"…You weren't late."

I looked up at that.

"…If I wasn't late, what am I?"

"…Whatever you did, this confirmation procedure would have gone ahead. If there's no new objection, it's set to automatically move to the confirmation stage once the set period passes. That happens the same way whether or not you came to see me."

"…What does that mean?"

"…It means there's no one in this procedure that it waits for."

I couldn't say anything for a long while after hearing that.

At first I took this as comfort. As meaning it wasn't my fault that I was late. But the more I turned it over, the more I understood this wasn't comfort at all. This was a far more frightening thing to hear than comfort. That I wasn't late meant it wouldn't have mattered even if I'd been as fast as possible.

"…So even if I'd filed a new objection that very night, the result would have been the same?"

"…No. I told you, if you'd filed a new objection, the procedure would have started over. That's different. But if you file nothing at all, the procedure goes ahead waiting for no one. That's the answer I can give you."

"…Then what was I waiting for?"

The Secretary didn't answer that question. It probably wasn't a question he could answer. It wasn't a question about what he knew — it was a question about what I had done.

Finally I asked just one more thing.

"…When did you know this would get confirmed, Secretary?"

"…From the beginning. The expected confirmation date gets set at the same time the threshold is set. It was already there in the very first meeting's materials, which I convened."

"…It was set from the very beginning?"

"…Yes. Though it was only an expected date, not a confirmed one. It was a date that could be delayed if an objection came in. Since there was no objection, it went ahead as expected."

Hearing that, I understood that the date set from the very beginning and the date I hadn't known about for those ten days were, in fact, the same date. It hadn't been hidden. Even when I read the third-session material, even when my eyes passed right over that sentence, I had already, all along, been holding that information in my own hands.

"…So was there anyone who registered for or against this confirmation?"

"…No. Confirmation isn't a procedure that asks for for or against. If there's an objection, it's blocked. If there isn't, it goes through, as is, once the set period passes."

I heard that and, only then, truly understood that confirmation wasn't something that happened because someone said "good." No one had approved. No one had stepped forward to oppose it either. It had simply gone through because no one stood in its way.

I looked up the full text of the confirmation notice and read it to the end. Forty-two pages. Grounds for confirmation, the final destination-site list, the implementation schedule. Items 3, 7, and 12 were included in the confirmed list too. With a footnote attached.

Items 3, 7, and 12 are confirmed as the best available destination sites as of the implementation date. Detailed improvements to be carried out sequentially following implementation.

I read that sentence three times. Three readings, and the meaning stayed the same. The three sites that had not been confirmed had simply been confirmed, under the name "best available destination site," for the very reason that they hadn't been confirmed. It wasn't that the waiting had ended. The waiting itself had ended.

At the very bottom of the last page there was, as in every document, a signature box. Empty. Looking at it, I wasn't surprised this time either. I already knew this wasn't a place that called for surprise.

The confirmed list had, listed one by one, the destination for all eleven hundred forty people. I ran my finger down the lines for the four hundred ten and counted again. The ones assigned to items 3, 7, and 12. Age range, household size, and beside those, one newly added column. Confirmed as of implementation date. The same typeface, the same size, as every other column. It wasn't that this line of four hundred ten was printed any differently. All of it sat together, in the same table, in the same way.

Looking at that table, I felt, for the first time, just how quietly a thing like this could pass by. No large print, no emphasis, no warning notice. Just one number in a table changing from "temporary" to "confirmed."

I took the confirmation notice and went to find Tyche. She already knew.

"…Hora, you heard the confirmation news."

"…Yes. I just finished reading the full notice."

"…How was it?"

"…Items 3, 7, and 12 got confirmed as 'best available destination sites.' It's not that they weren't confirmed because they weren't confirmed — the unconfirmed state itself simply got confirmed, exactly as it stood."

Tyche was silent for a moment, hearing that.

"…So it is. Even hearing it now, it sounds strange to me too."

"…You find it strange too, Tyche?"

"…Yes. But by the regulation, there's no problem. 'Best available destination site' is a phrase that's in the regulation. Used when there's nothing better available."

"…What is it that forced them to use that phrase?"

"…The implementation date. Because it wasn't pushed back."

"…Who didn't push that date back?"

Tyche thought for a while longer before answering this question.

"…No one pushed it back. There was no new reason to push it back, so it went ahead as originally set. To push it back, someone would have had to raise it — 'it needs to be pushed back' — and that never happened."

"…What if I'd kept filing objections?"

"…Then a reason to delay would have kept coming up. But you didn't file any objections in those ten days, Hora."

I couldn't say anything back to that. Tyche hadn't said it to blame me. She was simply stating a fact. Knowing that fact, I still felt uneasy.

I passed the water channel on my way home. The water was still flowing. I stood beside it a moment and wondered whether this channel, too, could someday be confirmed as some "best available" thing. Since it wasn't on any agenda yet, it seemed unlikely to ever be confirmed. Only once an item comes up as an agenda can it be confirmed at all, and only once confirmed can a way to undo it exist — I thought about that in reverse, for the first time, that day.

I told my aunt about the day. She listened for a long while and asked,

"…So what happens now?"

"…It's confirmed, so it'll go ahead on the implementation date."

"…Isn't there a way to undo it?"

I couldn't answer that question right away.

"…I'll have to look, to know."

"…Will looking turn one up?"

"…I don't know. But nothing's confirmed that there isn't one, either."

My aunt didn't say anything back to that. After a moment she asked again.

"…What was the Secretary's face like, when he said that?"

"…Same as always. Not sorrowful, not cold. Just a face stating a fact."

"…That's stranger, still."

"…I think so too."

My aunt got up to reheat the soup and said,

"…Hora. Those people — the four hundred ten. Do they know about this?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. Over those ten days I'd gone around meeting people, but I hadn't met a single one of the people actually grouped into the Seventh Classification. I only knew them by number and category. I'd never once seen a single face.

"…I don't know. The notice must have gone out to them too."

"…If it went out, what do you think they're doing right now?"

I never managed to answer that question. That night I pulled out the confirmation notice again and went over it once more, front to back. There was no mention anywhere of a procedure for undoing it. Whether the absence meant it was forbidden, or simply meant it wasn't written, I still couldn't tell the difference, even then.

That distinction is what I set out to find, starting the next day.

I hesitated a long while writing this chapter. It would have been so much easier to write, "I was late, and that's why it turned out this way." That would at least give the story a cause. A story where things would have turned out differently if I'd only been a little faster. But the Secretary didn't allow me that story. There's no one in this procedure that it waits for, he told me, plainly. At first I felt comforted hearing that. Thinking it over again, I understood it was a far more frightening thing to hear. If I had been the cause, at least next time I could have done things differently. But if there is no cause, then next time, too, I could miss it the exact same way.

That night, before I fell asleep, I pulled out the confirmation notice one more time. A forty-two-page document. I confirmed once more that nowhere in it was there any sentence saying "I was late" or "you were late." The concept of lateness itself didn't exist anywhere inside this document. No waiting, and no lateness. There was only the arrival of a set date, and the fact that on that day, the matter was processed.

(Continued in Chapter 23.)

Chapter 23. Undoing It

_Previously_ — The Seventh Classification matter was confirmed. It wasn't that Hora had been late. The Secretary had said, "There's no one in this procedure that it waits for." Hora could find no mention anywhere in the confirmation notice of a procedure for undoing it. Whether that meant it was forbidden, or simply that no such thing existed, she still couldn't tell.

The next day I went to the archive.

What I was looking for was simple: a procedure for undoing a confirmed deliberation. Cancellation, withdrawal, annulment — I didn't care what it was called. I already knew that every procedure in this city had a name. The objection-filing procedure had a name, the confirmation procedure had a name. Surely a procedure for undoing something had a name too, somewhere.

The archivist pulled out the index for the regulation book. A thick bundle of paper. Procedure names were listed in alphabetical order.

I went through from the beginning. Decision, review, dismissal, rejection. There. I went to "withdrawal." Not there. I went to "cancellation." Not there. I went to "annulment." Not there.

I went through the entire index again, start to finish. Reception, distribution, convening, deliberation, confirmation, objection, re-review, dismissal, notice, implementation, closure. All of it was there. Only the procedure for undoing something already confirmed was missing.

Just in case, I looked up similar words too. Correction, amendment, supplement. These three existed. Hopeful, I read the correction procedure first — and reading it, this turned out to be a procedure for fixing typos in a confirmed document, or simple numerical errors. That was different from undoing the decision itself. At the very bottom of the correction clause I found this sentence: This procedure does not alter the substantive content of the decision. Reading that sentence, I let out a small laugh. This city had even written down, ahead of time, the words "this is not undoing."

I felt a rush of excitement in that moment, the excitement of having discovered something. I want to set that excitement down honestly. In that moment I believed this was hidden evidence. That someone had deliberately left this procedure out. That belief collapsed completely by that afternoon.

I traced the index with my finger and counted it over several times more. There were eighty-one procedure names in total. Eighty of the eighty-one were things I'd already heard of, in this city, by now. Only the one — the undoing procedure — had never existed from the very start. I couldn't find any difference at all between those eighty and this one missing thing. The empty space wasn't marked or highlighted in any special way. It was simply, quietly, absent from where it should have been.

I asked the archivist.

"…Is there no procedure to undo a confirmed deliberation?"

The archivist checked the index again and shook her head.

"…Not in the index, no. But not being in the index doesn't mean it's not in the regulation. The index is built around the things that get used often."

"…So do I have to go through the full text of the regulation?"

"…The original text is right here. Would you like to look at the whole confirmation chapter?"

I took that chapter and read it start to finish. The requirements for confirmation, the effect of confirmation, the post-confirmation implementation procedure. Twenty-two clauses in detail. I read all twenty-two. Not a single one addressed undoing.

At first I thought this was some strange coincidence. It wasn't that exactly one of twenty-two clauses was missing. The concept of undoing itself did not exist anywhere within this chapter. It was as though this chapter had been written by someone who'd never imagined that such a thing could happen at all.

I combed through the entire regulation again. This time not just the confirmation chapter — the general provisions, objections, retrial, closure, every chapter. I hoped to find, somewhere, a sentence like "a confirmed matter may, in special circumstances, be reviewed again."

In the last clause of the general provisions, I found this sentence.

Matters not specified in this regulation shall not be processed without a separate procedure.

I read that sentence several times. What isn't specified isn't processed. The undoing procedure wasn't specified, so it wasn't processed either.

"…Is this saying undoing is forbidden?"

The archivist looked the clause over with me and tilted her head.

"…It doesn't say forbidden. It just says it isn't specified."

"…Is that different?"

"…It's different. Forbidden means don't do it. Not specified means... that the procedure itself simply doesn't exist."

At first I didn't quite understand that difference.

I asked the archivist one more thing.

"…Has anyone ever asked about this before?"

She thought for a moment before answering.

"…Let me think. I've worked here six years, and I don't think there's been anything like this in that time. If I check the index request records I could know for sure, but that would take some time to dig up."

"…Could I ask you to?"

The archivist happily brought out an old request-record binder. I spent the whole afternoon going through it. I looked for anyone who had made an index request using words like "withdrawal," "cancellation," "undoing." I went through several years' worth and found no such request. Most requests were practical matters — "inquiry on confirmed implementation date," "list of relocation destination sites," things like that.

Sitting in front of that regulation book that afternoon, I began to truly feel it might be possible that I was really the first person to ever ask a question like this. Being first sounds like it ought to be something remarkable, but in that moment I felt nothing of the sort. It was frightening, if anything. That I was now asking a question no one had ever asked — it felt like walking, alone, down some wrong road.

That afternoon I went back to find the Secretary.

"…There's no procedure in the regulation for undoing a confirmed deliberation. Is this forbidden?"

"…It isn't forbidden."

"…But there's no procedure."

"…Right, there isn't."

"…If it's not forbidden, why isn't there one?"

The Secretary thought for a moment before answering.

"…To forbid something, someone first has to propose a reason to forbid it. That's how every regulation in this city gets made. Someone proposes that it's needed, it gets reviewed, and through that review it becomes regulation. The withdrawal procedure isn't missing because someone forbade it. It's missing because no one has ever proposed it, up to now."

"…Not even once?"

"…Not that I know of."

"…So does that mean no one in this city's whole history has ever thought a deliberation went wrong?"

"…That's probably not true. There have been a hundred twelve objections filed. Plenty of people thought something was wrong. It's just that none of them ever carried that thought all the way to the proposal — 'there should be a way to undo something even after confirmation.' Most stopped at the objection stage, or where that didn't work, simply accepted it."
