"…Why do they accept it?"

"…Because the procedure had no flaw. In front of a flawless procedure, proposing a new procedure is a much harder thing to do than finding a flaw."

I stood a long while in front of that answer.

I want to set down this moment as precisely as I can, in this record. The withdrawal clause's absence wasn't a conspiracy. No one had hidden it. It was only that no one had ever said it was needed, so a procedure like that had never been born. Not a forbidden door — a door never built.

"…Then if I propose it now?"

"…You can propose it. But that's a procedure for making a new regulation, so it takes a long time. It would be hard for it to apply directly to this matter. The principle is that a regulation applies from the time it's made onward, to matters that come after."

"…So this matter can never be undone?"

"…There is, at present, no procedure to undo this matter."

I hung a small hope on that phrase, "at present." It sounded to me as though it meant — none at present, but perhaps someday.

"…Then is there another path I could use for this matter?"

The Secretary answered this question a little differently.

"…There's no path to undo it. But there is a path to open something new."

"…What's that?"

"…I'll tell you that another time. Today's purpose was confirming the regulation."

I found that strange. Why not tell me now, I wondered. But I didn't press further and went home for the day.

Leaving the reception desk, I ran into Tyche. I told her what I'd found out that day, exactly as it was.

"…There's no withdrawal procedure. Not forbidden, either — it just doesn't exist."

Tyche looked a little surprised to hear it.

"…I didn't know that either. Really, none?"

"…Right. I searched the whole regulation and found nothing."

"…That's strange. I always assumed it must exist somewhere."

I could tell Tyche really hadn't known. There wasn't the faintest trace of concealment on her face. Tyche knew this city's structure better than anyone, and yet this empty space was a fresh discovery to her too.

"…Why do you think it doesn't exist, Tyche?"

Tyche thought for a moment and answered,

"…Probably because it was never needed. I've rarely felt like a deliberation went wrong, in this city. The process has no flaws. But a flawless process doesn't always mean a good outcome — like this matter. No one ever built the procedure to fill that gap."

"…Did no one build it because they didn't know it was needed, or because they couldn't build it even knowing it was needed?"

"…I don't know. But either way, the result is the same, isn't it. That it doesn't exist, right now."

I couldn't argue with that. She was right.

I suddenly thought of the objection-status bulletin board. A hundred twelve filed, three changed. I asked Tyche about those three.

"…Those three that got changed through objection before — were those changed after confirmation too?"

Tyche thought for a moment and said she'd look it up for me. I went back to the archive that afternoon and looked up the three cases myself.

All three had happened before confirmation. Cases where, right after the district's decision came out — before the notice had even gone out — an objection had been accepted. I overlaid the dates of these three cases against the stage table in the confirmation regulation. Before confirmation, there had clearly been a door through which objections could pass. Through that door, three times, something had changed. But that door closed the moment confirmation happened. Beyond that, there was no door at all.

I want to set down this distinction precisely. There was a door called objection. It opened three times. But that door only opened before confirmation. A door that opens after confirmation had never been built, from the start. This is an entirely different matter from a door being locked. In front of a door that doesn't exist, no amount of searching for a key does any good.

On my way home I kept walking with the regulation copy in my hand. I'd read it all the way through, and the clause simply wasn't there. That day, for the first time, I understood exactly that "doesn't exist" could split into two different things. Something absent because it's forbidden, and something absent because no one has ever made it. The first was a wall. A wall you're not supposed to cross. The second wasn't a wall at all. It was simply ground no one had ever walked yet.

And yet the two came out to exactly the same thing. Wall or unwalked ground, I was standing in front of it, equally stuck, either way. There was only one difference. A wall, I couldn't get past. Unwalked ground — I might be the one to walk it first.

That night I wrote this distinction out on paper several times over. Forbidden — someone decided it must not be. Absent — no one ever decided it could or couldn't. I set these two words side by side and stared at them for a long while. It occurred to me, that night, for the first time, that the scarier thing in this city might not be the first one, but the second. In front of a forbidden door, at least you can resent someone. In front of a door that doesn't exist, you have no one to resent — you just have to stand there.

I want to set down, too, as I put this regulation book back in the drawer, that this isn't this city's malice. I wasn't digging through these clauses that day to hate anyone. I was just looking for a way through. And I confirmed that the way wasn't there. Confirming that it wasn't there was, in its own way, a result too.

I told my aunt about it, and she thought for a long while before saying,

"…So there was never a 'don't,' and never a 'do,' either."

"…Right. No one's ever brought it up, apparently."

"…Strange. A city this big, this old, and no one's ever once thought of it?"

"…That's what the Secretary says, anyway."

My aunt looked at me for a moment and said,

"…Hora, then maybe you'll be the first."

I couldn't say anything to that.

"…What's good about being first?"

"…I don't know. I don't even know if it's good or bad."

My aunt laughed at that. It was a slightly wistful laugh.

"…Your mother was the same. She never knew whether going all the way through with something was good or bad, and she went all the way through with it anyway."

I looked up at that. It had been a long time since my aunt had brought up my mother.

"…What did she go all the way through with?"

"…Oh, all kinds of things. I wasn't trying to bring it up right now — just seeing your face reminded me."

My aunt said no more and got up. That night I thought again of the last line in the twelve pages my mother left behind. I only hope you'll be someone who reads to the end. Reading to the end, and going all the way through — that night, they felt like the same words to me.

I kept thinking, until I fell asleep, about what it meant to be first. I was curious, too, about the "new path" the Secretary had mentioned. I decided to go find him again the next day.

(Continued in Chapter 24.)

Chapter 24. A New Deliberation

_Previously_ — There was no procedure to undo a confirmed deliberation. Not forbidden — simply absent. No one had ever proposed that such a procedure was needed. The Secretary said there was no path to undo it, but that "there is a path to open something new." Hora went home without learning what that meant.

The next morning I went to the reception desk the moment it opened.

The Secretary was already at his seat. Seeing me, he took out a document folder as though he'd been waiting to pick up where we'd left off the day before.

"…What you said yesterday. What's the path to open something new?"

"…A new deliberation."

At first I didn't understand what that meant.

"…A new deliberation? So you deliberate the same matter again?"

"…Not reopening the same matter — opening it as a new matter. In connection with the Seventh Classification relocation case, you'd bring forward a new agenda item requesting that the confirmed portion be reviewed again."

"…How is that different from undoing it?"

"…The result can end up similar. Once the new deliberation opens, part of the existing confirmed content could change, through the new deliberation's result. But procedurally, it's completely different. Undoing means erasing the existing decision. A new deliberation adds one more decision on top of it. The existing decision stays exactly as it was, and a new decision gets stacked on top."

I tried hard to understand that distinction.

"…So the original decision doesn't disappear?"

"…Right. In the record, both the original decision and the result of the new deliberation stay. Both remain. It's only that in implementation, the later decision takes precedence."

"…Then in practice that's the same as undoing it, isn't it."

"…In terms of the implementation result, it can look similar. But the record is different. This city erases nothing. A new deliberation keeps to that same principle too."

Listening to this explanation, I thought this was a solution very much in this city's own character. It doesn't erase a wrong. Instead, it stacks a new decision on top of it. The wrong stays recorded, exactly as it happened, and only its effect gets covered over by the next decision. I felt this was an honest way of doing things. And at the same time, I felt it was a frightening way of doing things too.

"…Has there been a new deliberation like this before?"

"…There has. I've convened several myself. But usually they're small in scale. Not a matter that questions the direction of a decision itself, the way this would — that's uncommon. Not like a document correction."

"…What about at this scale?"

The Secretary thought for a moment.

"…At this scale — not that I know of."

"…Not even once?"

"…Not since I've been convening, at least. I don't know the full record before that."

I heard that and understood this might become another "first." Just as no one had ever asked about a procedure for undoing something after confirmation, no one had ever opened a new deliberation on this scale either.

"…So is it hard to open a new deliberation?"

"…The procedure itself isn't hard. You fill out the agenda-filing form and submit it."

"…So I could do it right now?"

"…Yes. But one thing is needed."

The Secretary took a form out of a drawer and set it on the desk. I looked at the paper. It looked like the other forms. There were fields for the agenda's name, the related deliberation number, the grounds for bringing it forward. And at the very bottom.

Proposer's full name.

I looked at that field. It was different from the signature boxes I'd always seen on every other form. A signature box was a field you didn't have to fill. This one looked like a field that had to be filled.

"…Do I have to fill this in?"

"…Yes. A new deliberation can't be brought forward without the proposer's name."

"…Why? Every other procedure works without a name. Receiving, confirming, convening — all of them just use department and position."

"…A new deliberation is different. It's about reopening an existing decision. Someone has to step forward and open it. It's not a department opening it. A person opens it."

"…Why does it have to be a person? Can't a department name do it?"

"…A department knows its own responsibility. But proposing to open a new deliberation isn't within any department's responsibility. You can't file something outside a department's responsibility under a department's name. That's why a person's name is needed."

I went blank for a moment, hearing that. Everything I'd heard in this city, from the very start, had been about structures designed so that no one ever judged alone. And now this single form was pointing in exactly the opposite direction. Here, someone had to step forward alone.

"…Isn't that strange? This city is a place built so that one person doesn't judge alone. But a person's name is needed only when opening a new deliberation."

The Secretary nodded at that.

"…I think that's strange too. But as far as I understand it, up to confirmation, things move split among many people. But a proposal to reopen something confirmed can't be split. It doesn't work if several people each say a little of 'let's reopen it.' Someone has to say that whole sentence, entirely themselves, before the door opens."

"…Why can't it be split?"

"…If it's split, it isn't a new deliberation anymore — it's just an objection. Objections were already used up before confirmation. Saying you want to reopen something after confirmation is a much heavier statement than an objection. This city, it seems, hasn't yet found a way to share that weight."

I heard that answer and my eyes caught, again, on the word "yet." The Secretary kept using that word. Not yet, haven't found yet. I couldn't tell whether that word really meant things would be different someday, or whether it was just a word he attached out of habit.

"…So if I write my name in this field, what happens after that?"

"…It gets brought forward. Received at the desk, referred to the district, registered as a new agenda item. From there it's the same procedure as the original deliberation. Materials get gathered, a meeting is held, the Consensus reaches a decision."

"…How would my name be used in that process?"

"…It's listed as the proposer on the first page of the agenda document. And at every meeting, you'll be given a place to explain why you proposed this, Hora. Not just passing it on like the others do — a place where you'll have to step forward yourself and speak."

I heard that and had a faint sense that this would become the first time, in this city, that I placed the word "I" at the front of a document.

"…What happens once I write my name?"

I asked the question without quite knowing myself why I was asking it that way. It just felt like something I had to ask.

At that moment the Secretary's gaze dropped, slightly. His eyes, which had been on me, looked away from me for the first time. It was a very brief moment. Toward the form on the desk, and then toward the window that let in no view of outside. I didn't miss that movement.

I remember this moment exactly. Up until that day, the Secretary had never once looked away from me, whatever I asked. What he didn't know, he said he didn't know and looked at me straight on; what he knew, he showed me all of it, looking straight at me. This time was different. He wasn't trying to hide something. I know that. But he was looking at something. Something I couldn't see.

I only realized afterward that my voice had come out quieter than usual, asking that question. Those few seconds I remember with a strange clarity, even now. The way the corner of the document folder on the desk was slightly bent. The fluorescent light reflected in the window that let in no view of outside. The way the Secretary's hand lightly smoothed the edge of the form. Until just a few days before, I'd taken it for granted, in this room, that whatever I asked, an answer would come back immediately. That taken-for-grantedness broke, for the first time, in this moment.

I want this scene, writing it, not to read as dramatic. He wasn't startled. He wasn't afraid. He simply looked, for one very brief moment, at something, and then looked back at me. But that very brief moment has stayed with me larger than every piece of precision he ever showed me before.

The Secretary stayed that way for a few seconds, then looked at me again.

"…That..."

He started to speak, and stopped. I waited. It took some time before he spoke again.

"…Hora, could we leave it here for today?"

I was caught off guard for a moment. Up to that point, the Secretary had never once put off an answer. What he didn't know, he'd say, "I don't know," at once. This time was different. It wasn't even that he didn't know. It was that he wasn't going to answer.

"…Secretary, why won't you answer this one?"

"…It's not that I won't give you an answer. I have no answer to give right now."

"…Earlier you told me things even when you didn't know them. You said you didn't know, or that they didn't exist."

"…This isn't a question I can answer with 'I don't know' or 'it doesn't exist.'"

I didn't understand what he meant. But I could see, from his face, that pressing further would do no good. He wasn't trying to push me away. Something had genuinely come to an end right there.

"…Then when will you be able to answer?"

"…I don't know. This time, I really don't know."

I felt that this "I don't know" was different from every other "I don't know" I'd heard from him before. In every other moment, even while saying he didn't know, he could explain why he didn't know. This time he didn't even explain why. Or — maybe it wasn't that he couldn't explain, but that he'd decided not to. I couldn't tell the two apart.

This is the scene I would come back to, and look at longest, in this whole book. Even now, writing this record, I still can't fully explain why the Secretary looked away that day. I do come to learn the answer later, but that's much further ahead. In this chapter I'll only set down this much: that he looked away from me for the first time.

I took the form in my hand and left the reception desk.

Proposer's full name. That field was still empty. Walking with it in my hands, I thought about how this field was different from the signature box. A signature box was fine left empty. Leaving it unfilled was this city's habit. This field, left empty, meant nothing even started. A field that was fine empty, and a field that wasn't. That day, for the first time, I learned this city had both kinds of field.

Walking, I ran back through the faces of everyone I'd met so far. The initial receiving officer, the surveying officer, the format reviewer, the support-fund calculation officer, the convening officer, the record keeper. All of them had worked in fields without names. Department and position had been enough. Not one of them had ever, I thought, stood in front of a field like this. I felt, briefly, envious of them. Working in a nameless field carried no weight. What was always heavy was a field that carried a name.

On the way home I passed the water channel. The water was still making its earth-colored sound. I stood beside it and unfolded the form again. Proposer's full name. I thought, for a moment, about writing my own name in that field. I didn't take out my pen.

I stood beside that water channel for a long while. I still didn't know where that water came from or where it went. Only this — that this water administratively did not exist, because it had never come up as an agenda item, and that the form now in my hand needed a name in order to exist at all — carried the same weight for me that evening. To exist, someone has to step forward. If no one steps forward, however much something flows, it might as well not exist.

I told my aunt about the day. She took the form and looked it over for a long while.

"…The Secretary wouldn't tell you what happens once you fill this in?"

"…Right. For the first time, he couldn't answer."

"…First time?"

"…Yeah. Up to now, he's answered everything I asked. Even what he didn't know, he told me why he didn't know. This time even that didn't happen."

My aunt heard that and stayed still for a long while. I saw something on her face that wasn't usual for her. Not a worried face, not a face trying to stop me. It looked closer to a face trying to remember something.

"…Aunt, why the look?"

"…Nothing. I just feel like I've seen a field something like this once before, a long time ago."

"…Where?"

My aunt shook her head.

"…I don't remember well. It was a long time ago. So long I'm not even sure if this is a real memory or not."

I wanted to press her further, but she got up to clear the dishes and the conversation broke off naturally. I kept coming back to her expression, that night, for a long time.

My aunt handed the paper back to me and said,

"…Hora. I hope you don't decide right now what to write in that field."

"…I haven't decided yet either."

My aunt didn't say anything more. That night I put the form away in the drawer. The same drawer that held my mother's twelve pages, the first notice, the confirmation notice. The drawer had grown fairly thick by now. I didn't know, then, how many days or months that field would stay empty — or whether it might be filled sooner than that.

Writing this chapter, I've decided to stop here. What happens once you write your name in it — I didn't know that day. I'd like whoever reads this record to stay here with me, not knowing, too. The answer comes much later. Until then, I still believe this question ought to stay just as it is — an empty field.

At the end of Part One, I saw an empty signature box for the first time. I couldn't explain, then, why that was strange. Now I'm standing in front of another empty field. This one is different from that one. No one has told me it's all right for this field to stay empty.

(Continued in Part Three.)

The Unsigned Book, Part Three

Who Knows

Chapter 25. The One Who Wrote a Name

_Previously_ — There was no withdrawal procedure. No provision
forbidding it, either. There was nothing at all. Only, opening a new
deliberation was possible. It required the proposer's name. "What
happens if I write a name?" I asked, and for the first time, the
Secretary looked away.

Whenever I saw someone look away, I always used to think that person
knew something. After a few months in the Equity, I learned that was
the wrong guess. People looked away not because they knew something,
but more often because they didn't want to be bound by it once they'd
said they knew.

Did the Secretary know something, or did he just not want to be bound?
The next day I went to find him again. I asked the same thing.

"…What happens if I write a name?"

This time the Secretary didn't look away. Instead he said this.

"…That isn't something I can answer. You'd do better to look at past
cases."

"…Where do I look?"

"…It's all in the reading room."

I thought at the time that answer was kindness. Looking back on it
now, it was kindness and it was passing the burden along, at the same
time. I learned in this city that the two aren't always different
things.

So I went to the reading room.

The reading room was larger than the meeting hall. One whole wall was
shelving, though these days you read from a terminal, not paper. The
clerk at the desk had shown me how to search once, and I'd come back a
few times since. That day I had a purpose. To find whoever had written
a name in the signature box.

The method was simple. I typed "signature" into the search box, then
typed "entered" again. I expected almost nothing to come back, and
truly, nothing did. Nothing was itself a result. The screen read
"0 documents matching this condition."

I widened the condition. I entered only "signature box" and dropped
"blank." Then everything came up. Tens of thousands of documents. All
of them blank, of course. Finding the filled ones would take another
method.

I spent the whole afternoon that day changing my search terms. Some
of the people reading this record will find this tedious, and I
understand how they feel. I found it tedious too. But enduring
tedium was almost the only thing I was good at, so I endured it.

I only learned later that the people in this city who actually find
something are rarely the clever ones. They're the ones who can bear
tedium. The reading room clerk was like that. So was Tyche. So was
the person I would meet later. This is a fact that runs through this
whole record, so I set it down here, once.

In the middle of it Tyche passed by and stopped. She saw what I was
doing and sat down beside me.

"…What are you looking for?"

"…Someone who wrote a name in a signature box."

"…Is there such a person?"

"…I don't know yet."

Tyche looked at the screen for a moment and said,

"…It wouldn't be strange if there were. The regulations never barred
it. It's just low-probability. The chance that out of four hundred
people, someone would go and write their own name."

"…If it's low, does that mean never?"

"…No. If it's low, it just means it happens rarely."

That was true. Tyche was always right. I didn't know yet, then, why
that bothered me so much.

About three hours in, I found a different way. Not the signature box
itself, but the footnote that gets attached automatically underneath
it. The reading room clerk told me that in this city's documents, once
a signature box is filled, a small mark gets added next to the
document number.

"…There's a mark like that?"

"…There is, yes. It's used so rarely nobody knows about it."

"…It's used that rarely?"

"…I've worked here eight years, and I've seen exactly one document
with that mark on it."

I searched by that mark. One result came up.

A single name was written under the document number. The date was
eleven years ago. The deliberation district was the Second
Deliberation District, and the matter was a rezoning of a tract on the
southern outskirts of the city. On its face it looked like an
unremarkable matter.

I read the document from the beginning. As always, I read to the end.
The original matter ran forty-eight pages. Half was land registry.
Half was a record of opinions gathered.

The course of the matter wasn't different from any other deliberation.
Intake, review, confirming those involved, gathering opinions,
convening. Close to four hundred people had been involved. But at the
conclusion, the flow broke once. There had been an objection to the
result the Consensus had produced, and whoever filed that objection had
proposed a new deliberation. And in that proposal's signature box, there
was a name.

The name was short. One surname, one given name. I read it several
times. It wasn't a name I recognized. I checked whether anyone by that
name still lived in this city now. There wasn't.

The reason for the objection was written into the document too. The
original matter was to rezone the entire southern tract for warehouse
use. Several old houses stood on that tract, and once it was rezoned
for warehouses, those houses were slated for demolition. The demolition
itself wasn't the problem. The problem was that the matter contained no
procedure at all for where the people living in those houses would
resettle.

This wasn't something anyone had left out out of malice. That matter
dealt only with land use; relocating residents was a separate matter.
Each side had handled exactly what fell to it, correctly. But between
the two matters, nobody stood. The person who filed the objection had
pointed at exactly that gap. "If this matter passes, these houses come
down, and where those people go is in no matter at all."

The Consensus took in that objection. That it was taken in mattered. It
wasn't ignored. It was taken in and reviewed, and on review, judged
valid. But the procedure for accepting an objection and the procedure
for opening a new deliberation were different things. An objection
could be reviewed and end there. Only a new deliberation actually
changed anything. And the box for opening a new deliberation required a
name.

Absent doesn't mean dead. I kept confusing the two, back then. The
person could have moved. Could have changed their name. Could simply
have dropped off this city's roster. Absent can always mean several
things, and I kept leaning toward the heaviest one.

The new deliberation really was opened. The record said so. The
procedure again passed through nearly four hundred people, and a
conclusion came out. Part of the original matter was reversed. Only
part. Half of the southern tract stayed as it was; the other half was
confirmed for the new use.

That person's name was on the new deliberation's document too. In the
proposer's field.

What came after was the problem.

I looked for other documents carrying that name afterward. There were
some. Quite a few. That person had been called in on various matters
after that — as a witness, a confirmer, someone involved. The first
few months it was two or three times a month. The half year after
that, five or six times a month. Every document had a field confirming
that person had appeared, and every one of those fields was filled in.

And then, at some point between the spring and summer eleven years
ago, the documents carrying that name grew sparser, and then stopped
altogether.

I checked the date on the last document twice. Eleven years ago, early
summer. It was an ordinary confirmation request. There was a signature
showing that person had come. The next convening date was written in
too. Two weeks later.

I found that next convening's document. It was open. That person's
name wasn't on the list of attendees. It wasn't marked as an absence,
either. The field simply wasn't on the list at all.

I widened the search. By name, by document number, by the district
that person had likely lived in. Nothing came up. I checked the
document's revision history too, looking for some trace of deletion.
The Equity's documents keep their full revision history. There was no
trace of deletion. No mark of a correction, no mark of an erasure.

I thought hard, more than once, about rewriting this part. There was
a temptation to write that I'd found something hidden. It would have
made this story more interesting. But that isn't true. I found
nothing. There was nothing to find.

The record wasn't cut off. It simply had no more. I didn't understand
that difference well then. I understand it now. Cut off means someone
severed it. No more means it simply went that far and no further.

I went to that southern tract too. It was an hour's walk. The half now
confirmed for the new use had warehouse buildings standing on it. The
half left as it was still had a few old houses standing, just as
before. It looked like people lived there. Laundry hung out to dry.

Between the fact that the houses remained and the fact that person's
record didn't, I couldn't find any connection at all. The houses had
remained. That meant that person had achieved something. And yet the
one who had achieved it had vanished. Setting those two facts side by
side didn't settle my mind — it unsettled it. That there was no one
behind something that had gone right felt, somehow, stranger than
there being no one behind something that had gone wrong.

I can't name this feeling exactly. It wasn't relief. Relief should
feel like something had ended, and I felt nothing like an ending. If
anything, it felt like something had begun. I didn't know then what.

I asked the reading room clerk. Do you know this name, I said.

The clerk looked at the screen and shook her head.

"…First time I've seen it."

"…Would you know what became of this person?"

"…I wasn't here then."

"…Who would know?"

The clerk thought for a moment and said,

"…Well. I've heard there's still one person these days who writes a
name. Not from this city — someone who comes and goes."

I didn't let that pass. I hadn't planned to, that day, but I asked
without letting it go.

"…What's this person's name?"

The clerk told me a name.

"…Does this person still come here these days?"

"…I see them once or twice a month. I hear they do some kind of work
finding people, around here."

"…Finding people?"

"…I don't know the details. Moving from city to city, and if someone
asks them to find somebody, they find them. I've only heard it as a
rumor myself."

I had no real thought about that answer, hearing it. I supposed that
someone whose work was finding people might reasonably write their own
name. No one would ask someone to find a person for them without
knowing who was asking.

That was a naive thought. That night I didn't even know it was naive.
I had simply set one fact next to another — that the record of the
person who'd written a name eleven years ago had stopped, and that
there was, now, one person who wrote a name. There might have been no
connection at all between the two. I knew that even then.

And still I set the two side by side. It's hard to explain why. Maybe
after three hours alone in the reading room changing search terms, you
start wanting to connect at least one thing to another. I know that
isn't an honest way to think. I'm setting down here, anyway, that I
did it.

On my way home I passed the water channel. The western ditch was still
making its sound. I stood beside it for a moment and thought about
that person from eleven years ago.

A name had been written. A new deliberation had opened. Part of it had
been reversed. And then, called in again and again, until one summer
day the record simply stopped.

I told my aunt about this that night. She listened a long while and
said, "…So nobody knows what became of that person?" I said that was
right. My aunt said, "…Then it's unknown, Hora. Don't decide something
bad happened."

My aunt was right. I had already decided, by then. That something bad
had happened. There was no basis for it. Knowing full well that
absence itself can't be evidence, I still read it that way.

Writing this book, I look back on this passage more than once. This
was probably the first time I went against Tyche's logic. That day
Tyche had told me about probability, and I thought I'd found a hole
that probability couldn't explain. Looking at it now, that too was a
mistake. It wasn't a hole. It was simply an ending.

Whether it was my nature, or my mother, or having stared too long at
that empty signature box, I still don't know for certain. But I know
one thing. Tyche spoke of probability that day, and I couldn't refute
it. And still I kept looking for that document. Being unable to refute
something didn't make me stop.

That name — the one the clerk had told me, the person who still wrote
a name these days — I copied onto paper. I put it back in the drawer
where my mother's papers were kept.

Before I closed the drawer, I copied that other name, the one from
eleven years ago, onto the same paper. Setting the two names side by
side, there was nothing alike about them at all. Different surnames,
different numbers of syllables. And still I looked at those two lines
for a long while.

Looking back now, I think I had already decided something, that day.
I still didn't know what had become of the person from eleven years
ago. But I couldn't leave that not-knowing as simply not-knowing. I
needed to find one more name and set it up alongside, to feel settled.
What would Tyche have called this? Probably "a meaningless
connection." If Tyche had said that, I wouldn't have been able to
refute her. I had no basis to.

And still I put that paper in the drawer. That there are things you
do without a basis — I learned that for the first time, then.

I slept well that night. I don't know why. It wasn't because I'd found
an answer. Not one answer had come out of it. It was only that what I
would do the next day was decided, and that, I think, was what settled
me. In this city there are nights when just knowing what tomorrow holds
is enough to set your mind at ease.

The next day, I went looking for that person.

(Continued in Chapter 26.)

Chapter 26. Temel

_Previously_ — Eleven years ago, there was a person who wrote a name.
That person opened a new deliberation, reversed part of a matter, and
was afterward called in again and again, until one summer day the
record stopped. I heard there was still, these days, a person who
wrote a name, and I copied that name onto paper.

I didn't know how to go about finding a person. There was almost never
a reason to look for someone in this city. Whoever you needed was
always on the roster, and the roster had an address. You went to the
address and the person was there. That was all there was to it.

But the person the clerk had told me about wasn't on the roster. Of
course not, since I'd been told they weren't from this city. I went to
the front desk and asked about visitor records.

"…We don't keep separate visitor records. Only residents go on the
roster."

"…Then how do I find someone who comes and goes?"

"…Well. There's no way but to ask someone who's seen them."

Hearing that answer, I laughed a little. For all the Consensus's
transparency, this city had no way to find a single person. I only
understood later. The Consensus was transparent about what came before
a deliberation. A person who'd never come before one wasn't, to begin
with, something the Consensus had any business with.

It was exactly like the water channel. If it had never been brought
up, it was as good as not existing.

The person at the desk must have felt a little sorry for me, because
she added one more thing.

"…Oh — there is someone who's been coming and going lately. Every time
they file an application they always write their name, so everyone
remembers them."

"…Do they have a lot of applications to file?"

"…I hear they do work finding people. They apply for the reading room
often too."

I asked right then.

"…Where would I go to meet them?"

"…Hmm. Somebody said they'd seen them around the market."

I wondered whether that much could really lead me to a person. But
this city ran that way. The records were flawless, and whatever wasn't
in the records had to be found by rumor. Strangely enough, that felt
like the most human part of this city.

For three days I went from district to district asking people. I must
have asked around thirty. Most said they didn't know. Two said "I
think I've seen them." One person told me, "I think they're around the
north market these days."

The north market was the loudest place in the Equity. Everyone said
that even at its loudest, it would sound quiet next to another city,
but to me it was the loud one.

That person was standing at the far end of the market, in front of the
public notice board. City notices were posted there, and beside them
was one more small sheet, posted by no one I could name. It began,
"Looking for a person."

They were neither tall nor short. Looked to be in their mid-thirties.
Their clothes were worn but clean. Their face carried no particular
expression. I don't mean that unkindly. It was simply true. Not angry,
not glad to see me — they just stood there.

They had a bag slung over one shoulder, with a bundle of paper
sticking out the side. It looked folded and refolded many times, as
though it had been carried a long while. Their shoes were worn too.
Not shoes from this city. This city's shoes all came from the same
place, and those were a different shape.

Looking back now, I remember those shoes for a surprisingly long
time. That detail stayed with me, oddly. I think I recognized a
stranger from the shoes before I did from the face.

I went up and asked.

"…Excuse me — are you the one who writes a name?"

The person looked at me. Their face went thoughtful for a moment.

"…Write what?"

"…On a document. In the signature box."

"…Ah, I do. Why?"

Their tone was flat. Not wary of me, not curious about me either. Just
a tone confirming a fact.

"…What's your name?"

The person said a name. It was the name the clerk had told me.

"…Temel."

I stood there a moment not knowing what to ask. Temel didn't press me
either. He looked back at the notice board.

"…Did you put this up?"

"…Yes. I do work finding people."

"…What kind of people?"

"…People I'm asked about. When a family's been scattered, or word's
been lost, and someone asks me to find that person, I go find them."

"…Are you from this city?"

"…No. I go from place to place. This is my — I don't know how many
times already, coming through here."

The way he spoke was plain. It didn't sound like he was hiding
anything, or exaggerating anything. I found that oddly unfamiliar.
Everyone in the Equity was used to speaking with themselves one step
removed — "It was passed on," "It was put in order" — but this man
just said "I go find them." There was a subject.

I found that remarkable, then. Looking back on it now, it wasn't
remarkable at all. He just wasn't from this city. He simply wasn't
used to this city's grammar. And still, in that moment, it felt like
meeting someone entirely different.

I looked at the sheet on the board again. "Looking for a person. If
you know their name, their last known whereabouts, or any
identifying feature, please contact the address below." The name
written under it wasn't a name from this city.

"…Have you found this person?"

"…Not yet. I've been here three days now."

"…What happens if you don't find them?"

"…I go to the next city. Look there too."

"…What about the person here, then? The one you didn't find?"

Temel looked at the board once and said,

"…I leave the sheet. Someone might see it later and get in touch."

"…Does that happen?"

"…Sometimes. Very rarely."

He said "very rarely" as though it were nothing at all. No trace of
disappointment, no trace of giving up. He just seemed like someone
who'd lived by that kind of probability.

"…So do you file an application to find someone in this city too?"

"…Yes. Filed one yesterday."

"…Did you write your name in the signature box there?"

Temel gave me a slightly puzzled look.

"…It doesn't get accepted unless you do."

"…No, I mean — in this city, everyone leaves that box blank."

"…Blank? Why?"

I probably made just as puzzled a face, that time.

"…I don't know. Nobody writes it."

Temel looked at the board for a moment and said,

"…If there's a box to write in, don't you write in it?"

That was so simple, I couldn't answer for a moment.

Writing this record, I've read that line over many times. "If there's
a box to write in, don't you write in it?" That wasn't a question. He
didn't ask it because he was genuinely curious — it was something that
came out of him because to him, it was simply obvious. It wasn't
obvious to me. That difference in what was obvious was the difference
between this city and his.

I didn't know how to answer, so I asked something else. I felt like I
should ask more, but I couldn't get straight what to ask.

"…What happens if you write your name on an application? Some people
here are afraid of it."

Temel shrugged a little.

"…You get called in, sure. Every matter with that name on it, they
call you."

"…Are you all right with that?"

"…Not so much all right as used to it. I've been through it plenty of
times, in plenty of cities."

His face stayed the same the whole time he said it. Not resentful, not
proud. Just a face stating a fact.

"…So how many times have you already been called in here?"

"…This time? Twice. I only filed a day ago and they've already called
me in twice."

"…Twice already? For what?"

"…Once to confirm the application's contents. Once for another
department to confirm the same thing. They ask the same thing twice,
but I've come to expect it."

"…You've come to expect it?"

"…At first it annoyed me a bit. But being annoyed doesn't stop them
from calling either."

There was no resignation in that, and no patience — just a plain
familiarity. Like a rhythm worn into the body from long practice. That
frightened me. More than the irritation or the anger would have, that
flatness frightened me. I wondered how many times something had to
happen to a person before they got like that.

I thought about bringing up the name from eleven years ago, then
didn't. I still had nothing certain. Instead I asked something else.

"…Do you know why people in this city don't write their names?"

"…No idea. I'm not from here."

"…What about other cities, then? Do other cities not write names
either?"

"…Depends on the city. A place where it's completely blank, like
here — this is the first I've seen."

"…Oh — first time?"

"…Yeah. Usually a few people write it. Not everyone, but some. Here
it's really none at all, far as I've checked."

That surprised me a little. It meant this city wasn't just especially
blank — other cities still had a few people who wrote. So was this
city unusually far gone, or was it simply a different way of doing
things? I didn't get the answer that day.

"…So why do you write it, then? You don't have to here."

Temel looked at me for a moment. There was nothing particular in that
look. He was simply looking at me.

"…Let's talk about that another time. I need to go find someone,
right now."

Then he tore a sheet from the board, folded it, and tucked it away,
and walked off toward the market. I watched his back for a long while.

A woman running a stall nearby spoke to me.

"…Do you know that man?"

"…Just met him today."

"…He's an odd one. Only been here a few days and I couldn't tell you
how many times he's already been called into the office. But he never
once gets angry about it."

"…Guess he's just made that way."

"…Right? If it were me, I'd have lost my temper a long time ago."

The woman went off then to see to a customer. I stood there a while
longer. The market noise grew loud again around me — vegetables being
sold, prices being haggled over, a child crying. It was a loud place,
for this city.

The people of this city didn't look at Temel strangely. They found
him curious, but not something to be wary of. I found that a little
strange myself. Isn't the usual thing, when you see someone doing
something different from everyone else, to grow wary first? But here
people were simply curious. They didn't feel him as a threat. Probably
because writing a name wasn't treated as anything large enough in this
city to be a threat. It wasn't that it wasn't frightening because it
wasn't a big thing — it was that no one had ever done it, so no one
knew how big a thing it was.

On the way home I turned over what had happened that day. Nothing
unusual had happened. I'd met one person, and exchanged a few words.
That was all.

And still I slept badly that night. It's hard to say exactly why.
Probably because he was too ordinary. I think I'd been assuming,
without meaning to, that someone who wrote a name would look different
somehow. A different look in the eyes, maybe, or a heavier way of
speaking. Temel had none of that. He was simply a tired man. A little
worn, a little flat, with only the plainness particular to someone
who'd done this work of finding people for a long time.

I thought something else, that night. If Temel wasn't writing a name
because he was something special, that meant I could write one too.
The moment that thought came, my body went a little stiff. I hurried
to put the thought away. I told myself it was still too soon. At the
time, I really believed that.

I've felt the temptation, more than once, to make Temel out to be
something special in this record. Someone who knew something, someone
who already had it all figured out. But that isn't the truth. Temel
was, then and now, simply someone who wrote a name. That was all he
was, and that, strangely, was the strangest thing about him.

I'm writing this record much later, after everything has already
passed. So there are moments when the temptation comes to slip in,
as though I knew it at the time, things I didn't actually know then.
That's especially true when it comes to Temel. But I've decided to
resist that temptation, here. The person I was that day didn't know
who Temel was. I stayed not knowing for a long time, even after
meeting him several times more. So it seems more honest to write it
here, too, not knowing.

I told my aunt about that day, and she asked,

"…What does that man do?"

"…Finds people."

"…There's work like that?"

"…Apparently. He's not from here, she said."

My aunt wasn't especially surprised. She just said, "…That's
interesting," and let it go. My aunt was never one to be surprised by
much.

"…But Hora, why did you go to meet him?"

I thought a moment before answering. I wasn't entirely sure myself
what I'd gone hoping to find out.

"…No reason. I just wanted to see what kind of person writes a name."

"…And did you?"

"…I did, but I still don't know. He was just an ordinary person."

"…An ordinary person doing a strange thing, then."

My aunt's words stayed with me the whole night. An ordinary person
doing a strange thing. If it had been a strange person doing a strange
thing, I probably would have thought less about it. Temel had truly
looked ordinary. And yet he was doing, without a second thought,
something no one in this city ever did.

That night I opened the drawer and looked again at the paper with the
two names on it. The person from eleven years ago, and Temel. Their
handwriting was different, their ages were different, the paths that
had brought them here were different. No connection between them had
been confirmed yet. And still I stared at those two names for a long
while.

I would go on to meet Temel several more times after this day. Each
time, I think I expected him to tell me something remarkable. But
Temel never once did that. He was simply a person who wrote his name,
got called in, and went on to the next city. That was all he was, and
it took me a long time to truly see that "all."

The next day, I went back to the north market.

(Continued in Chapter 27.)

Chapter 27. Why Do You Write It

_Previously_ — I met Temel at the north market. He was a man who
moved from city to city doing work finding people, and in this city
too he had written his own name on an application. "If there's a box
to write in, don't you write in it?" That was so simple, I couldn't
answer.

The next day, Temel was in the same spot. In front of the notice
board, in almost the same posture as the day before. The sheets were
one fewer than yesterday. One new sheet had been added too.

I went straight to the market after school that day. I told my aunt I
needed to read more district materials. That wasn't entirely a lie. I
really was going to read something. It's only that it wasn't a
document — it was a person.

A few other people besides Temel were standing at the board. They all
seemed to have come to check their own notices. Nobody paid Temel any
attention.

"…Back again."

"…Yes. There's still something I wanted to ask."

Temel took his hand off the board and looked at me. There was no
annoyance in his face. Just a look that was making time for me.

"…What do you want to know?"

"…What I asked yesterday. Why do you write it? You don't have to."

But first, curious about the missing sheet, I asked that instead.

"…Did you find someone?"

"…No. The time limit passed, so it came down. That's the rule here.
After ten days it has to come off the board."

"…What about that person, then?"

"…I keep looking. Other ways. The board's just one way among
several."

Temel seemed to think a moment, then gave the same short answer as
yesterday.

"…Because there's a box to write in."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes."

I felt that answer wasn't enough. I thought there had to be more.
Some conviction, some principle, some resolve formed out of
something he'd been through. But I saw nothing like that on Temel's
face. It really was a face for whom that was all there was.

"…But nobody writes it. Here."

"…That's exactly what makes it stranger, to me. That there's a box
and nobody uses it."

"…That's strange to you?"

"…Where I'm from, it was. If there was a box, you put something in
it. Right or wrong, good or bad. Leaving it blank meant the box lost
its meaning."

I turned this over for a long time. I'm turning it over even now,
writing this record. Leaving a box blank makes the box lose its
meaning. I thought about the opposite too. Couldn't leaving a box
blank make that box grow heavier instead? That's how it was in this
city. If it had been filled, I never would have known how heavy that
empty signature box could be.

"…What was the place you're from like?"

"…I've lived in a lot of places. Not just one."

"…Where do you live now, then?"

"…It's hard to say I live anywhere in particular. I keep moving."

"…Isn't it lonely?"

Temel didn't answer right away. He looked toward the market for a
moment and said,

"…Sometimes. But there are times I meet the person I was looking for,
and that's what gets me through."

"…Do you meet them often?"

"…No. Not often. But sometimes."

I could only guess at what was folded into that "sometimes." I didn't
ask further. I'd learned in this city that some questions are better
left unasked, out of respect.

"…So what was good about writing your name here?"

Asking this, I expected a good answer. Something along the lines of
having gained something this city couldn't give.

Temel smiled a little at the question. It was an expression I hadn't
seen from him before.

"…Nothing good, particularly."

"…Nothing?"

"…No. All I got was being called in, over and over. But that's
something you brace for going in."

"…Then why do you keep writing it?"

"…Well. Writing it is my work. That's what finding people is. You
have to file an application to find someone, and the application
needs a name."

"…Not that. You're not even from here. Why bother?"

Temel looked at me for a moment. His face searched for an answer.

"…It's just habit, I suppose. I do it wherever I go."

"…Was it always like that?"

"…No. At first I didn't write it either."

That surprised me a little.

"…When did you start, then?"

"…A long time ago. I fell apart once, when I was young."

"…Fell apart?"

"…I misjudged something badly, once. After that there was a time I
lived as if running from it. Then when I came back, I started writing
it."

Temel told this very briefly. His tone made me hesitant to ask more.
I didn't ask more.

"…What do you mean, came back?"

"…Just that — I mean I decided to start again. Whenever I start
something over, I write my name first. That's my way of doing it."

I stopped more than once, writing this passage. Even now I still feel
the urge to press further into what happened to Temel. But I didn't
press him that day, and I can't write more than this in this record
either. Leaving what's unknown as unknown seems like the right way to
treat this person, at least.

Temel looked back at the board, as if changing the subject.

"…So is everything in this city like that — this business with the
signature box, and everything else too?"

"…Like what?"

"…Where there's no one doing anything, but there's still a result."

I thought about that for a moment. The Consensus, the four hundred
people, passing it on and putting it in order and confirming. All of
it fit.

"…Yes. Exactly like that."

"…That's something. I've never seen a city like this."

"…Something, or strange?"

Temel smiled a little.

"…Both."

That smile was the only light moment in the whole conversation that
day. It was only seeing him smile that I truly felt how flatly he'd
been saying such heavy things until then.

I think I was a little disappointed by this answer, at the time. I'd
expected some bigger reason. I'm ashamed of that disappointment now.
I myself read to the end simply out of habit, and yet I wanted a
bigger reason out of someone else.

Just then, a person passed by beside us. He was a man in worn clothes,
walking slowly, headed straight for somewhere. He glanced once at the
notice board and passed on without a word. I looked at him for a
moment, but there was nothing remarkable about him, and I soon turned
back to Temel.

"…So how many applications have your name on them right now?"

"…In this city alone? Three."

"…Three means how many times you get called in?"

"…Two more scheduled by tomorrow."

"…Isn't it hard?"

Temel shrugged slightly.

"…It's hard. But hard and not doing it are two different problems."

That was a short answer, but I held onto it for a long time.

"…Could I write it too, then?"

The question came out of me before I knew it. I surprised even myself,
asking it.

Temel looked straight at me. It was the first time his face had turned
serious.

"…You could. If there's a box."

"…What happens if I write it, though?"

"…You'd end up like me, probably. Called in."

"…Not that. What else changes?"

This time Temel didn't answer right away. He looked at the board for a
moment and said,

"…I don't know that either. What I wrote is something that happened
to me. Not something that's going to happen to you, Hora."

It was the first time he'd said my name. I don't remember when he
learned it. He must have asked at the front desk. That was how this
city worked.

That day, for the first time, I asked someone other than myself what
happens if you write it. And for the first time, I heard the answer,
"I don't know." I thought it was nothing much of an answer at the
time. I would come to learn later just how different a weight this
"I don't know" could carry. That's a story for later.

The sun was starting to lean. It was the hour the market began
winding down. I brought up the signature box a little more before I
left.

"…Why do you think nobody in this city writes it? What do you
think?"

"…I don't really know for sure. But the other places I've seen had
similar reasons, I think."

"…What reasons?"

"…If you write it, it becomes yours. Whether it's good or bad. Leave
it blank, and it's just something that happened — it belongs to no
one."

"…Isn't that comfortable, though? If it belongs to no one, nobody
gets wronged."

"…That's right. That's why everyone does it that way. It isn't a bad
thing. I envy that, myself, sometimes."

"…You envy it?"

"…Yes. Every time, something becomes mine. It would be easier for me
too, if I didn't write it. But I've found I can't."

"…Why can't you?"

Temel was quiet a moment. Then he answered, briefly.

"…I've tried not writing it, once. That was the worst it's ever been."

"…So you never know, every single time?"

"…Right. Every time, I don't know. I don't know each time I write
it."

"…How do you write it if you don't know?"

Temel shrugged again. It felt slightly different this time.

"…Not knowing isn't a reason not to write it."

Hearing that, I couldn't say anything for a moment. The market noise
went on around us. Someone haggling, a cart being pulled. I stood in
that noise turning that one line over and over.

Not knowing isn't a reason not to write it.

I copied that sentence onto paper that evening. I put it in the
drawer with my mother's papers. That drawer now held two names and one
sentence.

I imagine that drawer sometimes. Twelve pages my mother left behind,
and the short notes I'd laid on top of them, one at a time. My mother
could never have imagined other people's names piling up on top of her
own papers. And yet, strangely, I didn't mind that drawer growing
heavier little by little. If anything, it felt like that drawer was
becoming something that no longer belonged only to me.

I didn't open that drawer often. I only opened it when there was
something new to put in. That day was one of those days.

"…Doesn't it get old, being checked on like that every time?"

"…It does. But I can't just not go because I'm tired of it. If I
don't go, the application itself falls behind."

"…What happens if it falls behind?"

"…I find the person I'm looking for that much later. That's the part
I hate most."

"…Will you be here tomorrow too?"

"…Probably. Until the application's been processed."

"…When does that finish?"

"…I don't know. This place has a lot of confirmation steps."

"…So you'll be here a while longer, then."

"…Seems that way. No hurry, though. The person I'm looking for might
be somewhere around here too."

Hearing that, I felt something strange. Temel was taking a loss and
not even calling it a loss. He simply accepted it as something that
took time. I couldn't tell, that day, whether that was resignation or
ease.

I said goodbye and left. Walking away, I looked back once. Temel was
looking at the board again.

On my way home I stopped by the front desk and asked what the matters
were that kept calling Temel in. The clerk looked at the screen and
said,

"…Confirmation, confirmation, confirmation. All three of them."

"…Confirming what?"

"…Whether the application's contents match, whether he's really who
he says he is, that sort of thing."

"…They confirm the same thing every time?"

"…Seems that way. I don't know why, myself."

"…Isn't that strange? Why confirm something that's already been
confirmed?"

"…I don't know. I've thought it was strange myself, but I've never
found a way to stop it. It's a procedure written into the regulations."

"…Why is it written that way in the regulations?"

"…That's beyond me too. I didn't set it."

Hearing that answer, I thought of the other answers I'd heard around
this city for months. It was passed on. It was put in order. It was
convened. The clerk's answer had the same grain to it. No one had set
it, and only the confirming went on.

Leaving that spot, I felt I'd come to know one thing for certain.
Temel was someone who kept writing it, knowing full well it cost him,
and the reason was simple. There was a box. And yet that very
simplicity was what held me the longest.

Hearing this conversation, I could dimly guess at what would happen
the next day. Whether that guess was right, the next chapter will
show.

At home over dinner, I told Tyche briefly what had happened that day.
Tyche didn't eat with us that evening, but when we ran into each other
in front of the district hall the next morning, she brought it up
again.

"…Does he really keep getting called in like that?"

"…Yes. Three matters have his name on them."

"…That tells you why nobody writes it, doesn't it?"

"…I guess so."

"…So why does he keep writing it, then? It costs him."

"…I don't know. He said it was just habit."

Tyche tilted her head.

"…Habit basically means there's no real reason, doesn't it? So he's
just taking a loss over and over, for no real reason."

I couldn't answer that. What Tyche said didn't seem wrong. But
something felt missing. I couldn't explain, then, what it was.

I think I can say a little now. Tyche asked for a reason, and Temel
said there wasn't one. But having no reason and having nothing at all
aren't the same thing. Temel had no reason, but he had a way. The way
of writing it every time. I wonder now whether that way was standing
in for the reason. I hadn't worked it out this far, back then.

(Continued in Chapter 28.)

Chapter 28. The One Who Keeps Being Called In

_Previously_ — Temel wrote his name on every application. "Not
knowing isn't a reason not to write it." He said that name kept
getting him called in for confirmation after confirmation. Tyche
called it "a loss," and I couldn't refute her.

The next day, I went back to the market again after school. I wasn't
going to see Temel. I wanted to see what the confirmations looked
like — the ones he'd said would call him in "two more times."

I asked at the front desk about the schedule, and one happened to be
set for that afternoon. Since I had no district business at that hour,
I asked whether I could tag along. The desk clerk didn't find it odd
and said I could.

"…How long do these usually run, start to finish?"

"…Depends on the matter. Ten minutes if it's short, up to thirty if
it's long."

"…How many can be scheduled in a day?"

"…He's only got one today. But if several departments are involved,
some people have two or three in a day."

"…Does that happen often — someone like that?"

"…Not often. People who write their names are rare to begin with."

Hearing that, I confirmed again just how unfamiliar a sight this was
in this city. It seemed so unfamiliar that no one had ever seriously
thought about what it meant.

"…Is watching one of these normally allowed?"

"…It's not closed. Everything here is open."

That answer sounded obvious to me at the time. Looking back now, all
of this city's dread is contained in that one line. It wasn't closed.
Nothing was hidden. And still it was frightening.

Up until then I'd thought fear came from things that were hidden.
Frightening because you couldn't see it, frightening because it was
done in secret. This city was the reverse. There were frightening
things you could see all of, in full. I saw that with my own eyes for
the first time that day.

The confirmation room was small. One table, three chairs. One official
sat there, and Temel sat across from him. I sat in a chair in the
corner and watched.

Nothing was on the walls. There was one window, but like the meeting
hall's windows, it didn't show the outside. Every building in this
city was built this way. It didn't seem meant to keep the inside from
being seen — it was simply the kind of window this city used. Looking
at that window, I thought there probably wasn't another city with so
many windows you couldn't see out of.

The air was cool. Rooms like this were always a little cool. Whether
that was because no one was meant to stay long, or because there was
simply no heating at all, I didn't know.

The official opened a folder.

"…I'll confirm the application number. Your name is Mr. Temel,
correct?"

"…Yes."

"…And the filing date was four days ago, correct."

"…Yes."

"…And the application's content is a request to confirm a person's
whereabouts, is that right?"

"…Yes, that's right."

The official turned to the next page.

"…This was already confirmed yesterday, but it needs confirming again
today. I apologize."

"…That's fine."

This exchange went on for about ten minutes. The questions were almost
identical to yesterday's. Listening beside them, I thought something
was wrong, but neither the official nor Temel seemed to find it
strange at all.

The official asked for a signature. Temel wrote his name in the field
at the bottom of the confirmation sheet. His hand moved quickly,
practiced. Watching him, I felt again how thoroughly writing a name
had become second nature to this man.

The official stamped the sheet once and put the papers in order.

"…That concludes today's matter. Thank you."

"…Yes, thank you."

They thanked each other. I found that strange to hear. I couldn't tell
what either of them had to be thankful for, and yet both seemed to
mean it sincerely.

Once the confirmation was over, the official said,

"…The next confirmation is set for the day after tomorrow."

"…Again?"

"…This matter goes through three related departments. Each department
has to confirm it once."

"…Can't they all confirm it together, at once?"

The official checked the papers once and answered.

"…There's no procedure for doing it that way."

"…If there isn't one, can't one be made?"

"…That isn't within my authority."

That answer nearly made me laugh. Not because it was funny — because
it was too familiar. I only passed it on. I only put it in order. And
now, that isn't within my authority. It felt like every phrase I'd
heard in months in this city had gathered together, right here.

I couldn't bring myself to hate that official. He wasn't a bad
person. He was even kind. He read the papers carefully, and when he
apologized his face was sincerely sorry. And still, the work he did
was to make the exact same thing get confirmed three times over.

I asked the official something too.

"…Doesn't it wear on you, confirming the same thing every time like
this?"

The official looked a little flustered. It seemed to be the first time
anyone had asked him that.

"…I… I'm only carrying out the procedure I've been assigned."

"…But this is already how many times this person's come here."

"…That isn't something I have visibility into. I only see today's
matter."

"…Only today's?"

"…Yes. There's no one who sees the whole picture. Each matter is
handled separately, by its own department."

This may have been the most important answer in this chapter. That
there is no one who sees the whole. The official only saw today's
matter, and yesterday's official had probably only seen yesterday's.
No one had the whole picture of how many times Temel had been called
in that week. I was the only one who knew. And that was only because
I had followed him around.

Coming out of the confirmation room, I asked Temel,

"…Is that all? Is that really the whole of what it means, being
called in?"

"…Yes. That's all there is to it."

"…It's less than I expected."

Temel smiled a little.

"…That's right. Not much to it. But it keeps happening."

"…How long does it keep happening?"

"…As long as the application's still open. I've got my name on three
matters here, so until all three finish."

"…Do they finish, though?"

"…Usually. But if a new matter comes up, that keeps going too."

"…So it could never end?"

This time Temel didn't answer right away. He walked a moment and said,

"…Could be. I don't know either."

I let that "I don't know" pass without much thought, at the time. I
only realized later how often, and how calmly, this man said "I don't
know." It wasn't evasion. It was said because he truly didn't know.

"…Is it only like this in this city? Aren't other cities not this
bad?"

"…Depends on the city. Some finish with one confirmation. Some go
longer."

"…Which is this one?"

"…The long kind. I think it's probably because of how the departments
are divided. The more it's split up, the more people there are to
confirm it."

"…So what if the departments weren't split up?"

"…Then one department would have to carry the whole thing, and that
would be a problem too, probably. I'm not in a position to say which
way is better."

Temel's pace stayed unhurried even as he said this. No sign of rushing
at all. I found that strangely enviable. I think I'd have been angry
by then, in his place.

"…Doesn't it make you angry?"

"…It did, the first few times. Now I just tend to save the energy I'd
spend being angry."

"…Save it for what?"

"…For the next city."

That evening, Temel was called in again for another matter. A
different building this time, a different official. I went along
again. The room's layout was nearly identical. One table, three
chairs, a window you couldn't see through.

"…I'll reconfirm the application number. Your name is…"

"…Temel."

"…Yes, Mr. Temel. Apart from the previous confirmation, this
department needs to verify your identity again, please."

"…Yes."

"…What you're confirming today concerns the estimated district of
residence. Please confirm whether it matches what you told us
before."

"…Yes, it matches."

"…Thank you. Your signature, please."

Temel wrote his name again. I watched him do it once more. The same
hand movement as before. The same expression. There was weariness in
it, but no irritation.

Out in the hallway, other people were waiting their turn. They all
held papers, and they were all quiet. It was an ordinary sight in this
city. The only difference was that those people needed to come only
once, and Temel needed to come many times.

Coming out of this room, I asked,

"…How is this different from the one before?"

"…Different department. That one was the whereabouts-confirmation
department. This is the residence-district-confirmation department."

"…But they asked the exact same thing."

"…They did. But since it's a different department, they each have to
confirm it separately, I suppose."

"…How many more departments are left?"

"…One more. Tomorrow."

Writing this passage, I count the numbers again. Three applications,
a confirmation from each department, and it repeating. How many times
a single application could get someone called in — I made my first
rough estimate that day. It looked like it would come to at least ten
times over. And that was for only one of the three matters.

That day, I understood exactly what review was.

It wasn't punishment. There was no fine. No prison. No one laid a hand
on Temel. No one raised their voice. The confirmation rooms were
pleasant places, and every official knew how to apologize.

And yet it never ended.

As long as a document with a name on it existed somewhere, every
person and every department handling that document had to confirm
that name. Confirmation was a procedure, and the procedure was
correct. No one did anything wrong. And yet all those correct things,
gathered together, added up to calling one person in, over and over.

I wanted to call this a punishment. Calling it a punishment would
make it clear, somehow. But it wasn't punishment. A punishment has to
be a punishment someone decided on, and this was something that simply
kept going, decided by no one. That day, for the first time, I thought
this was more frightening than a punishment.

That day I went home and told my aunt all of it. The confirmation
room, the window, the signature, the second room, the third room. My
aunt listened quietly and said,

"…Is there a fine, or isn't there?"

"…No fine."

"…Does he go to prison?"

"…No."

"…Does anyone hit him?"

"…No. No one lays a hand on him."

My aunt thought for a moment and said,

"…Then what's frightening about it, Hora? That's just an
inconvenience."

I couldn't refute her right away. She was right. No fine, no prison,
no violence. "Inconvenience" wasn't a wrong word for it.

And still I kept thinking about that "inconvenience," that night. An
inconvenience two or three times a day, repeated for months, years.
Could that really stay just an inconvenience? I didn't think so. But I
couldn't find the words to explain that to my aunt, then.

My aunt asked again.

"…But why does that man keep writing it? He could just stop."

"…He said every application needs a name. That's what the work of
finding people is."

"…Then he should just not do that work."

"…Then he can't find anyone."

My aunt didn't answer that. Instead she looked at me a long while and
said,

"…You're seeing a lot of that man lately."

"…I am."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. It was too many visits
already to say it was just curiosity, and I wasn't even sure what I
wanted to find out, to say I was trying to learn something.

"…I don't know. I just keep wanting to see him."

My aunt didn't press further. She just nodded and turned off the
light. I lay awake a long while in the dark.

On the way home, Temel had asked me,

"…Why do you come watch this, Hora?"

"…I'm curious. What happens if you write a name."

"…Do you know now?"

"…A little. But I'm still not sure."

"…Not sure of what?"

"…Why this is so frightening. When nothing even happens."

Temel nodded.

"…That's right. Nothing happens. That's all there is."

Hearing that, I stopped walking. Temel stopped too.

"…But it keeps not happening. That's the problem."

I held onto that line for a long time. It keeps not happening. Not
that something bad happens, but that nothing happening keeps
happening, over and over. I saw that day, for the first time, how much
that could wear a person down, in this city.

That night, once home, I opened the drawer again. The one holding two
names and one sentence. I put nothing in it that night. Instead I just
looked into it for a long while.

Writing this chapter, I confirm again why this city is truly
frightening. No one did anything wrong. And yet a person truly did
end up called in without end. I learned here that this can happen even
without a villain.

I bring up this drawer more than once in this book. I wrote that I
put nothing in it that night, but in truth I nearly did put something
in. The sentence was "There is no one who sees the whole." I took the
paper out, and in the end put it back without writing it. It was
because I still wasn't certain what it meant.

The next morning, I told Tyche what I'd seen the day before. Tyche
listened quietly and said,

"…That's not a bad thing, is it. It's just that there are several
confirmation steps."

"…But it keeps happening."

"…That's probability. The more matters with a name on them, the more
there is to confirm. It isn't strange."

I couldn't answer that. Tyche was right again. And yet, standing
before that rightness, I couldn't shake the feeling that something was
missing.

(Continued in Chapter 29.)

Chapter 29. Tyche's Logic

_Previously_ — I watched Temel go in and out of the confirmation
rooms. No fine, no prison. Only, it never ended. Tyche called it
"just probability rising," and I couldn't refute her.

That afternoon, sorting district materials, I was left alone with
Tyche. The meeting hall was quiet. Everyone else had gone ahead, and
several unsorted folders were still stacked on the desk. Tyche brought
it up without much thought, while sorting papers.

"…You talk about that man a lot lately, Hora."

"…Temel?"

"…Yes. You go to the market every day, I hear."

"…There's a lot I'm curious about."

Tyche turned a page of the document in her hand and said,

"…I find that a little strange. What's there to be so curious about?
You've seen everything he does — writes his name, gets called in.
That's all there is."

That stung a little, hearing it. But there wasn't really a reason for
it to sting. Tyche wasn't angry. She was genuinely asking out of
curiosity.

Tyche had been born and raised in this city. Her parents had been born
here too, I'd heard. She hadn't been assigned here suddenly one day
like me — she'd grown up going in and out of the district hall since
she was small. Maybe because of that, Tyche seemed to have rarely
doubted this city's ways. That she didn't doubt them didn't mean she
was unthinking. It was the opposite. Tyche examined things more
carefully than anyone I knew. It was only that what she found, more
often than not, matched how this city worked.

"…I don't think that's all there is."

"…What else is there?"

"…I don't know. I just want to know why he lives that way."

Tyche stopped what she was doing and looked at me.

"…You keep trying to find a reason, Hora, but I don't think there is
one."

"…There isn't?"

"…No. He just lives that way. Maybe he learned it that way as a
child, or it worked for him in some city, or maybe it's just habit.
The reasons are all on that level. Nothing grand."

I wanted to refute Tyche, at this point. But I had nothing to refute
her with. Everything she'd said could all be true. Temel himself had
said, "It's just habit, I suppose."

I thought of the names I'd kept in the drawer.

"…What about that person we found in the reading room, then? The one
who wrote a name eleven years ago."

Tyche tilted her head.

"…I heard about that. Same thing. That person was just there, a
situation came up that called for an objection, and they wrote their
name. Not because they were someone special — the person who happened
to be there did what they did."

"…What about the record stopping afterward, though? That doesn't get
explained."

"…It didn't stop — it's just not there, Hora, you said so yourself.
Not there is just not there. They could have moved. Could have
changed their name. You're the one attaching meaning to it, not the
record."

I was caught wordless for a moment, hearing that. Tyche was right. I
had attached the meaning. The record had simply ended.

"…But Tyche, why are you so sure of that? That there's no reason?"

Tyche thought a moment and said,

"…It's not that I'm sure — it's just the simplest explanation. I
think of the simple reason before the complicated one. Usually the
simple one turns out right."

"…Always?"

"…Nearly always. That's what I've seen working here for years. Even
things that look strange turn out to be nothing much, once you dig
into them. A vacancy, a number, probability. That explains almost
everything."

Since it had come up, I asked what I'd been wanting to ask.

"…So is that true of my being in this seat too? Just a vacancy, a
number, probability?"

Tyche nodded.

"…Yes. Exactly that."

"…No other reason?"

"…None. The person before you quit, your number came up on the
roster, and so you sat down. It's the same for me. Me coming here as a
deliberation assistant — the person before me transferred elsewhere,
the seat opened, and I happened to apply right then. That's all it
was."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes. I've wished, myself, that there was some special reason I'm
here. But when you actually look, there isn't. There was just a
vacancy, and I fit it."

I looked out the window for a moment. The afternoon sun slanted in
across the stack of papers. It seemed like even this small thing ran
by this city's order, the way everything else did.

"…What about the water channel? Is that probability too?"
