"…The water channel isn't even a matter yet, so there's nothing to
explain about it. But if I had to say — that's probably nothing much
either. Water flowed from somewhere, followed the land, and ended up
here. Nobody sent it here."

"…You're sure?"

"…No, not sure. But it's the most plausible explanation. Compared to
someone deliberately building a channel to send water all the way to
this city, water just flowing downhill and ending up here is much
simpler."

I nodded at that. Tyche was right. I'd confirmed myself, more than
once, in this city, that the simple explanation was usually the true
one. Why the signature box was blank, why four hundred people were
involved — none of it, once you traced it through, turned out to be
some complex conspiracy. It was just that the procedure was built that
way.

The sorting had finished by then. Tyche pulled out the next folder and
went on talking. Hearing this answer, I felt something strange. Tyche
spoke about herself without any bitterness at all. She was calm about
it. That held me even more.

"…So doesn't that make you sad, Tyche? Being here for no reason at
all?"

Tyche smiled a little at the question. Not mockingly — it was a truly
gentle smile.

"…Do you think it would? I'm not sad about it."

"…Why not?"

"…There being no reason doesn't make the work I'm doing now
meaningless. I like the work I'm doing now. Why I came here and what
I'm doing here are different questions."

That was the first time I heard that distinction stated plainly. Why
you came and what you do being different questions. Tyche said it as
though it were nothing at all, but I held onto that line for a long
time.

"…Then what gets you through the day, Tyche? If there's no reason."

"…Having no reason doesn't mean there's nothing to get through the
day on. I just get through it liking what I'm doing today. I liked
sorting deliberation materials yesterday, and I'll do work like that
today too. That's enough."

"…Enough?"

"…Yes. I don't want much. Not some grand purpose or anything. If
today goes well, I'm satisfied with that."

"…That sounds a little lonely, to me."

Tyche smiled slightly at that. It wasn't a teasing smile.

"…Does it? I find it restful, actually. Watching people struggle to
find some grand purpose looks harder to me. It seems like they must
always feel something's missing."

"…Me?"

"…You're a little like that too, Hora. You keep thinking there must
be something more."

I couldn't answer that. It wasn't wrong.

"…But Tyche, I have thoughts like this sometimes. If all of this is
just probability, isn't that a little empty?"

Tyche stopped sorting the papers and looked straight at me. For the
first time in this conversation, I watched her seriously choosing her
words.

"…Hora — I know you want there to be a meaning. So do I."

I was surprised, hearing that. I hadn't thought Tyche carried a feeling
like that.

"…But?"

"…But wanting there to be one isn't evidence that there is."

I said nothing for a long while, hearing that.

Tyche went on.

"…I heard things like that a lot growing up. That there's a reason
this city is a place where nobody's wronged, that it can't be chance.
My grandmother used to say things like that too."

"…Why did your grandmother think that?"

"…I don't know. She just did. I grew up hearing it, and as I got
older I kept checking it — whether things had turned out this way
because there really was a meaning, or whether they'd simply turned
out this way and people had attached a meaning to it afterward."

"…What did you find?"

"…Almost always the latter. A vacancy opens and someone fills it; a
procedure exists and things go by the procedure. It was all explained,
without any meaning attached."

"…So your grandmother was wrong?"

"…Not wrong, exactly. My grandmother said it because she wanted there
to be a meaning. I understand that feeling. I have it too."

I remember this line exactly, even now. Word for word, without a
single mistake. Tyche wasn't saying this to win against me. She wasn't
trying to persuade me either. She was simply saying what she honestly
thought.

"…So do you just throw that wish away, then, Tyche?"

"…I don't throw it away. I just don't use it as evidence. A wish is a
wish, and a fact is a fact. I don't want to mix the two."

"…Is it bad to mix them?"

"…If I mix them, I end up seeing only what I want to see. That scares
me. Picking out only the things that look like they have meaning, and
not seeing the things that don't. In the end that's nothing more than
making up the story I want, myself."

That held me for a long time. Tyche wasn't cynical. If anything, she
was a very careful person. Someone working, every single time, not to
mix what she wanted with what was true.

"…So you don't believe in anything, Tyche?"

"…No. I believe what's worth believing. That nobody gets wronged when
four hundred people are involved — I believe that. Because it's
actually true. There's evidence for it."

"…If there's ever evidence that there's a meaning, will you believe
that too, someday?"

Tyche thought a moment.

"…If there is, sure. There isn't yet."

"…What could count as evidence?"

Tyche's face turned genuinely thoughtful.

"…I don't know. Honestly, that's the problem for me too — I can't
settle on it. I can't decide what standard would let me believe
there's a meaning."

"…What if there's no standard?"

"…If there's no standard, then no matter how strange something is, I
just end up explaining it away as a strange coincidence. That bothers
me a little too. I wonder whether I'll end up not believing anything,
no matter what I see."

That answer surprised me. I hadn't known Tyche held this much doubt
about her own logic.

"…So you waver sometimes too, Tyche?"

"…Not waver, exactly — I know there could be a hole in my way of
thinking. But I can't just believe anything to fill that hole. That
seems more dangerous, to me."

"…Dangerous?"

"…Yes. If I go grabbing at any story to fill one hole, eventually I
won't even be able to tell what's real from what I made up myself.
That's the thing I'm most afraid of."

Hearing that, I looked at Tyche differently. She'd seemed fearless, but
she had a fear of her own kind. It just pointed in a different
direction than mine. I was afraid there might be no meaning at all.
Tyche was afraid of believing there was a meaning where there wasn't
one.

Writing this passage, I feel again how careful a person Tyche was.
That's why she's hard to describe in this book. She wasn't a person
who didn't believe — she was a person afraid of believing wrongly.

That evening, walking home, I kept turning this conversation over. I
wanted to find something to say that would beat Tyche. I couldn't find
it. There wasn't a single wrong thing in anything she'd said.

Writing this chapter, I tried again to find something to refute Tyche
with. I still can't. Tyche was right. Most things really were
explained by a vacancy, a number, probability. I don't deny that.
Only, I could never quite bring myself to believe that explanation was
everything. I don't know why, myself. Not because I have evidence.

Tyche would have smiled at that sentence. "See — there's no
evidence." She'd be right. I still have no answer to that, even now.

I told my aunt about this conversation, and she asked,

"…So what did you say?"

"…I couldn't say anything."

"…Why not?"

"…Because she was right."

My aunt thought a moment and said,

"…Hora, being right doesn't always mean it's a good thing to hear."

"…What does that mean?"

"…Just that — being right doesn't mean it sets your mind at ease."

I thought about that a long time under the blankets that night. Tyche
wasn't wrong. But her being right didn't set me at ease. I learned for
the first time then that the two could go together.

Writing this chapter, I confirm again that I don't want to paint
Tyche badly. Tyche wasn't trying to persuade me. She was only saying
what she saw. And after that day, I came to like Tyche even more than
before. It sounds strange, but it was true. It was restful, being
beside someone honest.

I don't intend, in this whole book, to try to win against Tyche. It
doesn't seem to be a matter of winning or losing. Tyche is right, and
I feel that I'm right. Both were real. How the two are meant to stand
together, I still don't know, now any more than then.

The next morning, I decided to read a document together with Tyche. I
was curious what would look different, reading the same document side
by side. Tyche agreed without much hesitation.

(Continued in Chapter 30.)

Chapter 30. The Same Document, Different Conclusions

_Previously_ — Tyche said she too wished there were a meaning. "But
wanting there to be one isn't evidence that there is." I couldn't
refute her. The next day, we decided to read the same document side
by side.

The document was a newly assigned deliberation matter. A rezoning to
consolidate distribution warehouses in the city's eastern district. On
its face it looked like an ordinary piece of administrative
housekeeping. About forty pages. I planned, as I always did, to read
it start to finish, and Tyche planned, as she always did, to start
with the summary and the conclusion. This time we decided to do it
side by side, at the same table.

"…We're really doing this together?"

"…Yes. I want to see what looks different."

"…Sounds interesting. I've never done a comparison like this."

Tyche's face was genuinely interested. Not teasing me — curious,
because she'd never tried a comparison like this before.

Writing this chapter, I'm being careful. The temptation keeps coming
to write that I saw more than Tyche did, here. But that isn't true. I
only spent more time. Spending more time doesn't mean seeing more
accurately. I'm trying not to forget that, through this whole
chapter.

It was afternoon in the meeting hall. Light came in through the
window. Everyone else was away for lunch, and only the two of us were
left. Two copies of the same document lay side by side on the table.

We agreed to each read in our own way and meet again after. We hadn't
set an order. Tyche simply read first, in her own way. It took her
less than ten minutes. Tyche checked the summary, the conclusion, and
the progress table.

"…I've gone through it."

"…What do you see?"

"…Intake, review, confirming those involved three times, gathering
opinions twice, convening four times. All handled by regulation. No
objections filed. Confirmed."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes. Not a single procedural flaw. The confirmation dates all
check out, the number of those involved exceeds the requirement, and
the convening notice periods were all observed."

Listening to her, I could tell Tyche had read this document with
genuine accuracy. Tyche wasn't lying. The document really did say
exactly what she'd said.

"…This is a well-made matter, as far as that goes. Consolidating
three warehouses cuts the management staff needed and shortens
distribution times. It's good for people."

"…Nothing bad in it at all?"

"…Procedurally, no. What I can see is procedure. Whether the outcome
turns out good or bad takes time to know, but as far as this document
goes, there's no basis to call it bad."

Tyche set the summary down on the table as she said this. I picked up
the forty-page original stacked beside it.

I began reading the forty pages from the start, after that. Tyche
waited beside me, sorting other papers. She glanced at me now and
then, but didn't rush me.

It took about two hours. The consolidation itself was no problem.
Three warehouses were being merged into one, and the distribution
procedure would change only slightly.

The problem was on the twenty-eighth page. It said that of the three
existing warehouse managers, two would lose their posts in the
consolidation. A reassignment procedure was specified, but it said
only "according to the individual's wish." How that wish had been
confirmed wasn't stated.

I read that part over a few times. That no method of confirmation was
stated wasn't itself a flaw — there was no clause requiring the
regulations to specify a method.

The three managers' names weren't given. They were listed only by
number. One would stay in the new consolidated warehouse; the other
two were classified as "reassignment subjects." Of the two
reassignment subjects, one would move to a warehouse in another
district, and the other would be moved to office work related to
distribution.

I stopped there and looked out the window a moment, then read again
the part about the one being moved to office work. Managing a
warehouse had originally been physical work, and office work was an
entirely different kind of job. Whether this person truly wanted the
move, or had gone there simply because no other post was left, the
document alone couldn't tell me.

I stopped a long while at this part. If this person had truly wanted
the move, there was no problem at all. But whether they had truly
wanted it, or whether it had been written that way because there was
no other option, this document didn't tell me. That it didn't tell me
doesn't mean I could conclude it was the bad case. I know that too.

I kept reading through the rest of the pages. Budget items, changes to
logistics routes, adjustments to distribution hours. All unremarkable.
Aside from page twenty-eight, there was nothing that caught in my mind
in this document, either. I understood why Tyche called this a
"well-made matter." Most of it really was well put together.

I wrote earlier that reading all forty pages took two hours; more
precisely, half of that time went into reading and rereading those
twenty-eight pages several times over. The remaining thirty-nine
pages I read in an hour.

Having read through to the last page, I looked at the bottom field.

The signature box. Blank.

"…Finished?"

"…Yes."

"…What did you see?"

I told her about page twenty-eight. The reassignment of a manager,
the phrase "according to the individual's wish," the absence of a
confirmation method.

Tyche opened to that page again.

"…This? I saw it too. But this isn't a flaw. Confirming the
individual's wish is left to each department's discretion. That the
regulations don't specify a method is deliberate. Situations differ by
department."

"…But then there's no way to confirm whether that person really
wanted it."

"…It's not that there's no way — it means the department handled it
on its own judgment. That it isn't written in this document doesn't
mean it wasn't done."

I stopped for a moment, hearing that. Tyche wasn't wrong. Not written
down doesn't mean not done. I knew that too. And still that gap kept
catching at me.

"…So if I look at that department's documents, would it be
confirmed?"

"…There should be one. Should I look?"

Tyche really did get up and go to another reading terminal. She came
back about ten minutes later.

"…Found it. A distribution-office placement confirmation form. Here,
instead of the individual's signature, there's a record of a verbal
confirmation. It says the official spoke to the person directly, and
they said they were fine with it."

"…A verbal confirmation?"

"…Yes. That's a valid method of confirmation here too."

I took the confirmation form and read it. It really did say that. No
official's name was given — only "confirmed by official." Who that
official was, or how they'd asked, wasn't stated.

"…Do you think this is fine too, Tyche?"

"…Yes. It's procedure. A verbal confirmation is one of the official
methods. It's in the regulations manual too."

"…But even seeing this, I still don't know whether that person
really wanted it."

"…That isn't a question the document can answer, Hora. You'd only
know that by asking that person directly."

That was true too. I'd never met that person. I only knew them by
number.

"…Don't you ever want to meet that person, Tyche?"

"…Not particularly. What I can see is this far, and I don't think
going further is my job."

"…I want to meet them."

"…That's very like you, too."

Tyche wasn't saying that to be sarcastic. She was simply stating a
fact. I always wanted to go past the document, and Tyche did exactly
what could be done within the document. Both were work this city
needed.

"…But this document alone doesn't tell me whether it was really
confirmed or not."

"…No, it doesn't. But that isn't this document's problem — you'd
have to look at more documents from the related department to know.
This document accurately wrote down its own part. There's no reason
this document has to take responsibility for another department's
work too."

"…So you think this has no problem, then, Tyche?"

"…Yes. By my standard. There's a procedure, it was followed, and
there was no objection. That's everything this document needs to
confirm."

Hearing that, I pointed at the signature box again.

"…What about this, though? How do you read this?"

Tyche looked at the box.

"…It's blank."

"…Doesn't that seem strange?"

"…What's strange about it. Every document here is like this."

"…That's exactly it. That they're all like this is what seems strange
to me."

Tyche looked at me for a moment and said,

"…What do you see in this blank space, Hora?"

I couldn't answer right away. I hadn't worked out what to say I saw.

"…No one, where someone ought to be."

"…But why ought there to be someone? This is a place where the whole
decides, by design. It's not that an individual needs to write it —
being blank isn't the problem."

I've called this conversation back to mind more than once. Tyche saw
the procedure's precision. I saw the blank space. We both looked at
the same document, and neither of us lied. Tyche couldn't be called
wrong. I couldn't be called wrong either.

We sat a moment in silence. Tyche spoke again.

"…Hora, I don't think this city's way is bad. I think it's good,
actually. Leaving even a verbal confirmation on record is better than
leaving nothing at all."

"…That's true. I agree with that too."

"…But you want more than that, don't you? A signature. Something
written in that person's own hand, their actual name."

"…I think so."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer this question right away either. It was hard to
explain why a signature was better, what made it different from a
verbal confirmation.

"…I don't know. I just feel like if there's a name, it means that
person was really there. A verbal confirmation feels like someone
speaking for them, saying they were there."

Tyche nodded.

"…I understand that feeling. But that's separate from whether it's
actually true or not."

"…I know. But knowing something and it setting your mind at ease seem
to be different things."

"…Tyche, I'm not trying to say this document is wrong."

"…But?"

"…Just, it feels like something's missing. The procedure's all
correct, but it feels missing."

"…If something's missing, that's a regulatory violation. If it isn't
in the regulations, it isn't missing."

"…Can't something be missing even if it isn't in the regulations?"

Tyche went quiet at that question for a moment. It was the first time
she hadn't had an answer right away.

"…That… I'm honestly not sure, myself."

That short silence was the most striking moment in the whole afternoon's
conversation. Tyche always had an answer. But she paused for a moment
in front of this question.

"…You're not sure?"

"…No. Whether something not in the regulations can be called
missing — that doesn't seem like a question about the regulations. It
seems like some other question. But I can't explain what that other
question is, either."

"…So do you think there's something to this blank box too, then,
Tyche?"

"…No. I don't go that far. I still think this document, this blank
box, has no problem. It's only that the question itself, the one
you're asking, stays with me a little."

Tyche went on, sorting papers again.

"…Doing this work for a long time, I've come to see that people want
to fill a blank space with something, once they see it. Whether it
means something good or something bad. But a blank space is, most of
the time, just a blank space."

"…Is that your conclusion?"

"…Yes. About this document. Another document might be different.
I don't think the same way every time either."

"…So this isn't even a certain conclusion for you."

"…It's certain. As far as this document goes. Certain doesn't mean
certain forever."

That was a small comfort to me. Tyche was judging each case fresh,
too. It wasn't that the answer came out automatically.

"…Have there been times something caught at you too, looking at a
blank box like this?"

Tyche thought a moment and said,

"…There have. Not often. But when it does catch, and I check it, it's
usually nothing much. So now, even when it catches, I check first."

"…You checked this one too."

"…Yes. I checked, and by my standard, it was fine."

"…By my standard, something's still left over."

Tyche looked at me and smiled slightly.

"…Then we're both right. We just confirmed different things."

That was the end of that day's conversation. I hadn't won against
Tyche. Tyche hadn't persuaded me either. I came to understand, after
that day, that winning or losing hadn't been the point for either of
us.

Writing this chapter, I've decided to leave this conversation without
a conclusion. Drawing a conclusion would mean siding with one of us. I
don't want to do that. Tyche read that document accurately. I read it
accurately too. We simply saw different things in the same document,
and that difference wasn't due to either of us making a mistake.

On the way home, I thought about the person moved to distribution
office work. The one I only knew by number. If I ever met them, I
wanted to ask — had they really wanted the move, or had there been no
other option. I knew it was a little strange, thinking that without
even knowing their face.

I didn't meet that person, then. Actually meeting someone in this
city who's known only by a number happens much later than I imagined
at the time.

We didn't talk further, there. We had read the same document
together, and looked at the same blank box together. And in that
blank box, Tyche saw a regulation, and I saw a person. We had both
read that document accurately.

(Continued in Chapter 31.)

Chapter 31. For Such a Time

_Previously_ — Tyche and I read the same document side by side.
Tyche saw the procedure's precision, and I saw the blank box. Both
of us read it accurately. No conclusion came of it. That evening, I
went to find Temel again.

Temel was due to leave this city before long. His application was
nearly finished, and the person he'd been looking for, he said, had
gone unfound after all. Hearing that, I felt a rush of urgency. There
was still something I wanted to ask.

That evening, instead of the north market, I went to the lodging house
where he was staying. It was a small inn. Temel was sorting his
belongings when he saw me, and looked a little surprised.

"…What brings you here, this hour?"

"…I heard you're leaving tomorrow."

"…Day after. But I tend to pack ahead of time."

We sat on the wooden bench in front of the inn. The evening air was
cool. The smell of dinner cooking drifted from the mouth of the alley.
Temel had come out without finishing his packing. His bag was still
half open.

"…You can finish packing, if you want."

"…It's fine. Not much left, anyway."

"…Where are you headed next?"

"…Not sure yet, exactly. I'll probably decide once things here are
wrapped up."

"…You really didn't find the person you were looking for?"

Temel nodded.

"…Right. Must not have been here after all. Or I got here too late."

"…Doesn't that trouble you?"

"…It does. But being troubled and giving up are two different
problems. I'll have to look again in the next city."

Listening to him, I wondered what would happen to the three names he'd
put down here, once he left.

"…What happens to the names you wrote here, once you go?"

"…No, that doesn't end. If the matter's here, the review keeps coming
from here. Wherever I am."

"…So they call you in even if you're in another city?"

"…Yes. If a summons comes, I have to answer it. Not answering
becomes a record too."

"…Does that go on forever?"

"…As long as that matter's still open. I don't know when that ends,
myself."

Hearing that, I finally understood that this man kept dragging
something behind him even as he moved from city to city. I'd thought
until then that review was something that only happened inside this
city.

Writing this passage, it's strange to me now that I didn't press
further on this, that night. I think some other question was more
urgent to me that night.

I hesitated, then asked.

"…I still don't know why I ended up in this seat."

"…We talked about that before. Because of the number."

"…Yes, I know that. I'm asking if there's some other reason, besides
that."

Temel looked at me for a moment, then leaned back against the bench.
And his way of speaking changed.

"…Hora."

It was the first time he'd said my name like that. Not the polite
form. I tensed for a moment, but it didn't feel like rudeness — it
felt like a signal that something was about to turn more serious.

I hadn't particularly noticed, until then, that Temel always spoke to
me formally. Even with the difference in our ages, he had. But that
night, I clearly felt the moment his speech changed. It felt like some
formality had been set aside. Not in a bad direction — toward
something closer.

"…Maybe you're sitting here only because of a number."

"…Yes."

"…But there are still things you can do in a seat you got only
because of a number."

I sat still a moment, hearing that. It was a simple thing to say, and
that simplicity was what made it heavy.

"…What does that mean?"

"…The reason you ended up in that seat was a number. That's true. But
what you'll see there, what you'll read, what you'll come to
know — the number didn't decide that. That's what you do."

"…But I don't even really know what I saw. The empty box. I still
don't know what it means, either."

"…You saw it, though, not knowing."

"…I did see it, yes."

"…Then that's enough. Saying what you saw, the way you saw it — that's
what you can do."

"…What's so remarkable about that? It's just something I read."

"…It doesn't have to be remarkable. It doesn't need to be remarkable
to mean something."

"…But isn't that just because that's the kind of person I am, in the
end? I've got the habit of reading to the end. That's not something I
chose either. It's a habit I got because of my mother."

Temel nodded at this.

"…That's right. That might not be something you chose either. Habits
usually aren't."

"…So isn't it all just one coincidence linked to the next, then? A
vacancy, a number, a habit. Not one thing I actually chose."

"…Could be. But Hora, those coincidences linked up, and now you're
sitting here on this bench asking me this question."

I was caught wordless for a moment, hearing that. I didn't know what
to refute it with. Temel hadn't denied my logic. If anything he'd
granted that all of it was true, and simply set the next sentence on
top.

It occurred to me to wonder how Tyche would take this. She would
probably say something like this — that a chain of coincidences was
still just coincidence continuing, and that attaching meaning to it
was my own doing. I still thought that was right. And yet Temel's
words sounded equally right to me. That the two could both be right at
once was what confused me most, that night.

"…Tyche would probably argue with that, if she were here."

"…How?"

"…She'd probably say a chain of coincidences is still just
coincidence, and that attaching meaning to it is on me."

Temel nodded.

"…Could be. That could be right too."

"…But what you're saying is right too."

"…Both can be right, Hora. That isn't strange. It's the same thing,
seen, and standing differently."

Hearing that, I thought of the afternoon I'd spent reading the
document beside Tyche. The same document, different conclusions. This
time it was the same chain of coincidences, different conclusions.

"…Was it like that for you too? Back then, when you said you fell
apart?"

"…Yeah. I thought it was all coincidence, back then too. That I'd
ended up in that place, that I fell apart — I thought it was all just
how things happened to go."

"…And now?"

"…Even now, I can't say it wasn't coincidence. But what I did
afterward wasn't coincidence. That, I chose."

"…What did you choose?"

"…Deciding to start over. Deciding to write my name first. Nobody
told me to do that."

"…Why did you decide to start over, then?"

Temel was quiet a moment, then answered.

"…Running kept getting tiring. Even while I was running, something
always got left behind somewhere. There's the trace you leave without
writing it down, and the trace you leave by writing it down, and I
found the trace of not writing it more frightening."

"…Why?"

"…If I didn't write it, later on I couldn't even explain to myself
what I'd done. That was the scariest part. Not other people's
judgment — my own."

"…So that's why you started writing it?"

"…Yeah. At first, that was all it was. Not because it had a meaning —
just because it felt like I needed to be able to explain myself to
myself."

"…And now? Is that still the reason?"

Temel smiled a little.

"…A few more reasons have piled up since. But the very first one is
still the biggest."

I liked that answer. It was more believable for not being some grand
reason.

Listening to him, I was still curious about whatever had happened to
this man when he was young. But I didn't ask further. Tonight I wanted
to ask something else.

"…So does that mean there's a meaning to it?"

I held my breath a little, asking this. I really wanted to hear the
answer.

Temel didn't answer right away. He looked at the evening sky a moment,
then looked back at me.

"…I don't know."

"…What?"

"…I don't know either."

Hearing that answer, I felt strangely deflated. And at the same time,
strangely at ease. It was odd that the two could come together.

"…If you don't know, who does?"

"…Maybe nobody does. Or maybe it's just me who doesn't."

"…But everyone in this city lives as if they know. Tyche does too."

"…That's a different matter. There's saying you know something you
don't, and there's not asking at all because you don't know. Both
happen. I find something lacking in both, if I'm honest."

"…Are you neither of those?"

"…I just keep asking without knowing. That's the most exhausting way
to do it."

"…Then why say what you said? That there are things I can do. When
you don't even know."

Temel smiled a little.

"…Not knowing doesn't mean I can't do anything. I said it before,
didn't I. Not knowing isn't a reason not to write it."

In that moment I felt I understood why that line had stayed with me
so long, inside me. This man truly didn't know. And not knowing, he
wrote his name every single time. Not knowing whether there was a
meaning or not, he was doing the thing he could do.

I brought up review again. I'd remembered the day I'd followed him
through the confirmation rooms at the market.

"…What I saw that day, at the confirmation room. Is that still going
on?"

"…Yeah. There was one today too, a little while ago."

"…Do they call you in even before you leave?"

"…Yeah. Leaving needs confirming too. A departure notice, I think
they call it."

"…Do you write your name for that too?"

"…I do. There's another box to write in."

Hearing that, a laugh came out of me. It wasn't something to laugh
about, but I laughed anyway.

"…You really do write your name wherever you go."

"…I do. I get tired of myself sometimes, too."

"…And still you'll write it again."

"…Yeah. I'll write it again."

There wasn't a moment's hesitation in that answer. I found that
striking. I learned, right then, for the first time, that not
hesitating and being certain aren't the same thing.

"…Aren't you scared? Living like this all the time, when there might
be no meaning at all."

"…I am. But being certain there's no meaning scares me just as much.
Either way, being certain scares me more."

"…But there are plenty of people who are certain. Tyche too."

"…There are. But I don't envy that certainty so much as it scares me,
a little. Once you're certain, you don't have to look anymore after
that. You already think you know."

"…Do you dislike that?"

"…Not dislike it, exactly — I just can't seem to do it. I have to
look again, every time. It's tiring, but if I don't, I feel like I'll
miss something."

"…Miss what?"

"…I don't know that either. But if I let myself be certain, I'm
scared that even the not-knowing itself will stop being visible to
me."

"…How do you live without being certain?"

"…I just do today's work. Past that, I don't really know either."

I felt that this was similar to what Tyche had said, and also
different. Tyche too had said she lived liking what she did today.
But Tyche set that on top of a conclusion of "there isn't one," and
Temel was setting it on top of a conclusion of "I don't know."

I couldn't explain that difference exactly, that night. In the next
chapter, I think about it for a long time.

"…Hora."

"…Yes."

"…What I said before, about you sitting here maybe just because of a
number. I didn't say that to comfort you. I meant it could really be
true."

"…I know."

"…But the sentence after that is just as true. There are still things
you can do in a seat you got only because of a number. That isn't
comfort either. It's just a fact."

I sat a long while with those two sentences set side by side. That it
might be only a number, and that there were still things you could do
even so. They weren't opposites. Both could be true.

The day had fully gone dark. I said goodbye and stood.

"…You're going day after tomorrow?"

"…Yeah."

"…Will I get to see you again?"

Temel thought a moment and said,

"…I don't know. I don't know that either."

Strangely, that didn't make me sad. I smiled and turned away.

On the way home, I took the long way on purpose. The road that passed
the western water channel.

The water was still making its sound. I stood beside it a moment and
brought back the words I'd heard that day.

Maybe you're sitting here only because of a number.

But there are still things you can do in a seat you got only because
of a number.

I don't know. I don't know either.

Set side by side like this, the three sentences strangely sounded like
one. Clearer put together than they had been, heard one at a time.

I copied these three sentences onto paper that night. I put them in
the drawer with my mother's papers, alongside the two names and the
one sentence already there. That drawer had grown fairly heavy by
then. There was a weight I felt in my hand, every time I opened it.

My aunt was still awake when I got home.

"…You're late."

"…Yes. I went to see Temel."

"…He's leaving day after tomorrow, you said."

"…Yes."

"…What did you ask him?"

"…Why I ended up in this seat."

My aunt looked at me a moment and asked,

"…Did you get an answer?"

"…No. He said he doesn't know either."

My aunt gave a short laugh.

"…Why ask something he doesn't know?"

"…You have to ask, to find out whether he knows or not."

My aunt didn't answer that. She just turned off the light. I lay in
the dark and read those three sentences over once more, inside myself.

I learned that night, for the first time, how long the words "I don't
know" could hold onto a person. Tyche's "there isn't one," and Temel's
"I don't know." Both short sentences, but they settled inside me at
entirely different weights. Why they weighed differently, I couldn't
explain, then.

(Continued in Chapter 32.)

Chapter 32. The Answer That Is I Don't Know

_Previously_ — I asked Temel, "Does that mean there's a meaning to
it?" He waited a long while and answered, "…I don't know. I don't
know either." I felt deflated hearing that answer, and strangely at
ease at the same time. I sensed that Tyche's "there isn't one" and
Temel's "I don't know" were different, but that night I couldn't
explain how.

For the days that followed, I kept holding onto those two words.

I held onto them while working, and while eating. My aunt asked me
twice, "What are you thinking about so hard?" and I couldn't give her
a proper answer. It was hard to even call it thinking. I was circling
the same spot over and over, not moving forward.

There isn't one. I don't know.

The two phrases look similar on the surface. Both look like they sit
on the opposite side from "I know." But set side by side, they were
entirely different statements. There isn't one was a sentence that
closed a door. I don't know was a sentence that left the door open.

Writing this line, I'm not sure whether this is the right way to
define it. I'm still not sure, even now. But I'll set down honestly,
at least, that this is how I've gone on telling the two apart, ever
since that day.

The first thing that came to mind was the signature box.

Tyche had looked at that box and said, "Being blank isn't the
problem." That since this was a place where the whole decided, an
individual didn't need to write it. That was one conclusion. The
conclusion that there was no meaning to it. That the box was blank
simply because this city had been designed that way, and there was
nothing more to look for there.

Temel had looked at that box and drawn no conclusion at all. He wrote
it, he'd said, "because there's a box to write in." Why the box was
always blank, he didn't know either. He didn't know, but not knowing
didn't mean he did nothing. Not knowing, he wrote it anyway.

I set these two side by side and looked at them a long while. Tyche's
words gave me relief. Because there was a conclusion. With a
conclusion, I no longer had to lie awake over the question. Temel's
words gave no relief. Instead they kept making me do something. Since
I had to do something while still not knowing.

Here I feel the temptation to write Tyche unkindly — to make her
reaching a conclusion look like an escape. But that wouldn't be
honest. Tyche's conclusion was a conclusion with evidence behind it.
Nearly everything she had confirmed in this city backed that
conclusion up. She hadn't reached it out of laziness.

The second thing I thought of was the person from eleven years ago.

The record hadn't been cut off — it simply had no more. I'd held onto
that difference a long time back then too, and I hold onto it again
now, writing this passage. Cut off means someone severed it. No more
means it simply went that far and no further.

Tyche would have said this: that no more just means no more. That the
person could have moved, could have changed their name, could have
left this city. That either way, it was explained by probability. And
that explanation really did hold up. I couldn't refute a single part
of it.

I don't know what happened to that person. I still don't know. And yet
I couldn't turn that not-knowing into "so it's nothing." Not knowing
wasn't the same as saying it was nothing. Not knowing was only a
statement that I still didn't know.

Writing this sentence, I know how much I'm trapped in circular logic.
Not knowing is only a statement that I don't know — this proves
nothing. Tyche would have caught exactly this point. "That's just it,
Hora. That's exactly what I'm saying. Not knowing proves nothing." I
have no confidence to refute that. I still don't, now.

The third thing I thought of was the four hundred people.

In this city, an average of more than four hundred people are
involved in a single deliberation. At first I understood that number
as a safeguard. That if two hundred, three hundred people all pass a
decision through, there's no room left for any one person's malice to
slip in. That really was true. For months I went and found each of
those four hundred people, one by one, and confirmed that not a single
one of them held any malice. I only passed it on. I only put it in
order. I only confirmed it. All of them were honest.

Tyche explained this structure this way: that because no one decided
it, no one was wronged by it. That was a fact. I couldn't refute it.
And yet I was asking a different question on top of that fact.
Whether no one deciding it, and there being no meaning anywhere in
that whole, were the same thing.

Tyche would have said yes. That when four hundred small judgments
gather, that itself is the Consensus, and attaching any further
meaning to the Consensus is a thing people do, not a thing the
Consensus does. I thought this was right too. And yet, at the same
time, the fact that not one of those four hundred people had ever seen
the whole kept catching at me. I remembered what the official had
told me, before — "There's no one who sees the whole." If no one had
seen the whole, then it meant no one knew what the whole meant,
either. Not there isn't one — I don't know.

I know this distinction might look trivial. Whether it's there isn't
one or I don't know, in the sense that neither gives me an answer
right here, right now, they're the same. But I felt, for the first
time that week, that the two made me into different people. Thinking
there isn't one, I stopped there. Thinking I don't know, I kept doing
something. It wasn't an answer, but it took me somewhere, at least.

The fourth thing I thought of was the water channel.

Tyche had said this about the water channel: that the most plausible
explanation was that water, flowing downhill, had simply ended up
here. That this was much simpler than an explanation involving someone
deliberately sending it. I'd nodded at that. I'd confirmed myself,
more than once, that the simple explanation was usually the right one.

But the simple explanation being right, and that explanation being
everything, were different questions. That the water had flowed
downhill could be true, and that there was something more to why that
water happened to flow to this particular city — whether there was or
wasn't, I didn't know — could be true at the same time. That the two
didn't cancel each other out was something I noticed for the first
time, then.

This was a very small realization, but it felt very large to me. Up
until then I'd thought "it's explained" and "there's no meaning" were
the same statement. I'd thought that once something was explained,
that proved there was no meaning to it. But that wasn't so. An
explanation was an explanation, and a meaning was a meaning. The two
weren't fighting over the same ground.

I once tried saying this to Tyche. She answered, "Then whether
there's a meaning ends up unprovable too, doesn't it — since you're
saying it doesn't overlap with the explanation." I couldn't refute
that either. Tyche was always someone who could shake whatever I'd
built in a single step.

The fifth thing wasn't another person. It was my mother.

My mother had written twelve pages. Where things around the house
were kept, when to bring out the winter clothes. She wrote those
things first, and put the one sentence she left for me on the very
last line. I've thought about that order for a long time. Why put it
last. Had she been the kind of person who put important things last,
or had it simply drifted there as she wrote?

Tyche would have said this: that people usually write the easy things
first. That where things around the house were kept was easy to
write, and what she wanted to say to me must have been hard to write.
That putting off the hard thing was how it ended up on the last line.
That was a plausible explanation. Maybe that really was what happened.
Maybe my mother hadn't put that sentence last on purpose, and it had
simply been pushed there as she wrote.

I could accept that explanation. I did accept it. And yet, even having
accepted it, I couldn't turn into nothing the fact that that last line
had been waiting for me, of all people, at the end of the very two
years I'd let slip by. That the order was coincidence, and that I had
lost two years because of that order and finally reached that line —
those were stories on different layers.

Tyche folded these two layers into one. If the order was coincidence,
then my reaching that line was simply, in the end, a result
coincidence had produced. I think that's true too. And yet I can't
fold away, as mere coincidence, the fact that that coincidence stood
me, of all people, at that particular moment, in front of that
particular sentence. I know that being unable to fold it away isn't
evidence. I'm only setting down honestly, here, the fact that I
couldn't.

I tried to sort out these five things, sitting beside the drawer. I
wrote "there isn't one" on a paper, and beside it wrote "I don't
know." Underneath I wrote each of the five in turn — the signature
box, the person from eleven years ago, the four hundred people, the
water channel, my mother's last line. I started to draw an arrow, and
stopped. I didn't know which way to draw it.

I stared at that paper a long while, that night. More than once I
felt like a conclusion was about to come. And every time, just before
it did, it scattered again. Even now, writing this book, I still
haven't reached that conclusion. I'm not writing this chapter to hand
one over. I'm writing it to set down honestly, exactly as it was, that
I truly hadn't reached one, back then.

One thing I did seem to know. Tyche's "there isn't one" was a safe
place to stand. Standing there, you no longer had to be shaken. Temel's
"I don't know" wasn't a safe place. Standing there, you had to keep
looking. Keep asking. Keep doing something. Maybe Temel kept writing
his name for exactly this reason — because he kept standing in that
unsafe place.

But this isn't a conclusion either. Standing in an unsafe place
doesn't mean it's the right place to stand. I knew that then, and I
know it now. Being unsafe and being right are different questions. I
kept wanting to mix the two together. I also know that isn't allowed.
Tyche would have said exactly that.

Days went by like that. At the district hall I did the work I always
did. I read the documents I was assigned. Read them to the end. Went
into meetings. Heard, I'll put it in order, heard, it's been passed on
to me. On the outside, nothing had changed at all.

Tyche asked me once.

"…You're thinking about something different lately?"

"…Yes. But I can't really explain it."

"…What is it?"

"…There isn't one, and I don't know. Why they're different."

Tyche tilted her head.

"…Are they different? They sound similar to me."

"…I thought so too, at first. But I don't think they are, now."

"…How are they different?"

I tried to answer that question, but even after days of thinking about
it, no proper words came.

"…I don't know. I still can't explain it."

Tyche smiled slightly.

"…That's very like you, Hora. Saying you don't know what you don't
know."

"…Is that strange?"

"…No. Just — I'd have let it go if I couldn't explain something. But
you don't let it go."

That line stayed with me the longest, out of that day's conversation.
I hadn't let it go. I couldn't let it go. I still didn't know why.

"…Tyche, can I ask one more thing?"

"…Sure."

"…When you hear the words I don't know, what comes to mind?"

Tyche thought a moment.

"…That there isn't enough data yet. That usually means you'll come to
know it, once you gather more."

"…Is that always true? Gathering data means you'll come to know?"

"…No. There are things you never come to know. But in those cases, I
think it's more accurate to say there's no way to know, rather than
saying you don't know."

"…Is that different from I don't know?"

"…It's different, to me. I don't know means you might come to know
later. There's no way to know is just an ending."

I sat a while after hearing that, then asked.

"…So which is this? Whether there's a meaning or not."

This time Tyche didn't answer right away. Her hand, sorting papers,
stopped for a moment.

"…Honestly, I don't know which it is either. But I've folded it into
the there's-no-way-to-know side. It's more restful that way, every
day."

"…I haven't been able to fold it away yet."

"…I know. That's very like you too."

I asked my aunt the same question that night. She glanced at me while
setting out dinner and said,

"…What's so hard about not knowing? You just don't know right now."

"…But isn't that different from there isn't one?"

"…It's different. If there isn't one, then there just isn't one. If
you don't know, you don't even know whether there is one or not."

My aunt said that very simply. So simply that I felt suddenly
deflated. What I'd been holding onto for days, she'd sorted into one
sentence. But even the sentence she'd sorted it into wasn't really a
conclusion. Not knowing whether it exists or not. That was my aunt's
answer, and it was exactly the same place I'd been holding onto for
days.

Writing this chapter, I felt the temptation, more than once, to
invent a conclusion and slip it in. I wanted to end this chapter on
some plausible-sounding line. But writing it that way would be a lie.
In those days I reached no conclusion at all. I only went back and
forth, over and over, between there isn't one and I don't know. Even
now, writing this record, I'm not so different, in truth.

Only one thing has become clear. I understood the place where Tyche
stood. I couldn't refute it. And yet I couldn't stand in that place
myself. I don't know why. I truly don't know. That's all I can write
in this chapter.

That Friday night, something happened for the first time. The
Secretary came to find me, after working hours had ended.

(Continued in Chapter 33.)

Chapter 33. The Secretary at Night

_Previously_ — For days I held onto Tyche's "there isn't one" and
Temel's "I don't know." No conclusion came. And Friday night,
something happened for the first time. The Secretary came to find me
after working hours had ended.

The Secretary was standing in the alley in front of my house. At
first I didn't recognize him. He wasn't wearing what he always wore
in the district meeting hall. He had on an outer coat, and his hands
held nothing at all — not the usual folder. That was the first time
I'd ever seen the Secretary with empty hands.

"…Secretary?"

"…I'm sorry, at this hour."

His voice was the same. The tone I always heard. Words of apology
that still didn't sound like an apology. I found it strange to
realize that his way of speaking stayed the same even outside working
hours.

"…What brings you here, this time of night?"

"…I was hoping you might have a moment."

"…How did you know where I live?"

"…It's on the roster."

That answer was so obvious it was strange, hearing it. In that moment
I felt again how much this city's roster held. No one had hidden it.
Everyone had simply never thought to open that roster and look.

Writing this now, I notice this short exchange already summarizes
this whole chapter. What surprised me wasn't that the Secretary had
found something out. It had been somewhere visible from the start.
What surprised me was that no one but me had ever gone to look.

We sat on the bench at the mouth of the alley. It was a different
bench from the one I'd sat on with Temel, but for a moment the two
nights overlapped in my mind. There was one streetlamp, and its light
was yellow.

The Secretary was silent for a long while. I didn't press him. I'd
watched, for months, how this man carried silence. He wasn't someone
who hurried. Not so much that he didn't hurry — he seemed like someone
who had no reason to.

"…This is the first time you've come to find me."

"…Yes."

"…There's something you want to say?"

"…Yes."

The Secretary answered only that, and went quiet again. I didn't think
this man was hiding anything. If there was one thing I'd learned in
months in this city, it was that these people didn't hide things. It
was only that they were slow to bring things out.

"…Last time, I looked away."

This came first. I was surprised. It was the first time the Secretary
had brought that moment up himself.

"…Yes. I remember."

"…What you asked me that day was what happens if you write a name."

"…Yes."

"…I couldn't give you an answer then. It wasn't that I didn't know the
answer."

Hearing that, I asked right back.

"…Then why?"

The Secretary looked at the night sky for a moment. I expected
something grand to come next. What came out was short.

"…If I gave you an answer, it would become my saying it."

"…What do you mean?"

"…If I say I know something, from then on it becomes my judgment. I'm
not someone who judges. I'm someone who passes things on and convenes
them."

Hearing this answer, a line I'd heard once before came back to me.
"…How do I put in something that isn't there?" That there was no
subject. I'd thought that was an excuse, at the time. But that night I
felt again that it wasn't an excuse. This man truly was afraid of
attaching a subject to himself. Not so much afraid — he sincerely
believed that wasn't his to do.

"…But why did you come today, then? You didn't come here not to
answer, surely."

At this question, the Secretary looked straight at me for the first
time.

"…I felt this was something I ought to tell you."

"…Tell me what?"

"…That matter from eleven years ago. The southern tract."

For a moment I felt my breath catch. It was the person I'd found —
the one who had written a name, opened a new deliberation, and whose
record had then stopped.

"…How do you know about that matter?"

"…I convened that one too."

That one sentence was the whole of that night. I couldn't say
anything for a long while, hearing it. It meant the Secretary had
been standing in the same seat, doing the same work, eleven years ago
too.

"…Were you the Secretary then too?"

"…I did similar work. The title was different from now."

"…Then do you remember that person? The one who wrote the name?"

The Secretary paused, very briefly, at this question. I thought that
brief pause said something, and then set the thought aside. This man
hadn't looked away, and he wasn't choosing his words. It was simply a
face confirming a fact.

"…I remember."

"…What kind of person were they?"

"…A subject of convening. Beyond that, there's nothing I can tell
you."

"…What about what became of them?"

"…I don't know."

That answer was short. But being short didn't make it sound like
evasion. I could tell this man truly didn't know. If there was one
more thing I'd learned, living months in this city, it was that
someone who truly doesn't know speaks with a different tone from
someone pretending not to know while knowing.

"…How long have you worked here, Secretary?"

"…Twenty-three years now."

"…Twenty-three years?"

"…Yes. I came in as an intake assistant, at first. And worked my way
up to this seat."

"…Have you been in this city the whole time?"

"…Yes. Never moved."

Hearing that number, I tried to gauge how long, how many convenings,
this man must have carried out in this city. I couldn't quite gauge
it. Twenty-three years was longer than I'd been alive.

"…So how many people have you convened here?"

"…I've never counted. It isn't a number that can be counted."

"…But there were exactly two people who wrote their names. The person
from eleven years ago, and Temel now."

The Secretary nodded.

"…That's right. Two, in twenty-three years."

That number held me for a long while. Two, in twenty-three years.
Between those two people lay a gap of eleven years. Through that gap,
this city had kept turning, and the signature box had stayed blank the
whole time.

"…Secretary, was that person put under review too? Like Temel is
now?"

"…Yes. Several matters' worth."

"…For how long?"

"…What remains on record is about half a year."

"…And after that?"

"…They dropped off the convening roster."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. What I know is that from a certain point on, that
name was no longer on the convening list."

"…Secretary — could that still happen now, if someone wrote their
name?"

The Secretary answered this question right away.

"…I don't know. I don't know what happened to that person, so how
could I know whether the next one would end up the same way."

"…But the review keeps going. That much is certain, isn't it?"

"…Yes, that's certain. Once a new deliberation is opened, the
proposer's name attaches to that matter. As long as that matter's
alive, that name gets called into every related confirmation. This is
in the regulations."

"…You said it isn't punishment."

"…It isn't punishment. It's confirmation. But when the confirmation
never ends, I sometimes get confused, myself, about how that's
different from punishment."

I was surprised to hear this. It was the first time the Secretary had
said he himself was confused. And even "confused" came with the
subject "myself" attached to it. That night, this was one of only a
handful of times he'd used "I" or "myself" at all.

"…So is opening a new deliberation a good thing or a bad thing?"

"…That isn't a question I can answer. The result could be good, or it
could be bad. You only find out once it's opened."

"…What do you think, Secretary?"

"…I'm not in a position to offer an opinion."

"…This isn't working hours, though."

The Secretary paused a moment at this.

"…That's true. But even this seat is, in the end, tied to the work I
do. I can't fully step away from it."

Hearing that answer, I had no choice but to accept that this man
truly knew nothing more than this. The Consensus hides nothing. The
Secretary hid nothing either. It was only that there were things even
the Consensus didn't know, things even the Secretary didn't know, and
probably things no one in this city knew.

In that moment I felt something hollow. I think I'd half been hoping
this man would finally unburden some hidden truth he'd been carrying.
But there was nothing like that. He truly said only as much as he
knew, and said "I don't know" for what he didn't. I only confirmed,
that day, all over again, that this was this city's truly frightening
part.

"…Secretary, back then, what did you think, watching that person
write their name?"

The Secretary looked at me a moment at this question. His expression
didn't change much.

"…I thought it was unusual."

"…That's all?"

"…At the time. As the convening matters kept growing, later on I had
a few other thoughts too."

"…What thoughts?"

"…Why this person kept coming, I wondered. They came, every time, to
a seat they didn't have to come to."

"…You were the one calling them in."

"…Yes. I convened them. But being convened doesn't mean everyone
comes. There are people who don't come."

"…What happens if they don't?"

"…Processing gets delayed. That's all. That isn't punishment either."

"…But this person came, every time?"

"…Yes. Never once missed. Right up to the last convening."

Hearing that, I asked again.

"…The last convening? Did you have any sense, then, of what might
happen to that person?"

"…No. That convening was an ordinary one too. As far as I knew."

"…But after that, they were gone."

"…Yes."

"…Doesn't that seem strange to you?"

At this question, the Secretary was quiet a little longer than
before, for the first time.

"…It's strange. But it being strange doesn't mean there's anything I
can do."

I couldn't answer that. The Secretary didn't apologize. He didn't say
he'd done anything wrong, either. He was simply drawing, precisely,
the line between what he knew and what he could do.

"…Why did you tell me this tonight?"

The Secretary answered as he rose from the bench.

"…I received a report that you'd found that document. Reading room
records are kept. If I was connected to it, I thought it was right to
tell you myself."

"…You received a report? From whom?"

"…It comes up periodically from the reading room. Who has read a
document carrying a particular mark."

Hearing that answer, a small chill went through me. But that wasn't
hidden either. That reading records were kept had been an open fact
from the start. I simply hadn't known how they were passed on, or to
whom. Not knowing had been on my side.

"…So that's just procedure too, then?"

"…Yes. Procedure."

"…So you came to find me because of procedure, Secretary?"

At this question, the Secretary's expression shifted, slightly, for
the first time. It wasn't a smile. But it wasn't quite blank, either.

"…No. This isn't procedure. I came because I thought I should."

That one line stayed with me the longest of anything I heard that
night. That it wasn't procedure. Even as he said it, the word "I"
sounded awkward in his mouth. Like clothes he wasn't used to wearing.

"…Could you come like this again, next time?"

The Secretary thought a moment.

"…I don't know. I suppose that will be decided when the time comes."

With that, the Secretary said goodbye and turned back. He never did
apologize. I'd thought he had something to apologize for. But instead
of an apology, he'd given me a fact. I learned, for the first time
that night, that a fact can weigh more than an apology.

I remember this night for a long time. I can't write that I came to
like this man. I can't quite write that I came not to dislike him,
either. But this — that he'd carried out the same work, in the same
seat, for eleven years running, and that in all those eleven years, he
had come to find me exactly once with a step that wasn't procedure —
these two facts set side by side, I still don't know how to put them
together.

The next morning, I told Tyche briefly what had happened the night
before. Tyche listened quietly and said,

"…He told you that, himself?"

"…Yes."

"…That's surprising. I didn't think he'd say that much."

"…Do you not know the Secretary well either, Tyche?"

"…I've worked with him for years and I still don't. He's always in
that seat, but nobody seems to know what's behind it."

"…Is that strange?"

"…No. There are a lot of people like that in this city. The longer
they've worked, the more so. I think erasing yourself becomes a
habit."

"…Doesn't that make you sad, Tyche?"

Tyche thought a moment and said,

"…Not sad, exactly. I'm scared, sometimes, that I might become that
way too."

That was unexpected. I'd never thought Tyche carried a fear like
that.

"…You're afraid you'll end up like him?"

"…Yes. I like procedure. But if I like procedure for too long, I'm
scared that someday I'll be someone speaking without a subject too,
just like him."

"…You don't seem like you would, to me."

"…I don't know. He probably wasn't like that from the start, either."

With that, Tyche went back to sorting papers. I watched her back for a
moment, then thought of the seat the Secretary must have stood in for
twenty-three years.

Walking home, I looked up at the western sky once. I had no idea, that
evening, that I would soon hear about a sound coming from the western
water channel, different from its usual sound.

(Continued in Chapter 34.)

Chapter 34. The Water Reaches

_Previously_ — The Secretary came to find me outside working hours
for the first time. He said he had handled the convening for that
deliberation eleven years ago too. "…I convened that one too." There
was no apology. That evening, I heard that a sound different from
usual was coming from the western water channel.

My aunt stopped in the middle of setting out dinner.

"…Did you hear about the western ditch? They say the water's making
a strange, loud sound today."

"…Strange how?"

"…I don't know, I only heard it secondhand. Are you going to go
look?"

I set down my bowl and put on my coat. My aunt didn't stop me. She
knew too — in this city, if you're curious about something, going to
look is the fastest way to find out.

Outside it was already dim. Walking through a few alleys, my heart
beat strangely fast. I didn't quite know why. Whenever I felt
something was changing, I always hurried toward it. I don't know
myself when that habit began.

The sound of water reached me from the western alley on. It was
clearly louder than usual. Too widespread a sound to be coming from a
single ditch.

When I reached the end of the alley, I saw why.

The water channel had widened. It used to be a narrow ditch you could
barely set one foot across; now it was twice that width. The current
had picked up too. The water, once the color of earth, looked a
little clearer. It seemed as though more water had come pushing in
from somewhere beyond the city's boundary.

Watching this that night, I truly felt that this water had come
"inside" this city. When I'd first seen it, it had been flowing
somewhere at the western edge of the city, out at the boundary. Not
anymore. The channel had already worked its way into the very alleys
we walked through every day.

People had come out to stand beside it. Few faces looked startled.
Most looked curious. Two children had gone in barefoot and were
splashing at the water's edge, and a few adults stood watching them,
laughing.

I ran to the front desk. One person was on the night shift.

"…Excuse me — the western water channel. It's come inside the city.
Where do I report this?"

The clerk tapped at the screen. He searched a long while and shook his
head.

"…There's no matter number for this."

"…What do you mean, no matter number?"

"…To file a report there has to be a related matter, but this water
channel itself has never come before a matter. I don't know what to
file this under."

"…So you just can't do it?"

"…I can't open a new matter on my own judgment. That's outside my
authority."

I'd expected that answer, and still, hearing it out loud, it left me
stifled.

What Tyche had said before came back to me. "If it's never been
submitted, it doesn't exist. Not hidden — just not submitted." I'd
thought I understood that, at the time. But I'd only understood it in
my head. Only now, watching the water actually rise around my own
feet, did I feel in my body how frightening that sentence really
was.

The next day, on my way to the district hall, I ran into the
Secretary. It was the first time since that night a few days before.
I brought up the water channel.

"…Secretary, do you know about the western water channel? It's come
inside the city."

"…I know."

"…Then why isn't anything being done?"

"…There's no matter for it."

"…Can't you make a matter, Secretary?"

"…What I can make is a convening, not a matter. A matter only exists
once the department with jurisdiction takes it in."

"…Then which department has jurisdiction?"

The Secretary thought a moment.

"…This water channel's jurisdiction has never been settled. Matters
involving the city boundary belong to the outskirts management
department; matters involving water supply belong to the water supply
department. Which of the two this falls under, no one's decided yet."

"…So no one can do anything?"

"…Someone first has to propose which department it belongs to. Only
then can a matter be opened."

"…Can't you do that, Secretary?"

"…I could propose it. But I haven't yet judged this to be a problem."

"…Water's come into the city and that isn't a problem?"

"…Something bad has to happen for it to be a problem. So far, nothing
bad has happened."

That answer was exactly what Tyche had said. That two different
people had said the same thing confirmed for me, again, that this was
how the whole city worked.

That afternoon I went to the reading room and searched for whether
anything had ever come into this city this way before, without a
matter behind it. I spent half a day changing my search terms, over
and over. I found almost nothing. A few very old records turned up,
all trivial things. A tree that had grown up on its own by the
roadside. A garden plot that had grown up though no one had planted
it. Since neither had ever come before a matter, there wasn't even a
record of when the tree or the garden had first appeared. It was only
written that they'd simply been there, from some point on.

Looking at these records, I thought this city might have been built
this way from the start. Things that had gone through deliberation,
and things that had simply grown up with no one noticing. This city
called only the first kind "the city," and treated the second kind as
though it weren't part of the city at all. And yet the second kind
was plainly there too. The tree, the garden plot, and now this water
channel.

I asked the reading room clerk.

"…Have things like this ever come before a matter, later on?"

"…Sometimes. If someone feels it's a bother. But that tree's still
standing there without a matter, even now. So's the garden plot."

"…So if no one's bothered, it just stays that way forever?"

"…Seems like it. I'd never thought that was strange before, but
hearing you say it, it does seem a little strange."

"…So this water channel will just keep on being like this, until
someone's bothered by it?"

"…Probably. But everyone likes it, don't they? Is anyone likely to be
bothered?"

I couldn't answer that question. If truly no one was bothered, this
water channel would stay outside any matter forever. I learned, that
day, for the first time, that even a good thing could stay outside a
matter. The clerk said this and turned her eyes back to the screen.
The conversation ended there.

The next morning, the channel had widened further. The city still
hadn't dealt with it. Not so much hadn't dealt with it — there simply
was no procedure to deal with it at all.

On my way to the district hall I met Tyche. She knew about the water
channel too.

"…This really isn't going to become a matter?"

"…Someone has to bring it up for it to become one. It doesn't rise on
its own."

"…Then who's supposed to bring it up?"

"…I don't know that either. Normally, something like this needs a
settled jurisdiction before it can be raised, but this channel doesn't
have one settled either."

"…So it just stays like this forever?"

Tyche thought a moment.

"…No. Once someone feels it's a problem and raises it as a matter,
it'll get handled. But right now, no one seems to feel it's a
problem."

"…Water's come right into the middle of the city and that isn't a
problem?"

"…People like it. They drink it, children play in it. If it isn't
something bad, no one brings it before a matter. This city's only
built to respond to bad things."

That was true. And that truth was what chilled me.

All that week the channel kept widening, little by little. The city
still did nothing. People drew the water and used it. Did laundry in
it. Children splashed in it. One household brought a jar and filled
it there.

Near the market, someone set out a bench beside the channel. In the
evenings people gathered there, sitting with their feet in the water,
talking. Watching that scene, I felt again how quickly the people of
this city adjusted to something new. The astonishment didn't last more
than a few days. By the end of a week, everyone was acting as though
this water had always been there.

Someone built a small water wheel and set it up in the current. It
was a child's toy, but grown-ups would turn it once, passing by, and
laugh. Watching that water wheel, I felt something strange. On water
that had come before no matter, with no one's permission, someone had
built something with their own hands and set it up. That was a rare
sight in this city.

A child once showed me the water wheel, proud of it. "Look, I made
this myself." I told her she'd made it well. She turned the wheel a
few more times, delighted. That child had no curiosity at all about
where this water had come from, or whether it was in any matter. All
that mattered to her was that the water was there, and the wheel
turned.

Watching that child, I remembered what Tyche always said. That
nothing gets treated as a problem unless it's something bad. For that
child, this was exactly the right attitude. It wasn't something bad.
I felt no urge at all to grab her and ask, "Doesn't this feel
strange?" That wasn't hers to carry.

I'd thought this might be dangerous, but nothing happened. The water
was clear, and no one who drank it fell ill. It wasn't something bad.
It truly wasn't something bad.

Writing this passage, I wonder whether I myself had been hoping
there was something bad in this water channel. If there had been
something bad, it would have been easier, in a way — there would have
been a reason to bring it before a matter. But there was nothing bad.
It was simply good water, come into the city with no procedure at
all. That was what unsettled me most.

My aunt drew that water and used it too. One evening I saw her
carrying a water jar on her head and asked,

"…Aunt, did you check whether this is safe to drink?"

"…Check what. Everyone drinks it."

"…But we don't even know where it came from."

My aunt set the jar down and said,

"…Hora, water's always been something you drink without knowing where
it came from. Whether it comes from the sky or from a mountain.
There's nothing strange about that."

I couldn't answer that. It wasn't wrong.

That night, the woman from next door came over and talked with my
aunt about the water channel. Both of them talked about it as though
it were old, familiar weather. "The water's come in early this year."
"Right, and it's stronger than last year's." Listening to that tone, I
felt this water channel had already entered this city's everyday
language. It wasn't in any matter, but it was in their speech.

I think this is the quietest passage in this chapter. No great event,
no great line. Just two people talking about the water channel the way
they'd talk about the weather. But that quiet was what frightened me
most. I'd thought frightening things came loudly. This one simply
walked, quietly, into an evening's small talk.

That evening, I walked along the water channel. I wanted to see, with
my own eyes, how deep it had come into the city.

The channel ran from the western alley all the way to near the
market. Its width wasn't even. Narrow in some places, wide enough in
others for people to sit with their feet in it.

Someone I'd never seen before was sitting by the water. He had taken
off a pair of worn shoes beside the water and sat with his feet in
it, quietly looking at the water.

I walked past him. No one else looked at him. My own eyes went to him
only for a moment, and I didn't stop walking either. There was always
someone sitting by the water in this city; it wasn't anything unusual.

Looking back on that evening, I can't remember any one thing I saw by
the water as anything special. The children, the people doing
laundry, the people carrying water jars. And that man. All of it was
just the scenery of the waterside. I didn't fix my eyes on any of it,
that day. That itself struck me as strange, only much later.

Walking home, I ran into Tyche again. She was carrying a water jar.

"…You use this water too, Tyche?"

"…Yes. Everyone else does; there's no reason I shouldn't."

"…But doesn't it seem strange? Everyone using water that isn't even
in any matter?"

Tyche shifted her grip on the jar and said,

"…It's strange. But strange and bad are different things. I only
worry about the bad kind."

"…I worry about the strange kind too."

"…That's the part where you and I differ."

Tyche said that and smiled. It wasn't an unkind smile. It was just a
smile confirming that we were different.

Watching this water channel, I felt it again. The Consensus had
hidden nothing about this water. There was nothing to hide. It was
simply that no one had ever brought it before a matter. And yet that
fact — that no one had brought it up — hadn't stopped the water from
rising into the middle of the city. If a system only stops something
once something bad has happened, then anything that isn't bad can go
on coming in, without any check at all, for as long as it likes.

If this city was always asking me one question, it was probably
this: is something no one did the same as nothing happening at all?
I had only ever thought of this question in terms of people — the
person from eleven years ago, the four hundred people, the signature
box. But watching the water channel, I understood for the first time
that this same question applied just as much to something that wasn't
a person. Water that no one had blocked kept coming in, for no other
reason than that no one had blocked it. It was good water. But the
way it came in, itself, wouldn't let me rest easy.

I left my window open that night. The sound of water drifted faintly
down the alley. I fell asleep listening to it. The next day, I decided
to take a step I'd been putting off for a long time — going to meet,
in person, the one I had only ever known by number.

(Continued in Chapter 35.)

Chapter 35. One of the Seventh Classification

_Previously_ — The water channel had come inside the city, but with
no matter behind it, no one dealt with it. People simply began using
the water. That night, I decided to take a step I'd been putting off
for a long time — going to meet, in person, the one I had only ever
known by number.

The deliberation I'd first been assigned, long ago, concerned people
grouped under the Seventh Classification. The summary had been
accurate, the procedure had followed the regulations, and it was only
after reading the full text to the end that I'd understood what was
actually happening. Of the people reassigned by that deliberation, I
had never, in all this time, actually met a single one. The roster
held only numbers. 7-118, 7-119, 7-120. No name appeared anywhere.

I still don't think being called only by a number is a bad system, in
this city. There really was discrimination that it reduced. It's only
that, somewhere along the way, it kept nagging at me that I had never
once seen the person behind that number.

The day after I saw the water channel, I found the first number on
that roster and went to the reassignment district. It was about an
hour's walk.

The whole way there, I practiced what I would say. I tried starting
with, "Hello, I'm a district-assembly assistant member," but it
sounded too official, and I gave that up. "Do you happen to remember
me" was strange too — there was no reason a person I'd never met
would remember me. In the end I arrived at the entrance of the
district without having settled on a single sentence.

Writing this chapter, I admit again how long I'd put off this walk.
Earlier I'd said I wanted to meet that warehouse manager I only knew
by number. And still I put it off for months after that. I told
myself, at the time, that it was because I was busy. Looking at it
now, that was an excuse. I think I was simply afraid.

The reassignment district was newly built communal housing at the
city's eastern edge. The buildings were clean, well kept. I hesitated
a moment out front. I'd made up my mind to come, but standing at the
door I still didn't know what to say.

I gave my number at the front desk and stated my purpose. I said I was
a district-assembly assistant member, and that I had something to
confirm relating to a past deliberation. That much explanation was
enough. No one in this city pressed for more.

"…You're 7-118?"

The desk clerk sent word inside. A little while later, a person came
out.

They looked much older than me — around my aunt's age, maybe. Their
clothes were plain, their hands rough. There was no particular wariness
in their face at seeing me.

"…You're from the district assembly?"

"…Yes. I'm Hora."

"…Hora. What brings you here?"

Their voice was calm. Not knowing how to begin, I simply told the
truth.

"…The thirteenth deliberation I was ever assigned was the Seventh
Classification matter. I read the full text to the end, that time, and
since then I've never actually met anyone who really knows what
happened to them because of that deliberation."

"…So you came to find me?"

"…Yes. It might sound strange, but I just wanted to meet you."

They looked at me a moment, and smiled slightly.

"…It's not strange. If anything, you're the first person who's ever
come to me over something like this."

We sat on a bench outside the building. They spoke first.

"…I know that deliberation. It was the result that came down to me."

"…How was it, at the time?"
