"…Angry, at first. But looking back, it wasn't all bad. I got a place
to live here, and work too."

"…But the deliberation document only had the reassignment in it. It
didn't say how you were actually living, after."

"…That would be right. A document only writes down as far as the
decision. What comes after is just living, so."

That was new to me. Up until then I'd thought reading a deliberation
document to the end meant knowing everything. But life went on after
the document. That was a part no document could hold.

"…Where did you live before?"

"…South side of the city. I did small farm work there. Here, instead
of farm work, I manage this housing complex. It wasn't work my hands
knew, but I'm used to it now."

"…Did you like the farm work better?"

"…It's less about liking it or not — it was work I'd done for over
twenty years. My body didn't take to a different kind of work right
away, when I started."

"…That doesn't show up in the document either."

"…No. The document just says 'reassignment complete.' It doesn't hold
things like how long it took my hands to get used to it."

Hearing this, I thought of that warehouse manager Tyche and I had read
about, side by side. The one moved to office work — they were
probably going through something like a similar span of time right
now, I thought. I decided, in that moment, that I needed to go meet
that person too, someday.

"…You're 7-118. But what's your real name?"

Asking this, I worried for a moment whether I was being rude. But they
didn't seem to mind at all.

"…I have one, of course."

"…Could I ask what it is?"

They looked at me a long while, then said,

"…I'll tell you. But why do you want to know?"

"…Just — I wanted to call you something besides a number."

They told me their name.

I won't write that name here. There are several people in this
record whose names I keep from the page, but this person's is the one
I hold back the longest. I'll write the reason in chapter 48. For
now, I'll only set down the moment of hearing it.

Hearing that name, I felt strangely at ease. That there really was a
name behind a person I'd only known by number felt like a confirmation
I shouldn't have needed to make, and yet, needless as it was, it
landed heavily in me.

"…That's a pretty name."

"…Thank you. But no one calls me by it here."

"…Why not?"

"…The number's easier. It's on all the paperwork, and everyone calls
me by it. It hurt at first, but now I just take it as how things are."

"…It hurt, but you got used to it?"

"…Not so much got used to it — I just don't have the strength to be
hurt by it every time. Living comes first."

That sounded like something Tyche often said. Doing today's work. And
yet this person's "today's work" carried a different weight than
Tyche's had.

"…Are you all right now, then?"

"…I'm all right. The first few months after the reassignment were
hard, but I'm settled here now. There's work, there are neighbors."

"…Do you think everything about that deliberation turned out well?"

They thought a moment at this question.

"…Not everything. A few things I still don't understand. Why I had
to come here specifically, why nowhere else would do. But I have to
save my strength for that too — questioning it."

"…Temel said something about saving strength too."

"…Who?"

"…Ah, never mind. Someone I know said something similar."

Just then a child ran up and pressed close beside them. Looked around
seven or eight.

"…My neighbor's child. We got close since I came here."

The child looked me over and asked,

"…What's your number?"

I didn't understand for a moment.

"…Your number. Everyone here has one."

"…Ah — I don't live here, so I don't have a number."

The child looked at me, astonished.

"…There are people without numbers?"

The person beside her stroked her hair and laughed.

"…There are more people without numbers outside than with them, you
know."

The child ran off again, looking unconvinced.

I turned this short exchange over for a long time. To this child, a
number wasn't strange — it was simply how things were. I was the
strange one, for having none. Watching a child who'd grown up without
a number outside this city grow up, inside it, taking a number for
granted, I felt something I couldn't quite name. No one here was a
bad person, and still that seat didn't sit easily with me.

"…Why are you this curious, Hora? About someone like me?"

I couldn't answer this right away. I thought a moment and said,

"…I've only ever seen people inside documents. Only by number. But
that isn't really that person, is it. I wanted to know that."

"…What changes, knowing it?"

"…I don't know. I just think — once I know, I'll read that document
differently, the next time."

They nodded.

"…That sounds like reason enough to me."

I held onto that answer for a long time. Reason enough. I'd been
doubting, myself, up until then, whether what I was doing had reason
enough behind it. And of all people, the person who had actually been
inside that document was the one who told me my reason was enough.
That, strangely, left me feeling lighter.

"…But I still don't really understand what the Seventh Classification
actually is," I asked. "It's just a category, isn't it. But what
standard is inside it."

"…I don't know exactly either. I was just told, one day, that I'd
been placed in that classification."

"…Don't they tell you the reason?"

"…They do. It's all in the document. It's divided by a few standards —
residential district, occupation, household makeup. I read that
document too. But even reading it, I couldn't quite make out why
those particular standards had put me here, of all places."

"…Doesn't that seem strange to you?"

"…It does. But it isn't just me who's strange about it — hundreds of
people similar to me came in here together. So it wasn't like I was
singled out. That's a small comfort, at least."

That echoed something Tyche had said, talking about the four hundred
people. That no one was singled out. I only truly felt, for the first
time, how that could be a comfort, hearing it in this person's voice.
Reading it in a document, it had just been a number. Now, the person
behind that number was taking that same fact as their own comfort.

In that moment I thought of Tyche again. She would have explained
this situation in exactly those terms. And this person, in much the
same way Tyche did, was steadying themselves. Watching this, I
confirmed again that Tyche's logic really was, in this city, a way
that let people hold on. That's the reason I can't bring myself to
hate that logic.

The sun was going down. As I stood to leave, I asked one last thing.

"…Could I come see you again?"

"…Of course. Any time."

"…Next time, could I ask you about that warehouse manager too? I
wondered if you might know them."

"…Well, all sorts of people from all sorts of districts end up living
mixed together here. I can ask around for you."

"…Thank you."

"…But Hora — if you go looking for people one at a time like this,
when will you meet them all? There must be quite a few of us."

That made me laugh.

"…I might not meet everyone. But meeting one at a time seemed better
than not meeting anyone at all."

"…That's true too."

"…But next time, what should I call you? By number, or by name?"

They smiled a little.

"…Whichever's easier for you. Either's fine with me."

I said goodbye and turned to go. The whole way home, I repeated that
name inside myself, over and over. I didn't write it on paper. This
time, I decided not to put it in the drawer. I wanted to keep this
name somewhere other than that drawer. I didn't know exactly where,
even then.

Writing this record now, I still don't set that name down here. I'll
wait until chapter 48. Until that day, this name stays with me alone.
I think that's the most honest thing I can do, writing this chapter.

I got home and told my aunt what had happened that day. She listened
quietly and said,

"…Good that you got the name, then."

"…Yeah. But strangely, just that seems to have changed something."

"…What changed?"

I couldn't answer that right away. I only had the feeling that
something had changed. Putting that feeling into words would take more
time.

My aunt asked again.

"…Aren't you going to tell me the name either?"

"…No. Not yet."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. I just want to keep it that way."

My aunt didn't press further. Instead she said this.

"…You know, Hora, I've noticed lately — you keep holding things back.
The papers, and now this name too."

"…Is that strange?"

"…Not strange, but I do wonder what it's all for."

I couldn't answer that either. Even I didn't know exactly what it was
all for. But the act of holding something back, itself, felt like the
only way I knew to treat something as precious.

Writing this chapter, I bring that evening back again. For a while
after receiving that name, I grew strangely careful. Even reading a
document, whenever I saw a number, I became aware there might be a
name behind it. What had changed wasn't the document. The document
stayed the same. What had changed was me.

That night before falling asleep, I shaped that name silently with my
lips, without any sound. Somehow, saying it out loud felt like it
would use it up. Why I felt that way, I still can't explain exactly,
even now. I'll only set down here that the night passed quietly, that
way.

(Continued in Chapter 36.)

Chapter 36. Three Days

_Previously_ — I met, in person, one of the Seventh Classification
I had only ever known by number. That person had a name. I heard
that name and passed it on to no one. I didn't put it in the
drawer. That night I shaped it silently with my lips, without any
sound.

The next morning, getting ready for the district hall as usual, I
looked at the documents stacked on my desk. Three new matters had
been assigned the day before. I picked one up, and set it back down.

I didn't want to read it.

Writing this sentence, I still find it strange. Through this whole
book I've introduced myself as "someone who reads." Reading was the
one thing I was good at. And that morning, I didn't want to do the one
thing.

Why I felt that way, I still can't explain in a single sentence, even
now. It probably had something to do with how much I'd heard in just
a few days. Tyche's there isn't one, Temel's I don't know, the
Secretary's convening from eleven years ago, and now that name behind
a number. I hadn't come to know any of it by reading. Most of it I'd
come to know by hearing it, or living through it. And yet I felt
myself wanting to sort it all back into something like a document, and
I didn't like that in myself.

I don't know exactly why I disliked that urge to sort things, either.
Sorting isn't a bad thing. I've gotten through, all this time, by
sorting. It's only that, that morning, I wanted to choose leaving
things be, over sorting them. If you asked me for a reason, I couldn't
give one. That's the honest start of this chapter.

My aunt saw this and asked,

"…What's wrong, are you not feeling well?"

"…No. I just don't want to read, today."

My aunt wasn't especially surprised.

"…All right. Then rest."

I hadn't meant to rest. I didn't know exactly what I meant to do,
then, either. My hand simply wouldn't go to the document.

The first day, I did nothing at all. I sent word to the district hall
that I wasn't feeling well. That wasn't a lie. I couldn't explain
exactly what was wrong, but something was wrong, truly.

At home that day, my eyes went to the documents on the desk more than
once. I stopped my hand from reaching for them, more than once. I
learned, that day, for the first time, how strong a habit could be.

With nothing to do, I looked for something I could do with my hands. I
sat beside my aunt and helped with her sewing. Threaded needles,
sewed on buttons. While I concentrated on my fingertips, thoughts of
letters came less often. My aunt seemed to find it curious that I was
sitting beside her, and glanced at me more than once.

Around noon I went out to the back garden and repotted some plants.
While I handled the soil, my hands stopped thinking of other things.
With soil on my hands, I couldn't touch paper anyway. I liked that. It
felt like my body was, on its own, keeping me away from reading.

"…You don't usually do this."

"…I wanted to, today."

"…Bored?"

"…No. I just wanted to keep my hands moving at something."

My aunt didn't ask further and handed me another piece of sewing. I
took it and focused a long while on the sound of thread passing
through cloth.

Tyche stopped by that evening, worried about me.

"…Are you sick somewhere?"

"…No. I'm just not going to read anything today."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know. I just want to."

Tyche looked at me a moment and said,

"…That's not like you, Hora."

"…I know."

Saying that, I truly felt that I wasn't like myself. And at the same
time, I felt this was the most like myself thing I could do right now.
That the two feelings could exist at once was the strangest part of
that night.

On the second day, I learned that not reading anything was far
harder than I'd expected.

Out on the street, letters caught my eye everywhere. A notice in a
shop window, a bulletin on a wall, a paper in a passerby's hand. I
turned my eyes away on purpose to avoid reading them. I hadn't known
how hard that was until I decided not to read.

It was harder still at home. I kept thinking of the twelve pages my
mother had left, of the papers in that drawer. I didn't open the
drawer. If I opened it, I would surely end up reading something.

On the way back from the distribution station, I walked with my eyes
half lowered. Notices, announcements, posted bulletins. All of it
looked as though it were trying to say something to me. I worked to
take in none of it, and that cost far more energy than I'd expected.
By the time I got home I was far more exhausted than usual, though I
hadn't done any physical work at all.

I thought hard that night about where this exhaustion had come from.
My conclusion was that not reading was, in the end, an act in itself.
It wasn't doing nothing — it was continuing to choose not to read.
Choosing takes effort. I hadn't known that, until then. I'd always
thought reading was simply something I did without thinking.

What these three days meant to me, I didn't know exactly, at the
time. I don't know exactly now, writing this either. But I know one
thing. In these three days I decided nothing. I didn't do anything
you could call prayer. I never once asked to be shown something. I
simply didn't read.

My aunt asked on the evening of the second day.

"…Are you waiting for something?"

"…No."

"…Then why are you like this?"

"…I don't really know myself. It just feels like not reading is the
right thing right now."

My aunt didn't press further, hearing that. Instead she spooned an
extra scoop of rice into my bowl.

On the third day, I ran out of things to do inside the house. I
cleaned, folded laundry, and once all that was done, the afternoon
was left.

I went outside and walked along the water channel. The water was still
making its sound. I listened to it. Listening to a sound wasn't
reading.

Through these three days, trying not to look at letters, I ended up
listening to sound a great deal instead. The water's sound, people's
voices, the noise of the market. Things that were usually drowned out
by letters came through unusually loud, during those three days.

Passing through the market, I walked by the notice board. It was the
same spot where Temel used to stand. A few sheets were posted there. I
walked by without reading them. My steps slowed a little, though.

People were still sitting on the bench by the water, feet in the
current. I sat there too, for a moment. I didn't take off my shoes. I
just sat and listened to the sound of the water.

Sitting on that bench, I felt, for the first time in a long while,
what it was to do nothing at all. Not reading, not worrying — just
sitting and listening to water. I realized then how rare a time like
that had been for me. I was always reading something, or thinking
about what I'd read, or choosing what to read next.

An old man sitting nearby spoke to me.

"…Do you come here often, miss?"

"…I've been coming often, lately."

"…Something good must be happening. You look at ease."

"…Not something good, exactly. I'm just taking a few days off."

"…Resting's the best thing there is."

The old man said that and looked back at the water. I turned that
line over in my mind. Resting's the best thing there is. I didn't
fully agree with it, but somehow, that day, I didn't want to argue
either.

I sat a long while longer, listening to the water brush over the top
of my feet. The sun leaned a little lower. No one hurried me.

I ran into the Secretary by chance, in front of the front desk.

"…You haven't been in for a few days, Hora."

"…No. Personal matters."

"…Are you all right?"

"…Yes. I'm just trying a few days without reading."

The Secretary looked at me a moment, hearing that. He didn't ask what
it meant.

"…Then go on that way. When you're back, I'll help you catch up on
what's piled up."

"…Thank you."

That was all. The Secretary didn't press for the reason, and I didn't
explain it. This short exchange, that day, felt strangely restful to
me.

Turning to go, the Secretary added one more thing.

"…Hora."

"…Yes?"

"…Not reading anything at all seems like its own kind of way, too."

Saying that, the Secretary went back inside the front desk. I stood a
long while, trying to work out what he meant. I couldn't tell whether
it was meant as comfort, or simply an observation. Probably, for this
man, the two weren't different things.

That evening, the third day, Tyche came again.

"…Are you done now? It's been three days."

"…You were counting?"

"…Yes. I was curious."

"…I still don't really know. But I'm going to stop after tonight."

"…What's changed, not reading?"

I thought a long while before answering.

"…Nothing in particular came into view. I just learned how much I've
been living leaning on reading."

"…Is that bad?"

"…No, it isn't bad. But leaning on it, and being able to get through
without it — those seemed like different questions to me."

Tyche nodded.

"…If you found that out, then three days wasn't wasted."

I still don't quite know whether I can call these three days a fast.
I asked for nothing. There was no revelation. The sky didn't open, and
no one spoke to me. For three days, I simply didn't do the one thing I
was best at.

Before leaving, Tyche asked,

"…But why three days, specifically? Not four, not two."

"…I don't know. Three just felt right."

"…On what basis?"

"…None. It was just a feeling."

Tyche laughed at that.

"…It's been a while since I heard you say there's no basis for
something."

"…I know. It's strange to me too."

Writing this conversation, I laugh again. Tyche knew I was someone
who always tried to give a basis for everything. That I'd chosen three
days with no basis at all must have struck her as funny. It did me
too.

My aunt said one thing at my door that night before bed.

"…You'll go back to reading tomorrow?"

"…Yes."

"…Good, then. I figured you couldn't keep it up much longer anyway."

"…I thought so too."

My aunt gave a short laugh and closed the door. I lay in the dark and
went back over those three days. Nothing special had happened. I
simply hadn't read for three days, and for those three days the world
had gone on turning exactly as always. The water channel had kept
flowing, the market had kept bustling, and the signature box had
stayed blank, I imagined, the whole time. Whether I read or not, none
of it waited for me. That fact didn't make me feel small, and it
didn't make me feel large either. It only let me know that this was
so.

On the morning of the fourth day, I picked up the document on my desk
again. My hand felt a little unfamiliar. Like something I hadn't
held in a long time.

I read the first line. The familiar feeling came right back. I read
that document to the end, as I always did. There was nothing special
about it. It was an ordinary matter.

Reading it, I noticed something strange. Three days of rest hadn't
given me some new pair of eyes. I read at the same speed as before,
still read to the end, still looked for the signature box first. Not a
single thing seemed to have changed.

But there was exactly one small difference. Before, reading had been
something like breathing to me. It just happened on its own. Now,
reading felt like something I was choosing again, every single time.
If you stop breathing, you die. Reading was something I could live
without. I'd confirmed that in my body, over those three days. Which
meant that what I was doing now, reading, was something I'd chosen to
do even though I could have not done it.

This might sound like a grand realization. But I don't want to write
it that way. Just as a person doesn't suddenly become someone else
after three days without food, I didn't become someone else after
three days without reading. I'd simply done, again, after not doing
it, the thing I'd always done. Beyond that, truly, nothing happened at
all.

I feel the temptation to write that these three days took me nowhere.
But that doesn't seem exactly right either. I was still myself, three
days later. Only, over those three days, I confirmed — not without
choosing, but by choosing — for the first time, that I was someone who
reads. Whether that's a small difference or a large one, I still don't
know exactly, even now.

Having read the document to the end, I looked at the signature box at
the bottom. Blank. As always.

I looked at that box a long while. A little longer than before.

Tyche, sorting papers at the next seat, looked over at me.

"…How does it feel, reading again?"

"…The same. But the same feels strange, somehow."

"…What does?"

"…Nothing changed in three days, and yet I feel like I confirmed
something."

"…What did you confirm?"

I answered without taking my eyes off the signature box.

"…That I keep looking at this. That I didn't have to, and I keep
looking at it anyway."

Tyche didn't say much in response to that. She just nodded once and
turned her eyes back to her papers. I found that silence restful.

Recording these three days, I've decided to attach no ending to this
chapter. The temptation came, more than once, to write it as though
something great had begun. But that isn't true. What I did, after
three days, was simply pick up a document. Only, that hand, the same
hand as three days before, felt slightly different to me. I don't yet
have the words to explain that difference.

(Continued in Part Four.)

The Unsigned Book, Part Four

The Name

Chapter 37. What It Is to Write It

_Previously_ — Hora read no documents for three days. Reading was the
only thing she knew how to do, so stopping it was her fast.

On the third night I opened the drawer.

The same drawer that had held my mother's papers. Other things live there
now. A notice, a copy of a summary, and the second page of the work
guidelines I'd first been given. I took that page out and unfolded it.

There was a box at the bottom. A long, horizontal box. The signature box.

I had seen this box more times than I could count. Every day for over
two hundred days. It was on every document, and empty on every document.
I thought that was the rule. I still haven't fully learned, in this
city, how to tell a rule from a habit.

Below the box, in small print, it read: (This notice does not require a
signature.)

Does not require. I read that sentence again. The same sentence I'd read
two hundred days before read differently to me now.

Does not require doesn't mean do not. I already knew this. I'd learned
it back in Part Three. But knowing it, and holding it in your hand and
looking at it quietly, are different things.

For three days I read no documents.

At first I thought I was simply resting. From the second day it started
to feel strange. My hand kept reaching for the paper on the table. The
third time my hand reached for it, I realized what I'd been doing. I
wasn't holding myself back from reading — the habit of reading had
worked its way into my body and was moving on its own.

I already called this a fast at the end of Part Three. I didn't know
exactly why, then. Writing it again now, I can say it a little more
precisely. Reading was how I proved myself to myself. When I stopped
reading, I no longer knew what I was proven by.

On the afternoon of the third day, Tyche stopped by the house. She came
even though it wasn't work hours.

"…Are you all right?"

"…Yeah. Just not reading."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer. Tyche didn't press further either. She sat for a
while and then left. At the door she turned back once, as if to say
something, and didn't. I only understood what that had been much later.

I thought of the first day I'd asked the Secretary. When I'd asked what
happens if you write your name, he'd looked away.

After that I asked several other people the same thing. I looked for a
rulebook. I looked for a prohibition. There wasn't one. There really
wasn't one. All there was, was the box.

Nobody stopped me. I've written this sentence many times already, and I
will write it many more. It is the most repeated sentence I say in this
book. Nobody stopped me.

If someone had stopped me, it would have been easier, in a way. If
there's someone stopping you, you can be angry at that person. But there
was no one. The box was simply empty. Built, from the start, so that
anyone could fill it.

My aunt opened my door without knocking. She's a person who doesn't
knock. She's been that way since I was small.

"…Still not asleep?"

"…No."

My aunt came in and sat beside me. She looked at the paper and said
nothing. After a long while she asked.

"…What's that?"

"…Work guidelines. The first ones I got."

"…Why are you looking at that again?"

I couldn't answer. My aunt didn't ask again either.

My aunt didn't know, that night, what I was about to do. Neither did
I. Let me be honest as I write this chapter: I hadn't decided anything
yet. The decision comes much later. This chapter isn't the decision —
it's the place you have to pass through before it.

The place where you just look at the box.

I rubbed the box with my fingertip. The paper was just paper. No
special texture. With no ink on it, it was smooth. The other boxes —
the date box, the agenda number box — had letters printed on them, so
their surface was slightly rough. Only the signature box, having
nothing on it, was smooth.

I felt, for the first time that night, that the smoothness was
frightening. I hadn't known before then that nothing being there could
be frightening. If I'd believed emptiness was a wrong thing, I'd have
been afraid of it long before. As long as I believed the emptiness was
a rule, it only looked comfortable to me.

Beside the box, there was no instruction to write a name. No title, no
affiliation. Just a box. It didn't even say whether it was a place for
a name, or a place for something else.

I learned later. That box hadn't been designed, from the start, to
hold anything in particular. It was simply a box left standing by
custom. Long ago it had appeared in some form, and no one had ever
erased it, so it had kept being carried forward. There had simply been
no one to propose erasing it.

Not erasing something and keeping it on purpose are different things.
I confused the two for a long time. The people of this city confuse
them too. Not erasing something just means no one touched it. Keeping
it means someone did so with intent.

That night I suddenly wanted to look up old forms. Not the one I'd
taken from the drawer — the older forms in the archive room. But the
archive room was closed at night, and I didn't bother going out to get
a key.

This part might look trivial, but I want to set it down. I didn't
rush out that night to check. There was no urgency. I hope the reader
of this book won't picture me as someone being chased by something. I
wasn't being chased. I was simply looking, for a long time.

Instead I remembered what I'd seen in the archive room before. The day
I'd opened twenty pages from the front. Back then I was struck that the
signature box was empty; I hadn't paid attention to the shape of the
box itself. Recalling it again now, those boxes had all been in the
same place, the same size. I also remembered that on the older
documents the box was slightly wider. Which meant that even as the
city changed its forms several times, this box alone had never been
removed — only carried forward.

Not removed. I come back to that phrase again. It wasn't that no one
said to remove it — it could have been erased at any time, if anyone
had wanted to. The Consensus does actually remove things it judges
unnecessary. There are quite a few old procedures that have vanished,
which I only learned once I reached Part Four. But this box stayed.

Whether that's a meaningful staying, or just a staying because nobody
cared — I won't give the answer here. This whole book is my answer to
that question, and I'm saving the answer for the last chapter.

This box is on the side of the not-erased. And yet every time I looked
at it, it felt kept. Whether that's my own delusion, or not a delusion
at all, I still can't be sure even after finishing this book.

I thought again of the person who'd written a name eleven years before.
That one line I'd found once. A name, a date, and the record cut off
after that.

I never found out, in the end, why it cut off — whether the person had
died, or left, or simply stopped writing. I keep writing that the
Equity hides nothing, but on this one point I have to be honest. I
couldn't even tell whether I hadn't found it because it wasn't there,
or because it was there and I still couldn't find it. Full openness
doesn't always mean full discovery.

Temel is different. Temel is still alive, and is still being called
in. I've already written about that. If there's one thing the person
from eleven years ago and Temel have in common, it's this: once each
of them filled that box, their own name stopped being only theirs.
Wherever that name appeared on a document, it followed.

I also thought again of the moment I first heard the word review. The
Secretary had explained it wasn't a punishment. Not a punishment, just
being called out again and again. When I heard that explanation, I'd
thought, in fact, that it was more frightening for exactly that reason
— though at the time I couldn't say why in words.

I can say it now. Punishment has an end. There's a term, there's a
fine, and once you've served or paid it, it's over. Review has no end.
As long as even one case bearing that name remains, being called
remains too. In this city, deliberations keep coming into being. So a
person who has once written their name, in theory, never finishes.

Temel knew all this, and wrote it anyway. That was what frightened me
most, that night. Not that Temel hadn't known. That he'd known, and
written it anyway.

I was afraid of that. I admitted, for the first time that night, that
I was afraid.

But being afraid, and therefore not doing it, were different matters.
I hadn't yet bound the two together into one, that night. Later Tyche
would ask, and later I would walk without an answer. That night I
hadn't gotten anywhere near that. Admitting I was afraid was the whole
of what that night held.

My aunt got up and turned the light down a little. Then she sat back
down.

"…Hora."

"…Yeah."

"…Are you going to write it?"

I folded the paper. I answered as I folded it.

"…I don't know yet."

My aunt looked at my face and asked one more time.

"…Are you scared?"

"…Yeah."

"…I'm scared too."

I looked at my aunt. She didn't ask back what she was scared of. Neither
did I explain further. We just traded one sentence each and stopped.

My aunt never tried to stop me after that night. Nor did she push me
on. I think now that was the largest thing she could have given me. Not
making the decision for me.

My aunt seemed to believe that answer. And in fact, that night, I truly
didn't know. It would make this book tidier to write that I did know,
but it isn't true.

I promised at the start to write honestly. So I'll write honestly here
too. That night, I hadn't decided anything at all. I only looked at the
box, touched it with my fingertip, folded it, and put it back in the
drawer.

But after that night, I saw that box differently. Before, I had seen
something empty. Now I saw something that could be filled. The same
box — and yet the two are entirely different things.

Before closing the drawer, I looked once more at the first page of the
notice. You have been assigned. I read that sentence again.

You have been assigned. Still no one who had assigned it. Every
sentence in this city takes this shape. What happened is there. Who
made it happen is not.

That night, for the first time, I gave this grammar a name. Subjectless
grammar. The grammar the Secretary spoke in. This whole city was built
out of that grammar.

And the signature box was, within that grammar, the one place where a
subject could be put in. A place no one had stopped anyone from
filling. A place no one had ever filled.

I didn't close the drawer. I sat beside it for a long while.

My aunt went back to her room. I heard the door close. After that the
house grew quiet. The nights of this city are quiet by nature. There's
seldom any reason to make a sound.

I wrote before that I loved this city for its quietness. That's still
true now. Even that night, I didn't hate this city. I want to set this
down clearly. I wasn't looking at that box in anger, then. I looked at
it while still loving this city.

That loving something and leaving it as it is aren't the same thing —
that night, I began, faintly, to understand this. It would be a lie to
say I understood it exactly. It was faint.

I opened the second page of the work guidelines again. I looked at the
signature box again.

What I would write in that box hadn't been decided yet. More exactly,
even whether I would write anything hadn't been decided. I only looked
at it for a long time, the way a person memorizes a shape. So many
boxes across, so many down, the thickness of the border. I still can't
explain why I was trying to memorize such things.

One thing had become clear. That box had been there from the start. It
had been there my first day at work, in the very first deliberation
document, at the bottom of every document I'd read over more than two
hundred days. No one had ever removed that box. No one had ever hidden
it either.

If the Consensus had ever hidden anything, it would have hidden this
box. But the Consensus didn't hide it. Instead, every single time, at
the bottom of every document, it printed it honestly. Even empty, the
very fact that this box was there had never once been concealed.

I realized this that night. This city had hidden nothing from me. That
the box existed, that it went unfilled, that filling it wasn't
forbidden — all of it had been shown to me from the beginning. I was
the one who hadn't looked. For two hundred days I saw that box every
day, and never once asked what it meant.

Seeing the box and asking about the box are different things. That
night, for the first time, I asked.

Near dawn, I glanced out the window for a moment. I could still hear
sound from the direction of the water channel. Even at this hour, the
water was flowing. Water that had never once come before an agenda,
still flowing on, unconcerned with agendas.

Listening to that sound, I thought. There are things in this city that
go on without ever coming before an agenda. The water channel is one.
And maybe this box was one too. Whether anyone brought it up as an
agenda item or not, the box would go on being there. Whether I filled
it or not.

That thought put me a little at ease. Whatever I did, that box wasn't
going to disappear. There was no need to hurry. That was the one
conclusion I reached that night. That I didn't need to hurry.

I folded the paper again and put it back in the drawer.
I hadn't decided. But the question remained. I slept holding that
question.

(Continued in Chapter 38.)

Chapter 38. Why I Read

_Previously_ — On the third night, Hora takes out the signature box
again. The box had been there from the start. No one had ever stopped
her. The decision hadn't come yet — only the question remained.

The next morning I took out the full text of the Seventh Classification
deliberation again.

It wasn't because the three days of not reading were over. There was
still one more day left. But I decided to make that one document an
exception. I'd already read it many times, and reading it again wasn't
going to produce a new sentence.

It was a long time before I understood why I'd opened it again that
morning. I hadn't been trying to read it anew. I had been trying to
read the reason why I'd read it so many times.

My aunt asked, while making breakfast.

"…Weren't you not reading until today?"

"…Except this one."

"…Why."

I looked at my aunt without having an answer ready. She didn't wait for
one. She just ladled the soup. That's the kind of person she is. She
can ask a question and not feel slighted if it goes unanswered.

That morning, at the table, was the first time I genuinely asked
myself why I was holding on to this document the way I was. Before
that, I hadn't asked — I'd only read. Asking and reading, just like
looking at the signature box, turned out to be different things.

We ate later than usual. My aunt didn't hurry me either. The sound of
the water channel drifted in faintly from outside. That sound wasn't
any different from usual. Only I was a little different from usual.

I'd read this document to the end, from the front, once already. Over
forty pages. The summary was a page and a half, and it was accurate. I'd
read that summary, and I'd read the full text, and after that I'd read
it again, many more times.

Why did I read it so many times? At first I thought it was to check.
In case I'd missed something. But no matter how many times I reread it,
there was nothing I'd missed. I'd known from the start that the summary
was accurate.

So what was I trying to confirm?

I properly asked myself this question for the first time that morning.
Before, I hadn't even known the shape of the question. I'd simply kept
reading.

I thought of Tyche. She'd once asked me why I read the way I did. I
couldn't answer, then. I only said it was habit. Habit was true, but it
was a word that put off the answer rather than giving it. I hadn't said,
then, where the habit came from.

I wasn't trying to hide it. I simply didn't know either. For over two
hundred days I'd used that habit every day, and never once properly
looked into where it had come from.

I thought of my mother.

Twelve pages. I'd stopped reading at the third page, once. At the
sentence, "This should be enough to get you through the winter." I'd
thought that was all there was.

I read the rest two years later. On the last line of the last page,
these words were there. I only hope you'll be someone who reads to the
end. I missed a great deal because I wasn't.

I already wrote this story once, near the beginning. Back then I only
wrote it as background — as the reason I became someone who reads
everything to the end. I let the story be the story, and never brought
it back up after that. I need to bring it back up in this chapter. That
it wasn't background but a reason — I'm only admitting that now.

I'd calculated those two years many times. About 730 days. A sentence my
mother had written thinking of me had waited for me in a drawer for 730
days. I hadn't received that sentence for all that time.

Not received — that's not exact. Exactly: I hadn't read it. Receiving
and reading are different. The paper had been in my hand. I simply
hadn't read it.

That morning I thought again about why I hadn't read it, back then. I'd
been afraid. Afraid of the word last words. Afraid that once I heard the
last thing someone had to say, there would truly be nothing after it.

That fear wasn't a wrong fear. If you hear the last words, there really
is nothing after them. I had a right to be afraid of that. For an
eleven-year-old, that fear was only natural. That morning, admitting
this, I decided for the first time not to blame the girl I'd been.

If there was anyone to blame, it might have been the me of now — the
one who, for two years, hadn't given that fear a name and had simply
put it in a drawer. But even that's hard to blame. For a thirteen-year-
old, was there really any way other than putting it in a drawer?

Back to the Seventh Classification document.

Reading it over and over, I'd thought I was looking for something. A
hidden sentence, a missing word, someone's mistake. But there was none.
There really was none. The summary was accurate, the evidence was all
there, the procedure had no flaw.

And yet I kept reading it again.

That morning I admitted this. The reason I kept rereading it wasn't
that I thought something might be hidden in the document. It was that I
was afraid I might miss something again.

Like the sentence I'd missed at thirteen. Like the sentence I hadn't
received for 730 days. I never wanted to do that again. I'd already
written that at the very beginning of this book. But back then I hadn't
written it knowing exactly what it meant.

Now I know. I hadn't read that document to the end out of a sense of
justice. I read it because I was afraid of missing something again.

I opened the document again from the beginning. This time I wasn't
reading to check the content. I read it to see where my own reading
slowed down.

The parts covered in the summary I read quickly. I already knew that
content. The place it slowed down was always the same spot. The short
paragraphs wedged between the supporting materials and the record of
processing. The place where each person involved had left a one-line
note. "Confirmed." "Passed on." "No objection." Lines like that.

I learned, that day, why I'd always read those lines so slowly. It was
because that was the place that looked most like it had nothing in it.
Lines carrying no feeling, no meaning at all. I read them slowly because
I was afraid there was something in there. More exactly, I read them
slowly because I wanted to confirm that there was nothing there.

And there really was nothing there. I must have checked each of those
lines a hundred times over, in effect. To confirm there was nothing.

Doing this checking, I thought again of that time, and of those people.
The first person who'd said, "I only passed it on." The twelve more I'd
met after. All of them people who'd left one of those lines — "passed
on," "confirmed," "no objection" — as their own share.

Back then I'd thought I was going to them looking for responsibility.
Thinking about it now, maybe I was looking for something else even
then. Whether there was something more behind these one-line notes.
Whether, the way there had been more behind my mother's third page,
there was something more behind their one lines too.

There wasn't, for them. There really wasn't. That one line was the
whole of it, for them. I'd been disappointed by that fact, back then.
Looking at it again now, that disappointment was my own story too. Not
their problem — my expectation's problem.

This isn't an easy thing to say.

I may have wanted the reader who has read this record from the start to
believe I did all of this for a good reason. That would have been easier
to write, too. A righteous girl who couldn't bear to watch an injustice
and stood up. That kind of story is common, and it's common for a
reason. Because it reads well.

But that isn't the truth. I didn't read that document to the end for
the sake of the people bound up in the Seventh Classification. I read
it for myself. I read it because I never wanted to become, again, a
person who misses the last line.

Admitting this, strangely, put my mind at ease instead. I didn't know
what that ease was, then. I know now. A person who moves for a good
reason wavers when that reason wavers. Not a bad reason, but a person
moving for a reason that's entirely their own has, at least, no reason
of that kind to waver. I know what I missed. No one can argue me out of
that.

I think it's only fair to write this too. This realization didn't make
me a kinder person. I still barely knew the people of the Seventh
Classification. Not their faces, not their names, not their
circumstances. All I'd come to know was my own story. Knowing myself
isn't the same thing as having helped them. I'm setting this paragraph
apart so I don't mistake the two for each other.

Only — knowing myself honestly made what I did next honest. That much
is true.

That afternoon Tyche came again. It was the last day of the three.

She saw I had the document open, and looked a little surprised.

"…Reading it again?"

"…Yeah. I made this one an exception."

"…What's different about it?"

I thought for a moment before answering.

"…This is me reading why I read."

Tyche looked at that sentence for a long while. Her face wasn't one of
not understanding. It was closer to one that understood too well to find
anything to say back.

"…So, did you find the answer?"

"…Yeah."

"…What was it?"

I hesitated, then said it.

"…There's something I missed once, a long time ago. So I read so I
won't miss anything again. Not because of those people."

Tyche said nothing. She didn't tease me, and she didn't say it was
admirable. She just nodded once.

"…So what if it is."

I didn't fully understand, that day, what that meant. Whether it meant
it wasn't a bad reason, or that reasons were always this small and
personal to begin with, or just that it was no big deal. Whichever it
was, Tyche didn't let me down.

It would sound too grand to say this realization changed me. Not much
changed. I was still the same person, and I still had the same
document. Only one thing was different.

Before this, I'd thought of myself as an accuser. Someone who'd seen an
injustice and meant to expose it. An accuser has to stand on the right
side. I kept having to check whether I was really standing on the right
side.

That checking often froze me in place. Am I really right. Am I really
doing this for these people. Are my motives pure. These questions have
no end, and they keep you from moving until you find the answer. For
those couple hundred days, I'd been stopped in front of this question,
in effect, the whole time.

Now I was simply someone trying to fill a certain box. Someone who
couldn't stand an empty space — the way I once couldn't stand one. That
wasn't a matter of right and wrong. It was just my own matter.

Someone who wants to fill a thing doesn't have to keep proving their
motive over and over. They just see something empty and their hand
moves. I felt, that night, how large that difference was. I hadn't been
unable to move, all that time — I'd been inspecting myself before I
could move. Once I stopped inspecting, I could move.

An accuser has to prove herself. A person who fills a box just fills
it.

After Tyche left I sat by the window for a long while and looked
outside. It was the hour the sun goes down.

In that hour I thought about why I'd been able to say all this, even to
Tyche, only that simply. "There's something I missed once, a long time
ago." It was too short a sentence to hold the real reason. But I hadn't
found a way to say it any longer, then, and even now, writing this
record, I still can't fully find one.

Some things don't carry over completely into sentences. I've felt this
many times while writing this book. This chapter especially. I'm doing
my best right now to explain that day's self, and something still slips
out anyway.

Admitting that something slips out may itself be part of reading to the
end. Setting down even the part I couldn't fully capture.

That evening I took out the twelve-page paper again. The one my mother
had left. It had been at the very bottom of the drawer.

I'd read it all, two years before, but had rarely taken it out since.
There'd been no reason to read it. I'd already read all of it.

I hadn't taken it out that evening to find something new. I just wanted
to touch it. It sounds strange, but I wanted to feel the thickness of
the paper with my hand. The thinness at the point I'd stopped at the
third page, and the thickness once I'd read all twelve pages — the same
paper, and yet it felt different in my hand.

I opened the third page again. "This should be enough to get you
through the winter."

Back then I'd thought that sentence was all there was. Reading it
again now, in my mother's own way, that sentence had been enough on its
own too. Teaching how to get through a winter isn't not love. At
thirteen I'd thought it a small thing; now I think it was love too. It
just wasn't all of it.

There'd been more on the last page. Not knowing there was more had
made me who I was for two years. And now, I stand before the Seventh
Classification document carrying the person those two years made.

I put the paper back in the drawer. This time I set it side by side
with the work guidelines that held the signature box. I didn't mean
anything particular by it. My hand simply set it down that way.

Looking back later, that arrangement was strangely exact. My mother's
last line, and this city's empty last box, lay side by side in the same
drawer. Both were places that needed someone to fill them to be
complete. One, I'd filled far too late. The other, no one had filled
yet.

I slept deeply that night, for the first time in a while. It was the
first time in three days.

(Continued in Chapter 39.)

Chapter 39. Tyche's Question

_Previously_ — Hora faces the real reason she has always read to the
end. Not a sense of justice, but that she once missed her mother's
last sentence. She has become, not an accuser, but someone who wants
to fill a box.

Once the third day ended, I started reading again.

I didn't read documents at random, the way I used to. From that day on I
held to exactly one document. The Seventh Classification. I read it for
the twenty-first time, having already read it about twenty.

Reading it for a different reason didn't change how I read it. I still
read all forty-some pages from beginning to end, skipping no line. Only
this time, as I read, I began to think about what I would do.

My aunt hardly said anything to me those few days. She made meals, she
returned my greeting when I left for work, and that was all. I was
grateful for that. If someone had kept asking me about it, I probably
would have found it even harder to decide.

The decision comes later. At that point I was still standing at that
threshold. I was getting closer each day, but the approach wasn't at a
speed anyone could see. From the outside, I would have just looked like
a girl reading the same document over and over.

Tyche came often, around then. During work hours, and after work too.
No one had told her to. In her own words, it was "just because I was
curious."

I only found this strange later. When Tyche is curious about
something, she usually finds the materials and works out the answer
herself. Coming to visit a person, over and over, wasn't like Tyche. At
the time I just thought she was worried about me. Maybe the worry was
real too. The two things could be true at once.

The way Tyche came to see me was worth noting too. She always brought
something in her hands. Papers laying out evidence, statistical tables,
past deliberation cases. But she didn't lay them out in front of me. She
just sat holding them, and only took them out once I asked about
something.

"…You look at that one for a long time, whenever."

"…Yeah."

Tyche was quiet a moment, then asked.

"…Are you going to write it?"

I didn't answer right away. She didn't press me either. For a while we
just sat there looking at the empty box together.

I've revisited this moment many times since. It was the first time
Tyche had ever asked me something directly. Up until then Tyche had been
the one observing me. The one explaining things. This time she was the
one asking.

"…If you write it, what changes?"

The question seemed simple, but it wasn't. I already felt its weight at
the time. Tyche was asking me about the result. And I didn't know the
result.

I truly didn't know. I still don't know exactly, even now. That the new
deliberation would only be reversed in part — I only came to know that
later. What kind of thing review was — I only learned that in my body
once I reached that chapter. At that point I stood in front of this
question without having gone through any of it.

I thought of Temel just then. I could have asked him the same thing.
What changed, once he wrote it. In Temel's case there's a little more of
an answer. A few of his cases really were reclassified. But some others
weren't. I knew this, and still didn't bring it up to Tyche. I didn't
want to borrow a result that hadn't yet been confirmed.

I looked into Tyche's eyes. She was truly waiting for an answer. Her
face wasn't the usual one, where she offers her own answer first and
only asks for confirmation.

The office was quiet. Outside, the sound of the water channel could be
heard, very faintly. I listened to that sound while I tried to find an
answer. I wasn't trying to find a perfect answer. I just didn't want to
lie.

I answered slowly.

"…Maybe nothing changes. But it won't be empty."

Tyche was quiet for a long time after hearing that.

"…Is that all?"

"…Yeah."

"…I don't see what meaning that has."

I didn't argue back. I also had no argument to make.

"…I don't know either. But that's everything I have, right now."

I wanted to render this exchange exactly. I wasn't dressing it up to
persuade Tyche. I had no logic that day that could have persuaded her.
If I'd had one, I would have used it. I didn't, so I said what I had,
as it was.

That night, turning this answer over, I understood that it connected
to something I'd learned earlier. Reading the same document side by
side, Tyche saw the accuracy of the procedure, and I saw the empty box.
We were both reading it straight. That same difference was in this
conversation now. Tyche was looking at the fact that filling it changes
nothing. I was looking at the fact that even so, it stops being empty.
Both of us were looking at the same box and seeing different things.

I felt, for the first time that day, that these two sentences didn't
contradict each other. Maybe nothing changes, and even so it won't be
empty — these two things could stand together. I had thought, until
then, that they collided. They didn't.

Tyche kept turning my words over in her face.

"…I still don't really understand why it matters that it won't be
empty. Why is being empty a bad thing? Nobody filling it just means
nobody filled it. It's not empty because someone did something wrong."

"…That's right."

Tyche fidgeted with the papers in her hand for a moment. That wasn't
like her, usually.

"…I've seen a lot of deliberations, this whole time. Quite a few I sat
in on as an assistant. I've never once seen the signature box filled.
I thought that was just how it was. I thought it was a rule. But finding
out it wasn't — it felt strange."

"…What did?"

"…Why nobody had filled it — that felt strange. It's not that you're
not allowed to. I thought at first everyone just didn't know. But
everyone knows. Look it up in the rulebook, it's right there. Nobody
had just done it."

"…Is that what you think the probability is?"

"…No. This is the part probability can't explain. If it were
probability, somebody should have filled it at least once by now. But
once in eleven years — that's too rare to explain by chance."

I remember this part for a long time. It was the moment Tyche herself
admitted there was a part that probability couldn't explain. But she
didn't move from that admission to a different conclusion. She just
admitted it and stopped there. That was Tyche.

I felt, at that moment, the temptation to press the point with her.
See, it can't be explained, so there must be meaning in it — I wanted
to say that. I didn't. It felt wrong to take something Tyche had
admitted herself and use it to serve my own logic. She wanted to leave
that fact exactly as it was. I wanted to respect that way of hers.

"…And you still want to fill it?"

"…Yeah."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer this question right away either. I could have said
the answer I'd found before. That I'd missed something once. But that
wasn't an answer to Tyche's question. She wasn't asking about my
circumstances — she was asking about the meaning of the act itself.

"…I don't know. But does there have to be a reason?"

Tyche gave a small laugh at that. It wasn't a mocking laugh.

"…That's so like you."

"…What do you mean?"

"…Someone who just does something without a reason. I'm not good at
that. I always look for the reason first. If the reason doesn't turn up,
I can't move. So sometimes I can't do anything at all."

"…Is that a bad thing?"

"…No. But sometimes I envy it."

I thought about that for a long time. It was the first time Tyche had
ever said she envied me.

I was a little surprised, hearing that. I learned, then, for the first
time, what kind of person Tyche saw me as. Someone who moved without a
reason. To Tyche, that was probably a kind of person that couldn't be
explained. Tyche is a person who deals in things that can be explained.
Probability, procedure, a vacancy. All of it can be explained.

I was about to do something that couldn't be explained. In front of
Tyche.

I asked her something.

"…Why do you work here, Tyche? As a deliberation assistant."

"…Because my number came up right. And also because it suits me. It
fits."

"…What does fitting mean?"

"…I like explaining things. Figuring out why something turned out the
way it did."

"…Then explain this too. Why I'm doing this."

Tyche looked at me for a moment, steadily.

"…That one, I still can't explain either. You're one of the few things
I can't explain, Hora."

I found it strange that this came out of Tyche. She wasn't a person who
said comfortably that she couldn't explain something.

"…Doesn't it bother you, not being able to explain it?"

"…It does. But it's better than forcing an explanation onto it anyway.
I dislike that even more."

I liked that answer. Tyche didn't force herself to patch over a hole in
her own logic. If there was a hole, she just let it stay a hole.

Tyche asked again. This time her voice was a little more careful.

"…Aren't you scared?"

"…I am."

"…And you're doing it anyway?"

"…Yeah."

"…I don't understand it."

"…I don't fully understand myself either. But it turns out you can do
something even without understanding it."

Tyche didn't answer that. Instead she got up and went to the window. The
one that faced the water channel. That day the sound of the water was
unusually loud.

"…I still think all of this is probability. That you're sitting here,
that those people became the Seventh Classification — all of it is a
vacancy and a number and a procedure lining up. I think putting meaning
onto that is something people do because they're lonely."

"…I know. You've said that before."

"…But I don't want to stop you from filling that box, either."

The sound of the water broke off for a moment, then picked up again.
Probably the wind.

I was surprised by what she'd said.

"…Why?"

Tyche answered, looking out the window.

"…I don't know. There's just no reason to stop you, that's all."

This was an answer that sounded like Tyche. She hadn't stayed by my
side because she'd been persuaded. She stayed because there was no
reason to stop me. I think that difference is the most important part
of this chapter.

Tyche never changes her position after this. Not for a long time, not
even by the time this record ends. But from that evening on, she kept
staying by my side. Without ever being persuaded.

I count this among the most important discoveries in this book. Two
people didn't have to reach the same conclusion in order to stay
together. Tyche believed, to the end, in chance. I believed, to the end,
that there was something more. And yet, that night, we sat in the same
room, with the same document between us.

I think that was Tyche's own way. Staying beside someone while
believing it's chance. Being kind while believing there's no meaning. It
was nowhere in any proposal document, but I want to call that Tyche's
faith. She'd laugh if she heard me say that.

That night I asked her one more thing.

"…If I write it and nothing changes at all, then what does that make
me?"

This time it took Tyche a while to think.

"…Just someone who wrote it. That's all."

"…Is that all?"

"…Yeah. There's no such thing as the world taking notice of you or
anything. The Consensus doesn't do that. The Consensus just receives it
and processes it, that's all."

I didn't find that cold. I found it, instead, precise.

"…So I become someone who amounts to nothing."

"…No. You just become someone who wrote it. That's better than
amounting to nothing, I think."

Tyche probably wasn't saying this to persuade me. She was just saying
the fact. But it was the first time I felt comforted by her words. The
comfort I got from Tyche always took this shape. Not warm words —
precise ones. I understood, that day, that this was Tyche's own kind of
kindness.

We sat together in the office late into the night. We didn't talk much
more. Tyche looked over her own materials, and I read the document
again. The signature box was still empty.

The caretaker came to put out the lights, saw the two of us, and just
left. The same as he had, the very first night.

The two scenes overlapped for me. Two hundred and some days before,
the night I sat alone reading forty pages. And now, the night with
Tyche beside me. The only thing different was that one more person had
been added beside me. That one person made quite a large difference to
me, that night.

We packed up our things without saying anything. The silence wasn't
awkward.

Coming out late, Tyche asked.

"…I'll come again tomorrow. Is that all right?"

"…Yeah."

"…No question about why?"

"…I told you, you're allowed to do things without a reason too."

Tyche laughed. Then she nodded once and walked off. I watched her back
for a moment, then walked the other way.

That was how we parted. The night air was cold, and the sound of the
water channel followed behind me the whole way.

Tyche hadn't come out that night to persuade me. When I asked her
about it later, she said she'd simply been curious what I was thinking.
That that was all it had been. I think that was true too.

(Continued in Chapter 40.)

Chapter 40. If I Perish

_Previously_ — Tyche asks, "If you write it, what changes?" Hora
answers, "Maybe nothing changes. But it won't be empty." Tyche
stays close, still unconvinced.

I slept well that night.

I know it's strange, even as I write this sentence. That I slept well
knowing what I would do the next day. But that's really how it was. I
lay down in bed and fell asleep not long after, and I didn't wake up in
the middle of the night.

I've thought about this many times since. I write it knowing how
strange it sounds. I'd thought the night before a great decision was
supposed to be spent wide awake. At least, that's how it always goes in
stories. I didn't do that. Maybe it's because the sleepless night had
already come and gone before this. That night, back then. Maybe it had
all been used up then.

Just before falling asleep that night, I thought about one thing only.
What Temel had said to me. "Maybe you're sitting here only because of a
number. But there are still things you can do in a seat you got only
because of a number." I turned that over in my mind again and again.
Then I fell asleep.

Whether there was meaning in it or not, I still didn't know, even that
night. All I knew was that there were things I could do. That was
enough, I think I told myself, just before falling asleep.

I got up in the morning and washed and dressed as usual.

My aunt was making rice in the kitchen. The same time as always, the
same smell as always. I sat at the table, and my aunt ladled out the
soup.

"…You're heading out early today."

"…Yeah."

My aunt didn't ask any further. I didn't say anything further either.

That morning I'd thought I should tell my aunt something. That I
should let her know my decision. But I didn't. I opened my mouth to
speak and then just closed it again. Even now I can't explain exactly
why. Only that it felt right, that morning.

A declaration is for someone. It's made for the person listening.
That morning I felt there was no one for me to declare this to. Telling
my aunt wouldn't have let her do anything for me instead. The Consensus
isn't a structure that any one person can block or help. So saying it
would only have made my aunt carry a weight.

This wasn't out of not trusting my aunt. If anything it was out of
caring for her. Not telling her was the one kindness I could give her,
that morning.

After eating, I got up from the table. My aunt looked at me once. Was
there something different in her eyes from usual — I didn't notice it,
at the time. Looking back now, I think maybe I did know.

"…I'm off."

"…All right."

That was all. Closing the door behind me, I didn't look back. I never
did, on any other day, so there was no reason to do it differently that
day.

On my way to the deliberation district, I passed the water channel.

The same channel I'd first seen at the very start. It now reaches deep
into the city. I wrote earlier that people had started drinking that
water; that morning too, a few people were sitting by the water's edge.

I didn't stop walking as I passed it. I hesitated over this while
writing this chapter. It felt like something symbolic should have
happened here. The water speaking to me, or giving me some kind of
sign. Nothing like that happened. I just walked past, and the water
just flowed.

I'd been thinking about this water channel a lot, around that time.
Water that administratively doesn't exist, because it never came
before an agenda. And yet people drink it. It was fairly real, for
something that doesn't exist.

That morning I thought this water channel and what I was about to do
were headed in opposite directions. The channel kept flowing even
though no one had named it. The signature box stayed empty because no
one had put a name to it. One existed without an agenda; the other
looked, without one, as if it didn't exist. I didn't fully understand
that difference, that day.

One of the people sitting by the water glanced at me. I looked back at
that person. Neither of us said anything. We just passed each other.

In my drawer at home was the second page of the work guidelines. I
brought it out with me. There'd been no real need to bring it — there
would be a fresh form at the office. But I brought that paper anyway.

I don't know why. Maybe it was because it was the same paper I'd
rubbed with my fingertip, that night. Or maybe it was just habit.

The office was the same as always.

A few people had already come in. No one looked at me any differently.
I sat at my seat and checked the documents I had to handle that day. The
same as usual.

I worked as usual all through that morning. Whoever reads this chapter
will probably think I went straight to the signature box. I didn't. In
the morning I handled the other deliberations assigned to me. Putting
them in order, passing them on, confirming them. I have to admit, in
this chapter, that I too was a person who used that grammar.

This fact bothered me strangely. What I was about to do soon after was
break that grammar. And yet that morning I still worked exactly by it.
I put things in order, I passed them on, I confirmed them. I don't
want to call this hypocrisy. It was simply how it was. A person can't
just stop doing everything they do every day inside a system, on a
single day. You have to eat, and the work assigned to you has to be
handled.

I thought, that day, that it must have been the same for the
Secretary. He too lives speaking the same grammar every day. I
understood, for the first time that day, from his position, that a
person can't shed that grammar overnight.

Tyche came at lunchtime. She sat beside me as usual.

"…You're a little different today."

"…How so?"

"…I don't know. Just a little quiet."

I didn't answer. She didn't ask again either.

We ate lunch together that day. We didn't talk about anything special.
Tyche talked a little about the materials she'd organized the day
before, and I just listened.

I remember that lunch exactly, even now. What Tyche ate, what she
talked about. Even though it was such an ordinary lunch. Probably
because it was the last lunch I spent as an ordinary person. From that
afternoon on, I became a slightly different person.

In the afternoon I took out the Seventh Classification document.

I opened the last page. For the twenty-some-th time. The signature box
was there.

I've thought for a long time about how to write this moment. There was
a temptation to write it dramatically. My hands trembling, my heart
racing. To tell the truth, none of that happened. My hands didn't
tremble. My heart might have beaten a little faster than usual, but
that might just be me dressing up the memory now.

I flipped through the whole document once more. Over forty pages. The
summary, the evidence, the record, the notes of those involved. Even
reading it for the twenty-some-th time, my eyes still followed the
same order.

Writing this chapter, I thought about what was different about this
twenty-some-th complete reading from the ones before it. Nothing about
the content had changed. The summary was the same, the evidence was
the same. What had changed was only that I knew, this time, what I was
about to do while reading it. Before, I'd read it to understand it.
This time it felt like reading it to say goodbye to it.

Goodbye might sound like too strong a word. But that's really how I
felt that day. I was about to part with myself as a person who simply
read this document. Now I was about to become a person who had put a
hand to it.

I looked at that box once more. Still smooth. Still empty.

I still didn't know what would happen if I filled it. I knew about
Temel's review, and I knew the story of that person eleven years
before. But exactly what would happen to me, I didn't know. That not
knowing is the most honest fact of that day.

I didn't pick up the pen.

Instead I got up from my seat and left the office. I didn't fill it in,
that day.

I need to write this part honestly. That afternoon I didn't steel
myself and pick up the pen right away. I got up from my seat and went
out. And I walked. I walked with nowhere set in mind.

I walked once around the city. It was a familiar route. A route I'd
walked since I was small. My pace was neither fast nor slow.

I passed the First Deliberation District meeting room. The building
that held the seat I'd first sat in, once. I didn't stop walking — I
just passed it by.

I passed the school too. I remembered my aunt saying, "What matters is
near the front." That day the words sounded different to me. I didn't
think my aunt had been wrong. Only that this time, it wasn't near the
front — it was at the end.

I walked for nearly two hours. I wasn't walking to decide anything in
particular. I just walked. As I walked, I took in every corner of this
city with my eyes. Why that was necessary to me at the time, I still
can't fully explain. Only that it was necessary.

A city where no one queues. A city where the word favor is only used
when handing someone an object. I still loved this city. Loving it
still, and knowing that one of its most honest boxes stayed empty the
whole time — I knew both at once.

The two didn't contradict each other. I realized that as I walked.
Loving this city, and filling this box, were things pointing in the
same direction.

Walking, I thought of Temel. He wasn't in the city right now. I'd heard
he'd gone elsewhere to look for someone. I thought it was fortunate,
actually, that he wasn't there.

If he had been, I probably would have gone to find him and asked. Is
it all right to do this now, is this the right time. Temel probably
would have said, "I don't know. I don't know either." He'd already
answered that way once before. Rather than hear the same answer again,
I preferred to walk alone.

This wasn't avoiding Temel. I simply wanted, this once, to go without
anyone's confirmation. If you ask before you go, it becomes going with
that person's permission. I didn't want permission. That there was no
one, to begin with, whose permission I needed — that was something this
whole book had taught me.

I thought of the Secretary too. He would probably be there tomorrow
morning, in the seat where he'd receive my signature. He wouldn't be
surprised. I was strangely certain of that.

By the time the sun was setting, I came back to the office.

Most people had already left for the day. Tyche wasn't there either. I
sat alone at my seat.

Outside the window the sun was fully going down. The evenings of this
city are quiet. As I've said, it's a city with little reason to make a
sound.

I didn't go through some grand moment of resolve, that evening. The
decision hadn't arrived in a single instant. From back then to here, it
had built up little by little, over four days. Writing this chapter, I
kept telling myself I had to resist the temptation to turn it into one
dramatic scene.

I opened the document again. I looked at the signature box. This time I
didn't look at it for long.

I picked up the pen.

The pen in my hand was an ordinary pen. One of those common pens found
anywhere in an office, nothing special about it. I found that a little
funny. A moment like this felt like it should call for some special
instrument, and what was in my hand was just an ordinary pen.

I brought the nib close to the paper. It didn't touch. In those few
millimeters, I stayed still for a long time. I don't remember now
whether it was seconds, or minutes. Time felt strangely stretched out,
in that moment.

In that moment I thought of my mother. The last line of the twelve-
page paper. And I thought that this signature box, too, was in the same
way a last line. A box at the very bottom of the document, in the place
where everything ends. I had missed a last line once. This time I was
about to leave something on a last line, myself.

I didn't sign it that night. That night I only picked up the pen and
set it back down. I think it's right to end this chapter here. The
signature itself belongs to a much later part.

What I was trying to do that day wasn't the signature. It was the
decision. And a decision, from what I learned that day, wasn't a
declaration. It was simply going, the next morning.

That night, at home, I said nothing to my aunt. I ate, washed, and
slept. The same as usual. My aunt didn't say anything unusual either.

It wasn't that I pretended it was the same as usual. It really was the
same as usual. I wasn't afraid, that night. I know that's the strangest
part of this chapter. The fear came the next day.

I told Tyche this once, later. She said that was only natural. That
the time before a decision is the most frightening, and once it's made,
it's actually less frightening. That the real thing only starts after
that. Tyche was right. The reason I was at ease that night was that
nothing had started yet.

Between picking up the pen and putting it down, and actually writing
the name, there was still one more door left. I spent that last night
on this side of that door.

I gave this chapter its title later. I hadn't thought of these words
that night. This is a title I attached after the fact, while writing
this book. That night's self had no room to think anything so grand. I
was only thinking about going, the next morning.

The next morning, I woke as usual. And I left the house as usual. The
weather was clear, and the sound of the water channel was exactly the
same as the day before. This city stayed the same whatever decision I'd
made. I found that steadying, if anything. Nothing was standing still,
waiting for me.

I slept well that night. I set this down honestly too. I didn't toss
and turn, and I had no frightening dreams. I woke as usual in the
morning. I learned, then, that the night before a great day could be
this ordinary.

(Continued in Chapter 41.)

Chapter 41. The Signature

_Previously_ — Hora doesn't declare her decision. Carrying a resolve
that built up slowly over more than three days, she meets an ordinary
morning and leaves the house with an ordinary step.

I got to the office early that morning. A little earlier than usual.

No one was there yet. The hallway was quiet, and so was the meeting
room. I sat at my seat and took out the Seventh Classification document.
I opened the last page. The signature box was there.

I hesitated for a long while before writing this chapter. I didn't
know how to properly carry the weight of this moment onto the page. In
the end I decided to just write it exactly as it happened. Not to
invent anything to add weight to it. That's my rule for writing this
chapter.

The office was quiet. It wasn't yet fully light out. A pale light was
coming in through the window.

I looked through the document once more. It was habit. I didn't flip
through all forty-some pages again. I just checked the first page and
the last. The summary was there, and the signature box was there.

Even up to this moment I felt there was still something left to check,
one last time. Whether the evidence was right, whether the procedure
was right. In fact it wasn't that. All of it I'd already checked, weeks
before. What I really wanted to check that morning was whether my mind
was still the same.

It was.

I picked up the pen. The same pen I'd picked up the evening before.

This time I didn't stop.

I wrote my name in the name box. Hora. Two syllables. The sound of the
pen tip touching the paper was exactly the same as any ordinary time I
wrote.

That was really all there was. Even as I write this sentence I feel
the temptation to add something more. That my hand trembled, that I
cried. There was nothing. I simply wrote my name. The same motion of
the hand as any ordinary time I wrote my name on an application.

I once tried to time how long it took me to write these two syllables.
Probably two, three seconds. Two hundred-some days of time, three days
of resolve, four days of preparation, all ended in a three-second
motion of the hand. I thought for a long time that this imbalance was
strange. I don't think it's strange now. A great decision didn't need
to be expressed by a great motion.

Once I'd written it, I looked at the box.

It wasn't empty anymore.

Had I expected something to happen, at this moment? Looking back now,
I think maybe I had. A bell ringing somewhere, or someone rushing in,
or at the very least the air in the room changing. None of that
happened. The room stayed the same. The light outside the window
stayed the same. The document was just a document.

I sat for a moment and kept looking at the box. I watched the ink dry.
It took a few minutes.

Above my name, the other boxes came into view too. The date box, the
agenda number box. All boxes that had already been printed. The
signature box was the only one that had been filled by hand.

That contrast bothered me. The printed letters had been made by the
Consensus, and the two written syllables had been made by me. On that
paper, for the first time, something that wasn't the Consensus's had
come to exist. Very small, but clearly different.

I heard the sound of a door opening. It was the caretaker. He'd come to
do the morning cleaning.

"…You're in early."
