"…Yes."

The caretaker walked past without much thought. He probably didn't know
what I'd done. Even if he had, he probably would have walked past just
the same.

That moment was strangely comforting to me. That no one was looking at
me as anything special. I felt as though the world had ended, and yet
in the caretaker's unconcerned footsteps I confirmed that the world
hadn't ended at all.

The caretaker finished cleaning and left. I stayed sitting at my seat
the whole time. The document lay on the desk.

I don't remember exactly what I did during those tens of minutes. I
think I was in a daze. I wasn't deep in thought, and I wasn't anxious
either. Time just passed. All I remember is that the light outside the
window grew a little brighter.

I could have left this blank space out of the writing. But I think
this blank space matters in this chapter. Right after signing,
nothing happens. Truly nothing happens, so much that I don't even
remember what filled that time. I mean to leave this blank space as
it is, without inventing anything to fill it.

When it was time for the reception desk to open, I gathered up the
document and went.

The person at the reception desk greeted me with their usual face. I
held the document out.

"…Please receive this."

The person at the desk took the document and looked it over. They
flipped through a few pages, and their hand stopped at the last one.

I'd waited a long time for this moment. I thought this person would
say something. Be surprised, or ask a question. Nothing was said.

The person at the desk stamped it. Then they put the document in a
file box.

"…Received."

"…Yes. Thank you."

The person at the desk tore off a receipt slip and handed it to me. It
had a date and a number written on it.

"…Keep this with you."

"…Yes."

That was all.

I've thought many times, later, about how many more cases that person
at the desk must have processed that day. Probably several. Mine was
just one of them. There was no reason, from that person's side, for it
to be anything special. Even if it was the first time they'd seen a
document with the signature box filled, the reception procedure was
the same. Take it, check it, stamp it, file it.

I tried to recall again whether that person's expression had changed
at all, seeing the signature box. I don't remember. Probably there
was no expression at all. That person, too, was only doing their job.

I don't even know that person's name. I didn't ask. In this city, you
don't often ask such things. That person remains, to me, simply
someone who was sitting at the reception desk. And I probably remain,
in that person's memory too, simply one of the people who came in that
morning.

I gave this chapter the title "The Signature." It's the shortest
chapter in this book, coming after the longest preparation. I did that
on purpose. I know the reader will expect something dramatic at this
moment. I expected it too. There was none.

The weight of this chapter comes from the fact that nothing happened.
I rewrote it several times. Each time there was a temptation to add
something. To lay in description like background music, or to write as
if someone had been watching me. I decided against it. Because none of
that actually happened.

I've written many times that this city doesn't lie to me. That
principle held in this chapter too. The Consensus showed no special
reaction to my signature. That not reacting was itself an honest
reaction. Not ignoring it to hide something — simply that no such
reaction existed in the reception procedure to begin with.

Walking out of the reception desk that day, I half expected some kind
of murmur to rise up behind my back. There was none. The reception desk
was as quiet as it had been when I'd walked in.

I left the reception desk and went back to the office. An ordinary day
went on as usual. I handled the deliberations assigned to me. I put
things in order, passed them on, confirmed them.

Tyche came at lunchtime.

"…Quiet again today."

Instead of answering, I showed her the receipt slip. She looked at it
for a long while.

"…Did you write it?"

"…Yeah."

Tyche didn't say anything. She just handed the receipt back to me.

"…Let's go eat."

We went to eat as usual.

At the restaurant, Tyche asked again.

"…Weren't you scared? While you were writing it."

"…No, strangely, I felt nothing at all."

"…Why not?"

"…It was just two syllables of a name. I write that all the time
anyway."

Tyche thought about that for a moment and nodded.

"…That's stranger. That such a big decision ends with such a small
motion."

"…I think so too."

We ate the rest of our meal without much more talk. The restaurant was
crowded with people as usual, and no one paid us any attention.

I know this scene is strange. A person in this city had just written
her own name in the signature box, and right after, the scene was an
ordinary lunch. Tyche and I walked as usual, ate as usual. Tyche talked
a little about her own things, as usual.

Even by that evening, I felt no sign that anything had changed. Nor
did the next day. Nor the day after that. I woke every morning a
little tense, wondering if something had changed. But every morning
was the same as before.

The change comes much later. It comes quietly, without warning. It
wasn't there that day. On that day there was only the fact that one
sheet of paper had been received.

In the afternoon I ran into someone from another deliberation district.
That person treated me as they always had.

"…Hora, could you look this over for me."

"…Yes."

I took the document they handed me and checked it. It was my usual
work, and my hand moved as it always did. That person seemed to have
no idea at all what I'd just done.

This was, strangely, a comfort. What I'd done wasn't yet special news
to anyone in this city. For it to become news would take time. Or
maybe, in fact, it would never become news at all. That the document
had been received was itself an open fact, but there was no way to know
it unless someone deliberately went looking.

This city hides nothing. And yet it also announces nothing. Hiding
something and not announcing it are different things. I felt that
difference again that afternoon.

That night, at home, I told my aunt.

"…I wrote it."

My aunt stopped ladling the rice. She looked at me for a moment.

"…All right."

"…Aren't you angry?"

"…This isn't something to be angry about."

She went back to serving the rice. We ate dinner as usual.

I only understood, later, what that calm of my aunt's had been. She
hadn't been unsurprised. She must have already been expecting this
moment, for many nights before. Her calm was a prepared calm.

Once she'd finished serving, my aunt added just one more line.

"…Do you know what happens next?"

"…No, I don't."

"…Neither do I."

We didn't talk about it any further after that. We talked about other
things over dinner. My aunt talked about a tree the neighbors had
planted in their yard lately, and I listened.

I've replayed that evening many times, wanting to remember it exactly.
My aunt didn't show any sign of worrying about me. I didn't say
anything to reassure her either. We just ate dinner as usual. I still
think, even now, that this was the best thing the two of us could give
each other.

That night, before bed, I looked at the receipt slip again.

It had only a date, a number, and the stamp confirming receipt on it.
An unremarkable piece of paper.

I held that paper in my hand for a long while. It didn't feel like I
was holding anything grand. It was just paper. But that ordinariness
was exactly what I learned that day. A thing that changes the world
comes in a shape the world doesn't notice. At least, in this city, it
did.

I turned off the light and lay down. Nothing happened. The sound of the
water channel drifted in very faintly through the window, and faded
before I fell asleep.

I mean to close this chapter with that night. Writing this part, I
kept wanting to give the reader something more. A twist, a tension, at
least some sense of foreboding. But there was none of that, that
night. I simply fell asleep, ordinarily.

Looking back on it now, this ordinariness was the most honest face
this city has. The Consensus wasn't waiting to punish me. Nor was it
waiting to celebrate me. The Consensus had simply received it. What
happened to what was received would, in this city's own way, begin
quietly, following a set procedure.

I had no idea, that night, what shape that procedure would take. If I
had known, I probably wouldn't have fallen asleep so peacefully.

The next morning, too, I woke as usual. I went to the office and
worked as usual. No one treated me any differently.

This went on for several days. I came to work every day, put documents
in order every day, ate lunch with Tyche every day. I kept the receipt
slip in the drawer. Beside the second page of the work guidelines,
beside my mother's paper.

The three papers lay side by side like that. I would sometimes open
that drawer and look at all three together. All three were papers with
something written on a last line. One a line I'd missed. One a box no
one had ever filled. And now one a box I had filled.

I received no word at all, during those days. Nothing happened at
all. I didn't think it would last long. I just didn't know exactly
when it would end. I didn't know exactly how fast the Consensus moved.
It could be quick, or it could be slow. There was no way to know.

I enjoyed that quiet exactly as it was. Because I knew it was the last
quiet.

Tyche still came as usual, during those days. She kept asking me if
anything had changed, and every time I answered no. It really was
nothing.

"…That's strange. It feels like something should have happened by
now."

"…I thought so too. But there's nothing."

"…Could there just keep being nothing?"

"…I don't know."

We spent several days like that. I didn't know, at the time, that
those few days were the last peaceful time I'd spend in this city. If
I had known, I probably would have tried harder to feel that peace a
little longer.

(Continued in Chapter 42.)

Chapter 42. Review

_Previously_ — Hora writes her name in the signature box. The
reception desk stamps it and files the paper. Nothing happens. That
quiet goes on for several days.

On the sixth day, I received my first summons.

A notice came. The same kind of paper as when I'd first received the
work guidelines. You are requested to appear for the reexamination of
the deliberation concerning the Seventh Classification. Date: three
days from now.

Receiving this notice, at first, actually put me at ease. That
something had started felt better than nothing happening at all. How
naive that ease was, I already learned that very evening.

My aunt saw the notice and asked.

"…What's this?"

"…A deliberation reexamination. It says to appear."

My aunt read the notice once more.

"…So it's starting now."

"…Yeah. I guess."

She said nothing more. That night I read the notice again, many times
over. The words three days from now stuck in my eye especially.

I didn't know what to prepare during those three days, so I just spent
them as usual. There wasn't really anything to prepare. Why I'd read
that document to the end, why I'd signed it — those were already
established facts. There was nothing new to make up.

Three days later I went to the assigned room.

It was a small room. One table, three chairs. Two people sat across
from me. Faces I'd never seen before.

"…We'll confirm a few things regarding the proposal for a new
deliberation on the Seventh Classification matter."

"…Yes."

"…Would you state your reason for proposing it."

I spoke as I'd prepared. I pointed to a few pieces of evidence, and I
spoke about the gap between the summary and the full text. The two of
them wrote it down.

"…Confirmed. Thank you."

That was all. It took about twenty minutes.

Coming out, I saw someone else in the hallway. That person, too, was
coming out of a room, the same as me. Somewhat older. Our eyes met, and
that person nodded to me first.

"…First time?"

"…Yes."

"…You'll get used to it."

That person said only that and walked on. I couldn't tell, at the time,
whether that was comfort or a warning.

Looking back now, I think it was both. In this city, comfort and
warning often come in the same sentence.

I never met that person again. I've written before that meeting the
same person twice in review is rare — the same was true among the
people undergoing review with me. A different face every time. Whether
this was chance, or itself some kind of design, I never confirmed, in
the end. Confirming it would have meant going and asking someone else
again, and by then I didn't have much energy left for that.

I thought this was the end of it. I'd stated it, it had been accepted,
and I thought I'd move on to the next stage.

I was wrong.

Ten days later, another notice came. A different room this time,
different people. I was asked the same questions again. I answered the
same way.

"…We're confirming whether this matches your earlier statement."

"…Yes."

This one, too, took about twenty minutes.

At this second review, I noticed something strange for the first time.
Both the room and the people were different, but the questions were
identical, down to the last word. "Would you state your reason for
proposing it." I learned, then, that this was a line fixed like a
script. The people in charge changed, but the question didn't.

In that moment I felt this was a cold system, but by the time I left
the room, I'd revised the thought. It wasn't cold. It was just fair.
That the same question is asked no matter who conducts it. I realized
this was the same way as everything else in the Equity. A way in which
no one takes sides — that same way was being applied to review too.

Next, another notice came, not in ten days but in six.

I went again. I was asked the same questions again. I gave the same
answers again.

The third time I went, I asked something for the first time.

"…How many more times will this happen?"

The person across from me glanced at a screen for a moment and
answered.

"…There is no set number. You'll be convened whenever it's needed."

"…Who decides when it's needed?"

"…That's the procedure."

I didn't ask any further. I already knew that asking further wouldn't
bring a different answer.

On my way home I counted my review notices for the first time. I'd been
collecting them in the drawer each time one arrived, and by that day
there were three. Laying the three side by side, the gaps between the
dates stood out to me. Ten days, then six. No pattern.

I learned that day that the lack of a pattern was, if anything, more
of a weight to carry. If the interval had been fixed, I could have
predicted the next date and prepared my mind for it. Because there was
no pattern, I started each morning thinking a notice might arrive that
day.

The Secretary had once explained review to me, long ago. It was not
long after I'd first met Temel. What Temel went through. Not
punishment, but review. I thought, at the time, that I'd understood
that explanation. I hadn't.

Understanding something and living through it are different. I only
know the difference in my body now. When I was told about it, review
was a single fact. Living through it, review becomes a rhythm. Six
days, ten days, six days again. Being called out again and again at
unpredictable intervals.

I met different people at every review. A different face every time. I
almost never met the same person twice.

I learned later that this wasn't coincidence. The people conducting
review were meant to change every time as well. If one person kept
handling me, there was a risk that person would become the one
responsible. A structure meant to keep anyone from becoming responsible
was being applied even to the people who called me in.

I couldn't bring myself to hate this structure. Because the person to
hate kept changing. This was how this city treated me. A way of
offering no place for anger to land.

At first I thought this was a cunning move by the Consensus. A
structure that blocked resistance by never giving you anyone to be
angry at. But thinking about it again, this wasn't cunning — it was
simply this city's founding principle, applied to review in exactly the
same way. The principle that no one takes sides, and no one remains
the responsibility of any one particular individual. I wrote, near the
very beginning of this record, that I thought this principle was good.
Even now, I can't fully overturn that judgment.

Once, during a review, one of the people in charge said something
personal to me.

"…Why did you do this? You could have just lived quietly."

I wasn't prepared to answer that question. That person didn't seem to
be waiting for an answer either. They just seemed to be curious.

"…No reason, really."

"…All right. Let's move to the next question."

I remember this short exchange for a long time. That person didn't
blame me. They didn't sound like they couldn't understand, either.
They were simply curious, took my short answer as it was, and moved
on. The people of this city were mostly like that. They didn't press.

One day Tyche was waiting for me as I came out of a review.

"…Went again?"

"…Yeah."

"…What did they ask?"

"…The same thing. Reason. Evidence."

"…Same every time?"

"…Almost."

Tyche walked in silence for a moment, then asked.

"…Is it hard?"

I thought for a moment before answering.

"…It's not hard, exactly. It's strange. No one's angry at me, and yet
I keep being called in."

"…That's review. I understand now, why people are afraid of it."

Tyche told me, that day, about what she'd looked into herself.

"…I looked at the statistics on review counts. On average people are
called in two or three times a month. But the variance is big. Some
people go months with nothing, then suddenly get called in over and
over."

"…Why does that happen?"

"…There's no explanation given. It's just marked as procedure."

"…I know. Everything's procedure."

Tyche laughed a little.

"…That's this city's way, isn't it."

Tyche wasn't trying to persuade me that day either. She just brought
me a fact as it was. I was grateful for that. More than words trying to
comfort me, accurate information helped me more, at that moment.

Around this time I started thinking about Temel again. He'd been going
through this for years. I was already starting to wear down at six-day,
ten-day intervals, and Temel had been called in far longer, and far
more often, than that.

I thought I understood, now, why he'd seemed so unruffled. He wasn't
unruffled — he'd simply grown used to it. He must have had no choice
but to get used to it. You can't keep being freshly startled, every
time, by something that never ends.

Around then I thought about writing Temel a letter. I didn't know where
he was now, and I had no real way to send a letter anyway. In the end I
didn't write one.

If I'm honest about why I didn't write it: I didn't want to be
comforted by him. Whatever he might have answered, I knew, in the end,
that I had to go through this alone. It must have been the same for
him. Review wasn't something anyone could go through in your place.
That no one can take it on for you might be the most exact definition
of review there is.

My aunt said nothing every time I came back from a review. She made
meals, and I ate them.

One evening she said just one thing.

"…Tell me if it's too hard."

"…I told you, it's not hard."

"…Then what is it."

I couldn't answer. She didn't ask any further either.

At the time, I couldn't find the right word for my own state. Hard
wasn't the right word. Frightening wasn't quite right either. The
closest word was probably "ongoing." It just went on. That was the
nature of review.

Even on days with nothing scheduled, I found myself habitually checking
the notice box. The first thing I did on waking each morning was look
at that box. If it was empty I felt relief; if there was something in
it, I just accepted it.

I only noticed this habit had formed after the fact. At first it was
something I did without being conscious of it. One day it struck me
that checking the notice box had become the very first thing I did
every morning. If reading had once been my old habit, waiting had
become my new one.

My aunt seemed to have noticed this habit too. One morning, seeing me
walk toward the notice box, she said quietly, "…Nothing today. I
already checked." I was so grateful for those words that I couldn't
say anything back.

I thought about these two habits side by side. The habit of reading
had been something I chose. The habit of waiting had formed without
my choosing it. Both had made me who I was.

I might well have been angry that a habit I hadn't chosen had formed
in me, but strangely, I wasn't. This too was a result of having signed
it. When I signed it, I hadn't foreseen this habit specifically, but
just because an unforeseen result had come, I didn't want to take the
signature back.

Review did not hurt the body. It did not lock anyone in a cell. It did
not take anyone's money.

The one thing review did was call people in. And not stop doing so.

I understood, around then, why this was harder to bear than
punishment. Punishment is built on the premise that there's an end.
Serve your term, and it's over. Review isn't built on the premise of
an end. As long as even one case with my name on it remained, a reason
to call me in remained too.

And in this city, deliberations keep coming into being. So, in
theory, I could be called in forever.

It took me time to accept this fact. At first I felt it was unjust.
Having no visible end was a shape unlike any procedure I'd known
before. Nearly every procedure in this city has a start and an end.
Deliberations begin and end in confirmation. But review had no
confirmation. It was a structure that couldn't be confirmed.

I came to see this differently, later. At that point I hadn't reached
that place yet. Back then I was simply enduring this endlessness.

About a month in, I felt my body starting to adapt to the rhythm of
review. When a notice arrived, I no longer felt startled. I just
checked the next date.

I couldn't judge, at the time, whether this adapting was good or bad.
I'm still not sure, even now. Adapting means it has become bearable, but
it also means it has become normal. I didn't want to accept being
called in over and over as normal. But my body was already accepting
it.

About a month and a half in, coming out of my eighth review, I stopped
and counted, on impulse. Eight times. Every one a different person,
every one the same question.

The number eight, by itself, isn't very large. But if I added up the
mornings I spent, between those eight, checking the notice box every
day, that became a far larger number. Review's real weight lay less in
the time actually spent being called in, and more in the time spent
waiting, wondering if I might be. I understood this exactly, around
then.

Around then, Tyche said something to me.

"…We still don't know if this will lead to a new deliberation. It could
just be the procedure continuing on its own."

"…I know."

"…And you'll keep going anyway?"

"…Yeah."

Tyche didn't ask anything further.

I remember this short exchange for a long time. Tyche didn't try to
stop me. She didn't cheer me on either. She just told me one fact,
heard my answer, and let it go. That was Tyche's way of dealing with
people. I didn't feel, at the time, that it was cold.

I never stopped counting after that, either. The moment I stopped
counting, it felt like this would stop being something I'd chosen and
become just something that was happening to me. I kept adding those
numbers to the end of the paper in the drawer.

(Continued in Chapter 43.)

Chapter 43. The New Deliberation

_Previously_ — Review went on with no fixed rule. Six days, ten days,
six days again. Tyche asked, "And you'll keep going anyway?" Hora
answered, "Yeah," and Tyche asked no further.

About two weeks after the eighth review, a notice came.

This one was a different kind. Not a request to appear, but a notice of
outcome. Seventh Classification reexamination matter, deliberation
result confirmed. I looked at that sentence and for a long while
couldn't open the envelope.

I had imagined this moment many times. In my imagination, the hand
opening the envelope trembled. In reality it didn't tremble. I simply
opened it. That my hand didn't tremble felt a little strange to me, in
the moment — but thinking about it now, it was only natural. I'd
already been holding on to this one document alone for two hundred-some
days. I'd grown as used to it as a person could.

The result document ran forty-two pages, with eighteen more pages of
supporting materials attached. I didn't start with the summary. I read
it from beginning to end.

The front section explained the reexamination procedure. It set out how
the new deliberation I'd proposed had been convened, what materials had
been reexamined, and how many people had taken part. This time it
wasn't four hundred, but six hundred and twelve. More people had been
involved in the reexamination than in the original deliberation.

I paused for a moment at this number. I couldn't tell, at the time,
whether more people having looked at it was a good sign or a bad one.
I'm still not certain, even now.

The result appeared on the twelfth page.

Regarding the above reexamination matter, the following is hereby
confirmed. Among the list of relocation-target sites, the unregistered
residences falling under items 3, 7, and 12 are converted to
formally registered sites, the grounds for their provisional
designation having lapsed. Residents of these residences are removed
from the Seventh Classification, and released from relocation-target
status.

I read that sentence three times.

Even read three times, the sentence stayed the same. Items 3, 7, and
12.

I looked further back to find the number of people covered under those
three items. There was a table on the sixth page of the supporting
materials. Ninety-one residents under item 3, sixty-eight under item 7,
fifty-four under item 12. Added together, two hundred and thirteen.

I sat with this number for a long time. It wasn't a small number. I
knew these people would now go on living without hearing the words
unregistered residence attached to their names. That much was real. It
wasn't false.

I turned the page.

Excepting the above items 3, 7, and 12, the remainder of the list
retains the original decision. No grounds for reexamination were
confirmed.

Two hundred and thirteen out of one thousand one hundred and forty.

The remaining nine hundred and twenty-seven stayed as they were. The
document didn't write anything new about those nine hundred and
twenty-seven. It only said "the original decision is retained." There
was nothing after that one line. It didn't re-explain the grounds, and
it didn't apologize. It meant there was no reason to make a new
decision about something already decided.

I sat for a long time that day with those two numbers side by side.
Two hundred and thirteen, and nine hundred and twenty-seven. I knew I
was supposed to feel glad, looking at the side of two hundred and
thirteen. And I really was, a little glad. But I couldn't help looking
at the side of nine hundred and twenty-seven too. That number just sat
there. It hadn't been erased.

Reading this result for the first time, I think I'd expected something
dramatic. Someone who'd done wrong being exposed, that person being
punished, and not just two hundred and thirteen but all one thousand
one hundred and forty coming back. There was no such scene.

I should be honest here. I thought of the old stories I'd heard as a
child. Stories where someone who drove an innocent person to ruin ends
up hanged on the very gallows they built. I did, a little, expect
something like that. I hoped some such name would appear, somewhere in
this result document. Who had first designated item 3 as provisional,
who had decided to leave the rest as they were. No such name ever
appeared, to the very end. There had never been a place for one to
appear. This document had no one who had driven anyone to ruin, so
there could be no scene of that person being punished, either.

I thought again that this was this city's most honest face. Without a
villain, there is no scene of a villain falling. That fact held me for
a long time.

After closing the result document, I walked straight to the area under
item 3. It was a place I'd never been. Low ground, not far from the
water channel. There were a few houses, and laundry hung out, no
different from any other day. I think I'd expected something to have
changed there. A flag hung up, or people gathered and celebrating.

There was nothing. One person was splitting firewood in a yard, and
gave me only a light nod when they saw me. I had no way of knowing
whether that person knew that their name had just been removed from
the unregistered list. I wanted to ask, but couldn't. It felt like a
question I had no right to ask.

Walking past that yard, I thought about when and how this news would
reach each of the two hundred and thirteen people. It would come as a
notice. On the same kind of paper as any other notice, in the same
kind of print. Maybe those people, too, would receive it and be unable
to open the envelope for a long while, the way I had been. Or maybe
they'd just open it, read it, and be done. Either way, I wouldn't see
that moment. I would stop here, and each of them would receive that
paper in their own place, on their own.

The Secretary called me in that afternoon.

"…You'll have seen the result."

"…Yes."

"…This reexamination proceeded according to procedure, and has been
confirmed. If you have an objection, you may raise it again the same
way."

"…What about the remaining nine hundred and twenty-seven?"

"…No grounds for reexamination were confirmed for them. No new
evidence came forward."

"…Why didn't any evidence come forward? They're the same kind of
people."

The Secretary looked at his papers for a moment.

"…There was evidence that items 3, 7, and 12 had been provisional
designations. The other items were formal designations from the start.
That's a different matter."

Hearing this answer, I couldn't find anything to argue back with. The
Secretary hadn't lied to me. He'd told me exactly and fully why those
three items alone had been resolved. That was this city's way. It
doesn't hide anything. And yet I still felt some kind of wronged
feeling. I couldn't put my finger on the shape of that feeling, that
day.

"…So what more can I do now?"

"…You can open another new deliberation for the remaining matter. If
you find new grounds."

"…What more grounds would be needed?"

"…I don't know that either. That's for the reexamination committee to
judge."

Hearing that, I remembered an earlier moment, long before. The
Secretary had answered the same way, then. When I'd asked what happens
if you write your name, he had looked away for the first time. This
time he didn't look away. He was simply saying as much as he knew.

"…What do you think of this result, Secretary?"

The Secretary paused a little at this question.

"…That's not mine to think about. I only convene and pass things on."

"…Even so. Just as a person."

The Secretary looked at me for a moment. There was something in that
look, but I couldn't read what it was, that day.

"…Two hundred and thirteen is a good thing. That much I do think."

"…And the rest?"

"…The rest — it was put in order that way."

I felt a brief catch in that sentence. Very short. I didn't miss that
catch, but I didn't press further, that day. The Secretary moved on to
the next document, and I asked nothing more.

Tyche came that evening. Her face said she'd already seen the result.

"…Two hundred and thirteen."

"…Yeah."

"…That's not a small number. I honestly thought it might not happen at
all."

I was surprised by that.

"…You thought it wouldn't happen?"

"…Yeah. Looking at the statistics — cases where a reexamination
overturns even part of the original decision — even across ten years
of records, there are hardly any. And usually only when a procedural
flaw is obvious. This case wasn't that obvious."

"…But it happened."

"…It did. I still can't fully explain why. I can say it happened
because there happened to be grounds about the provisional
designation, but I don't know if that's all there was to it."

Hearing this, I looked at Tyche again. She, too, was admitting there
was a part she couldn't fully explain. But that admission didn't lead
her straight to a different conclusion. It was the same as before.
Tyche left the hole a hole.

"…And the rest? What do you think about that, Tyche?"

"…That just stays as it is. Because no grounds came forward. I don't
think that's a bad thing. Overturning something without grounds
wouldn't be fair either."

"…But they're people. Nine hundred and twenty-seven of them."

"…That's right. That stays as it is. I don't feel nothing about that
either."

Tyche's expression didn't change as she said this. She was someone who
could say something was sad without wearing a sad face. I knew this
wasn't hypocrisy. Tyche really did carry sadness in exactly that way.

"…I don't know whether I should call this winning or losing."

"…I don't think it's either."

"…Then what is it?"

Tyche thought for a moment.

"…Just that two hundred and thirteen people changed. I don't think
there's anything more than that."

Tyche started to get up, then sat back down.

"…But Hora, I did learn one thing from this."

"…What?"

"…I've told you almost everything can be explained by a vacancy, a
number, and probability. I still think that. But looking purely at
probability, this reexamination was really unlikely to happen. The
procedural flaw wasn't obvious, and there was no guarantee that more
people being involved would flip the result."

"…But it did flip."

"…Yeah. So I file this away as a low probability that happened to be
realized. Low isn't zero, after all. But… "

Tyche paused for a moment.

"…But what?"

"…Every time a low probability gets realized, calling it just
probability feels strange sometimes. I know it's strange of me to say
that. But if I don't file it that way, I fall apart. So I file it that
way."

I sat with this for a long time. Tyche wasn't saying her own logic was
wavering. She was saying that the reason she held onto that logic
wasn't the certainty of the logic itself, but the fact that she'd fall
apart if she didn't. I think that was the most honest thing Tyche ever
gave me.

That night I told my aunt the result. She listened quietly, then asked.

"…So is it good, or not good?"

"…I think it's both."

"…What does that mean."

"…Two hundred and thirteen came through. Nine hundred and twenty-seven
didn't."

My aunt said nothing for a long while, then spoke.

"…So that's the result, then."

"…Yeah."

"…It doesn't feel satisfying."

"…No, it doesn't."

I want to set this exchange down exactly. My aunt didn't try to
comfort me. I didn't try to reassure her either. We simply set those
two numbers side by side and left it there.

That night I took the result document out again and looked all the way
to the last page. There, at the bottom, was a signature box. It was
empty. Empty again, this time too. In the end, no one at all had
confirmed the reexamination, either.

I didn't copy out the list of the nine hundred and twenty-seven names
separately. I wasn't sure I had the right to copy them out. Instead I
wrote that number at the top of the paper in the drawer. 927. I meant
not to erase it.

Writing this chapter, I keep asking myself again. Was this a victory.
I don't think so. If it were a victory, something should have ended.
This hasn't ended. Two hundred and thirteen came back, and nine hundred
and twenty-seven stayed as they were. I won't erase that remaining
number from this record. The moment I erased it, I felt this book
would end up doing the same thing this city has always done.

The next morning, on my way to work, I passed the water channel again.
The water was flowing as usual. I stopped in front of it for a moment
and tried to put yesterday's events in order in my mind. It didn't
come together well.

Two hundred and thirteen people no longer hear the words unregistered.
Nine hundred and twenty-seven still do. I hadn't drawn that line. I had
only opened a new deliberation, and how far that deliberation would
reach hadn't been mine to decide. The three items — 3, 7, and 12 —
had existed first, and the people caught under those items had existed
first. I had only opened a door; who would walk out through that door
wasn't something I got to choose.

Thinking this, I remembered again what Temel had once said. That there
are still things you can do, even in a seat you got only because of a
number. I did what could be done from that seat. That the work only
reached two hundred and thirteen people was part of that work too.

I carried this thought with me through the whole day's work. I handled
the other deliberations assigned to me. I put things in order, passed
them on, confirmed them. Like every other document in this city, those
documents too had signature boxes, and they too were empty. Looking at
those boxes, I wondered whether a day would come when someone filled
them too. That day had not yet come.

At lunchtime, Tyche asked again.

"…So what will you do now? About the remaining nine hundred and
twenty-seven."

"…I don't know. I'll have to keep looking for grounds."

"…And if there aren't any?"

"…I'll still look. And if there aren't any, then there aren't any."

Tyche looked at me for a moment, then nodded. She didn't try to stop
me, and she didn't push me on either. Her face simply accepted that
answer as it was.

*That evening I opened the drawer and looked again at the paper marked

  1. The number I'd written that I wouldn't erase. I left that paper where it was, at the top of the drawer. I didn't want to put an unfinished number away as if it were finished.*

(Continued in Chapter 44.)

Chapter 44. What the Names Mean

_Previously_ — The new deliberation's result came in. Of the one
thousand one hundred and forty, two hundred and thirteen were
released from the Seventh Classification. The remaining nine hundred
and twenty-seven stayed as they were. It wasn't satisfying. Hora
wrote the remaining number in the drawer and decided not to erase it.

It was about two weeks later that I heard Temel had come back to this
city.

The person at the reception desk told me. "…That person who does the
searching for people, you know. He's back again." The moment I heard
that, I dropped what I was doing and went to the north market.

Even now I can't fully explain why I hurried so much, right then.
There wasn't really anything left for me to ask Temel. I just heard the
news, and my body moved before I thought about it.

Temel was in front of the notice board. The same posture as before. The
same bag, only his shoes looked a little more worn.

"…Back again."

Temel saw me and smiled a little.

"…Oh, Hora."

"…Who are you looking for this time?"

"…No, this time it's something else. Just passing through."

"…Just passing through?"

"…Yeah. On my way to another city. Stopped by here on the way."

I didn't believe that. This wasn't a city you'd stop by on the way to
somewhere else. Still, I didn't press him on it.

"…What about your open cases? You said you had three here."

"…All finished. Found one, couldn't find the other two."

"…What about the one you found?"

"…Got word to the family. That's the best moment there is. It doesn't
come often, though."

"…And the two you couldn't find?"

"…Just left the record. Someone might pick it up and keep looking,
someday."

I felt that his way of speaking was exactly the same as before. Calm,
unhurried, never calling a loss a loss. Months had passed, and this
person hadn't changed.

"…So this time you're really just passing through?"

"…Yeah. Nothing to do in this city, this time. But I felt like I
should come here anyway."

"…What does that mean, felt like you should come?"

"…Can't really explain it. Sometimes a thought like that just comes to
me, walking along. Not often."

"…Why?"

Temel gave a small laugh.

"…I don't really know that either."

We sat beside the notice board, as before. I told him everything that
had happened. Two hundred and thirteen, nine hundred and twenty-seven,
everything I'd felt caught between those two numbers. Temel listened
to all of it. Without saying anything, the whole time.

"…That's it? That's your reaction?"

"…What more did you want?"

"…I don't know. Something like — that's amazing."

Temel shook his head.

"…Nothing amazing about it. Two hundred and thirteen is two hundred
and thirteen. No more, no less."

This indifference stung me at first. Looking back now, that was the
most exact reaction Temel could have given me. He didn't turn me into
a hero.

We sat in silence for a while. The market made its usual sounds.
Somewhere far off, someone was haggling.

"…Hora."

"…Yes."

"…You've never asked me, have you. Why your name is Hora."

I was a little surprised by the question.

"…I never thought about it. It's just my name."

"…Never wondered what it means?"

"…I asked my aunt once. She said she doesn't know. My mother chose it,
and she's gone now."

Temel looked out toward the market for a moment before speaking.

"…I met an old record-keeper once. Someone who knew the old languages.
Digging up where names came from was a hobby of his. I asked him about
a few, once."

"…Mine too?"

"…Yeah. He said it comes from a word written ὥρα. It means the hour —
the appointed time."

I couldn't say anything, hearing that.

The appointed time. Hearing those words, I thought back over what I'd
spent sixteen years doing. Reading to the end. Reading to the last
line. Waiting for the last line to come. I know it's a name that was
given by chance. My mother may or may not have known its meaning when
she chose it. And yet, in that moment, I couldn't shake the feeling
that something fit into place.

I thought again of the twelve pages my mother left. I'd read that last
line two years too late. That a person who missed her own appointed
time should be called by the name of the appointed time felt, that
day, a little strange to me. I don't want to say the name led me
somewhere. I don't want to write this record that way. But once I knew
how closely the name and the way I've lived overlapped, I could never
again think of the two as entirely separate.

"…You knew that, and never told me until now?"

"…You never asked."

"…Would you have told me if I had?"

"…Yes. No reason to hide it. Nobody ever asked."

I thought that line summed up this whole city. Nothing hidden. Simply,
no one had asked.

Writing this sentence, I counted how many times I've written something
like it, from the start of this book to now. Not written down, or not
asked. In this city, the reason for not knowing something was always
one of those two. It was the same with the meaning of a name. It hadn't
been hidden. Simply, for sixteen years, no one — not even I — had
asked.

Just then Tyche arrived. She seemed to have heard at the reception desk
that I'd gone to the market, and followed me.

"…What are you two up to?"

"…Talking about what names mean."

"…What names mean?"

I told her, exactly, what I'd just heard. At first Tyche's face was
unmoved.

"…That's what your name meant, Hora? That's interesting."

Temel looked at Tyche for a moment and said.

"…Want me to look up yours too?"

Tyche shrugged.

"…Go ahead. I am curious, I guess."

"…It comes from a word, τύχη. It means chance — luck."

Tyche laughed, hearing that.

At first it was a short laugh. Then, strangely, it went on. I watched
it from beside her. She covered her mouth lightly with one hand and
kept laughing. A little water gathered at the corner of her eyes; I
couldn't tell whether it came from laughing, or from something else.

"…Chance. My name."

She said that and laughed again. This time the sound was smaller.

In that moment I thought of Tyche's grandmother. A story she'd told me
before. Her grandmother had been the kind of person who said there was
a reason this city was one where no one is wronged, and that such a
reason couldn't possibly be chance. Tyche had said she'd grown up
hearing that and spent her whole life checking it, and that nearly all
of it turned out explainable by a vacancy and a number and
probability. I imagined, briefly, what that grandmother would have
said, seeing this moment.

I thought Tyche would say something. She always did. Tyche was someone
who put things into words, whatever they were. This time, even after
the laughter faded, no words came.

She opened her mouth once, then closed it again.

I waited. Temel waited too. Neither of us pressed her.

Tyche, in the end, said nothing at all.

I want to render this silence exactly. Tyche gave me no answer that
day. I don't want to fill that silence with anything. What Tyche was
thinking, I don't know. I didn't ask. That wasn't a question that was
mine to ask.

Writing this part, I felt several temptations. The temptation to write
that something in her shook, in that moment; or, on the other hand,
that her own conviction only hardened further. I decided to write
neither. I couldn't clearly read either one on Tyche's face. Laughter,
and silence — those two things were all I saw. I won't invent anything
beyond that.

A stillness settled over that spot for a moment. A sheet of paper on
the notice board fluttered slightly in the wind.

Beside us, someone in worn clothes walked past at a slow pace, unnoticed
by anyone.

The sound of the market pressed back in around us. Tyche got up first.

"…I'll go ahead. I've still got some work left."

"…Okay."

She said only that, and walked off. Her stride wasn't much different
from usual. Only I watched her back for a little longer than usual.

Watching her go, Temel spoke to me.

"…Guess I brought up something I shouldn't have."

"…No. Tyche's the one who asked you to."

"…That's true, but."

Temel let the end of that sentence trail off. I wondered for a moment
whether he knew something more, then realized he didn't. Temel had
only passed along a name, the same as with me. What that name would
touch inside the person hearing it — even he couldn't have known that.

"…Does telling someone something like this change a person? From what
you've seen."

Temel thought for a moment.

"…Some people change, some don't. I'm just the one who passes it on,
so I don't get to see what comes after. I pass it on and move to the
next city."

"…This time you're not moving to the next city. You're staying to
watch."

"…So I am. Might stick around a little this time, I'm thinking."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. Just a feeling I have."

I felt, then, that Temel was holding back his words more than usual. I
should have asked why, and didn't. Thinking about it now, I think he
too was gauging, in that moment, what he'd just opened up. He'd only
said one name, and he'd watched, right in front of him, that name touch
something inside Tyche.

"…Do you know what your own name means?"

Temel thought for a moment.

"…Never asked."

"…Why not?"

"…Wasn't curious. I'm more used to looking for other people's names
than my own."

That answer felt exactly like him. I didn't press any further.

That evening, at home, I told my aunt about this. She listened quietly,
then said.

"…That's what your name meant?"

"…Yeah. Did Mom know?"

"…I don't know. I don't remember your mother liking that kind of
talk."

"…Aunt, do you think I'm really living out that meaning?"

My aunt, ladling the rice, looked at me.

"…I don't know that either. Some people live out their name's meaning,
and some don't."

I nodded at that. My aunt gave no certainty either. I found that,
somehow, a relief.

"…Why do you think Mom chose that name for me?"

"…Your mother chose it, so you'd have to ask your mother. I don't
know."

"…But if you had to guess."

My aunt set down her spoon for a moment and thought.

"…Your mother was someone who decided things ahead of time about
everything. The day to put away the winter clothes, decided ahead of
time. The day to buy rice, decided ahead of time. So maybe she picked
a name like that for that kind of reason."

"…Is that the same thing as this meaning?"

"…Well, I don't know that either. Just that she was that kind of
person."

Hearing that, I thought of the twelve pages my mother had written. My
aunt might have been right. My mother was someone who decided things
ahead of time. And yet the very last sentence she left for me hadn't
been placed somewhere fixed in advance — it had been placed somewhere
it could easily be missed.

"…Did you hear about Tyche?"

"…You just told me. Laughed, and said nothing, you said."

"…What do you think about that?"

My aunt thought for a moment, then spoke.

"…I know people like that. People who laugh and then can't speak.
That's not a bad thing. It just means there's nothing to say, in that
moment."

"…Isn't having nothing to say a bad thing?"

"…No. Having nothing to say is an answer too, Hora. Don't keep trying
to squeeze an answer out of her."

I thought about that for a long time. I think I'd been treating Tyche's
silence as if it were something lacking. Only after hearing my aunt's
words did I come to think, again, that the silence itself might be
whole on its own.

The next morning I went to work as usual. The meeting room was the
same as always. Tyche, too, was out as usual, sorting through
materials. She said nothing about the day before. Neither did I bring
it up first.

"…What's the schedule today?"

"…Two requests to view records in the morning, one meeting in the
afternoon."

"…Got it."

We started the day as usual, that way. On the surface, nothing about
the day had changed. I was, if anything, grateful for that
ordinariness. It meant Tyche wasn't avoiding me over what had happened
the day before.

From that day on, I came to hear my own name differently than before.
Every time someone called out, "Hora," its meaning would flicker past,
just for an instant. The hour. The appointed time. I still don't know
whether that's true or not. Only that, after that, I could never hear
it again the way I once had, meaning nothing at all.

Tyche never brought up her own name's meaning to me again, after that
day. I never asked either. That silence continues on for a long time
still to come.

Writing this chapter, I set the two names down side by side. The
hour, the appointed time. Chance, luck. The two words look like
opposites. What is appointed, and what is not. And yet I'm still not
certain, even now, whether the two are really opposites. An appointed
hour can still, for someone, arrive as a moment of pure chance; and
chance, looked back on later, can look as if it had been appointed all
along. I won't decide, in this book, which of the two is right. I'll
only set down, exactly, that the two were spoken that day, in the same
place, from the same person's mouth, side by side.

(Continued in Chapter 45.)

Chapter 45. Tyche's Reprieve

_Previously_ — Temel told the two of them what their names meant.
Hora — the hour, the appointed time. Tyche — chance, luck. Tyche
laughed on hearing it, and after laughing, could say nothing at all.
The next morning, the two of them worked as usual.

For several days after that, Tyche was largely no different than
usual.

She sorted materials, sat in on meetings, ate lunch with me. The
subject of names never came up again, not once. I thought that was
Tyche's own way of doing things. Letting some things simply pass.

During those few days I wanted to ask her, several times, why she
hadn't been able to say anything, that day. I didn't ask. Writing this
book, I've thought many times that holding that question back was the
right thing to do. I still think so now.

Temel left this city the next morning. I couldn't go to see him off. I
had a backlog of notices to process. I heard later from the person at
the reception desk that he'd shouldered his bag and gone quietly, as
usual. Hearing that news, I felt a pang, but soon acknowledged that this
was just like him. He was a person who moved on without leaving
anything behind. This time, though, he'd left behind two names.

One thing, though, had changed. Before this, whenever Tyche organized
deliberation materials, she liked to cite probability and statistics.
That this agenda had turned out this way because of how many people
were involved, and so the probability was high, that kind of thing.
During those few days, Tyche never once used that word. I only noticed
on the third day.

"…Tyche, you haven't been talking about probability lately."

"…Have I not? I hadn't noticed."

"…You really haven't. I counted."

Tyche laughed a little.

"…You really do count things like that, Hora."

Then she changed the subject. I didn't press her further.

I've thought about this small change many times since. Whether not
using the word probability meant something, or whether there had
simply happened to be no material that week calling for it — I never
did find out, in the end. I could have asked Tyche again, and I didn't.
I felt it was right to leave some things unconfirmed.

On the sixth day, Tyche came to find me first.

It was near the end of the workday. Only the two of us were left in
the meeting room. Tyche stood for a moment with the papers she'd been
organizing still in her hand, then spoke.

"…Hora."

"…Yeah."

"…I've been thinking, ever since then."

I stopped what I was doing. Tyche's face looked like she'd come
prepared with something. She hadn't brought materials the way she
usually did. She'd come empty-handed.

"…I've always said that wanting there to be a meaning, and believing
there is one, are different things."

"…Yeah."

"…That still hasn't changed. It's the same now."

Hearing that, I tensed a little. I wondered if Tyche had come to lay
out some conclusion.

"…But the truth is, that day, hearing what my name meant, I did laugh.
I still don't really know why it was funny to me."

"…Why do you think it was funny?"

Tyche thought for a moment.

"…Probably because it fit too well. I'm someone who's said, my whole
life, that everything is chance — and my own name literally meant
chance. That struck me as absurd."

"…But what that means, I still don't know, even now."

I nearly asked what she meant by that, then held back. Tyche went on.

"…The reason I couldn't say anything after I laughed was that I didn't
know what to say. I felt like I should say something, but I didn't know
what. That had never happened to me before."

"…Do you know now?"

"…No. I still don't know. That's why I came. To tell you I don't
know."

I expected, in that moment, that Tyche would say something different.
Maybe I even hoped for it, a little. That she thought there might be
meaning after all. That the probability of a name fitting this well
was too low. That she might fill, differently this time, the hole
she'd admitted to before, further back.

Tyche walked to the window. The one that faced the water channel. The
sun was setting, and the light outside the window was red.

"…I've worked it out like this. The person who chose my name was my
parents. It's unlikely my parents knew the Greek word τύχη when they
chose it. My family weren't people who'd studied that kind of thing.
Probably they liked the sound of it, or it was a name they'd heard
somewhere."

"…So the meaning attached itself by chance."

"…Yeah. The meaning attached itself by chance. Actually, I even ran
the numbers on it."

"…You ran the numbers?"

"…Yeah. Roughly how many names are used in this city, how many of
those carry a Greek meaning, and of those, what the odds are that the
meaning would happen to match the person's character. I calculated it,
roughly."

"…And?"

"…Low. Low, but not zero. About that."

I thought, in that moment, how like Tyche this was. Even in a moment
like this, she was someone who didn't let go of calculation. But what
came after that calculation surprised me.

"…But…"

Tyche stopped. I waited.

"…That I grew up to be someone who believes in probability and
chance the way I do now — I think all of that can be explained too.
My grandmother's words, the environment I grew up in, the work I
love. All of it has a reason. It's not because of my name."

"…But?"

Tyche turned to look at me.

"…But that my name happened to mean that, and that I happen to be
living out that meaning — calling even that a coincidence has started
to feel a little hard for me now."

Tyche kept looking out at the water channel for a while. I didn't
press her. The clock in the meeting room sounded unusually loud. I
thought, over those few days, that Tyche hadn't been sleeping well at
night. The skin under her eyes was darker than usual.

"…Actually, I meant to come say this yesterday too, and didn't."

"…Why not?"

"…I couldn't get it sorted out, what to say. It's still not fully
sorted out today either, but I came anyway."

I held my breath and waited for what would come next. I felt something
large was coming.

Tyche looked straight at me and said.

"…I suppose my name meaning that is a coincidence too. Isn't it?"

I opened my mouth to answer that question.

Tyche didn't wait for my answer.

She turned around just as she was, gathered her papers, shouldered her
bag, and walked toward the door.

"…See you tomorrow."

Her voice was no different than usual.

The door closed.

I stayed alone in that room for a long while. Outside the window it
had gone fully dark by now. The sound of the water channel came in
over the windowsill.

I looked at the chair Tyche had been sitting in. The papers she'd been
holding still lay on the table. I gathered them up and put them in the
file box. Something Tyche would ordinarily have done herself.

Putting those papers away, I felt strangely urgent. I wanted to chase
after Tyche and finish giving her my answer. I felt that if I opened
the door and went out, I could probably still catch up to her. I
didn't. I already knew, in that moment, that if Tyche had wanted an
answer, she would have waited.

I stood there for a long while. What I'd meant to say stayed right
there in my mouth. I never told anyone that, in the end. I don't intend
to write it down in this record either.

Turning off the light and leaving the meeting room, I ran into the
caretaker in the hallway. He looked at me and asked.

"…Why are you leaving so late today?"

"…Oh, a conversation ran a little long."

"…What about?"

"…Just, about names."

The caretaker nodded, not looking particularly curious, and walked on.
I was strangely grateful for that indifference, that day. That the
world was turning as unremarkably as ever — that short exchange
confirmed it to me again.

I've rewritten this scene many times. Each time I wanted to revise it
so that I answered her with something before Tyche walked out the
door. But that wasn't the truth. Tyche really did leave without waiting
for an answer. I decided to leave that fact exactly as it was.

Tyche hadn't asked that question wanting to be persuaded. Nor had she
asked to be refuted. Even now I don't know exactly who that question
was aimed at. It could have been me. It could have been Tyche herself.
It could have been no one at all.

At the very front of this record, I meant to write that this book was
the story of two people arriving at different conclusions over the
same document. Writing this chapter, I feel again how exact that was.
Tyche and I heard the same two names on the same evening, from the same
person's mouth. I saw something in them. Tyche saw a question. Neither
of us was wrong.

The next day Tyche really did come in as usual.

"…Good morning."

"…Yeah, good morning."

Neither of us said anything about the day before. I didn't bring it up
either. We sorted materials as usual that day, and ate lunch as usual.

At the restaurant, Tyche said, out of nowhere.

"…Hora, remember what you told me before. That maybe nothing changes,
but even so, it won't be empty."

"…Yeah, I remember."

"…That kept coming back to me, strangely, this whole time. I think
yesterday's question was something like that too. My answer doesn't
change. I still believe in probability. But I think it's better that I
asked that question than that I never did."

"…Why?"

"…Because once I asked it, it stopped being nothing at all. It's not
empty anymore."

I couldn't say anything back, hearing that. Tyche was handing my own
words back to me, in her own way.

"…So you've filled something too, then."

"…Filled what?"

"…That question. Like a signature box."

Tyche gave a short laugh at that.

"…It's not that big a deal. But, well, maybe it's something like
that."

We finished eating after that, with nothing more said. The restaurant
was crowded as usual, and no one paid us any attention.

I don't convert Tyche in this book. I have no intention of doing so.
Tyche lives on, even after asking that question, as someone who still
believes in probability and procedure. Only, I think that question
probably never entirely vanished inside her. If it had vanished, she
wouldn't have asked it to begin with.

When I told my aunt this story, she was quiet for a long while.

"…So what did you answer?"

"…I couldn't. Tyche didn't wait."

"…Good."

"…Why?"

My aunt set down her rice bowl.

"…Some questions aren't meant to get an answer. You just say them to
someone."

"…Then why say it at all?"

"…So you can move on to what comes next. Not saying it and moving on,
and saying it without hearing an answer and moving on — those are
completely different things."

"…How are they different?"

My aunt thought for a moment.

"…If you don't say it, it's as if the question never existed. If you
say it, even without hearing an answer, the question stays. Somewhere."

I turned that over. I thought about where Tyche's question was, right
now. In the air of that meeting room. Inside Tyche herself. Or inside
me. I couldn't settle on any single one of those. Probably it was all
three.

I thought about it for a long time. I still can't be certain whether
Tyche's question was really that kind of question. But I do know she
would have waited if she'd wanted an answer. Tyche was a person who
waited. Always. Only that day, she didn't.

"…Aunt, you've never even met Tyche. How do you know her so well?"

"…I don't know Tyche. I know that there's a kind of person like that.
A person who asks a question and then runs. I was like that too, when
I was young."

"…You were?"

"…Yeah. I once asked your mother a question, just one time, and left
without hearing the answer."

I'd never heard this before.

"…What was the question?"

My aunt laughed a little.

"…I'm not going to tell you that. That one's mine."

I didn't press any further. I realized, for the first time that day,
that my aunt had a drawer of her own too. And that maybe everyone in
this city was carrying, somewhere, one question they'd never been able
to speak.

That evening I opened the drawer and looked again at my mother's twelve
pages, and the short notes I'd kept there all this time. I didn't
write Tyche's question down on paper. It wasn't the kind of thing that
could be written down. Instead of writing it, I decided just to
remember it, for a long time.

I gave this chapter the title "Reprieve." A postponing of judgment.
That night, Tyche postponed judgment on herself. She didn't say it
wasn't chance. Nor did she reconfirm that it was. She just left one
question behind and went. I don't know when that reprieve will end.
Maybe it never will. I think that, too, is Tyche's own to keep.

Several more weeks passed after that. Tyche was still someone who
spoke of probability. Still precise, still kind. Only, from that night
on, Tyche gained a habit of pausing mid-conversation, sometimes. Very
briefly. A habit she'd never had before.

I never asked what that pause was. It might have been nothing at all.
It might just have been that Tyche had grown a little more careful,
since that day.

I wrote, somewhere in this book, that I wasn't going to try to win
against Tyche. I keep that promise here too. Tyche hasn't changed. If
anything has changed, it's only that Tyche now carries, with her, one
question she asked herself. I think that's enough. Maybe that's the
whole of what one person can do, in this city.

(Continued in Chapter 46.)

Chapter 46. A Document with a Name on It

_Previously_ — Tyche asked, "…I suppose my name meaning that is a
coincidence too. Isn't it?" And left without waiting for an answer.
Hora, in the end, said nothing at all. A few days later, the two of
them were back at work as usual.

About a month after Tyche left without waiting for any answer, the
reading room clerk called me over one day.

"…Hora, could you take a look at this?"

What the clerk held out was an official document from another city. It
was an exchange record between deliberation districts, the kind of
thing that circulated occasionally. This one was marked as a case study
in administrative procedure improvement.

"…What about it?"

"…Your name's in the signature box here, Hora."

I took the document from her right there.

It bore another city's name. Somewhere I'd never been. At the very
bottom of the document, in the signature box, was written "Hora." The
handwriting wasn't mine. The stroke order was different, and so was
the angle of the strokes.

"…I didn't write this."

"…What? Then who—"

"…I don't know. I've never even been to that city."

The clerk checked the screen.

"…The receipt date was three months ago. You were here then, weren't
you?"

"…Yes. I was here, undergoing review, at that time."

"…Then it's definitely not you."

"…Yes, definitely not me."

I felt something cold pass through me, in that moment. It wasn't quite
the same as fear. More than that, it was simply strange to me that my
own name had been written somewhere I knew nothing about.

I studied that handwriting for a long while. The strokes were long and
straight. My own writing tends to be short and a little crooked. My
aunt always said my writing looked rushed. This handwriting wasn't
rushed. It was neat, careful, as if it had been practiced.

That neatness bothered me strangely. When I'd written my own name
before, I'd just written it the way I write anything, ordinarily. That
was the moment I wrote took about three seconds. This person seemed to
have written those two syllables far more carefully, far more slowly
than I had. I tried, for a moment, to imagine what that person must
have felt, sitting in front of that box. The imagining didn't last
long. I knew too little.

The clerk didn't take it very seriously.

"…Probably just someone with the same name. There are a few other
Horas in this city too."

"…There are?"

"…Yes, it's not a common name, but it's not so rare either."

I nodded at that explanation. It sounded plausible. The same name,
different person. There are plenty of people in the world who share a
name. I let it go, that day.

Writing this chapter, I don't regret not pressing further, that day.
Even if I had, no answer probably would have come of it. I simply
want to set down exactly what happened that day.

That night I read the contents of the document again. Before, I'd
only skimmed the result and moved on; this time I read it to the end.
It was habit.

The content concerned a registration reexamination. In that city too,
there had been people bound up in some classification, a new
deliberation had been opened, and only part of them had been
reclassified. Not all — only part. I found that shape familiar.

I realized, that day, for the first time, how closely this shape
resembled what I had gone through. Not all, only part. The same
pattern. I couldn't tell whether this was chance, or whether such
deliberations simply tended, as a rule, to end this way. I had no way
of confirming it.

Then, two weeks later, another document arrived.

This time it was from a different city. A different region again from
the first. The signature box again bore the name "Hora." This time the
handwriting was different again, different even from the first.

I laid the two documents side by side. The same name, two different
hands. There was something strange about it, even setting aside a mere
shared name. I grew curious what these names were doing. I read through
the contents of both documents again.

Both cases were similar. A case study in administrative procedure
improvement. Reexamining an existing decision, and reconfirming part
of it anew. Documents received with the signature box filled.

I went to the reception desk and asked.

"…How far are these cities from here?"

"…Far. One's about ten days away, and the other is even farther than
that."

"…Can you find out whether anyone from those places has ever come
here?"

"…We have no way to check that. We can't see the roster of another
city."

"…Then can you at least confirm that this really wasn't me?"

"…The handwriting is different, so that much is confirmed. But beyond
that, there's no way for us to look into it further."

I stood there a moment, hearing that answer. This city hides nothing.
But this time, it wasn't a matter of something being hidden — there
simply was no way to know, to begin with.

That evening, the first person I thought of was Temel. As far as I
knew, he was the only person who traveled between cities.

I held onto this thought for a long while. But I soon realized it
didn't add up. The first document's receipt date was three months
before. Temel hadn't even come to this city yet, at that time. It was
much later — the day he told us what our names meant — that he'd
arrived. There was no reason, and no time, for him to have written my
name into another city's document in advance.

I set down here that I suspected him. And I set down, alongside it,
that the suspicion was wrong. To this day I still don't know who wrote
those two documents. All I know is that it wasn't Temel.

When I told Tyche about this, she was unimpressed at first.

"…Isn't it just someone with the same name? It's not that unusual a
name."

"…But it's two of them. In two different cities. Both filled in the
signature box. The box that nobody here fills."

Tyche's face grew a little thoughtful at that.

"…That is a little strange. Filling in the signature box is rare
enough on its own — that two people with the same name would both have
done it."

"…Right."

"…But that's not something probability can't explain either. There's
exchange between these cities, so word that one of them had filled it
in first could have spread to the other. Someone might have followed
suit."

"…Followed my name?"

"…Not your name specifically — it could just be that two people who
happened to both be named Hora each did it on their own."

"…But why now, of all times? It's been since I signed."

Tyche took a moment before answering that one.

"…That's explainable too. That you signed it, Hora, might have become
known among the people of this deliberation district, and spread to
other cities from there, and a few people followed suit. Since filling
in the signature box is so rare, word that someone had done it might
well have traveled far."

"…So it started because of me."

"…Not started, exactly — more like, triggered. You didn't tell anyone
to do it."

I thought that distinction was fair. I hadn't told anyone to do
anything. And yet the very possibility that something I'd done might
have moved someone else's hand, somewhere I'd never see, felt heavy to
me all the same.

I couldn't fully believe that explanation, but I had no grounds to
argue against it either. Tyche's account, once again, was the simplest
explanation there was.

I won't try to win against Tyche on this point either. She might well
be right. Shared names, rumor, an overlap of coincidences. All of it
could happen. I only want to set down this one fact exactly. I never
wrote the names on those two documents. That's the one thing I know
for certain.

I told the Secretary about this too. He studied the two documents for
a long while.

"…Has anything like this happened before?"

"…Not as far as I know."

"…So this is the first time?"

"…As far as I know. I have no way of checking the records of other
deliberation districts."

"…Is there any way to check?"

"…No. Each deliberation district manages its own records separately.
There's no place where everything is gathered together."

Hearing that answer, I thought back to something I'd heard earlier.
That no one holds the whole picture. Back then, it had been said about
the people who called Temel in. Now I was hearing the same thing said
about my own name.

"…Secretary, so there's no way at all to look into this?"

"…Not at the deliberation-district level. If you wish, you could raise
it as a matter for full review."

"…So, another new deliberation?"

"…Yes. The same procedure."

Hearing that, I hesitated. I'd already been through one new
deliberation. I knew, in my body, what rhythm review moved to. That
day, I didn't have the confidence to start that rhythm all over again
for this.

"…Are you not going to open one?"

"…I don't know. Not right now."

The Secretary added nothing to that answer. He simply nodded once and
put his papers in order.

"…Doesn't this strike you as strange, Secretary?"

"…It strikes me as strange."

That short answer surprised me. It was rare for the Secretary to say
anything carrying feeling.

"…But finding it strange doesn't give me anything more I can do about
it."

I nodded at that. In this city, the distance between finding something
strange and being able to do something about it always went uncrossed,
that way.

A few days later I went to the reading room and looked again into how
many records existed, in this city, of the signature box ever being
filled. The one person eleven years before. Temel. And me. That was the
entire record for this city. Records from other cities I had no way
to see, here.

I laid those three names side by side and looked at them for a long
while. All three, without knowing one another, had each filled the
same box for their own separate reasons. I tried, several times, to
connect those three names to the unfamiliar handwriting bearing my own
name from other cities. It wouldn't connect. I decided to stop trying.

The reading room clerk, tidying up, asked me.

"…What are you looking for?"

"…Just counting how many people in this city have ever filled the
signature box."

"…How many is it?"

"…Three."

The clerk looked a little surprised.

"…That's few. I only just learned that too, though. I've worked here
for years, and I've never once counted that."

"…Nobody ever counts it?"

"…No reason to. Everyone just passes over that box without looking,
generally."

Hearing that, I thought again of how, in this city, there are always
one of two reasons for not knowing something. It isn't written down, or
nobody asked. This time there was a third. Nobody had ever counted.

That night I put both documents in the drawer. Beside my mother's
paper, beside Temel's name, beside the notes of these two hundred-some
days.

When I told my aunt about it, she studied the documents for a long
while, then spoke.

"…Is this supposed to be a frightening story?"

"…I don't know. I don't even know if it's frightening or not."

"…Are you sure you didn't write it?"

"…Yes, I'm sure."

My aunt handed the documents back to me.

"…Then just write that down. That you didn't write it. The rest isn't
something you can know."

"…But Aunt, I have this strange feeling."

"…What kind of feeling."

"…Like something keeps going even without me. Like something I did is
still walking around out there, without me."

My aunt looked at me for a moment, then spoke.

"…Is that a bad feeling?"

"…I don't know. It doesn't feel bad, but it's not comfortable either."

"…Your mother probably felt that too. She probably never imagined that
last line she wrote would sit in a drawer for two whole years without
her, and then finally reach you. But it got there in the end, didn't
it."

I couldn't say anything back to that. My aunt sometimes pointed out,
just like that, connections I hadn't seen.

I thought she was right. I don't know why these two documents exist. I
don't know who wrote them. There's only one thing I know. I didn't
write them.

Writing this record now, I have to set down one more thing. In these
last few days, while writing this chapter, a third document arrived.
From a city farther still than the first two, different from both. The
content was similar too. A registration reexamination, a partial
reclassification, my name written in the signature box.

Only after receiving this third document did I accept that this
wasn't a coincidence happening once or twice, but something continuing
to happen. Tyche would probably say even three times can be explained
by probability. She probably would. I can't argue against that
explanation. I only set down, alongside it, that I no longer know when
this will stop, or whether it will continue at all.

When I began writing this record, I already knew where this story
would end. But that a new document would arrive while I was writing
this very chapter — even I hadn't known that in advance. I decided not
to invent anything, and to put it in exactly as it happened.

Before closing this chapter, I want to leave this fact exactly as it
stands, in this record. The only time I ever wrote my name in a
signature box was that once, in this city, on that one document. And
yet my name now appears in at least three other documents, in three
other cities. I didn't write those names. Someone is filling the same
box. Who that is, I never managed to find out, in the end. This record
ends without giving that answer.

I know this is the most uncomfortable part of this book. The reader
will probably want to guess the answer before I do. I wanted that too.
But I close this chapter not knowing the answer. If there's one thing
this city has taught me, it's not to fill in what I don't know as if I
did. That's exactly like this city's signature box. When it's empty,
you leave it empty.

(Continued in Chapter 47.)

Chapter 47. The Secretary

_Previously_ — Hora's name appeared on documents from three other
cities. She wrote none of them. Who did, she never managed to find
out. This city hides nothing, but this time there simply was no way
to know, to begin with.

The Secretary came to find me around the end of the workday.

It was within working hours. Unlike that night, long before. That time
we'd first met outside of working hours; this time it was an ordinary
weekday afternoon. He came carrying a single folder. Nothing about him
seemed different from usual.

"…Hora, do you have a moment."

"…Yes."

This meeting was unlike any review I'd had before. No one had called me
in. He had come to find me on his own. That had never happened before.
It was an unusually quiet afternoon. The other seats were already
empty, and the late sunlight was coming in long through the window. I
watched that light and thought, briefly, that the light must have
looked something like this the very first time I'd walked into this
building.

We sat in an empty meeting room. The Secretary set the folder down on
the table. But he didn't open it.

"…I'm not here about paperwork today."

I was a little surprised. He had never come to find me without a
matter of business before.

"…Then what is it?"

"…It will soon be fifteen years in this post."

"…That long?"

"…Yes. Since before Hora was born."

His expression barely changed as he said this. It was the same face as
always.

"…How many people have passed through this seat, in that time? Assistant
members, like Hora."

"…No count was ever kept, but it's probably over a hundred."

"…Are all of them remembered?"
